NATURAL PUNISHMENT 

"You've got the mountain on one side and the swamp on the other and only a little strip to cultivate. 

But you've got a good sheltered place fer raisin' cattle. 

They'd do well if you'd a good barn. You ought to fix that roof and that basement wall, too. Once I tried to mend a roof like that, but it was cheaper to build it all new." 

William Reed, tall, slim, with unkempt hair and beard, glared through horn-rimmed glasses at rotund, jovial-looking George Clay, who had come to buy calves, and utterly unconscious of the resentment he was stirring, continued to gaze at the tumble-down farm buildings and rambled on. "I got out timbers from the swamp in the winter and had my barn all done afore hayin'. You can trade logs fer lumber and shingles. Look at that kitchen roof, too! Your women folks-" "Stop, George Clay," shouted Reed in a shrill voice. "Don't you come over here to snoop into my way of living. You've got the better part of my place; but I've no use for your advice-nor a cent of your money." 

Clay recoiled as if hit and his bald head turned crimson. "Now, Reed," he began, “don't take it that way. I didn't mean to stir ye. I had no idee you were still mad because I bought the farm of Van Alstyne.

After your sister and her husband went back to the city and put the land up to sell, you oughtn't to care who bought it." "Never mind the farm, but don't you come round here meddling," rejoined Reed; and he turned and walked toward the barnyard gate to indicate that he wished to end the interview.

Clay mounted his wagon and took the reins. "I hope there ain't no hard feelin', Mr. Reed. Bein' a neighbor,

I suppose I spoke tolerable free, but I didn't mean no harm." He paused and gazed at Reed with a conciliating look. "I wouldn't have come to buy your calves if

I'd thought I was goin' to stir your grudge. We're both of us getting too old to cherish hard thoughts." 

Reed gave no answer, but slammed the barnyard gate and fastened the rusty wire that held it shut. 

Clay's horses had begun to move slowly toward the highway when they were suddenly checked. "Whoa! 

Well, I'll be goldarned! I almost forgot that pesky little rascal. Joe! What's become of Joe? Amanda said I'd go off and leave him some day. Joe!" he called again.

A woman of spare figure came to the door of the house. "Dora took the little boy down to the road to pick dandelions." 

Down at the bridge two or three hundred feet distant where the highway crossed the sluggish stream was a meadow golden with dandelions. There Clay saw a straw hat flying about merrily. "Joe! Joe!" he called more vigorously; and the straw hat became still and disappeared. "The little rascal hears me and won't answer," the farmer chuckled to himself. 

Again he called, "Joe, you come here!" and to emphasize his command brought the butt of his whip 1

NATURAL PUNISHMENT 

9 against the side of the wagon-box with a resounding crack. His gesture had no effect on the child but the horses plunged forward and ran speedily down the rough slope to the bridge, the heavy wagon careening from side to side in imminent danger of capsizing and the driver shouting at the top of his voice and cursing mildly until at the bridge he brought the excited team with difficulty to a stop.

No child was in sight but tell-tale bunches of dandelion blossoms lay on the bridge.

Suddenly from beneath a log in the abutment issued a youngster shouting, "There's a phoebe's nest down here with four birds in it, and a mamma muskrat just swam along with grass in her mouth. And Dora knows where a turtle has laid its eggs.' "Never mind the turtle's eggs. Get into this wagon before these blasted horses back me off this bridge."

At that instant another head emerged from beneath the logs, a head with golden hair and shy but mischievous eyes. Even the restive horses did not prevent the farmer's pausing. He inquired playfully, "Is your name Dora?” "Yes, Dora Gregory Reed," she responded gravely. "Well, my name's Clay; but I'll have to cut my visitin' short fer these horses ain't feelin' sociable. 

Come on, you young rascal." "I want to see the turtle's eggs.' 99 "Come on," and the farmer, leaning over the side of the wagon, seized the small jacket and the struggling boy came in willy-nilly. 

While the wagon was drawing away the little girl shouted mischievously, "Come back to see the turtle's eggs.' 22

As the horses trotted briskly along the road both boy and man looked back. The boy saw the childish figure still standing on the bridge and swinging her sunbonnet. The father gazed at the rocky hill that rose steep behind the shabby house, the narrow strip of meadow, and the great stretches of dark hemlock swamp. "Strange man, that Reed," he muttered. "Things never go well with him. He can't do nothin' right but fiddle his violin; and he blames me because his sister done him wrong."

One July afternoon when Dora was eight years old Mr. and Mrs. Hamblin came down from the hill behind the Reed homestead and asked Mrs. Reed to go to town to do her shopping. Mr. Reed soon after took his scythe across the brook to cut the marsh grass. 

Dora, left alone, sat in her playhouse under the large cherry tree and prepared to give medicine to a sick doll. 

At a slight sound she raised her eyes. Before her, standing confidently, she saw a bronzed and ruddyfaced barefooted boy of about ten years of age. "I'm Joe Clay," he explained; "I've been fishing for chubs in your crick." For a moment he gazed expectantly. "Don't you remember how we found a phoebe bird's nest under your bridge?" 

If Dora did remember, she confirmed the recollection with no sign, but looked down at her dolls or at most sent a timid glance from under long brown lashes. "Look," said Joe, as he unrolled a burdock leaf and displayed a little red salamander. "See how quick he turns over. I found him under a log at the edge of the swamp." "There's lots of them under the stones in the gully," exclaimed Dora brightly.

NATURAL PUNISHMENT 

11 "There ain't any phoebes under the bridge. I was looking when your mother went away.' "They're hatched and gone but there's a nest in the barn." 

Into the barn the children hastened. Swallows, twittering as they flew, darted back and forth high among the rafters. In the lofty window at the peak of the roof, a dark speck in the dusty beam of sunlight, sat a phoebe giving its nervous call. "See the nest," exclaimed Dora pointing to a high beam near the window; and without waiting for a reply she hastened up a ladder fastened to an upright, crossed the mow of hay, and again went upward by means of a row of pegs set in a vertical timber until she stood beside the nest and only two or three feet distant.

When Joe who had crowded after her waited his chance to see the four pale pink eggs, she made room by leaping with a laugh back to the soft hay ten or twelve feet below. Joe quickly followed. After many such leaps the children placed a pile of hay on the barn floor and tobogganed over the edge of the mow. They waded in the bin of oats; they ran the old fanning mill and filled the air with chaff and dust from the floor.

They found with glee that Joseph was strong enough to draw the light two-wheeled road cart that Mr. Reed drove to town when he went alone. While Dora shouted lustily at her somewhat frisky steed, Joseph raced back and forth first on the broad floor and then out on the level plot before the barn. When finally the logic of the game required that the horse should run away, Joseph went rather too far toward the declivity leading down to the highway; and as he attempted to stop, the vehicle overturned.

Mr. Reed, who had been dimly conscious of unwonted shouts and laughter, started for his home at a run when he heard Dora's scream. Joe, meanwhile, with blood trickling from his nose had been able to extricate himself and drag out Dora who had fallen behind the dash and was little hurt. Mr. Reed arrived just as Dora rose to her feet, sobbing with terror both because of the accident and the appearance of Joe's bloody face. Mr. Reed's own appearance was scarcely less terrifying. His eyes seemed to be starting from their sockets with excitement. "Are you hurt, Dora," he shouted; and then to see that there were no broken bones swung her arms and made her walk a few steps back and forth. "No, it's Joe," she screamed, "look at his face." "That's nothing," said Joe calmly. "I've just got a little blood running from my nose." And he jumped around and swung his arms to demonstrate his soundness. 

Mr. Reed's breath came in gasps and he sank upon the bench under the cherry tree weak from excitement and exertion. He said, "Take him to the house, Dora, to wash his face; and bring me a glass of water." 

Dora returned quickly, and Mr. Reed soon felt stronger, and made his way slowly up the hill. He met 

Joe coming from the porch looking much the better for his use of cold water and soap. "Joe Clay," he said with difficulty, "once I told your father to keep off my place I don't want either of you to come here again—”

He would have said more, but found the effort too difficult, and disappeared with Dora into the house. 

At supper that evening Joe's father asked him to account for a black eye and swollen nose. He said, "I went to William Reed's brook to fish for chubs and

NATURAL PUNISHMENT 

13 ANDA 

I got to playing with Dora. Nobody was home and I drew her around in their two-wheeled cart. It ran away from me going down the hill and tipped over on us. Dora wasn't hurt, but it broke a thill." 

Mr. Clay did not speak at once. Finally he said, "I'm sorry, Sonny; I got into trouble myself on that same hill. Probably you don't remember it. It was when you was a little chap. Did you offer to pay William 

Reed fer the thill?" "No." "I hope you didn't run away?” "No; Mr. Reed was excited and I didn't have much chance to talk." "Well, you must go back tomorrow and pay him.” "I don't want to go to Reed's." "You must. I don't blame you fer breakin' his cart, fer I know you didn't mean no mischief. But if you've done him injury, you've got to face him like a man and make good the damage. "Reed's a mean old fellow. I don't want to see him. 

Send Theodore over to take the money," Joe shouted in angry tones. "You will go yourself," the father answered firmly, "and Theodore will keep on with his work."

Joe made no further reply, but resolved to find some way of avoiding the disagreeable interview. Theodore 

Cobb, who with his wife Amanda and daughter

Sophrona had lived in the Clay house after the death of Joe's mother, was a source of aid in all emergencies. 

Joe followed him to the barn when he went to do the milking, but Theodore was deaf to argument.

The next morning, nevertheless, Joe approached his father with an air of business. He proposed that he sell a heifer calf which he had been given to raise, and

that part of the money be used to pay for the thills and the remainder be given to Theodore to induce him to go after his day's work in Joe's stead to settle for the accident.

Joe's father shook his head and answered solemnly, "No, Joey, there ain't nothin' else to be done. You have got to go and settle with Mr. Reed yourself. I can see you're dreadful set agin it. But 'twouldn't be right to give it up jest because it's so much agin your taste. I don't like to have you go alone if it's bitter to you, so I'll go along. I'll stay by ye; but 'twas your accident, and you should stan' up and settle with Mr. 

Reed like a man."

Neither Mr. Clay nor Joe was in the mood for conversation as they drove along the swamp road. The father brooded over the disagreeable necessity of compelling his son to undertake duties that were unpleasant, and Joe was cherishing angry and rebellious thoughts. 

They found Mr. Reed on his knees before the barn busily engaged in an attempt to splice the broken thill with a strap. "Good morning, Mr. Reed," said Joe bravely. "I've come to pay for breaking your cart." 

Reed rose to his feet. "I told you once I don't want you to come here for that or any other reason," he answered angrily. "I won't take your money.' "I didn't mean to break the thills and I'd like to pay," continued Joe in weakening tones. "The boy has his own money, Mr. Reed; I think it would be a lesson to him if you'd let him make good the damage," urged Mr. Clay conciliatingly. "George Clay, you've always thought your money would let you do what you please, but I want you to know your money has no power over me."

NATURAL PUNISHMENT 

15 "Nobody is goin' to make you take my money nor 

Joe's. But I think you would be showin' a Christian spirit to encourage them that wants to make up fer what they've done amiss. Whatever your reason fer holdin' a grudge agin me, I think you ain't got no call to feel hard towards a boy who wants to do right after he's done wrong." "Don't come around here preaching. All I want from any of you Clays is to keep off my place." "It's a queer disposition you've got, William Reed," said Clay gathering his reins. "You make such a fuss about a little thing like a pair of thills and you won't raise your finger to help a boy." 

While William Reed glared angrily, George Clay turned his vehicle and started for home. "Well, Sonny, we done the best we could," he remarked cheerfully. "I told you it wouldn't do no good," snarled Joe who was still nursing his anger. 

The farm wagon rattled noisily along the road and into the barnyard at Clay's. Joe disappeared and did not come for supper until after his father and the Cobbs had eaten. Sophrona approached him in a playful mood but as she received a rebuff returned to her mother in the kitchen. When Joe had finished he stuffed his pockets with doughnuts and crept away to the pasture down at the edge of the swamp. There he brooded over his fancied injuries and finally planned to punish his father for making him go to Reed's by hiding in the barn all night while his father searched for him. 

He decided to wait at the edge of the swamp until the cows had been turned into the pasture; and then in the gathering darkness attempt to creep into the barn unobserved.

While he sat between the spreading roots of a stump cherishing bitter thoughts, the sun slowly sank and with level beams lighted the buttercups in the grass and the tufts of everlastings; the woodchucks slipped reluctantly from their perches on the stone walls and found their burrows. The brown thrasher in the elderberry bushes sang his evening song, but the quivering notes brought no response from Joe. Dogs barked in the distance, belated breezes stole drowsily through the hemlocks, and dusk came.

Joe then rose and crept undiscovered through the hollow to the rear of the barn. He climbed into the mow and, having eaten his doughnuts, burrowed with his feet into the hay and prepared to sleep. As it grew darker and darker his anger gradually wore away; yet when he heard his father enter the stable and hitch up the team to begin his search, he made no sound. 

He slept and later woke with a start. Was he still in the nest he had formed, or was he half over the edge of the mow about to fall to the barn floor? Had his father returned? The darkness seemed to lie on him like a weight. He dared not turn; he scarcely dared breathe. At last he cautiously felt his way to the ladder and descended and ran across the road to the house. 

A lamp on the dining table was turned low and another light shone from his father's room. He leaped sobbing into his father's arms. "I hid in the barn because I was mad." "Well, get into bed, Sonny," his father said gently. "We'll talk about it tomorrow."

The morrow was Sunday and the family drove as usual to the Hemlock Methodist Church. After the morning service Sophrona and Joe went to their Sun-

NATURAL PUNISHMENT 

17 day school teachers, and Mr. Clay took his place in the 

Reverend John Bosworth's Bible class. The lesson was on the Calling of Samuel and a discussion arose concerning the bringing up of children. Mr. Bosworth, who had been educated in a normal school, was strong in his recommendation of natural punishments. He said that if a child broke its toys, it should be made to do without toys. If it broke a pane of glass, it should sit in the cold. Mr. Clay was interested in the theory because it seemed to relieve a parent from the disagreeable necessity of making his punishments a personal matter. He asked Mr. Bosworth how the theory could be applied to the case of Joe who had hidden in the barn in order in order to cause his father to worry. 

Mr. Bosworth advised that Joe should be made to sleep two nights in the barn.

Mr. Clay was inclined to question the propriety of such a penalty for Joe since the boy was only ten years old and had never before been guilty of so obstinate conduct.

William Allen and other members of the class, however, championed the clergyman's side of the argument. 

They said that since the fault had not become ingrained there was more hope that the punishment would be remedial.

In the afternoon Mr. Clay called his son to him and explained gently what a natural punishment is. He said that since Joe had caused anxiety by attempting to sleep in the barn, he must now sleep there two nights that he might experience anxiety and so atone for his offense. 

Joe became solemn but made no objections. 

In the evening he and his father lingered about the dining-room later than usual; but finally Mr. Clay took the lantern and said he thought it was time that Joe

should put on his overcoat and go to the barn. They climbed to the mow and after Joe had crept into his nest Mr. Clay kissed him good-night and returned to the house.

Although it was late Mr. Clay did not feel like going to bed immediately. He took up a newspaper, but could not read. He went to bed, but could not sleep. 

He kept thinking of Joe. Was he asleep out there, or were his lashes wet with tears? Poor little motherless boy! Suppose by staying alone he should learn out there that he could do without friends, that he could get along without a father's sympathy as he had learned to get along without a mother's? Mr. Clay leaped from his bed and huriedly put on his clothes. He did not wait for the lantern but entered the barn and in the darkness made his way up the ladder to the mow. He thrust his feet into the hay beside his son's and pressed the moist cheek against his own. "I'm goin' to stay with you tonight, Joe, and I'll stay with you tomorrow night, too. I don't doubt but what

I'm partly to blame. I think there's been something wrong in the way I took to bring you up, and I won't leave you to bear the brunt alone. We'll take the punishment together."

II 

Among the passengers of a New York Central train in September, 1888, was a young woman who was on her way to Hemlock, a remote hamlet in the outskirts of the Adirondacks. By a sudden reverse of fortune it had become necessary for her to support both herself and her widowed mother. As she had been brought up in comparative luxury and had not been trained for any remunerative employment there seemed to be nothing else to do but to attempt teaching, and she had accordingly accepted a position in the Hemlock 

High School. 

When the young woman left the train at Boughton where she was to take a stage for Hemlock her mind was filled with apprehension for not only was she without experience as teacher but was also ignorant of the surroundings into which she was going. The two or three passengers who had come with her from the train quickly disappeared and left her to attract the curious gaze of a small group of ill-looking loungers who strolled over from the produce office or descended from the pile of freight and trunks in the baggage room. 

In response to her inquiry for the stage driver a short, fat, middle-aged man who wore ragged clothes and a small soft hat with a narrow brim came from the baggage room and dragged her trunk across the 19

platform to a mud-bespattered two-seated canopy-topped spring vehicle to which were hitched a pair of wornout gray horses. A mail pouch that had come on the train was hung over the dashboard. The driver assisted her to the rear seat and in a few minutes they had passed the row of dingy shops and had driven down a long, dusty street lined with small cottages and were out on the open highway traveling northeast. "How far is it to Hemlock?" she asked of the slovenly looking driver. "Eleven and a quarter miles from that red barn," he replied, pointing with his broken whip.

The road followed a lively stream of dark water strewn with large boulders. The air was sweet and clear and the hills bright with autumnal foliage. 

Crickets sang in the brown grass. Bluebirds and meadowlarks whistled across the fields. 

The young woman under the influence of the autumn afternoon seemed to have forgotten her melancholy thoughts and was eagerly observing a brightly-colored migrating warbler that had alighted in the branches of a small hickory tree when the driver turning half around ejaculated, "My name's Colegrove. Are you the new teacher ?" "Yes, I am Miss Stevens." "Well, I hope you won't have as bad a time as the last teacher had. The boys hadn't any use fer him." "What was the trouble?" "Why, he was a stuck up kind o' feller. He wanted to turn everything upside down. Nothing in the town was good enough for him.” "So the trustees discharged him, did they?" "No, they didn't discharge him. He jest left. He was a city chap and couldn't stand up before the boys.

That's why they got a woman this time. They think that maybe the boys won't be so rough with a woman teacher." He paused a moment and then added with a confidential air, "You jest stand in with Jed Brooks, and I guess you'll be all right." "Jed Brooks! Who is Jed Brooks?" "He's the son of one of the trustees. He always has a finger in the trouble. Once he put a hen into Henry

Johnson's desk, and when Henry raised the lid it flew out and busted his glasses. Henry couldn't see a dumbed thing without his specs, so he went to Albany fer a pair and never come back. I hear he got a job on the railroad."

The horses pulled the creaking vehicle slowly over the crest of a little hill and down a long slope where an extensive view opened out. On the front and left were seen the blue peaks of the distant Adirondacks, and nearer were golden brown mountains or hills, and in the foreground was a dark level expanse of woodland or swamp that extended as far as one could see. They followed along the side of the swamp and came to a comfortable farmhouse where the road was flanked with large barns equipped with silo and windmill. "Here's where George Clay lives," remarked the stage driver. "He owns all the land way up behind the village almost to the head of the swamp. Used to be two farms. Old Ephraim Clay, his father, cleared this land; t'other farm he bought of John Van Alstyne. 

William Reed was madder'n hops too when Clay got it. 'Twas left to Reed's sister, Dora, who married Van

Alstyne; Reed thought it ought to went to him. The 

Van Alstynes was city folks who couldn't stand it away from the railroad; and when they left, Reed thought the hull thing was going to be his'n without his stirring

a hair. When he found different, he went to George 

Clay with some idee that Clay was sech a clever good fellow he was going to lend him the money to buy the place back. Of course, Clay, after waiting a spell, bought the land fer himself; and I reckon you'd a done the same, if I'd been Reed and you'd been Clay."

The driver paused to salute a farmer who was taking a load of buckwheat bundles into one of Clay's barns. "That's Theodore Cobb," he continued. "He's Clay's hired man. They all live together in the big house. 

Clay had the finest wife in the country, but she died soon after her baby was born."

A few minutes after leaving the Clay homestead the stage began to climb the ridge into the little village of Hemlock, a town of dusty streets, ill-kept lawns, broken fences, and unpainted houses. The stage driver again became talkative as his rickety vehicle crawled up the main street. At the crest of the hill, set back from the road and nearly surrounded with evergreens was the only attractive dwelling in town, the somewhat imposing stone residence of Dr. Gregory, the Presbyterian pastor. 

The church that stood beside Dr. 

Gregory's home was a dignified wooden structure that had been built in pioneer days. Dr. Gregory had preached in that edifice, the driver said, for nearly thirty years. The Presbyterians weren't anxious to have him. leave. He owned the parsonage and had some money and was not too particular about collecting his salary. 

The Methodist church was at the other end of the town. 

They would not pass it; for as a matter of course Miss 

Stevens was to go to the residence of Mrs. Parker who always boarded the teachers. Almost down to the 

Methodist church was the low one-story school building of two rooms. And just beyond the stage driver stop-

ped at Mrs. Parker's boarding house and delivered the new teacher to her landlady.

That evening at supper the new teacher was introduced to Zackery Andrews, who, Mrs. Parker said, had been "professor of the school," for five years. He seemed to be a quiet person neither aggressive nor effusive in either manners or speech. He took Miss 

Stevens to the parlor and informed her confidentially of the conditions she was about to meet. He had come from outside. The town, he said, was controlled by 

Methodists. Ezra Brooks and William Allen, two of the most influential of the trustees, attended that church. 

It would be well for her to try to avoid trouble with 

Brooks's son, Jed, and with his son's friend, Ed Flanagan. The best way was to let those boys do about as they pleased and to ask them easy questions that she was sure they could answer. By no means should she take any notice of their absences, for the less time they spent at the school the better.

The coming year the teachers were to be relieved from the strain of trying to make the pupils pass the state examinations. Professor Andrews said he had several times attempted to have these examinations discontinued but the trustees had insisted that the school pass enough pupils to secure for the district the Regents Bonus Fund. But now a compromise had been effected. 

By hiring a woman to teach in the high school and reducing the salaries of the grammar school teachers, the district had saved enough to make up for the loss of the bonus formerly paid by the state for passing pupils. 

The teachers were now free to teach what they pleased and as they pleased without fear of outside examinations or inspections. Her work ought not to be hard for in addition to the classes in reading she would have

only physiology and Latin; and as for Latin he would lend her copies of Bohn's translation of each of the Latin texts she was to teach. He would be glad to have her consult him at any time; but it must be understood that if she had trouble with Jed Brooks, she was not to come to him for aid, for he could do nothing to help her.

When Professor Andrews went to his room Mrs. 

Parker came to the parlor and supplemented Professor 

Andrews' advice that Miss Stevens ought to attend the Methodist church. "Dr. Gregory is too coldblooded," she said. "I could never get warmed up in his church. Dr. Gregory doesn't know what's going on right under his nose. None of those Presbyterians do. Last winter Reverend Graham almost closed up 

Fegley's blacksmith shop. The boys fight there awful; and upstairs in the old wagon shop Fegley's got a poolroom where they drank and played cards till 

Graham stopped it. Rev. Graham ain't afraid to say right out in the pulpit just what he thinks of any one.”

After a night filled with apprehension because of difficulties known and unknown, the new teacher accompanied her landlady to the Methodist church.

The edifice was a barren-looking building both without and within; and the seats were woefully uncomfortable. The singing was discordant and the sermon dreary and long. But at the conclusion of the service the new teacher was received with much handshaking and cordiality. She was particularly impressed with the friendly reception given her by jolly George Clay who immediately invited her to bring all her scholars and spend the evening at his home,

The Reverend Jeremiah Graham ostentatiously welcomed her to the church and said that the teachers who came into the town could always find a home there. He said he wanted her to help in the Sunday school and the Young People's Society. He had been hoping she would assist in the choir, but he had noticed that she did not sing. "Why, Mr. Graham, you overwhelm me," she replied with a laugh, almost won over by the good-will and enthusiasm that prevailed. "I don't think I'll promise all that until I find what is expected of me at the school."

III THE NEW TEACHER 

Monday morning long before the first pupil could be expected to arrive the new teacher had in some degree remedied in her school room the janitor's neglect. At least some of the dust had been wiped from the window ledges and desks. She had brought prints from her trunk and had fastened them to the walls. 

On her desk was a great bunch of blue asters. 

She had copied on the blackboard the order of recitations for each of the two rooms as it had been given her by Professor Andrews when the door opened and with hesitating manner there walked in a little girl of about fifteen years. She advanced a few steps and stood awkwardly in the middle of the floor, but she smiled very brightly. 

Miss Stevens returned an encouraging glance, "You have come early." "I wanted to get a good seat. I want to sit over here," she replied, walking rapidly across the room, "third from the back on the side. That's where I sat in our school." "Then this is your first term here?' "I've been to the Van Alstyne school. It's at the corner, right near our house. Miss Haskins is the teacher." "What is your name?" 26

THE NEW TEACHER 

27 "Dora Reed." "Did I see you at church yesterday, Dora? At the 

Methodist church?" "No, ma'am, we're Presbyterians. Dr. Gregory is my great uncle. My middle name's Gregory. My first name is after my aunt, Mrs. Van Alstyne." "I am glad you are to be one of my pupils, Dora.

There on the board is the list of studies. Mr. Andrews -Professor Andrews-says you are to select what you wish. What will you take?" "I want to study bookkeeping." "Very well; then you recite in that to Professor Andrews the last class before noon. What else do you want?" "Well, I don't know," she replied with a questioning look; "I thought I would study arithmetic, but it isn't on the list." "Didn't you study arithmetic in the Van Alstyne school?" "Yes, every day, but Miss Haskins said that arithmetic is good for bookkeeping. Miss Haskins says that if I study bookkeeping, mebbe she can get me a position," she remarked a little proudly. "What does your mother want you to study?" "She died when I was nine years old." "Well, take the reading class any way," replied Miss 

Stevens, glancing at the clock. "It's the first recitation in the afternoon."

At that instant the door burst open and two stalwart girls, shouting and laughing and catching hold of one another, fought their way with great uproar to the rear corner seat behind Dora.

In reply to Miss Stevens's look of inquiry one girl cried, "Last term Susie Tyler sat behind me and kep' pullin' my hair.” "I didn't neither." "Yes, you did." "Stop!" commanded Miss Stevens. "Your name is 

Susie Tyler? And yours is-?" "Belle Jones."

She then directed the girls to take seats in different parts of the room. For a moment the girls studied her calm eyes and unbending mien, and then reluctantly obeyed. During the forenoon forty-seven boys and girls were registered in the two rooms, and the studies to be taken by each had been arranged at least temporarily and no very serious breach of discipline had occurred.

At one o'clock, when the school reassembled after the noon hour, the classes were called according to the schedule. In Miss Stevens's room the first was the class in reading. In addition to a dozen girls who had elected that work, were six or seven boys from across the hall. In particular Miss Stevens noted Henry Brush and Joseph Clay whom she had met at the Methodist church and Jed Brooks whom she recognized by his size, for she had been told he and Joseph Clay were the largest boys in school. Jed seemed to be large rather than strong, for he had a flabby face and a weak chin; his body appeared to be loosely knit and his eyes were dull.

According to the pedagogical tradition in Hemlock as transmitted by Professor Andrews, Miss Stevens asked the pupils to place on their desks the text-books they intended to use in the class in reading. Six had 

Sanders's Fifth Reader, five had the fourth reader of

THE NEW TEACHER 

29 the same series; the rest had other varieties of reader or no book at all. She found by inquiry that several of the pupils had already spent one or two terms reading from one of these books and knew the selections almost by heart.

She said, "Since some of you have already used these readers, let's lay them aside for another term, and this fall we'll try to find an interesting story that we can read together. There's a cheap paper edition of Scott's Talisman. I think I have a copy in my desk-" 

From the beginning of the hour Miss Stevens had been surprised at the quiet that had prevailed in the room. As she made her suggestion for the term's work every eye was on her. Even Jed Brooks, she observed, was leaning forward with an eager expectant look, and his face was almost radiant with attention as she walked across the room to get the volume. She raised the top of the desk- "O-0-0-0-0-0," she exclaimed impulsively. Poking its head from behind a chalk box, was a snake that seemed to be about two feet long. Its slender red tongue was flashing but the shapely hand of the teacher passed into the desk, straight to the neck of the snake, slowly but as unhesitatingly as if the power of a glacier were behind it. "Some one has given me a snake! Or perhaps it has crawled into my desk." And smiling her sweetest smile, she drew the reptile out darting its tongue and coiling around her wrist. "It's a milk snake! Pardon me, if I wait a few minutes before I tell you about the book, this little creature is so interesting. No wonder it looks so clear and bright; it gets a new skin several times a year.

And the little ones just hatched are even brighter and redder than this. It lives principally on mice and toads. See how it winds about my wrist! This kind of snake doesn't seem to care so much as other snakes for the sunshine. It is content to live in dark barns and stables where mice are abundant. People who have seen it there with the cows have foolishly thought it was seeking milk, and so have given it its common name. How could they think that so little a creature could drink much milk! Like turtles it lays eggs covered with a white leather-like skin."

Before Miss Stevens had proceeded to this point in her talk she had secured the sincere attention of each of her pupils. Dora Reed in particular had watched every movement as Miss Stevens brought out the snake and had listened intently to each word. As the last sentence was spoken, Dora turned her head just in time to see across the room a pair of brown eyes looking straight at hers.

Miss Stevens continued, "It lays about ten eggs which take a long while to hatch; but at last the young snakes make holes in the sides of the leathery shell and thrust out their noses for air. In a day or two they leave the shells altogether. They are gentle little things and are easily domesticated. That is true even of those that are grown up. The milk snake is so little afraid of men that it sometimes comes into houses in search of mice. We ought not to injure it or fear it, since it is perfectly harmless. Perhaps this little fellow crawled into my desk looking for mice. Let's see if there is any opening. Oh ho, here is a cigar box! Some person who was afraid of snakes brought it in this box. He didn't dare touch it and carried it in a box. He must have been some one without

THE NEW TEACHER 

31 much courage, a very timid person, to be afraid of a little creature like this." 

Everyone was looking at Jed Brooks; and Henry 

Brush shouted out, "What you blushin' for, Jed? 

Yer jest the color of yer necktie." "Jed didn't dare touch it," exclaimed Susie Tyler. "I helped him put it in the box." "Well, Jed, if it is your snake-" began Miss 

Stevens as she walked toward him. "Don't get too near, teacher; he'll faint," shouted 

Belle Jones. "Take it away; go off; I don't want to tech it," ejaculated Jed with evident sincerity. "Jed won't handle it," said Miss Stevens, "unless

Susie or I put it in his box for him. But we don't wish to trouble Jed or annoy the poor snake, so I'll take it to the door and let it go." "Now," continued Miss Stevens, "we've had a pleasant time, but this is not a class in nature study. 

We must return to our novel. I find we can get this edition of Scott's Talisman for fifteen cents a copy. 

It is a story that everybody will enjoy, so I am going to send to New York for copies enough for the class.

As I shall have one or two copies of my own, any of you who do not care to buy one may use mine; but

I think you will all like the story so well that you will wish to own the book yourselves. Until the books come, I shall read to you from my copy and talk to you about other books I like."

The classes in first and second year Latin that followed were small and passed off without untoward incidents. Could the new teacher have heard the talk that went around town that evening, she could not have failed

to be pleased with the results of her first day at school. 

A successful career was predicted since no one could fail who had shown her ability to handle a snake and outwit Jed Brooks. "Don't try anything on her; she got ahead of Jed 

Brooks!" was the favorite comment.

Jed certainly was placed in a dilemma. The etiquette of Hemlock, although not given to chivalry, would not permit him to resort to physical force; he knew his brains were too dull to beat her at a game involving wit; he merely would invite additional teasing, if he sulked. There was nothing for him to do but acknowledge as gracefully as he could that the lady "had put one over on him." It was accordingly for some time his unpleasant duty to smile and look pleased when someone called from across the street, "Hey, Jed, wouldn't you like to handle a snake?"

IV NEW INTERESTS 

For Miss Stevens the succeeding days passed pleasantly. There were difficulties to be sure. Almost every noon the boys fought at Fegley's blacksmith shop; and the classroom at one o'clock was frequently abuzz with excitement as spectators told details of the encounters. Often Joseph Clay or Ed Flanagan came back looking the worse for their experiences, but they themselves had little to say about what had happened. 

Sometimes small boys with bloody noses and torn clothing crept into Professor Andrew's room and whispered hints of the rough treatment they had received from Jed Brooks or other bullies. Professor 

Andrews never interfered. He told Miss Stevens that one rule he had laid down was to assume no responsibility for misconduct which occurred beyond the limits of the school property.

For the first few days after the new teacher had shown her love for snakes, the children had deluged her with an assortment of creeping and crawling things. But gradually a change appeared in their interests; and old books from dusty attics and volumes from parlor whatnots were brought to school to elicit the teacher's admiration. The new spirit reached even Susie Tyler, who proudly displayed Queenie 

Hetherton by Mary J. Holmes, of which she had read 33

every word. When the copies of the Talisman came it was a new experience for the teacher to read the story with boys and girls who never before had been acquainted with a good book or had seen a picture of life different from Hemlock. As the teacher saw their thoughts widen, she felt like an explorer, leading a company of adventurers into a new world, the old world of chivalry and romance.

Sometimes the boys or girls remained with her after school to talk about animals or reading. The most interesting among these was Dora Reed. One

December afternoon the quaint, gray-eyed girl sat patiently at her desk until the last pupil had gone. 

When finally Miss Stevens came down the aisle and sat in the seat before her, Dora said, "You know Miss

Haskins told me she could get me a position and I decided to be a bookkeeper.' "Yes, I remember, Dora." 99 "Well, lately I've wondered if-you think I could be a teacher." "Certainly I do, if that is your serious choice." "Then you think I ought to stay in high school?" "You ought to go to college." "Father hasn't the money, and I couldn't earn so much." "There are ways of earning money while in college. 

That's not the hardest part.' 

"What is the hardest part?" "Sticking to your resolve through all sorts of discouragements. I believe people who are in earnest can make themselves into almost anything they wish." 

The teacher returned to her desk to attend to her records and Dora walked to a window and stood thoughtfully looking out. The blue Adirondacks were

35 spread before her. A few minutes she stood; and then gathered her books and prepared to leave. On her way out she paused at the teacher's desk. "I am going to try," she said; "and no matter how hard it is, I'm not going to give up." 

The next day when Joseph Clay, with a black eye and swollen cheek, came late to the reading class, Miss Stevens asked him to remain after school. "Why were you late today and why were you absent last Thursday?” "Last Thursday I found an otter trail in the big swamp. 99 "Found an otter trail? Was that a sufficient reason for missing a day of school?" "It was," replied Joe with triumphant air: "I got the otter and sold his hide for fourteen dollars." "Would you stay out of school any time you can make a little money?" "I don't think fourteen dollars is a little money; but if I had a good job I wouldn't be in school." "I should like to shake you, Joe Clay, you learn so easily and do so well, and yet you say you don't care about school." "I like school," replied Joe glibly, still elated over his success in trapping the otter, "but I don't see any profit in taking up studies I don't plan to use." "I suppose you find more profit in fighting at the blacksmith shop," Miss Stevens snapped. "How did you get that black eye?" "Well, there didn't seem to be much to do at noon and I just happened in at the shop. The boys were fooling round as usual, and there was a feller there that I'd never seen before. He got kind o' fresh; and

I talked back. As it turned out, he was the new

blacksmith's helper; and the first thing I knew I was getting it good and plenty." "So you had to give up, did you?" asked Miss

Stevens; and in spite of efforts to maintain a stern and moral demeanor a naughty, human look of amusement and curiosity crept into her blue eyes. "Well, not exactly," replied Joe regarding her doubtfully. "As soon as I got acquainted with his ways, he was willing to stop; and so was I." 

For a moment each gazed at the other uncertain of the significance of a quizzical expression that had become evident in both pairs of eyes. "I'm not going to blame you for fighting," said 

Miss Stevens suddenly. "That's not exactly why I asked you to stay. What I want of you now is to help me plan some decent amusement that will give the boys something to think about. Don't they ever play football?" "As far as I know, there's never been a football in 

Hemlock," replied Joe with rising interest. "Then," ejaculated Miss Stevens, "whatever else I do, I'm going to enjoy the distinction of bringing the first football into this town. Don't you think playing football would be better than fighting?" "Yes, ma'am." 

Suddenly her mood changed. "Won't you help me, Joe?" The deep blue eyes, looking so earnestly into his, overthrew his guards, but delayed his reply. "It would be so easy," she continued, "for a boy like you to find some other way of settling quarrels. Just to help me, won't you agree not to fight?” "I won't fight, ma'am-unless it's necessary." "That settles that," exclaimed the teacher, abandoning her sentimental mood. "Fighting won't be neces-

V 

THE PARTY 

On Friday evening soon after seven o'clock, Joe drove from the barn with his bay team hitched to the large farm sleigh. He called for Miss Stevens and began to collect the boys and girls. At the head of 

Main Street he drove into the country and up the hill to Jones's.

As they approached the front of the house, the door opened and Mr. Jones shouted, "Get on yer duds, Belle; yer feller has come."

Joe had scarcely time to introduce Mr. Jones to the new teacher before Belle came on the run and climbed to the front seat beside Miss Stevens. "I'm going to drive," she cried. "Ain't Belle a corker!" chuckled Mr. Jones to Miss 

Stevens. "Anybody'd think we didn't have no horses. 

Belle's set on driving strange teams, especially if she thinks they're lively. I don't believe she ever goes sleigh riding but she tries to do the driving. Belle knows how to manage horses and fellers too." And he laughed loud and long. 

Joe took his seat between Miss Stevens and Belle; and cautioned Belle to be careful on the hill. "Aw go on," she replied. "I know this hill better'n you do. I drove our black colts to the post-office in seven minutes the day before yesterday." 38

THE PARTY 

39 "In seven minutes!" exclaimed Joe. "Why, it's a good mile and a half." "I was in a hurry. Dad wanted me to take a letter down before the stage left, and I kept foolin' round till he jawed me. That made me mad. I told him I'd get the letter in all right. After I went round that turn and got out of sight of the house, I let the horses gallop every step of the way to the bottom of the hill." "Did you get the letter in?" asked Miss Stevens mildly. "Huh, Colegrove was at Fegley's blacksmith shop getting a shoe set and was fifteen or twenty minutes late in getting started. But if he'd gone 'twouldn't made no difference to me. I wouldn't have let on. I'd drive clear to Boughton to ketch him before I'd let 

Dad get a laugh on me." 

Under Belle's skillful guidance the horses trotted briskly down the hill to Main Street and the rest of the company was soon collected. Belle sent the horses along rapidly and Joe grew nervous as they approached the curve near his home. He cautioned Belle; but her reply was a resounding crack of the whip. The horses leaped forward with tremendous speed. A great shout came from behind and Joe turned just in time to see Charlie Haskins and Susie Tyler, who had been on the rear seat, disappearing over the back of the sleigh.

Susie and Charlie, however were uninjured and in a few minutes the company stood before the open door receiving a welcome from Mr. Clay. His hearty laughter resounded through the still night. “Didn't like Belle's drivin', eh? Ha, ha, ha! Come right in, come right in. Well, Joe, I hear you have a little

accident. Ha, ha, ha! Good evenin', Belle.

Joey had you sit in front to do the drivin', did he? Ha, ha, ha! Who is this? Miss Stevens? Glad to see you. 

Come right in and get warm; you must be froze."

The company placed their wraps on the bed in the spare bedroom. They were then conducted by Sophrona Cobb across the hall to an ample parlor where a great iron stove roared with a fire that made its sides glow. The room was carpeted with brussels and upholstered in red plush. On a small table under a large bell glass was an elaborate bird of paradise, which Mr. Clay said he had won several years before at the glass-blower's exhibition. There was also a flying-squirrel that Joe had shot when a very small boy and which Mr. Clay had had mounted at Boughton. Conch shells, coral fans, and other curiosities were displayed on the mantlepiece, and on a whatnot, and on the top of a small organ. Crayon portraits and paintings both good and bad adorned the walls. "Make yourselves to home. Go anywheres you want to. You can play games here, or in the dinin'room, or in the kitchen. Don't mind Amanda. Do jest as you want to and have a good time," said Mr. 

Clay; then as Belle Jones approached, he laughed and shouted, "So you was able to manage them horses, was you, Belle?" "Manage 'em? Just feel of my arm; feel of that muscle," she said, bending her arm for Mr. Clay's inspection. "I got that muscle breaking our black colts. I'd like to see a team I couldn't manage.' 99 Sophrona, who felt her responsibility as hostess, drafted Henry Brush for a game of checkers with her father and then announced, "We're going to play

41 "That's right; form a circle," cried Mr. Clay. "Mildred Dawson, you go in the center. I'll get a cane."

"Can you name any building in Hemlock that could be secured for the use of the high school students as a reading room and gymnasium?" "The Grange Hall," answered Mr. Clay with good humor so evident that his face was wreathed in smiles. "You're smiling; you're it," cried Sophrona. "Yes, Mr. Clay; you're it," said Miss Stevens.

43 "Yes," admitted Mr. Clay, "I'm it; but if the young folks are ready to start a new game I'd rather sit over there and talk to you about the Grange Hall." 

When Miss Stevens had explained her plans to Mr. Clay he promised cooperation. He wondered why no one had ever thought of that before. When he was a boy there was something going on in the neighborhood all the time; but now it was so dull the young people left town as fast as they grew up. From time to time as they talked they were forced to watch the games. Spinning the Plate had given way to The
