THE COAT OF MANY Colors 

"She seems like a nice girl. She has a queer father.

He was always chasin' things he couldn't ketch. When old Reed died William got sour because most of the farm went to his sister, Dora, who married Van

Alstyne. He has never been able to make a cent from the land that was left him but he used to make a little money fiddlin'. He had an orchestra that played at dances; and sometimes he brought his fiddle to the 

Grange. You'd forget where you was to hear him play. But nowadays nobody can get him out for love or money. He never comes to town unless he has to bring a load of wood. You say Joey is one your best pupils?" he inquired with fatherly pride. "He is so good a student that I hope you're going to send him to college." "If Joe wants to go to college, he certainly can go," rejoined Mr. Clay energetically; "but I don't think college would do him any good. He's only seventeen but is already interested in farmin' and business.' 99 "Oh, I don't agree with you," replied Miss Stevens with smiles. "I think college is worth while for everyone no matter what business he takes up." "Well, I don't know," answered Mr. Clay; "I can see how anybody who wants to be a doctor, or lawyer, or astronomer, has got to go to study; but I think a boy who plans to take up business or a trade ought to get down to work jest as soon as he can. If he's good for anything, and knows what he wants, it's bound to come out; and if business ain't born in him, there ain't no college can put it in." "Even if college education didn't help a farmer's business isn't it possible that it might make him happier as long as he lived?"

THE PARTY 

45 "As fer as I have observed," replied Mr. Clay dryly, "those farmers who have been to college ain't no happier'n the rest of us, but are inclined to spend their time lookin' for some slick way of gettin' out of work. I'll wager that there are more college graduates in the poor houses in this part of the state than on all the farms."

Miss Stevens thought it was time to change the subject and remarked, "You seem to be enjoying these games with the boys and girls." "About the only game we play when we don't have company is checkers. All Theodore cares for is his checkers and work." “You find him´a very satisfactory assistant then?" "Assistant? Why, you'd think he owned the place from the way he works. Nobody ever had to drive him to the field. He come here seventeen year ago,, soon after he and Amanda was married. They lived in my tenant house over across the road, and Sophrona was born there. But they hadn't been there more'n a year before they had to move agin and come over to look after Joey and me. "Amanda isn't like Theodore," he continued. “She can laugh as well as work; and she and Sophrona and 

Joe and I have a good time even when we don't have no visitors. Sophrona's like her mother; she's goin' to have a lot of fun in her. Amanda used to have a dreadful time to make her mind when she was little. 

Amanda would scold and take on, and Sophrona would do jest the opposite of what Amanda said. If I wanted Joey to do something, I always made it into some kind of play. "I remember one mornin'," continued Mr. Clay, leaning back in his chair reminiscently, "when So-

phrona wouldn't let Amanda put on her dress; she was bound to run around in her shirt. Joey heard the commotion and got up and said he wasn't goin' to get dressed neither. I jest opened a picture book where there was a picture of a soldier, and tied a bit of tape around the sleeve of his little jacket; and I dressed him in two minutes. Amanda said I spoiled the child with such idees. I don't think play ever hurt him none. Anyhow that mornin' when I put him on a horse and drove to the field, Amanda was still tryin' to get Sophrona dressed."

The company needed no urging when Amanda called them to supper. Sharp appetites and the tempting display of food impelled them to find seats quickly, even at risk of being separated from best friends or sweethearts. At the ends of the table towered glasses of green and yellow celery. Domes of quince jelly threw a ruddy light on the snowy linen.

In the center before Mr. Clay was a large golden brown turkey, flanked by covered dishes of turnip, potato, and squash. Prints of yellow butter, plates of white and brown bread, and dishes containing various kinds of pickles and other delicacies filled all available spaces.

After the feast was ended the guests returned to the parlor and danced Virginia reels. "Reverend 

Graham is dead set agin dancin'," observed Mr. Clay, "but I never could see that it was worse than Snap and 

Catch 'Em that they play right in the church."

Just then the music stopped and several of the girls rushed toward Miss Stevens and Mr. Clay shouting, "Miss Stevens, show us how to waltz." "Yes, please, please," came from all sides.

THE PARTY 

47 

Mr. Clay smiled approvingly, "Yes, Miss Stevens, I would want to learn myself if I wasn't too old." "You're not too old, Mr. Clay; come on, come on," they shouted. "Let Miss Stevens show you and we'll all learn." 

Mr. Clay raised his arms and advanced toward Miss

Stevens. Sophrona returned to the organ and began a waltz. The company that had been laughing uproariously when Mr. Clay started to dance soon became quieter. If he was a little stiff and awkward, he was at least an apt pupil. It was suspected that perhaps this might not be the first time he ever had attempted to waltz.

For a few minutes Miss Stevens continued her instruction with one pupil or another and then, glancing at the clock, said it was time that she should gather her brood about her and depart. The young people were enthusiastic over the progress made in learning to waltz and insisted that Miss Stevens should continue her instruction when the gymnasium was opened in the Grange Hall.

The sleigh was soon at the door and Joe drove back to town. He left his guests one by one at their homes and last of all drove to the four corners by the swamp. 

There a dim light shone from a window and he heard the quavering notes of a violin as Dora Reed disappeared through the opened door.

VI 

JED'S APOLOGY 

And so it happened after numerous interviews with 

George Clay that in due time an innovation appeared in Hemlock. A boys' gymnasium with home-made apparatus was established in the basement of the Grange Hall; and on the first floor was the nucleus of a library and reading-room. The new interest in reading spread even to the older people and the Grange Hall was also used as the headquarters of a Chautauqua Literary and Scientific Circle that had been organized with the approval of the Rev. Jeremiah Graham. Miss Stevens was greeted everywhere with words of commendation; the towns-folk vied with one another in praising the energetic teacher.

At noon and after school the football bounced merrily in the broad, snow-covered meadow behind the school building. The boys were divided into two groups and played the association game. Good fellowship and a spirit of friendly rivalry seemed to have put an end to the fights.

After the novelty of the game had somewhat worn away skating temporarily took the place of football.

No one could cross the great swamp in summer, but in winter skaters penetrated its dim depths and on still nights their shouts and laughter could be heard echoing from the distant mountainside. One Friday it had 48

JED'S APOLOGY 

49 been arranged that the boys would build a fire near an open space where games could be played. Almost everyone in school had planned to be present. Dora had her father's consent to go with Mabel Dawson.

That afternoon as the last class was about to be dismissed and the pupils were preparing to go home, Jed 

Brooks got into a quarrel with Ed Flanagan and used a coarse word indisputably audible to Miss Stevens and the girls. Somewhat similar incidents had occurred previously but the teacher had thought it wisest to ignore them on the supposition that the expressions were unheard. On this occasion, however, no such course was possible. The offense was too blatant. "You will apologize here Monday when you return to this class," ejaculated Miss Stevens indignantly. "Mebbe I will, and mebbe I won't," snarled Jed. "You made a fool of me once, and I don't cak'late you will agin." 

The following Monday afternoon classes passed off quietly until the fateful fifth period. In came the pupils, and among them Jed Brooks. His face was whiter than usual, but there was an unwonted definiteness in his movements that showed that he had decided upon his course of action. As he swept his glance about the room a desperate and ugly light seemed to shine from his eyes.

Miss Stevens looked at him sternly and pointed to the front of the room, "What have you to say?"

He rose from his seat, walked to the spot indicated, and in the death-like silence of the room uttered these words as deliberately as if reciting from a catechism: "I apologize for what I said; I shall not do sich a thing agin. I am no gentleman."

Looks of astonishment flashed from face to face. 

Only Dora Reed and Joseph Clay did not raise their eyes. "Mildred Dawson," said Miss Stevens, "read from the beginning of the nineteenth chapter." The leaves rustled; Mildred's clear voice filled the room and soon the class in reading in Hemlock High School was doing business as usual. 

With a feeling of profound relief Miss Stevens proceeded with the recitation but when Joseph Clay was called to read, she noticed that several strips of plaster were attached to his face and head, a matter that had escaped her attention during the excitement of the previous crisis. Her first thought was that Joe had been fighting, but she dismissed the idea as improbable. 

There had been no fights at the blacksmith shop lately; and besides Joe seemed to have absorbed the spirit of her undertaking and had shown himself in various ways her foremost ally. A further glance revealed that the injuries were not bruises from blows of a fist but rather scratches or cuts as if he had collided with some obstacle.

She was satisfied that Joe had not broken his promise, but she wondered whether any of the pupils would think, as she had done, that he had been fighting. It might be well to give him a chance to explain. Anticipating one of Joe's comical replies, such as he "had been trying the strength of a pine stump" or "wrestling with the handles of a side-hill plow," she asked with playful mien, "You haven't been fighting, have you, Joe?" "Yes, ma'am, I have." "How did that happen?" "I have nothing to say."

JED'S APOLOGY 

51 Joe's tones were so determined and conclusive that the teacher's playful mood vanished instantly. In her confusion she dismissed the class, although a minute or two of the period remained, and the boys were forced to wait in the hall before they could enter Professor Andrews's room.

When the last of the pupils had gone, Dora approached the desk of the agitated teacher. "You asked me to stay with you some night and father says I may this evening, if you want me." "Certainly I do," Miss Stevens replied, forcing a cordial smile, although the time for the visit did not seem convenient. "Yesterday I found another book that I know you'll like. It's Tennyson's Idylls of the King. 

We can have a good time this evening reading it together."

Dora had long looked forward to such an occasion. 

When Miss Stevens had read to the class in school she had listened with attention almost religious. It had seemed to her that there was no other face so lovely and so full of meaning, no other voice so clear and musical. It would be heaven to have Miss Stevens read to her alone. 

But that evening when the dream came true, Dora was ill at ease. After an hour or so Miss Stevens, noting her restlessness, found a convenient point to leave the story. She handed the book to Dora and told her the volume was hers to keep and read when she found time.

After looking at trinkets and pictures, they prepared for bed. When the room was dark, Dora crept close to her sympathetic friend. "I want to tell you something. Joe Clay wasn't to blame for fighting. He

didn't break his word. He did it to make Jed Brooks apologize." "I should have guessed that." "Last night when I saw that the boys had lighted the fire, I went down to the path where they enter the swamp. I put on my skates and started to find Mabel 

Dawson, Sophrona Cobb, and the rest of the girls. I had gone but a few strokes when I saw Jed Brooks coming and I hid in some hemlocks. Jed went to the path and took off his skates, and just then Joe Clay skated by fast and called to him.

They had a quarrel, but I couldn't hear much that was said. I was so frightened that I didn't know what to do. First I started to skate toward the fire; then I turned back. They were on land; and had begun to fight. Joe still had his skates on and couldn't keep his feet. Jed kept flying around him and hitting him, and Joe stumbled, and fell once or twice to his knees. 

Then he saw he couldn't fight with his skates on, and he started to unbuckle the straps. When he bent down 

Jed swung his skates and hit him over the head. That's how he got those cuts. Oh it was awful! I felt as if 

I must run and fight with Joe-or go call the boysbut I stood still. When Joe got his skates off I could hear the blows when they struck with their fists; and 

Jed was yelling, and at last started to run; but Joe caught him and threw him down. I guess he choked him, for I heard Jed beg. Then Joe made him promise, and made him say over three times those words he spoke in school. Then Joe let him up, and threatened him, and let him go.

When I got up to Joe, he lay on the ice dazed like; and his face was covered with blood; and I was frightened. "Joe, Joe!" I shouted. He opened his eyes

JED'S APOLOGY 

53 when he heard me call. I wiped the blood from his face, and for some time he didn't get up; and he was ashamed because I had to help him. He wouldn't let me go for the boys, and he said he would put on his skates and skate home with me.

I tried to make him walk back to the road; but he wouldn't hear me. He laughed because I worried over his cuts where the skates hit him. I wanted him to go right home, but he made me skate down to our meadow to a gravel bank where the turtles in the summer crawl up from the mud. When he left me at the house I was afraid he couldn't get home alone and I watched him through the window until he crossed the bridge and disappeared down the road."

VII THE MEETING OF THE TRUSTEES 

Miss Stevens's attempt to make the Grange Hall into a community center succeeded beyond her expectations. 

The C.L.S.C., or Chautauqua Literary and Scientific 

Circle, rapidly increased its membership. A boys' athletic club had its headquarters in the gymnasium. A game room had been added. Several times during the past months the new teacher had been reminded of her half-promise at George Clay's to organize a dancing class but had postponed the undertaking for lack of time, although she believed that such a class offered the most agreeable approach to social training that the young people of Hemlock desperately needed. Finally, however, she yielded when several adult members of the C.L.S.C. offered to relieve her of the supervision of the library and reading-room if she would instruct the class.

When the spring term began, botany took the place of physiology. Long before the first leaves appeared the pupils searched dooryards and hedgerows for swelling buds of horsechestnut, elder, and hickory to study protective wrappings or mathematical spirals. The zest for exploration spread even to Ed Flanagan for before anyone else had found a flower, he came to school one late March morning with a shoe box in which he said he had brought the teacher a bouquet. The pupils scat- 54

THE MEETING OF THE TRUSTEES 

55 tered with hilarious laughter when they discovered that the box contained skunk's cabbage; but they soon returned to the teacher's desk as she pointed out the marvels of that product of icy waters-purple mottled, golden spadixed, mystic as a gnome risen through the earth from a Persian cave. Ed was so pleased at being the hero of the day that a fortnight later he brought the teacher flowers of more fragrant odor.

Soon after skunk's cabbage came arbutus bloom from an upland clearing; and from dry southern slopes were brought the first hepaticas. Marsh marigolds soon dotted Dora's meadow; then beside the highways came dandelions, numerous as stars. Trilliums fluttered in open woods, and where it was denser grew jack-in-thepulpit, and Solomon's seal with cabalistic underground stem. To the country boys and girls these had been familiar sights, but now they saw them with different eyes. After Miss Stevens and her flock had searched them out in lowlands and over hills they became symbols of companionship and the romantic thoughts of youth. 

One morning early in June as Miss Stevens was on her way to school she met Ezra Brooks. She spoke of Jed's irregular attendance at school and remarked that Jed had been absent the day before. Mr. Brooks had not been aware of his son's absence the previous day but said he would question Jed about it when he returned that evening.

At the beginning of the fifth period when Jed entered for the class in reading Miss Stevens asked him to explain his absence of the previous day. Without a word he took from his pocket a neatly written note and handed it to her.

"Miss Stevens, Please excuse Jed for being absent yesterday, June 2, 1889. I needed his help in the store.

EZRA BROOKS" 

"Who wrote this note ?" "My father wrote it this morning just before I come to school. Can't you read his name there?" he shouted insolently. "This is a falsehood, a forgery!"

Affecting great anger, Jed seized his books and said, "Aw go on; I'll tell my father about this. We'll see whether you can call him a liar." And without waiting to be dismissed he went out, slamming the door so that the schoolhouse shook. 

Saturday afternoon Mr. Brooks, Mr. Allen, and Mr. 

Wood called at Mrs. Parker's to see Miss Stevens.

Mr. Allen said that complaints had been made about two or three matters that the trustees thought it wise to inquire into, that in particular they would like to know what passed between Jed Brooks and her Friday afternoon. She gave an accurate account of the interview, but was several times interrupted by Mr. Brooks who maintained that she had humiliated his son by calling him a forger and that she had referred disrespectfully to himself, one of the trustees. 

When she could no longer endure the goading and insinuation, Miss Stevens said she did not care to defend herself further against the charges, but since all the trustees were then present it seemed a proper time to inform them that Jed Brooks greatly impeded the work of the Hemlock High School; and that since 

Jed did not care to make use of the opportunities offered him, it was better that he be dismissed. She

THE MEETING OF THE TRUSTEES 

57 began, thereupon, to give a detailed account of the various acts that formed the basis for her conclusion but was suddenly stopped by Mr. Brooks who said the committee now sat in judgment on her, not on Jed. 

He said that Hemlock didn't want to employ any teacher who had to stir up ill-will between pupils in order to maintain her discipline. Since she had shown she was a believer in apologies, she should herself apologize to Jed on Monday or give up her position. 

Miss Stevens asked Mr. Wood and Mr. Allen whether they would support Mr. Brooks in such a decision.

Mr. Wood said that the authority of the trustees must be maintained. Mr. Allen said that whatever was true of other complaints it was known that she had exceeded her authority. It was a matter of common knowledge that she had, of her own initiative, for instance, encouraged dancing, had done away with text-books long used in the school and had required the young people as a part of the school work to engage in novel reading. 

Mr. Brooks said that the matters mentioned by Mr. 

Allen could easily be adjusted through conference with Professor Andrews or himself. It was of main importance that she show her submission to authority by telling his son Monday morning that she regretted her hasty words. The three trustees then arose and departed.

As soon as the door closed, Mrs. Parker, who during the interview had remained out of sight in the diningroom, ran pell-mell into the parlor. "You will apologize, won't you, Miss Stevens ?" 

Miss Stevens's contemptuous glance was her only reply.

Mrs. Parker who had a kind heart as well as a desire to keep her boarders hoped that if the proper influ-

ences were secured, Miss Stevens might still retain her position. She determined to obtain the help of both 

Presbyterians and Methodists. She went to the Parsonage and laid the tale before Dr. Gregory who informed her that he himself knew nothing of the circumstances, that it was proper that the quarrel be adjudicated by those appointed to attend to such things, and that he did not wish to interfere with matters that did not concern his own church.

When Mrs. Parker appealed to the Reverend Jere miah Graham, he said that he had already discussed the affair with Mr. Allen and that they had decided that any person who encouraged young people in dancing round dances was not fit to be a teacher. He himself had offered to Miss Stevens the opportunity to work in his Sunday school and Young People's Society, and she had not found time to assist in those duties although she had devoted hours to out of school activities of her own choosing. "I Monday morning the trustees met in the schoolhouse to make arrangements for getting a new teacher. "I didn't think she'd go," said Mr. Wood. understood that she had to have her salary to support her mother, but there must have been some mistake about that or she would never have directed us to give the balance we owed her to the new library." "Oh you can't tell," said Brooks. "She was as independent as a frog in the big swamp." "We ought to have interviewed her," remarked Allen, "and found out what her ideas were before we hired her." "This ought to be a lesson to us," continued Brooks. "Hereafter we want to get young teachers that are just out of school. They're cheaper and they'll do as they're

THE MEETING OF THE TRUSTEES 

59 told. She wrote that she had never taught and so I voted for her. But she fooled us; she had seen too much of the world." "It's their disposition, not how old they are," said 

Allen. "Professor Andrews never started to turn the world topsy-turvey with new ideas." "He can at least keep order without getting one pupil to fight another," came from Brooks. "He's a good scholar, too," added Wood; "he told me he can teach every subject in the course without doing any studyin'." "Well," concluded Brooks, "let's not worry about what's done, but let's take time enough now to hire somebody who conforms to our ideas of what a teacher ought to be."

VIII A VISIT FROM MRS. VAN ALSTYNE 

One day in early March in the winter following Miss 

Stevens's departure from Hemlock as Dora Reed reached her home on returning from school, Mrs. Hamblin met her at the door. "Your father is sick, Dora. His heart is bad. He was at our house and we brought him home. Henry has gone for Dr. Dawson. I thought they would overtake you on the back." way

Only in a dim shadowy way had Dora foreseen the possible illness of her father. Within her remembrance he never had had occasion to summon a physician. 

That he should now be brought home ill, seemed to 

Dora but the prelude to his death.

As she entered the bedroom she saw him lying on the white bed with face and hands begrimed by labor, and he was deathly pale. His eyes opened an instant as she spoke his name and closed again quickly; but he extended a hand for his daughter to hold, and soon was gone.

Henry Hamblin's sleigh which drove up beside the house was the first of a long succession of vehicles which came and went during the next two or three days. The unfrequented road became a well-traveled highway and the lonely farmhouse was filled with unfamiliar faces. 

Unfamiliar pieces of furniture were scattered in the 60

A VISIT FROM Mrs. Van Alstyne 

61 rooms and the old familiar household articles appeared in new places. Mere acquaintances greeted Dora as if they were old friends, and some old friends were distant and peculiar as if a great obstacle had arisen to interfere with the flow of friendship. Life that was a strange mixture of old and new eddied about Dora and she saw it as from an island and was not a part of it. 

Among the friends and acquaintances who visited the Reed homestead at this time were two who had not been seen there in years. They were Dr. Daniel Gregory and Mrs. Dora Reed Van Alstyne. Dr. Gregory's in terests centered in his pulpit. After his niece, Mrs. 

Reed, had died, he gave little heed to Dora. He was the more inclined to forget her because her father increasingly neglected church. Mrs. Dora Van Alstyne was William Reed's sister, but her sympathies had been alienated partly because of the singular personality of 

William Reed and partly because of disagreement over the division of their father's estate. William Reed's sister Dora had been her father's favorite child. At home she had received every advantage that her father, a well-to-do farmer, could provide. At seventeen she was sent to a stylish city school where she received training that put her out of sympathy with farm life. 

Her chief interest was music. She entered a conservatory, and had a brilliant career, until suddenly she fell in love, and gave up her course to marry John Van Alstyne. 

When William Reed, Sr., died, he owned considerable property.

In addition to the homestead under 

Hamblin hill, and a great expanse of swamp, he had acquired from a man named Wade many money-getting acres to the south. To his daughter, Dora, he willed this land then known as the Wade farm; but

to his eccentric son and namesake, he gave the old homestead. It undoubtedly was his intention that with the old homestead and three hundred acres of swamp was to be included the fertile land across the highway east of the schoolhouse, which was not a part of the new place and had belonged to the original Reed farm; but Mrs. Van Alstyne disputed her brother's claim. 

Rather than take the controversy to the courts William Reed, though much embittered, preferred to labor for his living on the stony hillside all his remaining life. 

The land inherited by Mrs. Van Alstyne was so romantically situated and was so fertile that John Van Alstyne planned to leave the city and make his home on this farm. He built a little cottage up in the hills in which he expected to live while he constructed an elaborate country house. Before a year had gone by, however, he and his wife had lost all desire to live in the country; and the large house that he had planned was never built. William Reed and the other country folk were found to be unfriendly, ignorant, and vulgar. 

After living in Hemlock, therefore, for scarcely a year, Mr. and Mrs. Van Alstyne offered their farm for sale and returned to their city home.

George Clay with practical good sense had readily appreciated the value of the fertile flats bordering the swamp; and was able to buy the land at a bargain.

For many years he had obtained from this property all the profits he had anticipated; and yet he had thought more than once of disposing of the farm, for he had discovered that his deed had transferred to him not only the land, but also William Reed's ill-will. The fact that he was in no way responsible for Reed's loss of the land had not lessened Reed's resentment. From

A VISIT FROM MRS. VAN ALSTYNE 

63 the point of view of Reed's emotional and illogical nature, Clay was the cause of his poverty. 

In the years that had gone by after Mrs. Van Alstyne had returned to the city she had regretted her estrangement from her brother and had often contemplated making some measure of reparation; but on account of the various causes which commonly are found to be good and sufficient reasons in such cases, she had done nothing. On her arrival at the old homestead, however, she was so touched, not only by the bareness of her brother's dwelling but also by the sweet and pathetic figure of her niece and namesake that she decided at once, if the child showed the proper instincts, to take her home to New York and educate her.

Dora, however, was not immediately after her father's death in a proper condition to plan wisely for her future. She had received some hint of her aunt's intention, but it had raised in her no hope that here was a way that might lead to that richer life of which she recently had a glimpse. The suggestion rather roused in her a feeling of resentment due to her father's hardships and her aunt's aloofness. A little warm sympathy could easily have dispelled all her opposition. A lavender ribbon which Miss Stevens had given her she wore in her hair or kept near her.

The morning after the funeral Dora had risen early and had waited somewhat unhappily for the appearance of her aunt. Mrs. Van Alstyne had given directions that the Hamblins were to eat by themselves and that she and Dora were to take breakfast together at a later hour. "I arranged it so, Dora, because I wanted you to sleep, and because I cannot endure that dreadful Mrs. Hamblin. She looks as if she had been thrown

together rather than grown like other people; and her manners at table are unspeakable." "She has worked hard for three or four days and looks bad now, but she has been very kind to me." "Oh, I appreciate all that, my dear. Leave that to me. We shall not go away indebted," replied Mrs.

Van Alstyne with a complacent smile. 

Dora seemed scarcely to comprehend her aunt's words and her eyes wandered to the high shelf opposite her, where she saw her father's violin lying out of its case in danger of a draft from a partly opened window. Even her aunt's presence was hardly sufficient to restrain her from leaving the table and restoring the violin instantly to its case. 

Mrs. Van Alstyne observed her niece's fixed gaze. "I meant to speak to you about your father's violin.

It's a valuable instrument. I was looking at it yesterday. I remember the characteristic way he got it. I thought the violin on which he and I had learned to play had an excellent tone; but he was never satisfied with it. He traded his best horse for this instrument. 

Don't think of disposing of it, Dora. You will wish to play it yourself some day. It's worth three hundred dollars at least."

The dishes rattled a little; the clock ticked loudly on the mantel. "It has been hard for you, Dora. This is something that all of us have to go through, and before we can realize what has happened we have to plan for the future," continued her aunt with a pleasant smile. "If you come to New York with me, I'll give you a cozy room where you can have everything just as you want it; and we'll get you the best teachers of music; and

A VISIT FROM MRS. VAN ALSTYNE 

65 in school you'll meet girls of your age from good families whom I'm sure you'll soon like much better than the people in Hemlock; and you'll have pretty gowns; and I'll show you how to wear them becomingly. 

You'll have plenty of ribbons—you won't have to wear a lavender ribbon with a blue waist. And we'll go to concerts, and sometimes to the theater-"

Dora seemed unconscious of what her aunt was saying, and a tear or two coursed down her face while she crowded potato into her mouth. 

Mrs. Van Alstyne was able to endure the spectacle for only a minute. "Child, why do you eat in that manner?" she exclaimed in tones of mingled anger and disgust. "What would I do if you should eat that way in New York?" "I don't want any more victuals," wailed Dora, crying in earnest now; and, seizng her father's violin and the case, she fled to her room.

Later in the day Mr. Hamblin, who was to be the executor of the estate, Dora's great uncle Gregory, and her Aunt Van Alstyne assembled in the dining-room to consider what was best to be done with Dora. Mr. 

Hamblin reported that it would be hard to sell the farm but he would try to rent it. He thought that as long as Dora was in school it would be well for her to make her home at Dr. Gregory's since it would be difficult for her to walk to town from Hamblin hill. Dr. Gregory said that if it seemed wise that Dora should come to the parsonage his sister Lodica and he would do their utmost to give her a comfortable home and Christian training. Mrs. Van Alstyne spoke with enthusiasm of the advantages that Dora would find in Dr. Gregory's home. She said that she herself had no child and had

thought of taking Dora with her to New York but had concluded that Dora would not be happy in the kind of life she would have to live there. The three decided, therefore, that as soon as affairs could be properly arranged Dora should make her home with her Uncle 

Gregory.

.IX THE PARSONAGE 

Dr. Gregory had preached at the Hemlock Presbyterian Church for nearly thirty years, and had lived in the village all his life except his school days and three years constituting a brief but eventful pastorage in a New England town of some size and social pretension. There he had married a wife and there he had buried her. In Hemlock he had always dwelt in the commodious stone residence which his father had erected beside the church at the highest point of the ridge chosen as the site of the village. The dwelling, placed back from the street and the church, was known as the Parsonage, but it was the property of the Gregory family.

The prospect would have been wide and pleasing had not a row of high balsams shut off the view in all directions except the front and toward the church. From the lofty cupola, however, one could see over the treetops. In front were little tumbled hills. Behind was farm land sloping to the level expanse of dark green swamp a mile distant; then came ridges of hills in alluring succession until the blue Adirondack peaks met the sky. Larger and more lucrative pastorates than that of the Hemlock Presbyterian Church had from time to time been offered the scholarly clergyman, but he had preferred to remain at the home of his 67

childhood, content with his personal income and the modest stipend provided by his somewhat neglectful parishioners. 

It was with no little dread and awe that Dora Reed became an inmate of this house. Dr. Gregory was a man of impressive appearance. He walked with dignity and spoke slowly in a deep voice. His massive features, deep-set eyes, and preoccupation indicated a man whose interests were intellectual. His house was like him dignified, gloomy, severe. Mahogany and haircloth furniture, heirlooms both paternal and maternal, filled the parlor, library, and living rooms, and crowded against the high bedsteads in the chambers. 

Blinds or heavy curtains shut out much of the light that sought to force its way through the windows of many small panes.

Miss Lodica Gregory, the mistress of this large silent house, was a tall, faded, wrinkled woman with yellow skin, blue eyes, and light auburn hair. She daily occupied herself with little duties and turned over all larger matters of thought or action to her brother, or to the old servant who for many years had taken almost complete charge of the household work. She gave Dora a large, quaint room with a view to the east and was solicitous for her comfort. She tried to talk pleasantly to her about her studies or the affairs of the church. She said that if Dora found young people in the congregation who were congenial that she might invite them some evening to the Parsonage.

As far as Dora was able she took part in the housework and learned her lessons beyond possible reproach. 

When Dr. Gregory was out, she stole into the library and study and glanced at the shelves of large dingy books most of which concerned theology. In the brief

THE PARSONAGE 

69 intervals that she remained within the gloomy precincts she scarcely saw one volume that Miss Stevens had mentioned, or that appeared attractive. Most of her leisure hours she devoted to practice on her violin but discovered before long that her uncle for some reason was prejudiced against that type of instrument. It was true that he never requested her to lay it aside; but when she played he would leave his work and wander restlessly with a disturbed expression in his countenance. More than once he had found occasion to extol to Dora the merits of piano and organ, and had commented on their dignity and practical utility at home and in church and their fitness to express all the higher moods of musical feeling. He said that if Dora felt so disposed, he would arrange with the church organist to give her lessons on the old Chickering piano in the parlor, with the understanding that as soon as she was sufficiently trained, instruction should be shifted to the pipe-organ in the church.

This prospect, alluring as it was to a girl of Dora's temperament, would have been insufficient to induce her to lay aside her beloved instrument, had it not been for extreme desire to please her uncle and a vague feeling in her own breast that the violin and the ten thousand memories it awakened were out of harmony with the surroundings of the Parsonage and belonged to an existence that could never be brought to life again. 

After considering the matter for a day or two, she took the violin and reverently laid it in its case and beside it placed the faded lavender ribbon and shut them away in her closet.

Sunday in some respects was the dreariest day of the week. At her old home, Sunday had a pleasant significance; for she and her father had been accustomed

on that day after practice on the violin to take long roundabout walks to Hamblin's and they often climbed to a lofty ridge where they could look out over the valley. But walking on Sunday was not to be contemplated at Dr. Gregory's. Dora was permitted to leave the grounds about the Parsonage only to go to church and Sunday school. Her reading was also closely limited. Only the Bible and "books such as Pilgrim's 

Progress" were fit to be read on the Sabbath day.

The Sunday morning church services, however, Dora seldom found dreary. As the low rumble of the organ filled the quaint church and the choir rose to sing the familiar hymns, the spirit of worship and praise took possession of her heart. She gave little attention to Dr. 

Gregory's long doctrinal sermons. Often instead of listening she turned the pages of the hymn-book and searched for hymns that she loved or gave way to daydreams. Sometimes her mind wandered to "Sweet and low, sweet and low, Wind of the western sea." or to other songs learned from the treasured books that she had received from Miss Stevens; and she often dreamed of the days when she herself would become acquainted with whatever was lovely and of good report, and after college would sit at the feet of men and women who knew all beautiful and great things, and would study until there was no more to be learned. 

And often during the sermon her thoughts turned from the future to the past and she saw again the spare furrowed face of her gray-haired father opposite her at the breakfast table, or as he sat on Sunday morning before the geranium shelf in the kitchen window with

THE PARSONAGE 

71 the sunlight streaming down on his gray hair and beard and lovingly fingered his violin. And sometimes another face appeared, pale and white. Blood trickled down the forehead, skates rang upon the ice, and shouts echoed from a snowy hill.

Joseph Clay had called once but had been slow and reticent in Dr. Gregory's presence; and the clergyman who at first had been gracious and genial seemed to relax his efforts to be entertaining as soon as he learned that the caller was the son of Methodist George Clay. 

After the Sunday dinner Dora would often steal away to her room and on to the attic with its one bright oval window at the rear and at the front an army of lurking shadows. There she found old dresses and trinkets dear to the heart of an imaginative girl. Close under the eaves she once discovered a box of books that had been denied a place in the Doctor's study. Among these was Cudjo's Cave which she guiltily devoured until the sun sank and the skulking shadows came from the corners of the attic and made her shrink away to her room. Often she longed to climb the stairs to the cupola but her way was barred by rusty lock and hasp.

One rainy Sunday afternoon in April when she had read a romantic tale found in the box of discarded volumes Dora felt unusally adventurous and bold. She seized a hammer, drew a staple, raised the ponderous trap door, and climbed into the cupola. The rain was over and the setting sun was shining through the broken clouds. Its level rays struck the bare treetops in the village still covered with drops of water from the shower, and made the separate drops sparkle like jewels. Presently the sunlight fell on the nearest hill to the east, which was yet dead with its winter brown-

ness, except where young pines, scattered over its surface, wore a remarkable freshness, and where a field of winter wheat under the influence of the rain assumed for the first time its delicate spring green. Indescribably beautiful on the sunlit hill was the glory of the dead grass changing to mellow browns and lighted with the varying greens of pines and wheat. In the woods on either side, each tree though more than a mile away stood out distinct from those about it-dark green of old pine interspersed with the golden brown of the tops of maples. At the crest of the hill there was seen one solitary spreading evergreen-the umbrella tree, as it was fondly called by the children of the village; and as the light increased the tree flared like a flame against the sky. When the clouds shifted, the sun's rays moved on and touched at the left a higher hill, which in strange contrast with the gray hills in the foreground, shone with resplendent brightness until in turn its glory passed to hills beyond. The umbrella tree slowly faded. 

To the north the swamp was already hidden in shadows, and the village was gloomy and bare. Dora turned over slowly the great trap door, fearful lest discordant groans should come from the rusty hinges, and with overflowing heart stole on tip-toe down to her uncle and aunt.

CALVES 

Dr. Gregory had published one or two volumes concerning theology and was preparing a history of Presbyterian doctrine: He was so much interested in his chosen work that he gave little attention to the changes that were occurring in the customs of the people about him. He dressed neatly but followed modes that were many years old. Changes he usually regarded as innovations. He was not naturally cold or unfeeling, but he had spent so much time in his study or in the society of his mild and colorless sister that he had become selfcentered, and failed to sympathize with the points of view of people of active life. 

His sister was scarcely more progressive. She bought the best materials for Dora's dresses but made them up with little respect for the prevailing styles. Once when Dora asked to have a waist made with the large sleeves then in vogue, Miss Gregory was not enthusiatic, and referred the matter to her brother. It was his way not to forbid his ward to choose, but to overwhelm her with arguments that permitted but one conclusion. 

He said Dora might have the large sleeves if they would make her happier, but he thought slavish adherence to style trains one to follow the lead of others for the sake of doing what they do and without making use 73

of one's faculties to determine whether the procedure is best.

During the first weeks that Dora made her home at the Parsonage she found her recreation chiefly in literature. She read her favorite books again and again until the shadowy characters took life and became living beings, while her uncle and aunt, whose interests were so far removed from her own, and many of the people she had formerly known, began to move like shadows. At this time Dora was in danger of becoming like her father a dreamer of dreams and a seer of visions. After Miss Stevens left Hemlock pupils seldom remained at school after classes were over. Dora in particular was accustomed to leave promptly as her uncle and aunt seemed to worry if her absence from the Parsonage was prolonged. But one afternoon when pear trees were white and dandelions gleamed at the roadsides, a group of high school pupils urged her to join them in a woods excursion. Joe Clay was going. 

As they left town white-throated sparrows sang in the hedges; and as they climbed upward the veery's ringing call came from low evergreens in a ravine. On they went, lured by early spring, until at the top of a ridge they came to the umbrella tree, a spreading pine unique in appearance, probably because some camper once had removed the lower branches. There they rested, and looked down on Hemlock with its white steeple and long tree-lined street. "Did you know Jed Brooks has left Hemlock and gone to the lumber woods?" inquired Belle Jones. "That's the place for him; I hope he'll never come back," snapped Mabel Dawson.

CALVES 

75 "I wish he'd come back," observed Belle. "He's been taking me to the dancing class at Clifton Friday nights." "Too bad our dancing class had to be given up," remarked Joe. "Nobody wants to go to the Grange Hall just for books and games," asserted Belle. "I'm reading a queer book. I found it in the garret at the Parsonage,” said Dora. "What book?" asked Joe. "Its' called Paul and Virginia. It's a story of how a boy and girl were cast on a tropical island and lived all by themselves for a long time." "I wish that would happen to me," sighed Belle. "What happen to you?" asked Joe. "Why, be cast on a desert island. I'll bet they had a good time with no work to do and nobody to boss them round."

Meanwhile Henry Brush had been searching everywhere and gathering a great pile of burdock burrs, intending to show the girls how to make a basket. Belle snatched a handful and threw them at Henry. He returned them. Soon Belle was rushing around and shouting at the top of her voice; and the fun grew fast and furious. Joe pursued Dora, who perhaps with more exertion might have escaped him. He held her tightly, when he caught her, and scarce realizing what he did, crowded handfuls of burdock burrs into her hair. "Go away, you awkward gawk," shouted Mildred when she saw what a mess Joe was making. "You don't know how to treat decent people." "Go away; you are no gentleman," cried Dora hysterically as she guessed the seriousness of the situation.

Mildred and Dora tried to remove the burrs, but it seemed of no use. Dora's hair was of a texture that made it almost impossible to withdraw them. She was not especially angry with Joe, but was thoroughly alarmed as she realized in what condition she must appear that evening before her uncle and aunt. It was already growing late. The walk had taken longer than she had expected. On the way home they stopped once or twice to try to make her hair more presentable; and it was almost dark when they reached the Parsonage. 

One evening several weeks later Amanda and Sophrona were preparing supper. They had spread the white cloth and had set the table. The platter of great slices of pink corned beef was in its place; so were the cucumber pickles, and the cheese, and the glass dish of preserved purple plums, and the black chocolate cake.

The tea-kettle was steaming on the stove and the potatoes were ready to be creamed. But Mr. Clay and

Theodore were late in returning from Boughton, and Joe was still mowing in the ten-acre lot. "Hadn't you better call Joe, Sophrona? He'll want to stop to do the milking, if he knows they haven't come." "He knows they haven't come. He can see the road. 

He'll stop when he gets ready," answered Sophrona somewhat petulantly. "Then you'd better bring up the cows so they'll be here when he wants to milk," suggested Amanda.
