ΧΙ 

On account of the condition of her hair and her delayed return from the umbrella tree Dora received that evening in her uncle's study the first severe rebuke of her life. Dr. Gregory said it was inconsiderate of her to alarm him and Miss Gregory by remaining out so late; and the company she was in could not have been of the best. People who would treat her as she had been treated must be deficient both in breeding and feeling. Until she showed more judgment he would expect her to consult her aunt before she agreed again to take part in such excursions with those Methodist boys.

Dora's feelings were hurt by this reproof. Until this time she had been careful to act even in the smallest matters (excepting only her adventures in the attic) exactly as she thought her uncle and aunt would wish. 

She had followed scrupulously every petty regulation. 

Nevertheless she had been scolded like a careless child, as if she had no will of her own. It made her rebellious.

For a brief time she instituted a deliberate change in her manners and conduct. At Dr. Gregory's stately board she had hitherto been alert to observe the table customs of her uncle and aunt and to follow their example, but now for several days she ate her food "like 84

a little animal," and thereupon drew upon herself many a merited rebuke. Once when she was in the garden she cast her eyes to the top of a tall evergreen and formed a plan to play the tom-boy. On the farm she had often climbed trees to look at birds' nests, or for the joy of climbing. She did not act at once, but waited until one morning when she knew she was about to be summoned. A little out of breath and with some fear and trembling, she reached the top of the balsam. Soon her aunt called. "What is it, Aunt?" "Where are you, Dora?" "Here." "Where?" "Here, right above you." "Dora! Oh, Doctor! Dora! Come down." "Yes, I'm coming. Is there anything the matter, Aunt ?"

While Dora was still more or less under the control of this disposition to assert her personality and have her own way in defiance of her uncle's and aunt's rules. and regulations, some of Belle Jones's friends who had attended the dances at the Clifton Hotel planned to give a large party to the young people of Clifton and the students of the Hemlock High School. They decided to have a masked ball. Although the Reverend 

Jeremiah Graham and many people connected with his church were distinctly opposed to the undertaking, they had with George Clay's help secured the Grange Hall; and a great deal of interest had been aroused, and a considerable proportion of the young people were making plans to be present. To Dora's rebellious fancy, although she had no idea of going to the dance, it

seemed a great lark to ask Miss Gregory if she might attend. 

And so it happened one June afternoon that she found her aunt, all of a faded color, seated with her sewing at the grape vine arbor. She looked up pleasantly at Dora with blinking eyes as if she found even the moderate light too intense. "Well, Dora?" "Would you mind if I should go to the ball at the 

Grange Hall tomorrow night?"

Miss Gregory gasped and shifted her work so heedlessly that she pricked her finger and a little drop of blood soiled her sewing, but she replied in unmoved tones. "Speak to Dr. Gregory; it will be just as he says but I didn't suppose you would care to go." "Oh, yes, I am just crazy to be there. Mildred 

Dawson, Sarah Wood, and all the girls are going; and a whole lot are coming from Clifton." "I should be afraid I might meet some unpleasant people—but do just as the Doctor says." "I believe I'll speak to him about it.” "You may get his advice if you wish, but I didn't suppose you would be interested in such a gathering."

Dora retired with more or less excitement to the seclusion of her room. She understood that her aunt's suggestion that she consult Dr. Gregory was merely a roundabout way of indicating that the proposition was ridiculous. Since nothing very startling had come of her asking her aunt's permission, Dora felt like giving her aunt a real thrill by taking her at her word and actually appealing to the Doctor. The more Dora thought about it, the more she felt inclined to light the bombshell and observe the explosion. She actually went to the door of the Doctor's study, but he was not

in. She took a magazine and attempted to read a story, but was unable to keep her mind on the tale. 

She took the key to the church and thought she would practice on the organ as she had done frequently of late. 

She entered the solemn quaint edifice and climbed to the organ loft. Again she read the bronze tablet which recounted how the organ had been erected in memory of her great grandfather.

This organ was presented to 

Hemlock Presbyterian Church by 

Calvin Hanford Gregory in loving memory of his father 

Giles Wentworth Gregory

One of the earliest residents of Hemlock and One of the founders of this church She seated herself at the keyboard and played some simple chords, and then turned to the old hymns, hoping that the sweet and solemn airs would allay the feverish excitement that disturbed her; but in vain. For hearts plotting mischief they bore no message.

The next afternoon in the study she found the grave clergyman as usual busily writing at his desk. When she entered, a little out of breath, he glanced up from his paper with a slight frown, looked back at his work, and then laying down his pen looked again at his niece and smiled pleasantly. "What may I do for you, Dora?" he asked kindly. "There's going to be a masked ball at the Grange 

Hall tonight, Uncle. May I go?"

For a moment a confused expression filled his face. 

Then snatching off his spectacles, he rose. "What,

what, what! Go from the Parsonage to take part in a witches' rout? Child, how little you understand!" "I wouldn't be up late; I would come home early," pleaded Dora. “I could dress as a ghost by wearing a sheet over my ordinary clothes; and I do so wish to go." "Wish to go?" he repeated with rising indignation. "Wish to leave this Christian home to crowd unprotected into that devil's den? Well may one say Satan has entered into you! Do you not know that if you were to mingle in that assemblage tonight the hideous recollection might haunt you all your life?" "I did not think that there was anything wrong in it," said Dora looking up with melting eyes into his face, for in spite of all obstacles she still cherished a dim hope that she might be permitted to attend. "All the other girls are going." "All the other girls are going! I shall not attempt to argue with you. I will try to shield you from your undisciplined impulses. Go to your room. At a later time I shall determine what course it is best to take. 

In your room contemplate prayer fully what you planned. You need not communicate with any of us until tomorrow. After supper Jennie will bring you food." 

Dora's knees were growing weak, and the Doctor's anger was so evident and his manner so peremptory that the rebellious girl ventured no further reply, but went at once to her room. She did not remain there, however, but climbed the bare pine stairway to the attic. 

She did not at this time lift the door to the cupola. 

To gaze on green fields and wooded hills on a quiet 

June afternoon ill comported with her mood. Nor did she handle the garments and relics that had delighted her on previous occasions. These were all displayed

openly before her eyes. In the past she had scorned as dishonorable to pry into any closed box or to lift the cover of a trunk, or to invade in any way what she considered private rights. Now it was different. Pulling a staple was too tame; she would explore the old trunks and chests. He said I kept bad company, that 

Satan has entered into me, she murmured in justification. 

What might she not find in them? Rare costumes, ivory fans, curios from strange lands, idols brought by some missionary from across the sea, quaint ribbons, perhaps necklaces and sparkling jewels-but, oh the horror! What if she should raise some long-resisting lid and find shrouded in mildew the contorted form of that unfortunate maiden who in play hid in the great trunk long ago and undiscovered died there! Away with such fancies! She rushed to the window. were the tall evergreens, and the green fields, and at the foot of the slope the level expanse of dark swamp, and beyond, dimly seen, the little group of dull buildings where Dora Reed once lived. There

Slowly she returned and lifted the cover of a trunk of odd design. Ah, there were the quaint ribbons and the ivory fan; and with them were a high comb, peacock feathers, artificial flowers, and an old poke bonnet. 

Another trunk was filled with picture frames and mottoes worked with yarn on cardboard. One trunk was locked; but from another she brought to light a red negligée gown figured in black with crook-necked gourds. Beautiful woven counterpanes in blue and white were stored with patchwork quilts of hideous color. 

But she wandered back to the trunk that was locked.

This no doubt held the flashing jewels, or silk gowns

perhaps, or the maiden-but she must not think of that. 

She pulled a little at the lock. She turned and there on the floor lay the hammer near her. She had already abused confidences and had dishonorably meddled with private matters, but could she go further and break open this lock? She was not trusted; she kept bad company; Satan had entered into her. She took the hammer and the young arms made strong by rough farm life quickly snapped the catch. She raised the lid. 

God's mercy, was it the maiden! There lay a marvelous gown of lace and yellow silk, slippers of white satin, a cameo brooch, and daintiest underwear. Dora regarded the wonderful garments with awe. Hardly did she dare touch them; but through lust of the eyes, conscience weakened. She held the gown before her and pressed it against her shoulders and waist. She could resist no longer; she slipped off her clothes and robed herself in the dainty costume. Within the snug corsage her heart throbbed loudly as on tiptoe she stole down to her room to stand before the mirror. 

Pale yellow silk, fresh as a jewel from its casket, creamy lace woven of infinite pains and infinite thousands of stitches. This she saw in the mirror, and more-a sweet young face with fair high forehead and delicately rounded chin, mild gray eyes, now a little furtive and a little troubled, a plump throat sweeping gracefully to a white bosom. A long time she gazed at the face and figure. The cheeks grew pink; the light in the eyes changed. A look of evil determination drove out finer sentiments as an owl drives out pigeons from a belfry. She turned from the mirror suddenly. She had determined to go to the masked ball.

With such cunning as she possessed, Dora made her plans so that her absence would escape detection, Since

her aunt usually retired early and attended to the locking of all doors, it seemed possible to steal down the front stairs while the doctor was in his study engaged in his evening's work, and pass into the drawing-room and out through the side door that opened on the east porch. This door she would also use as a means of return.

This plan seemed practicable enough; but in case her uncle failed to sleep, and should prowl about to lock doors, as he had been known to do on one or two occasions, she could reenter by removing the screen from a drawing-room window. By means of the ladder in the barn, if worst came to worst, she could enter a window of her own room and could then go downstairs and unlock a door and replace the ladder. Since her uncle's bedroom was on the other side of the house and her aunt's over it, and since Dr. Gregory when at work could scarcely be roused from his thoughts even by a person who entered his study, the plan seemed feasible in every respect. 

This plan she determined to follow, and accordingly she replaced the gown in the attic and sat down and tried to read until Jennie came. It was not long before the old servant entered, put down her tray, and departed without speaking a word. 

That was a good omen. Evidently it was understood that she was to be left in solitude to commune with her own thoughts until the next day. She felt, therefore, that she could make her preparations without fear of interruption.

XII 

When it was near her aunt's retiring time Dora went to the attic, secured the ivory fan and comb, and from the old trunk again took up the marvellous costume. 

From a piece of pearl cambric she made a mask and drew it closely about her face. Then she arranged her hair as she had seen it in old pictures and over it sifted powder until the natural color was quite disguised. 

The white satin slippers were a little too long, but an old essay stuffed into the toes made them fit well enough. When all was arranged and the gown fastened with the cameo brooch she regarded herself in the mirror. She looked as if she had just stepped from the frame of an old painting. A French duchess, she thought. Oh, yes! To disguise her voice she would pretend to speak French. She had studied the language for half a year at high school. 

Soon she heard Jennie slowly climb the back stairs to her bed; and a little later, after the bolt was slid into place at the front door, she heard her aunt come up the stairs, enter her room, and close the door. Then Dora crept breathlessly down the front stairs. Through the parlor door she could see the light that came from her uncle's study. On tiptoe she entered the drawingroom, and turned the key in the lock, and passed out. 92

It was a dark warm night. Fire-flies were flashing their lights and beetles buzzed and blundered through the shrubbery. The fragrance of newly-opened syringas scented the air. She crept softly over the lawn to the church. In the shadow of a tree she hesitated a moment. The sounds of conversation and laughter came from the Grange Hall. As no one seemed to be abroad, she darted down the street and turned in at the brightly lighted building. The new dance began as she climbed the stairs. She passed by the main door and went to the end of the hall and entered by the little alcove. There beside a girl dressed as a ghost she took a seat where somewhat screened by drapery she could survey the company at leisure.

The dancers seemed like grown men and women and many of them wore striking costumes. They were dancing a square dance. Many must have come from 

Clifton. But there was Belle Jones dressed like an old lady with a bonnet! How could she ever hope to deceive anyone? Every movement, every gambol, seemed to shout the name of Belle Jones. She was dancing with a heavy set sailor evidently from out of town. And there, Mildred Dawson, a Scotch lassie, shouldn't have worn that plaid ribbon; no one could forget how it used to set off her black hair when she wore it at school. 

Was Joe Clay there? They said he was going. 

Could that ponderous George Washington be Joe? No, he was too heavy and slow, and his shoulders were not so straight as Joe's. See that Robin Hood in green velvet, with a plume in his cap. What a wonderful costume-but it was not Joe's. The wearer was too slim. 

That Topsy was comical. She was setting the hall in uproar with her antics. She wore a black mask and apparently used upholstery stuffing for her hair. How

about that Uncle Sam? He was of Joe's build. Just then the music ceased, and Uncle Sam looked upward to the lamps. One was smoking and he turned it down. 

Dora drew in her breath audibly, and the ghost stared at her. Dora thought, who but Joe would think of the lamps or would care for anything at the Grange Hall? 

Dora rose and walked out among the dancers. From all sides they came crowding about her and made audible comments concerning her costume. They said that never before had they seen anything half so beautiful. In spite of disguises she recognized several of the people who lived in Hemlock. Belle Jones came with her sailor whom she called Ty Richards. To all their comments she made no reply except to turn to one after another inquiring in tones as nasal as she could command, "Parlez-vous francais?" 

Uncle Sam drew near. "Parlez-vous francais?" she repeated. Joe shook his head, but asked for the dance that was beginning. They swept out into the throng of dancers. Oh, the joy of the waltz. They seemed to glide on music's wings-silently through all the noisy company. Topsy, who had found a partner able to duplicate her gambols and gesticulations, drew near and intentionally bumped into them. Robin Hood and the ghost passed by dancing gracefully. What wonderful music, Dora thought. It was Ford's orchestra, but they had never played more divinely. When a breath from a window lifted her partner's hair, she saw a scar that brought back the memory of cold moonlight and a white forehead oozing blood. She hesitated. Would it not be better to reveal herself now, for she had come late, and the ball would soon be over? She decided to delay a little, and be able to tease Joe about his dancing a whole dance without recognizing her.

When the musicians ceased playing, Uncle Sam bowed gravely, not even attempting to say Au revoir, and walked away in the direction of Topsy. Suddenly the ghost darted to Dora. "Quick, take my ring. It is the gift of my friend who is looking at every girl's hand to find me." 

Dora, forgetting that she was not supposed to understand English, put the diamond ring on the third finger of her left hand, and strolled through the room, using her fan vigorously and making the jewel scintillate and sparkle. George Washington, glorious in cocked hat and magnificent plum-colored suit, rushed at the flash as a trout dashes at a fly. "Now I have you!" he gasped. "I recognized you by that haughty air of yours"; and then as the music began and they moved away in a polka, “you—you have been very naughty, Tilly-to keep me searching so long."

Dora realized then that to carry out the ghost's plan it would be necessary to use English and speak in tones that could pass as the ghost's disguised voice. Her partner seemed to be still agitated and was hauling her this way and that and stepped once on her white slippers. "Very naughty!" he murmured again.

By this time Dora began to suspect that the ghost had played her a trick, for it was clearly evident that 

George Washington couldn't dance. Either he had not kept in practice since 1799 or his dancing had been less ponderous than that he was now engaged in. He hopped up and down and his feet were always out of place.

"You were to blame,” replied Dora in high falsetto. "What did people think-to see me wear this ring and you dancing with all the other girls?" "Don't talk in those queer tones, Tilly. It was because I didn't know you," he replied as he pressed her hand and stepped again on the white slippers.

They were nearing the chair where the ghost was seated, and Dora seeking a way out of her predicament, exclaimed, "Didn't know me! Then we'll stop dancing right here. Come over to the light and look at my hands"; and she displayed a little vainly her white palms and long fingers made supple and shapely by playing a violin. "To think you'd go around and look at all those ugly hands and not know mine." "Don't speak so loud, Tilly. From the way that ghost acts I'm sure she heard you." "What's the difference. You don't know me; anybody else might as well have your ring. I'm going to give it to this ghost right now."

Over bewildered George Washington's mouth the ghost quickly laid a hand. “Bennie, dear, don't you know Tilly?"

The polka was not quite over and Dora took the ghost's chair and observed the dancers. Joe and Topsy were cavorting, and seemed to be having the best of times. At the end of that dance she determined to tell 

Joe who she was.

When the music stopped Joe found a seat for Topsy and sat beside her. Dora started toward them, but was overtaken by a group of maskers who asked a great variety of questions. To all remarks she replied, “Je ne parle pas anglais." Suddenly into the circle of questioners came Robin Hood. "Donnerez-moi le grand plaisir de cette danse, mademoiselle? Mon

francais n'est pas le meilleur mais je ferai aussi bien que je peux."

Utterly astonished by the outpouring of French, and not knowing just what he had said or how she had replied, Dora found herself entering the new waltz. 

Here was a turn in affairs she had not foreseen; as for speaking French she was at the end of her rope.

When again her partner began to deluge her with a succession of French sentences she interrupted him. "You have broken through my disguise; I cannot speak French." 

Robin Hood laughed. "I noticed that your French was peculiar, but I thought you came from some section of Canada. You have no need of disguise, as I have never been in this part of the country before." 

Dora soon found that Robin Hood was a confident, graceful dancer-the superior of any in Hemlockexcepting only Joe. He had spent a summer in France, was visiting in Clifton, he liked the mountains. Yes, he cared for music, he sometimes played the violin, and 

Humoresque was a favorite selection.

Dora noticed that Joe was dancing again with Topsy, more sedately now. If he were to be told who the 

French lady was, he must be told soon, for there were but two more dances and at the last dance every one unmasked. Of course she would not stay for the final dance, for if she did all Hemlock, including Dr. 

Gregory, would learn that she had attended the ball. 

Perhaps it might be just as well to tell Joe some other time. "Hasn't this waltz been fine !" exclaimed Robin 

Hood as the music neared its close. "Lovely! Is this the end?"

"Perhaps there'll be an encore," he replied, leading the applause.

There was no encore, but they danced together again. 

All went pleasantly until Dora lost her slipper. The paper she had stuffed into the toe flew out on the floor. 

Robin regained both the paper and the shoe and offered 

Dora the paper first, saying with a low bow, "Dainty as the slipper is, it needs to be filled out with this." 

Dora, a little piqued by his affected manner and the accident which had drawn the attention of two or three onlookers, cried impatiently, "Throw the paper away, please; and give me my shoe." "I beg your pardon for the mock heroics," he answered. "I was making believe I was the prince and you were Cinderella." 

Dora relented a little. "I don't think it nice to be called Cinderella, as if I knew nothing but pots and pans. "Pots and pans! Cinderella was the most beautiful lady at the king's ball, but I won't need to call you 

Cinderella if you'll tell me your name."

The dance ended. Dora prepared to leave before any one unmasked. "If you won't tell me your name, I shall play the part of the prince until I find you," he threatened as he walked with her toward the alcove. "Better not depend on the slipper," Dora called back. "Remember, it doesn't fit."

Earlier in the evening, while Dora at the parsonage was arraying herself in the yellow silk, Joe had taken 

Mildred Dawson to the Grange Hall. A masked ball was something new in Hemlock. Joe was expecting to enjoy the novelty of the occasion, but was provoked that Dr. Gregory's clannishness and stupid regulations

had deprived Dora of the fun. After he had danced with Mildred and Belle Jones and other Hemlock girls that he recognized, he turned his attention to the strangers from Clifton. He had been amused by a ridiculously dressed Topsy who in square dances had drawn the attention of everyone in the hall by her comical performance. He had stood in the circle of admirers of the respendent French lady who came late. She had turned toward him just as the music began, and on the spur of the moment he had asked her to dance. The 

French lady was an accomplished dancer and easy to lead. With scarcely an effort they circled among the maskers. Although they were unable to converse, it seemed as if they had always danced together. He wished to dance with her again, but when the music stopped, as it would be awkward in the interval to try to talk with her, he hastened across the room to intercept Topsy.

Topsy's face was completely hidden by a black mask (probably used as an agreeable substitute for burnt cork), but one could at least talk with her. She used a negro dialect so perfectly that Joe even peeked around the edges of her mask and looked at the back of her neck to see whether her skin was really white. She was bright, too. Belle Jones couldn't match her sharp answers. When the polka began she amused the onlookers with mild contortions and double or delayed responses but kept the rhythm. Joe entered into the fun and accompanied her as well as he could. The little minx laid her head against his shoulder and in more ways than one attempted to flirt. Why not? It was a part of the character. A bit of sentimentality on such an occasion was no crime. Who under the sun

could she be? No one in Hemlock could take dance steps so cleverly.

At the conclusion of the polka several of the boys crowded around and tried to get Topsy's attention. The most insistent was the broad-shouldered sailor from 

Clifton. "Oh, no," exclaimed Joe, "clear out, every one of you. I'm not going to give Topsy up after only one dance."

In the waltz that followed she danced more conventionally and talked seriously although in negro dialect. 

Evidently Topsy had sense as well as ability to make fun. Joe grew more and more interested. He urged her to give her name, and where she lived so that he could see her again. "Kyahn yo' wait til' we all gwine unmask?" "No, I dance the last dance with Mildred Dawson, and I want to know now." "Ef I tol' yo' my name praps yo' wouldn't think it purty." "Oh, yes, I would! I'm going to see more of you even if I have to drive to Clifton.' 

"Dat's sut'n'y fine yose comin' ter see me, mista, but yo' kin save yo' oats."

She raised her mask, disclosing to Joe's astonished eyes, not the sophisticated countenance of a stranger,, but a familiar wholesome face, overcast now with a troubled and somewhat resentful look. 

Joe sank into a chair gasping. "Sophrona! Sophrona!" His face grew red, and Mildred who was standing by for the last dance had difficulty in getting his attention. They circled out into the room. With considerable embarrassment Joe attempted to recall what he had said to Sophrona. To Mildred's chatter he replied only in monosyllables. Gradually, perhaps

influenced by the tender strains of the Blue Danube waltz, his embarrassment passed away and there came a feeling of remorse. Had he ever before this evening. seen Sophrona with the eyes that others saw her? Had

Sophrona been getting a square deal during the years that she and he had been growing up together? 

When Dora left Robin Hood at the alcove she hastened into the corridor and down the stairs before any one followed her. In the street no one was visible. She soon crossed, and plunged into darkness near the church. She hugged the evergreens until she was opposite the drawing-room door. There she paused and listened.

A little breeze sighed through the balsams. A bat fluttered in and out among the shadows of the trees. 

Suddenly a sleeping chipping sparrow sang so near at hand it startled her. The gravel of the walk crunched beneath her feet. On tiptoe she climbed the porch steps and turned the knob of the drawing-door door. It was locked. Dr. Gregory's sleep must have been disturbed and probably he had walked around to try the doors. 

Dora turned to the window and attempted gently to remove the screen. It came away without a sound. 

She entered and replaced the screen, but dropped her brooch. She swept her hands over the floor again and again, but could not find it. That was bad. The bedrooms were so far away she thought it safe to light a lamp. The brooch must be found. She lighted the large lamp that stood on the center table and took it in her hand to search for the brooch.

She heard a sound behind her. She turned. There on the haircloth sofa, rising into sitting posture, was Dr. Gregory. But what a look and what a gesture, as

with drawn face and deeply sunken eyes, he extended his arms and uttered a moan. Dora stood holding the lamp above her head frozen in awful terror; and so they gazed at one another for a moment, but as Dora gradually became conscious of where she was and her disguised appearance, she tore off her mask and shouted, "It is Dora, Uncle; I have been to the ball in the dress I found in the old trunk."

Dr. Gregory placed his hands over his forehead and eyes and gasped or sobbed, "I was so suddenly awakened from sleep, child, my nerves were shattered.

We are such poor creatures that even the night frightens us." 99 Dora placed her lamp on the table and started to go toward her uncle; but he, mistaking the movement, said, "Do not leave; stay with me." "Yes, Uncle." Dora knelt before him in the yellow dress.

He placed his hand upon her hair. "I was thinking of long ago: What you asked this afternoon and my harsh words made me think of long ago. I could not sleep. I walked through the house many times and I lay upon this sofa; and then slept. But I dreamed; and my thoughts were still of her. When I woke I saw you standing here in her dress."

Dora drew her breath convulsively and attempted to speak. "Stay with me, Dora. I must ease my heart. Let me tell you about her. I was a serious student at the seminary, and my roommate kept saying to me, 'Wait until my sister comes; she will make you laugh and leave your books.' And when she came he called her 

Kate, but I playfully called her Allegra, for I found her all happiness, and the name clung to her. Many times

看 

​ 

while a student at divinity school I went with him to his stately home. Allegra always smiled to see me come. Why, her friends could never discover; no more could I. My passion burned like a fire. Once, suddenly, I told my love for her; and she, childlike, clasped her arms about my neck and said she loved me. "I came again, and said it could not be. I was too gloomy and solemn to bind a flower, a sunbeam, to me; but she only laughed and said we had pledged our words. 66 66 'Your friends will not approve our marriage.' "My friends will love what I love.' 

"And this the following months proved true. on this cushion, Dora.) I became the pastor of the church in which we were married; and we lived near her father's house. Our home was filled with friends and merriment. She had a bay hunting horse with white forehead and white feet. She called him Nicodemus. On summer evenings we would ride along the river road-I, awkward, heavy, sober-faced; she light as thistle-down. When I was at my sermons she would gallop off alone. One day she went to ride and Nicodemus came back without his saddle. I started quickly, but met her friends bringing her bruised body home. "Sometimes I see her as she rode away, and sometimes in this dress, the dress she wore the evening I first saw her. Don't weep, Dora," and he stroked her hair caressingly. "Even our errors God can use.

Perhaps now we shall be able to aid each other. My life would have been too hard but for the unspeakable solace I have found in religion. I have found comfort also in books. But as age has come, I have thought that had God's providence ordained that there had been a child, my life would be less barren. Perhaps God in

His mercy has brought us together. You need counsel; and I need the affection of a heart like yours." 

Dora impulsively threw her arms around her uncle's neck and kissed him. "Oh, I can never atone for what

I have done, for the shock I have given you, and for my ingratitude. No one was ever so cruel as I." "Let us not blame either ourselves or each other," he said, "but let us begin anew."

XIII 

NEW SONGS AND OLD ECHOES 

The change that was made in Dora's life as a result of her conversation with Dr. Gregory on the night of the ball was scarcely less than that she experienced when she first came to the parsonage. She talked freely with her uncle on a multitude of topics and gave less time to day dreams. As Dr. Gregory obtained a new view of Dora's character he let the petty rules and regulations become a dead letter. Gradually Dora was permitted to turn back the blinds and let sunshine into the gloomy dwelling. With her aunt's cooperation, furniture was more agreeably placed and curtains more tastefully disposed. Fresh flowers appeared each summer morning in the drawing-room, and whether obtained from the swamp or hillsides, Dora discovered. that her uncle could name nearly every species. He seemed to be as good a botanist as was Miss Stevens.

Dr. Gregory's study, which formerly had been a place that required all Dora's courage to enter, became one of her familiar rooms. In the period of reconstruction following her war of rebellion, she had with contrite spirit told her uncle of her reading old novels in the attic, but she received no reproof. Dr. Gregory with a smile showed her on the upper shelves of his library, volumes of Scott, Dickens, and Thackeray, bound in beautiful leather. With his own hands he sent 105

Josephus and Rollins to the top and permitted Dora to place the novels within her reach.

Dora was so surprised that her uncle had works of fiction in his library that she even asked him whether he cared for Elaine and the Princess. He shook his head. He knew them but dimly, but he spoke with enthusiasm of Young's Night Thoughts, Many passages he had remembered from the days of his youth and he still liked to quote the poem in sermons. passage in particular he recalled: One "Pygmies are pygmies still, though perched on Alps; 

And pyramids are pyramids in vales.

Each man makes his own stature, builds himself; 

Virtue alone outbuilds the pyramids; 

Her monuments shall last when Egypt's fall." "That third line," said Dora, "Each man makes his own stature, builds himself,' reminds me of something Miss Stevens once told me. She said, 'We can make ourselves into whatever we will.""

Dr. Gregory said he fully endorsed the sentiment, but he knew of Miss Stevens only through rumors concerning the quarrel she had had with the Methodists in connection with her activities at the Grange Hall. 

He admired Paradise Lost and considered it the greatest of all poems. His wrinkled face and dark eyes grew luminous as in rich, deep voice he recited the magnificent lines in which Satan is described: "Darkened so, yet shone 

Above them all the Archangel; but his face

Deep scars of thunder had intrenched, and care 

Sat on his faded cheek, etc."

NEW SONGS AND OLD ECHOES 

107 Dora was so deeply impressed with her uncle's recital of the verses that she seized the first opportunity to read from each of the books he mentioned. To her great disappointment she could not become interested in 

Young's Night Thoughts. And Paradise Lost, also, she postponed to a time when she could hear more of it from her uncle's lips, or until she had become older and wiser.

On another occasion, one Sunday afternoon, she and her uncle were talking about the hymns. He considered them a high type of poetry. The best, he thought, was, Holy, Holy, Holy, Lord God Almighty! 

When Dora expressed a preference for Lead Kindly

Light, Dr. Gregory acknowledged its beauty, but said that its origin made it hardly suitable for frequent use in the Presbyterian church. Both united in praising 

Whittier's stanza: 

"I know not where His islands 

Lift their fronded palms in air; 

I only know I cannot drift 

Beyond his loving care."

When Dora first came to the parsonage she had noticed her uncle's interest in flowers, but his pleasure in poetry she had never suspected. It seemed as strange to her as if her aunt had suddenly taken to skipping the rope or playing with dolls. In Dora's lonely life literature had for two years occupied a foremost place; and now she had learned that Dr. Gregory also had found it a source of pleasure and solace. But the thought came to Dora that literature for him was but one interest among his many interests. He had had opportunities to become acquainted with whatever the

world had to offer in knowledge and art. He was not the result of chance as most of the people in Hemlock seemed to be. His personality was the product of his will. He was a man who had made himself into what he would.

From this time on, Dr. Gregory seemed to Dora a different man in the pulpit. She was eager to learn the subject of his discourse, to guess why he had chosen that subject, and what his reasons were for treating it as he did. For her the sermon was more than moral lecture; it was a dial or register of her uncle's will and mind. And a feeling of pride came over her when her own personality showed an influence on the church service, for not rarely the doctor read from the pulpit hymns that she had discussed with him in his study. 

Dora's feeling that the minister's sermons were more interesting was not due entirely to her new view of his character, or to her devoted attention. There seemed to be a change in the man; somewhere he had found a source of inspiration. The congregation noticed it. 

The doctor's parishioners began to break their longcontinued silence, and on one or two occasions referred to his sermons with words of approbation. They remarked among themselves that he less often discussed theological doctrines and dwelt more frequently on the narratives of the Bible and ideals of living; and his words had become so simple that even a child could understand them. The source of the minister's rejuvenation no one guessed. The members of his church would have been surprised had they been told that possibly the discouraged clergyman had found a single pair of bright eyes more stimulating in his ministerial labors than a church full of sleepy ones.

XIV UNDER THE UMBRELLA TREE 

Stimulated by her uncle's interest, Dora resumed the study of botany that she had begun with Miss Stevens.

Often she climbed over the hills or searched at the edge of the swamp for unfamiliar plants. One day when she had found a buttercup that she could not identify she asked her uncle for aid. He, however, would have nothing to do with her Gray. He went in search of his old copy of Wood's Class Book of Botany and soon had the plant located as Ranunculus recurvatus. Evidently he had found the plant before, for July 15, 1851, was noted in pencil in the margin of the yellow page. 

As a young man he had botanized over these very hills, and he was often able to direct Dora to localities where she could find rare plants. 

One July morning following a discussion with her uncle concerning different species of Pyrola and Chimaphila, Dora and Joseph Clay started out to find the spotted wintergreen. It grew, Dr. Gregory had said, on a dry slope near the umbrella tree. The day was clear and cool, just right for a climb. As they walked along the village street vireos high in the maples overhead sang, "See me here, way up here," endlessly 

"I wonder if it was a vireo," remarked Dora, "that 

Tennyson had in mind when he wrote: 109

'a little... bird,

That has but one plain passage of few notes, Will sing the simple passage o'er and o'er 

For all a . . morning, till the ear 

Wearies to hear it.'" "It's a good description of the vireo's singing," said

Joe, "but I don't know whether there are vireos in England." "Neither do I," said Dora, "but I know there are skylarks. It's too bad that Longfellow never wrote a poem about the hermit thrush. I heard one sing in this ravine last spring." "He left that poem for you to write," said Joe. "You might begin now by telling me just how he sang." "Well, I don't know," said Dora. "I guess I won't blame Longfellow. It would be hard to put a hermit's song into words. I remember he made me feel as if there were only two beings in the whole world—he and I." "That's a good beginning," said Joe. "I don't know as I can go on," said Dora. "I had seen a little bird under the hemlocks that looked like a veery, but I thought it might be a hermit. I had come several times but hadn't seen it, until one evening after a rain I was creeping along over the wet leaves and moss, the air fresh and forest-like, the light so dim and everything so quiet I would have started if I had stepped on a twig, when somewhere out of sight across the ravine he began his song. I felt as if the place itself were singing, or as if the place made the bird feel just as I did."

UNDER THE UMBRELLA TREE 

111 "I guess he did feel about as you did. He won't sing unless he's in such a place in the woods, and everything quiet and just right." "Isn't it strange he likes to live in such places? I think he is the most remarkable bird." "I prefer the catbird. He sings in your own syringa bushes." "Now you've spoiled my poem."

They left the ravine, and on a dry slope, beside a stump, under scattered pines and hemlocks, found the spotted wintergreen still in bloom. At the top of the hill they turned toward the edge of the woods. They waded through forests of mandrakes bearing their halfgrown glossy green fruit. There a cicada shrilled, and they passed out into the sunshine of an open field where goldenrod grew, and where goldfinches flashed along in billowy flight singing their cheerful songs. Near the umbrella tree they sat down with their backs to the stone wall that marks the crest of the hill. 

A pleasant breeze was blowing; and great masses of white clouds, ponderous, yet airy, drifted across the sky and piled up in the north and in the south. Recent rains had washed the air and there were miles of crystalline blue. The earth, except where oat fields were changing to yellow, was fresh and green. In the broad valley a little square of dark balsams located the cupola of the parsonage, but the rest of the village was almost hidden under maple trees. At their feet were waving grasses; across the valley slowly moved the shadows of drifting clouds. The breeze brought the whistle of quail, the call of young birds, the hum of bees, and the odor of berries and flowers.

"Do you still think you won't go to college?" Dora asked. "You remember you half-promised Miss 

Stevens you'd go." "I've decided I can't take the time." "What doesn't take time? It takes time to live." "And money, too. If a fellow wants to look forward to comforts and conveniences for his family, he's got to succeed in business. I'd be willing to take a short course somewhere, if I was sure it would help any.' "We are both looking for education that will make us happier," said Dora, "but in different ways. Yesterday I took my violin out of a closet where it had been lying unused for a long time. It was all out of tune. 

As I tightened the strings I thought I was just like my violin. Before I could act as I ought, I needed somebody to take me and tune me." "I understand. You mean that before we go after money we ought to fit ourselves to enjoy the things where we live; but I don't believe any college trained city person could sit here and enjoy this view this morning more than you and I do, or poetry and novels more than we do, or music more than you do. Why go away to learn to enjoy such things? We've been enjoying them right at home. As Dad says, some people think the longest horned cattle are always somewhere else." "It doesn't seem as if we could enjoy ourselves more than we have this morning, but that's only our natural feeling. We don't know. Some person better educated might see things we don't see, and perhaps even feel more than we do. Maybe we wouldn't enjoy literature at all if Miss Stevens hadn't come to Hemlock; and I'm sure I wouldn't care so much for music

UNDER THE UMBRELLA TREE 

113 if it hadn't been for my father. What we have learned so far is due to our teachers, and unless we are satisfied to live as we are now, we have to leave Hemlock to find teachers to help us.' 

"I'm going on with my studies right at home," responded Joe. "I'm keeping up my Latin, and every 

Sunday morning before church I have a fine time. reading Vergil's Ecologues. Professor Andrews got me a pony, but I hardly ever use it. I got a lot of telephone books from New York. If I went away to study they'd have to give their lessons out of these same books. They're the best telephone books there are. I can ask questions of an engineer I know, but I get on pretty well alone." "You are able to do that," replied Dora, somewhat regretting the aggressiveness of her recent argument. "You always could work out everything by yourself, but I seem to need somebody to help me.' 

"How long do you expect to study?" inquired Joe. "I can't see any end. It seems as if the more a person studied, the more he would want to know." "Well," responded Joe, rather gloomily, as he helped her to her feet, "I don't know as I see any end to my work with telephones. Father and I had a talk at Clifton with the president of that telephone company they're forming over there. He told us just how to get a charter. I want to build Hemlock the finest telephone line there is anywhere; then if it pays well, add extensions where they are needed all through this part of the country. I believe a person could follow that business all his life." 

Down the hill they went, flushing indigo birds and redstarts from the second growth. A wood pewee

whistled from a low limb. "He doesn't sing much at a time," commented Joe, "but he's the only bird that sings every day from spring to autumn." They turned toward Jones's and followed the highway back to town. 

At the Presbyterian church they parted, Dora entering for her daily practice on the organ, Joe going home to cut the oats.

XV THE NEW TELEPHONE 

Dr Gregory would have arranged to relinquish his pastorate to a younger man and go to live with his brother in the West had it not been for his desire to remain with his niece until she went to college. Further acquaintance with her disposition and judgment had brought confidence. Young people from both churches came frequently to the parsonage. Perhaps as a result of youthful company, Dr. Gregory became more interested in the affairs of the village and was less irritable. 

His sermons were not a whit the poorer, although on many an evening the old piano was played at a late hour while boys and girls engaged in adolescent frolics. 

During the fall of 1890 Dr. Dawson and two or three other prominent citizens had taken up Joe's suggestion for a telephone line. The doctor and the Clays had driven to Clifton to consult Mr. Wade, who was an old friend of George Clay's. As a result of prompt action and Mr. Wade's expert advice, they had been able to perfect a Hemlock organization and secure a charter at the same time that one was granted the Clifton company which had been organized several months earlier. 

Wade had had experience in forming business organizations and, with friends in the legislature, was able to secure for the two companies charters giving them the right to construct, acquire, sell, or lease telephone lines 115

in Adams and Van Buren counties. Most of the provisions of the two charters were identical, for the understanding was that the two local companies would unite as soon as various extensions had been made and all was working well.

After the charter was secured a meeting was held in the Grange Hall to secure subscriptions for stock. 

Neither the Clays nor Dr. Dawson had the least anxiety about the outcome as approval for the project had been universal, and nearly all the farmers in the vicinity had driven in for the meeting. Dr. Dawson offered to make the appeal. He was the only physician in Hemlock and knew every one within miles. He did not doubt that those accustomed to receive implicitly his medical suggestions would also accept his financial recommendations.

He said that few companies had more promising territory. The new line would extend from the railway and telegraph at Boughton through Hemlock to Wade's exchange in Clifton. There it would connect directly with the Western and Eastern, and, as soon as Wade's company could build its extension to Hawley, also directly with the Bell. But more important than expected traffic, said Dr. Dawson, was the charter. If 

Hemlock never owned a mile of wire some companies would be willing to give thousands of dollars for a charter that conferred the right to do business in the unrestricted manner permitted by the franchise he had obtained for them.

At the present time telephone companies were starting all over America, and each was trying to get ahead of the other. In northern New York the Western and

Eastern and the Bell were the most important. They were building lines in every direction and were scram-

THE NEW TELEPHONE 

117 bling to occupy all the territory they could before any other company got in. He had already seen some W. and E. men looking over one of the local routes, but 

Hemlock had the laugh on them as it had secured that right of way several weeks ago. The doctor said he had made a study of telephone conditions and knew how to deal with them. Within one year, he prophesied, Hemlock would be able to sell at a profit either to the W. and E. or to the Bell. All who invested in 

Hemlock stock could expect to double their money. 

After the farmers had gone and Joe had totaled the results, it was found that sufficient poles had been contributed and the right of way was completed, but little more than one-half of the stock had been subscribed. 

Dr. Dawson was furious. "These farmers," he roared, "are so used to holding on to their money that they wouldn't give you fifty cents for one dollar bills."

George Clay calmed him. He said that a lot of the farmers earned so little they couldn't pay their taxes. 

He took paper and figured for a few moments, and then announced that he would double his own subscription and Joe's and so complete the required capital. 

It was hoped that actual construction would begin as soon as spring opened. Nevertheless one matter after another delayed the execution of the project. No one in Hemlock had had any experience in constructing telephone lines. Joe had been studying his books assiduously and thought he could manage it all right, but Dr. Dawson insisted on bringing in a W. and E. foreman, Tyler Richards, recommended by the doctor's friends in Clifton, to inspect the line as it neared completion.

It was the middle of August, 1891, before the line was ready for use. The Hemlock instrument was to be

THE COAT OF MANY Colors located in Dr. Dawson's office in the same building with the post office. There was much excitement on the day the telephone was installed. Among the crowd that gathered to watch Richards make the final connection could be seen many of the prominent people of the town. The postmaster had left his office in charge of Charlie Haskins, his new clerk, and from a position that permitted him to see the final operations was talking with the Rev. Mr. Graham and Mr. Allen. Dr.

Dawson was rushing around busily and making a pretense of superintending the work. In the hallway Silas 

Horton and Fegley the blacksmith were having an argument about what would happen if lightning struck the line. A group of women and girls, among whom were 

Sophrona, Sarah Wood, Susie Tyler, Belle Jones and 

Mildred Dawson, stood just inside the door; and another group were out on the sidewalk, thinking that the safer place if anything should go wrong.

When finally the last wires were fastened and the battery connections screwed down, Richards made a final inspection, cooly gave the bell a few turns, and while the crowd was silent with awe, took down the receiver, "Hello, Boughton." There was a faint, scratchy reply and a small boy darted out from under the telephone through the crowd to the street. "I heard somebody in Boughton say 'Hello,'" he shouted, and ran home with the news.

Richards gave Dr. Dawson some instructions, and stepped aside to talk with Belle Jones and the girls. 

Soon the bell rang and the doctor placed the receiver to his ear and said, "Hello, do you hear me? Why, I can hear you plain as day. You could, could you, etc."

THE NEW TELEPHONE 

119 After George Clay and Joe had taken their turn, Joe started to make his way through the crowd that packed the office to suffocation. "When will you be back?" asked his father. "Not until late." "Certainly not!" observed Belle Jones. "He's going to the Parsonage to tell Dr. Gregory the telephone's running." 

Ty Richards and everybody else laughed; but Joe, looking neither to right or left, pushed his way to a hitching-post, untied his little gray team, and drove directly to the Parsonage.

"How can you tell so soon? Miss Stevens said it disciplines the mind, and makes it easier all our life to learn everything else." "I hope that's true; but after this I'm going to try to discipline my mind by learning something I know I can use." "I should think you'd like to know what the people used to think centuries ago."
