Dr. Dawson's as excited as a hen with chickens. I thought perhaps you'd like to drive down to Fox's. I've got to pay him for setting some poles."

XVI 

ALONG THE SWAMP ROAD 

In a few minutes the little gray horses again sped down the street. Through the woodbine that covered her porch, Livy Horton peered out eagerly as they went by. She thought it a pretty sight to see the lively team and flashing carriage, Joe Clay's ruddy cheeks, and

Dr. Gregory's slender neice in white dress sitting up so primly.

Down the slope from the village stretched the long line of new telephone poles-yellow, with shreds of bark still hanging. Bluebirds sang from the wires. Along the fences banks of goldenrod and clusters of asters were coming into bloom.

A little out of town they met John Murphy who sat crouched over on the high spring-pole seat of his lumber wagon bobbing up and down like a grasshopper. 

He saluted with his whip as if it were a sword and mumbled, “Hello, Joe," and on he went half asleep. "I thought he was going to run into us," said Joe. "I don't believe he hears the bluebirds or sees the asters," said Dora. "No, nor Dora Reed's white dress and gray eyes," added Joe.

From nearby orchards came the odor of harvest apples. Grasshoppers and crickets sang in the grass. A fish-hawk circled lazily over the great swamp. 121

At the place where the swamp road comes in and the telephone line crosses the highway, Joe stopped. He leaped over the fence and tried the tautness of the cable that guys the pole where the line changed its direction. 

He pointed to the freshly turned earth where the cable disappeared in the ground and attempted to tease Dora by telling her that a deadman was buried there. He soon explained, however, that a deadman is a log buried horizontally to serve as an anchor. Since the ground was soft and the deadman not very long he had feared that it might not hold and had complained to Fox about it; but thus far there had been no trouble. 

A half mile further on they found Fox and paid him for digging the holes, and Joe spoke again about the deadman. "When that deadman gives out," Fox drawled, as he thrust the bills into his trousers pocket, "lemme know, and I'll stan' over there and hold the line up." "Just the job for you," retorted Joe as he turned the team around. "You'd make a great success as a deadman."

Fox seized a strip of splintered board that lay beside the road and gave the off horse a resounding slap. The team started away at a run, and reached the top of the hill before Joe was successful in quieting them. "That Fox is always up to some darn foolishness," grumbled Joe, "but he's a good worker when you can stand over him and tell him what to do."

There at the forks they chose the long way back to Hemlock, the sandy and rough trail that leads along the swamp, bordered here and there with stump fences, or stone walls hung with clematis and ivy, and sometimes merely hedged with sumac and blackberry bushes, where brown thrashers and indigo birds flit continually

ALONG THE SWAMP ROAD 

123 in and out, and catbirds sing steadily throughout the summer.

Joe's eyes turned toward the slight girlish figure in the airy white dress and broad straw hat. He saw the sunshiney hair, the delicately rounded features, and the large gray eyes that drooped their long lashes suddenly when they detected his searching gaze. "I was thinking," said Dora quickly, "that summer is almost gone. See how the robins are gathering in flocks and the woodbine is even turning red." "Yes, last night when I drove the cows home, the crows flew over to the woods near the umbrella tree as they do in the fall." "Usually the seasons are just long enough, but this year I hate to have summer end." "I am surprised," answered Joe. "I thought you wanted summer to pass quickly because you're going to college." "I would if it weren't for leaving Hemlock and all the people I like." "I guess you'll have no trouble to make new friends where you're going." "What do you mean " demanded Dora. "Are you implying I forget my friends easily?" "Not at all,” replied Joe, shaking free a rein that was caught under a neckstrap. "It's only natural to think less about old friends when you are making new ones every day." 

Dora exclaimed lightly, "You can make new friends in Hemlock. Then there's Sophrona. You always seemed to think a good deal of her." "I don't know how I could think more of her; but she isn't the kind that wants to go outdoors. She's bread and butter at home."

"Mabel Dawson likes to go after flowers; and only the other day Sarah Wood told me she wants to learn. all she can about birds."

Joe turned indignantly. "I'd rather study ancient history than be with Sarah Wood." 

Dora was not quite ready to forgive him. "I think it would be different if you knew her well." "Do you think I'd like to be with her after having known you?" he asked with feeling.

Dora relented. She looked toward him with wistful eyes. "What is the matter, Joe? Don't you want me to go to college?" "I wouldn't have you do anything but go to college. 

I wouldn't have you stay; but when you speak of other girls you don't understand how different you are from everybody else." "I'm afraid, Joe, you're cherishing an ideal of your own building." "I know the source of the ideal," he answered, "and for that reason-I love you more than I love my life."

Dora began to speak, but the words would not come. 

He saw her lips tremble and exclaimed remorsefully, "I didn't intend to tell you now. I didn't mean to let you know until you were through college.' 99 "That's all right," moaned Dora. "I knew it long ago and it's made me afraid to be with you alone." "Then you don't love me?" he inquired sadly. 

Dora looked at him almost fiercely. "I love you better than anyone else in the world." But the words seemed to be spoken in anger, and no love-light shone in her eyes. "I don't understand you." "I've never been able to make you understand. 

There's a long way ahead of me. I can't trust myself.

ALONG THE SWAMP ROAD 

125 

I can't love anybody now. I've no right to love anybody now." "If we love each other now, isn't that enough? Isn't love the best right there is for loving?" "Suppose some day one of us should change?" "This would be a sad world," replied Joe, if people had to wait for all their happiness until they were sure it never would be lost." 

Her mood changed with his words. "If we love each other now, we've a right to be happy now. If there is trouble ahead, we shall suffer enough when it comes." 

On Dora's red lips Joe imprinted a lover's kiss. "Geewhitiker!" he exclaimed, "look at the cardinal flowers we're passing."

Where a ditch entered the swamp they gathered longplumed marsh grass and splendid cardinal flowers. 

Large yellow and black butterflies fluttered over the tops of the boneset and pink eupatorium, or were beating their way up the breeze that brought the heavy odor of buckwheat bloom from a roadside field. Great blue herons swept solemnly along in dignified flight or perched one above the other in the tops of dead ash trees far out in the swamp.

In after years when other winds brought to Joe the buckwheat odor from snowy fields, he saw again in his memory the gleaming white dress flecked here and there with brilliant fallen petals from the cardinal flowers and gray eyes shaded with long moist lashes— 

As they approached the four corners at the Van

Alstyne schoolhouse they looked across to the dingy buildings of Dora's old home. "I wonder if it's always so," she remarked thoughtfully, "when a person has left her old home and sees it again-such a mingling of pleasant and sad memories? Sometimes I long to

watch the swallows in the barn and walk about the fields as I did as a little girl; and at other times I should like to go so far away I could never see it again." "Perhaps there'll be only pleasant thoughts when you return to the Parsonage. "I don't know. Last night a letter came from Uncle

Calvin. He has been lonesome since his children married. As soon as I go to college, Uncle Daniel and

Aunt Lodica will go to St. Paul to live where they can have city conveniences and medical care. 

Calvin is a physician."

Uncle "Then you won't come back to Hemlock so often?" "If I don't, you must write to me often-or come to college to see me or come where I spend the summer. Now's the time to promise!" "I promise," answered Joe.

As they drove up the slope into the village they met John Murphy driving down the hill. He sat in exactly the same attitude and raised his whip with exactly the same gesture he had used earlier in the afternoon. He mumbled, "Hello, Joe," and drove on. "Why, Dora!" exclaimed Joe merrily, "poor old John 

Murphy doesn't know what happened this afternoon.

He drives right along with his head down. It's the same old world to him."

They laughed; and all the remaining way to town their eyes were lighted with love and happiness.

XVII WILLIAM REED'S DREAM 

As George Clay entered Dr. Dawson's office, the physician shouted, "Do you want to sell your telephone stock?" "I don't know. Are you so much encouraged you want to spec'late?" Clay inquired with a laugh. "Not exactly; but it's not going to prove a bad venture at that. The Western and Eastern manager called me this morning and wants our price on the whole line, equipment, right of way, and all. He offered nine thousand five hundred. He wants a through line from 

Clifton to Boughton." "I should think they could save money by usin' our wires." "The Western and Eastern have an idea that they are on the track of a receiver and transmitter that won't infringe on Bell patents. Tyler Richards tells me they're trying to get the shortest lines in every direction and as soon as their new instruments are perfected they expect to come out as rivals to the Bell. But I'll make them pay better than nine thousand five hundred, if I find that's in their bonnet," said the doctor confidently. "Don't depend on Ty Richards. Wade says he tried once to be a little slick with the Clifton company." "I never refuse any information," said the doctor. 127

"But there wouldn't be no harm in goin' a trifle slow.

When Wade builds his line from Hawley to Clifton we might make a dicker with the Bell. I don't like them 

W. and E. fellers. They're a shifty lot." "Leave that to me," responded Dawson, nodding his head wisely. "The W. and E. are just so shifty that 

I'll give them the chance to overreach themselves."

At home that evening when Mr. Clay discussed this conversation with his son, the latter seemed less inclined to sell to the W. and E. than was Clay himself. "We built our line to give Hemlock good service," Joe remarked, "and that's one thing we couldn't get from the W. and E. If we want to speculate, the first thing to do is to expand by extending our lines to the towns near us." "I don't expect there'd be no trouble with Dawson about that. He wants to run connectin' lines in every direction." "We ought to be able to build the South Hills extension this year. There'd be a lot of traffic over that line. 

Those people down there are mighty anxious to reach the produce dealers in Clifton." "Then let's call a corporation meetin' and vote the capital for the extension while Dawson's in the mood." "It's going to take a lot of money to get over those hills near Flanagan's.' "Dawson won't stop at nothin' now he's had that offer from the W. and E." "Then let's go ahead with it, but " Joe hesitated. "I've another plan to use some of your money and nearly all of mine; and I think I'd better tell you about it now." "What is it, Sonny?"

WILLIAM Reed's Dream 

129 "I've been thinking about it a long time, but until lately I've considered it too foolish to talk about." "Go ahead, Joey," urged Mr. Clay, gazing at his son with large and sympathetic eyes. "I want to drain the big swamp." 

Mr. Clay raised his eyebrows and whistled slightly. "That was William Reed's dream," he exclaimed, but quickly added, "I've no doubt you might git along where a man like Reed would fail." "I've heard that Reed had some such plan, and I've wondered how far he got with it," responded Joe. "Oh, that was before you was born," remarked

Mr. Clay reminiscently. "He called a meetin' of the farmers and wanted them each to pay in two dollars an acre, and he want goin' to give them a minute of time to think it over. I rose and asked him if he had an understandin' with the mill regardin' water rights. 

He said he hadn't done nothin' about it. Everybody laughed, and although he got up agin and made a long, crazy speech, the whole thing went to smash right there." "What did he say in his speech?" "Huh, nothin' in partic'lar. He sort o' raved about my havin' got all his best land, and how when he was tryin' to make his three hundred acres of swamp tillable, I was still opposin' him. But nobody paid any attention to what he said. They all knew that when I bought the Van Alstyne farm it had been on the market several months and Reed had found he couldn't raise the money." "The water rights wouldn't make me any trouble," said Joe, returning to the previous line of thought. "How's that?"

"When I was down at Clark's last Thursday Clark spoke to me about buying the mill. He says the logs around here have given out except a few hemlocks sledded in from the swamp. He wants me to take over the property and build a grist-mill. He offers all his rights for fifteen hundred dollars; and that includes fifty or sixty acres of land under water that I think I could drain." "Then you'd have no trouble with the water rights; but you'd have no end of trouble to git the farmers round here to stan' their share of the expense." "I have thought of that, too, and I have decided to ask those who don't want to pay to contribute a strip of their swamp bordering the crick on condition that the company lowers the water sufficiently to permit culivation of the rest of their land. Since we'll give them the right to take off all the timber, we won't have much trouble to get them to sign. If they will give about a third of their swamp land for draining the rest, it will make the company a bigger profit than it could get from any cash payments." "Mebbe that's so; but it would take a lot of money to do the excavatin'." "Not so much. As you know, it's the limestone ridge on which Hemlock village is built, and this house is built, that holds back the water of the brooks that come down from the mountains and so makes the swamp. 

The ledge of rock is narrowest at this end of the millpond, near Fox's, not down at the dam. It's where we dive off when we go swimming. You know in that place there's fifteen feet of water right off the edge of the rock; and it is only a few feet through to the gravel. 

If the rock was blasted out at that point, the water

WILLIAM REED'S DREAM 

131 would rip out the gravel in a hurry, and go under that culvert near Fox's down to the main crick. The water seeps out there as it is, and that's what makes Fox's swale." "Why not go careful and try and see what results you can git by removin' the dam and scrapin' out the channel at the mill? That ought to lower the mill-pond at least six or seven feet." "I want to get the outlet low enough to drain the whole swamp. If I can get that rock at Fox's blasted out this fall, we can straighten the channel in the middle of the swamp next summer. If we can get down fifteen feet at Fox's we ought to get a flow of water swift enough to cut out the mud and sand the whole length of the swamp and make the channel deeper and deeper as time goes on. Taking out the dam might drain the lower half, but I don't believe it would make a good swift current clear up to the east end." "That's right. When Reed was runnin' the lines of the Van Alstyne farm he had the surveyor calc❜late it fer him. He said they would have to lower the channel fer forty rod below the mill-dam to drain clear to the upper end of the swamp. He told me that jest takin' out Clark's dam would make most of our land dry or at least good fer pasture. Perhaps it wouldn't pay you to do that blastin' at Fox's till you found that our farm couldn't be made dry without it. Anyhow, you'd find it hard to git any help from the people near the head of the swamp. They haven't even paid taxes on that 

Strong property for years. Hamblin hasn't got nothin' to pay. The chief benefit would come to the Reed farm. That property would be mighty valuable if you could make it dry; but I don't suppose you would col-

lect anything there, fer I know you wouldn't want to ask anything of Reed's daughter-especially while she is tryin' to git through college." "No, I wouldn't look for any money there." And with a voice a little tremulous he added, "It's for her, Father, that I want to drain the swamp."

For a moment Mr. Clay looked inquiringly into his son's face; and then extending his hand said, "All right, Joey, we'll blast out that rock at Fox's if it takes all the dynamite in the county."

XVIII 

DORA'S LETTER 

One October afternoon the gray team driven by Theodore turned in at the gate and Sophrona was greeted effusively. She was returning after her first week at the business college at Clifton. Her face was radiant with smiles and she seemed very glad to be home. "I had no idee how much store we set on you till you went away, Sophrona," exclaimed Mr. Clay, laughing and talking all at once. "How's school?" asked Joe. "Fine. Mr. Keeler says I write so well I needn't take penmanship at all unless I want to; and one day I got the highest mark in bookkeeping." "At that rate," said Mr. Clay, "I guess you'll keep the books of the telephone company all right." "I can all but the arithmetic. Miss Smith started to scold me about my arithmetic and said she wouldn't give me a job if I didn't do better. I told her I didn't want any job because I was secretary of the Hemlock 

Telephone Company." "I'll bet she didn't believe you," volunteered Joe. "I'll bet she did," rejoined Sophrona indignantly. 

Theodore, who had been putting up the team, entered and handed Joe a letter. "The postmark's Houghton; guess it's from Dora Reed."

133

Joe laid the letter face downward on the table. "Ain't you going to read it?" demanded Theodore as he took a seat opposite Joe. "Oh, Father," exclaimed Sophrona, raising her brows and cocking her head, "he wouldn't read it to us. It's a love letter! And is very private.' "Well, if it's a love letter," rejoined Joe, "I am very sure I won't read it while Sophrona is around. I don't want to add to any of the sentimental ideas already in her head."

He started to put the letter in his pocket, but Sophrona snatched it quickly and ran around the table with Joe after her. Even after he caught her she crouched over and held it so tightly that it was some minutes before he could recover it. Each one was laughing his loudest, and Joe with great good humor, although he gazed for a moment rather dolefully at the mussed and crumpled envelope.

Soon after, however, he escaped to his room and read as follows: 

Dear Joe: 

Your second letter I found on the table in the hall when I came down from my room to go to Latin. I kept it in my Livy during most of the recitation and I am afraid I didn't pay very good attention to what 

Professor Chapin was saying, for when he called on me, I jumped up suddenly, and the letter fell on the floor.

First I tried to get it, but it went so far under my chair and the girls sat so close I couldn't reach it. 

Then I started to recite, but I was so embarrassed that I wasn't able even to find the place. The girls laughed, but Professor Chapin was very kind. He gave the class a long talk on geography and made a diagram of

DORA'S LETTER 

135 the battle. When he called on me again, I got through all right.

The girls tried to tease me all the way to the gym; but when we got there, as there was fifteen minutes before drill, I slipped away from them, and went up into the stairway that leads to the tower. Just before the top there is a little window all cobwebbed and dusty that looks out over the campus. I sat down there on the stairs and read the letter through before I had to go. But I've read it once or twice since, for all those little things that happened at the Parsonage after I left were just what I wanted somebody to tell me; and there's nobody but you who could tell me; or who would understand what I wanted to know. I had to cry, tooit was so sweet of you to speak as you did about the things over which I had worried. I am not going to think of them at all if I can help it for months to come. 

I am still keeping my room at Mrs. Hedger's where 

I engaged board through the registrar before I came, but I expect to make a change soon. The day I came to Houghton I noticed the steep rocky hill east of the town, and I resolved to climb it to get the view as soon as I could. One afternoon just after college opened I started out and was having a most delightful time. It was good to find Cornus canadensis and Spiranthes gracilis and others of our old friends growing here almost as beautifully as on the slope below the umbrella tree. I also found several new plants not in bloom, and one golden-rod with flowers packed at intervals in great bunches that I have not yet attempted to name. 

At the top of the hill I found a girl who spends a great deal of time out of doors although she doesn't know many of the flowers. She is a senior and has told me all about the college. When I left her, after we had

walked back to town, she asked me to sit with her at chapel Monday morning. That is quite an honor. The different classes, you know, are assigned to different parts of the church. The freshmen sit in the rear and the seniors way down near the platform. 

Monday morning I stood at the big oak tree in front of the church as we had agreed; and soon she came, smiling very cordially, and we went in. I was almost scared to death to go so far up in front and sit with a senior on each side of me and seniors all around. For a little while I felt as if everyone was looking at me; and then I forgot all about it, for I happened to notice that the organist was unlocking the keyboard of the organ. She was on our side and we were near her. She has a very fair complexion and light hair which flies out a little from her head. As she began the voluntary as the people came in, she raised her face a little, and the light gleamed in her hair, and I thought I never saw anything so beautiful. I wondered if ever I should sit there and play while all the seniors and professors and all the other distinguished people joined in a hymn; but I didn't follow the thought for it seemed too much like a dream.

I have thought more about music than anything else since I have been here. I will tell you why. Last 

Thursday evening was the greatest event of my life.

I heard Thomas's orchestra. I enclose a program which 

I secured for you, and you may keep it. As all the instruments burst forth in that grand overture, I felt faint and weak. The first of my dreams had come true. 

With my own ears I had heard the best of musicians interpret the composition of a great master. Each number seemed to lift me to new heights of feeling. And at last when the violins breathed forth the sad and

DORA'S LETTER 

137 tragic strains of that Beethoven symphony, I was so agitated that if the concert had not then come to its close I could scarcely have listened longer. While I live in 

Houghton, I suppose I can often hear good music; but if I were never to hear a note of music again, I feel as if I could live forever on the memory of that night. 

Pardon me, Joe, for leaving the thread of my story in the church. When we came out, the senior, whose name I had learned is Fanny Wheeler, asked me to call at her room. She insisted finally that I take tea with her a day or two later. When I went to her address I was surprised to find that she lived in the Mu Phi house. 

I met a delightful group of girls there. We had a dainty supper and then danced and sang. When I was ready to leave they asked me to go the next day with them in a barge to a football game at Franklin. I was so astonished that I hardly knew what to say; but after a moment's deliberation I áccepted with due decorum and gravity, for it finally dawned on me that the occasion for all these kindnesses was that I was being rushed to join a sorority.

I was never so happy before in all my life. On the day of the game I dressed the best I could and went to the sorority house. The barge, a great five-seated carriage, was already at the door; and we started as soon as Miss Bailey, the chaperon, came. She is one of the instructors in English. Besides the chaperon there were fourteen girls and not a man in the barge. Fanny 

Wheeler drove. She seems to be able to do everything. 

I guess she can equal Belle Jones in managing horses.

You ought to have seen us when we started out with all our colors flying. We had Houghton pennants, and 

Mu Phi pennants, and one or two of the girls carried 

Franklin flags. We sang songs and rounds; and when-

ever Miss Bailey would let us, we gave our cheers and yells. She didn't approve of our giving a yell if there was anybody around. But as the girls selected her because in addition to being a charming lady she is also a little near-sighted, and as one of the girls was appointed to keep her attention at critical moments, she didn't curb our freedom very much.

When we reached the field there was an immense crowd, and we thought at first we shouldn't be able to see the game, but Miss Wheeler knew somebody who made a place for us; and we drove right up to the ropes and saw the entire game from the barge. I had never seen a college football game before and did not understand it very well, but there was a great deal of excitement and Franklin won. After it was over several of the Franklin boys that the girls knew came and spoke to us, and also one or two of the students from Tech. "On the way back I sat next a slender little girl with light brown hair, blue eyes, and dainty regular features who is one of the best musicians in college. Elizabeth 

Fairchild is her name. She lives in New York and her mother is an old friend of my aunt. She has told me a great deal about Mrs. Van Alstyne that I never heard before. She says my aunt is known throughout the city as a patron of music and is very generous in helping young students. Elizabeth is a freshman and was to room just a little way from the sorority house with an older girl who has now left college. She has invited me to share her room, and I think I shall, for I am afraid I have fallen in love with her she has traveled and knows so many things and yet is in every way sweet and modest.

Last evening when I came from my last recitation I found a note awaiting me. It was a formal invita-

DORA'S LETTER 

139 tion to join Mu Phi. I was almost afraid to go down to dinner for fear of showing how happy I was. Still, I didn't know what to do. I wanted so much to join that 

I thought I couldn't at all consider the other side as uncle would wish me to; and I knew I should feel better if someone advised me about it. I wished that I could talk with one of the girls' mothers; but as I could think of no one else to whom I could go, I went to the dean. 

I have learned since how great a risk I took, considering the fact that I wanted my adviser to say “Yes.” 

The girls say the dean is old and unsympathetic, and finds pleasure only in making the students study and in enforcing the college rules. But when I went to see her I didn't know that. At first she didn't seem to understand what I asked, and she made me tell it over three or four times; and then she inquired all about my family and Hemlock. She began to put on her wraps and I thought she wasn't going to give me any answer; but she called me back and said that if they had really invited me, and if I wanted to join, she didn't see any great harm in it; for Mu Phi was considered the most select sorority in Houghton, and the faculty was rather favorably disposed toward it, inasmuch as its members were comparatively refined and were reasonably good students. Like a little goose I went away happy and at once wrote my acceptance of the invitation. Today 

I received my pledge pin. When I went to show Elizabeth, I found to my delight that she was wearing one, too.

Such happy thoughts as these hold my attention most of the time when I am not studying. Yet my mind often goes back to the day the train slowly pulled out of the Boughton station and I saw my uncle stand ing beside you on the platform waving his last good-

bye. Since he has now gone to the West, I suppose I shall never see him again. How fortunate I was to be able to make my home for three years with him. I appreciate now what he did for me. Like visions, bits of my life at the Parsonage often rise before me. At the college church if the minister's language grows simple and eloquent, I see again my uncle's tall form in the pulpit. When I bow my head, there comes the scene of family prayer. Best of all is the recollection of his kindly face as he talked with me in his study. His letters now have to take the place of those talks. 

As I sit alone in my room the thought often comes to me, how strange a thing is this life of ours-how little is the space in which we live and act, and how far on every side stretches out the land of dreams. At home on the farm, the future was ominous and uncertain. At the Parsonage, my days on the farm became a shadowy tale-a piece out of somebody else's life. 

The future then became a vision, but unreal. Here in college I can scarcely believe in past or future. I find myself stationed on a little spot of reality and sometimes doubt if I am sure even of that. I see the fly buzzing on the window pane, and I wonder when I shall awake, and find myself a fly buzzing on the window paneroused from my dream of human life. "Pardon me, Joe, for yielding to these fancies. I shall not utterly give way to my dreams. The old oaks of this campus and the hills about, if nothing else, will keep me from doing that. Nature is the thread of reality that has run unchanged through all. The dandelions have blossomed through all my dreams; and through all, you gather them with me. "Every afternoon I take a walk. Thus far the weather has been glorious. It seems almost as if nature were

DORA'S LETTER 

141 richer here even than at Hemlock. I cannot describe to you the beauty of the autumn days lighted with soft golden haze, nor the mysterious sweetness of these nights. When I close my books at night it is often late and all has grown quiet. The little browsy breeze that rustles through the branches calms me and prepares me for sleep. Now as I write beside the open window, I hear only the chirr of crickets. All other sounds have ceased. I am alone with you. If I have any secret words to tell you, no one will hear them. 

And so before I go to sleep, I'm going to whisper a little love message. I love you. I have not worried since your second letter came. What I have written shows how happy I am. My love grows neither less nor more.

I am content to keep it so. As far as I am able to judge, I have no other desire than to hold for you the place in my heart that you found last August as we drove along the swamp road.

Dora

XIX 

SOPHRONA'S CURIOSITY 

Sophrona pursued her course at the Clifton Business

College throughout the winter, and in March returned to Hemlock, the proud possessor of a recommendation from Mr. Keeler, who assured all whom it might concern that Sophrona Cobb was proficient in bookkeeping, was satisfactory in arithmetic, was able to take simple dictation in shorthand, and was competent to operate a typewriter accurately and with speed. Sophrona entered at once into her duties as secretary of the Hemlock Telephone Company and spent several hours each day at the exchange building. After the South Hills extension was built, business greatly increased. The number of instruments connected with the exchange multiplied rapidly. Sophrona spent part of her time with her accounts and part at the switchboard with Mildred Dawson and did not seem to find her task laborious. 

One Sunday in April after dinner when Mr. Clay,

Theodore, and Amanda had retired to their bedrooms to take naps, Sophrona, Henry Brush, and Joe were engaged in conversation in the parlor. "You must find it awful lonesome, Sophrona," remarked Henry, "to stay up in that exchange all day with nobody around." "It isn't as lonesome as sitting on the top of a pole all day as you and Joe do," she retorted. 142

SOPHRONA'S CURIOSITY 

143 "You're shut up all the while, and we're out doors." "But you don't know what's going on; I know everything that happens in Hemlock." "You aren't supposed to pay any attention to what people say," observed Joe gruffly. "Guess I couldn't tell whether the line is busy if I didn't listen in," retorted Sophrona. "The other night 

I was with Mildred Dawson when Charlie Haskins came in to go to bed. I said we three knew more of what's going on in Hemlock than anybody else in town.

Charlie said he knew more than we did, for he listened to the telephone messages at night, and daytime reads the cards in the post office." "If he wants to work in the post office daytime and then tend the exchange at night, he's welcome to learn about everybody's business for all of me," remarked Henry. Charlie stood it for quite a while but yesterday he complained to Dr. Dawson." "Doc won't stop it," observed Henry. "He cares too much about business to call down one of the patrons of the line." As there seemed to be nothing else to do Henry soon took his leave.

SOPHRONA'S CURIOSITY 

147 When Sophrona saw that he was well on his way toward town, she hastened upstairs to Joe's room. 

Looking out over the farm from a rear window, she saw that Joe was on the crest of the hill examining the young trees in the orchard. She turned quickly toward 

Joe's desk and lifted a tin box and shook the padlock, but it was securely fastened. She searched through all the drawers but seemed to find nothing that held her attention. At last she raised the desk blotter, and a smile, half vindictive, crossed her face. She had found an envelope addressed to Dora Reed. It was unsealed. 

Sophrona again looked from the rear window, and then drawing the letter from the envelope sat on the floor and read as follows: 

Dear Dora: 

Before me on my desk is the little tin box that holds your letters. It is wide open and the padlock lies beside it. In it I have just replaced the letters -nine in all. I have not been to church this bright 

April morning. I have thought it better to remain at home to read every word that you have written. 

What pictures they give of your moods! They seem to represent all your feelings from the heights of confidence and happiness in your second letter in which you told of being asked to join your sorority to the despondency of this last tiny note in which you say you are so blue.

Sophrona, the little scamp, has always tried to snatch one of your letters when she has found me reading one, and she is always prying around my desk. Her efforts will be in vain. I shall never leave the box unlocked. Your letters are not for her eyes. She is growing into a very attractive

girl, but lately for some reason she has taken delight in pretending that she is empty-headed and heedless. She has not deceived me. I know that at heart she is a sensible girl, and I think as much of her as if she were my own sister. 

I must tell you of a long trip I am planning to take as soon as haying is over. The W. and E. line to Plattsburg is out of order so much of the time that our company has been unable to get some of its long distance messages to the Bell. I myself think that long distance business is bound to go to the line that is most reliable and that people can hear the best over. For this reason I intend in

July to take my bicycle and ride along the W. and 

E. line from Clifton to Plattsburg and try to find out what it is that causes them so much trouble. 

Then I'll follow the Hudson River line to Albany and look over the exchange there and try to discover why Bell service is better than that given by the W. and E. I am looking forward to this trip with a great deal of interest for I have never had a chance to study the construction of any of these long lines; and I have never visited any large city like Plattsburg or Albany.

The principal excitement in Hemlock lately has not been over the telephone but over the draining of the big swamp. I have not told you before because I did not know how it was going to turn out. Last fall a company was formed, and a contract was let to blast out the rock at the east corner of the mill pond. They worked at it off and on all winter. I helped survey to straighten the channel through the middle of the swamp. 

It was

SOPHRONA'S CURIOSITY 

149 beautiful in there sometimes. In places where the hemlocks grew thick, the snow lay in the branches and shut out a great deal of the light, and the ground was often bare and dry underneath. We used to build a fire and make coffee at noon, and we sat there on the roots and ate our lunch almost as warm and snug as if we were in a tent. 

About three weeks ago the last blast was fired where they were working near Fox's. You ought to have seen the water go down that slope. It cut a deep channel, and there's a big stream running there now, but not a drop goes down to the old dam. Of course, the water will go lower when there's a channel cut to the new outlet and the creek bed is straightened; but even now at your end of the swamp it all looks different. Everybody's making plans to clear up the land and raise celery and onions. In time the old swamp ought for miles to be a level stretch of the best black gar- den land in the state of New York. You had better not plan to sell your farm until you can get the right price. You ought to be able to rent it now at a figure that will produce a good income. 

But I am sure Mr. Hamblin understands all that for I talked with him the other day. 

Of course, you know, Dora, that the trip I have arranged for next July wouldn't stand in the way very long, if you should decide to visit Hemlock this summer. I can't tell you how much I should like to walk with you around your old home. Now a person can go with dry shoes anywhere but in the creek bed; and the stream in the middle of the swamp is larger than before. I have been working

this winter on a canoe in which we can collect flowers the whole length of the swamp, if you should come. 

Why, Dora, did you write a letter and tear it up? You have no need to worry. Remember that you are as free now as when you were a little girl to love anybody else than me. All I ask is that you follow day by day the dictates of your own heart.

JOE 

Sophrona read it all very patiently and slowly, but she read none of it a second time. For a few moments she sat with the letter lying on her lap, and as she gazed from the window toward the sprouting orchard and the blue April sky flecked with white clouds, little lines of stress and trouble worked their way across her winsome and child-like face.
