THE INVITATION 

About three weeks after Joe sent to Dora the letter that Sophrona read, he received the following reply:

Dear Joe: 

Put off your trip until later in the summer, and come to see me, as once you promised to do. 

Please do this for me. My aunt, Mrs. Van

Alstyne, is to have a house party during the second week in July at her lodge in the mountains, and she wants you to come. Let us talk over the old times and climb the mountains together, just as we used to do.

My aunt and Mrs. Fairchild, Elizabeth's mother, came to college a few days ago to visit Elizabeth and me.

They entertained us and some of our friends with dinners and drives, and we had a lovely time. I had no idea that my aunt was so delightful a person. She took us to a tea in Pomeroy and introduced us to a distinguished lady who is a leader in the social circles of that city. My aunt has a great many musical friends. She didn't seem to be able to do enough for me after she had talked with Professor Olmsted about the courses I am taking and the progress I have made. She insists on my spending all my vacations with her 151

me. and has arranged the house party especially for She has invited several musicians in Pomeroy and some of the young people she is helping, but I don't know how many will come. Fanny 

Wheeler, and Elizabeth, and her cousin, will certainly be there, and I want you to meet them and

I want them to see you, and I know we shall have a delightful time.

I don't know that I have told you how much I like Elizabeth. No one ever had a more loyal friend. I have told her about Hemlock and she asks questions about every little thing. She says she likes you and Uncle and Miss Stevens, but she doesn't like the rest of the people there. Don't you think it lovely in her to be so interested in everything? A few weeks ago Professor Olmsted asked me to go to Boston to help in giving a concert. I was wild with joy. You should have seen Elizabeth's face when I told her. She looked so glad and happy, and her smile was like a burst of sunshine, and all for me. I wonder if I was ever so glad for any one else. She and I have been friends ever since we went to the football game at Franklin, but she never expressed in words any of her friendship for me until she went to New York for the Easter vacation and wrote me a letter, the first 

I ever received from her. I cried when I read in the letter that she loved me best of anybody in the world except her mother.

It was so funny, Elizabeth went down to her home in New York and she told me she expected to see her cousin there, Arthur Pendleton, who also lives in New York. You remember I told you he is a Tech student and was here to see Elizabeth

THE INVITATION 

153 soon after Christmas. (You will see him in July for he has been invited to the house party.) Well, the day after Elizabeth went home, the maid brought in Arthur Pendleton's card. I wish you could have seen his face when I told him Elizabeth had gone to New York. Nevertheless he decided to remain in Houghton a day or two, and as it turned out he stayed the entire week, and Fanny Wheeler and I saw quite a good deal of him. I know you will like him when you meet him in the mountains, he is so full of fun. Fanny Wheeler said she never passed a pleasanter vacation. He and a friend of Fanny's took us on several drives, and one evening we took a walk down to the Fish Hatchery.

That was a weird experience. A single line of planks fastened end to end over the crest of the dam furnishes a footing for the man who operates the gates.

In daytime the rushing water that passes underneath makes one's head whirl. I don't know what inspired us, or what made us attempt in the night what I had never dared do in the daytime. There seemed to be a strange fascination in walking across in the darkness, knowing that a single misstep would plunge us over the dam into the swollen stream that goes swirling down among the rocks. We four sat on the center plank with our feet almost touching the rushing water and gazed down into the black abyss, and talked about life and death and other solemn things. 

I am glad Elizabeth's cousin is going to be in the party with us next summer, for I want you to see him. He has been in the Adirondacks before, and I think you and he will become friends at once.

He likes the mountains and is interested in nature and literature and the things you enjoy. 

My trip to Boston comes next week. I was told 

I never could get permission to go and miss two of my examinations. We have a new dean now. 

She is quite different from the old one. Although she is young and full of fun, they say it is just as hard to get permissions from her as from her predecessor. But Fanny Wheeler, who knows all about the college, told me what to do. She told me that Miss Sherrard, the new dean, likes red. 

Fanny insisted that I wear a red dress if I wanted to be successful. I never wear red, but finally I consented to wear a red waist that Fanny borrowed for me, and so arrayed I called and was entirely successful. I not only got permission to miss the exams, but also to give up some of my regular work next fall, and take more music. 

Fanny engineers all the affairs of the Mu Phi girls. 

You will see her next summer at Elizabeth's. She is like you! For she can do almost anything.

Oh, it is beautiful in Houghton now. It is the most heavenly part of the spring. "The lovely town is white with apple bloom." In almost every old-fashioned dooryard great clusters of lilacs are hanging. Downy green leaves are unfolding on poplars and elms, and high in the branches orioles are calling to one another-and to me. 

In green lawns golden dandelions are shining. The air is sweet and the sky deep blue. Everything is so beautiful that it makes my heart ache.

Next July I don't want to have to try to tell you in a letter how the mountains make me feel. I want you to be with me. Don't let that trip or

THE INVITATION 

155 anything else prevent your coming. If you can't come at the time of the party, come later in July. 

In August I have agreed to return to Houghton to act as organist in a church in Pomeroy in place of Professor Olmsted who is to be away on vacation. Since my aunt's friends in Pomeroy are eager to have me come, and Professor Olmsted says I must not let the summer go without practice, I do not wish to miss this opportunity. Professor Olmsted is very encouraging, but sometimes I grow discouraged when I think of all the years of work ahead of me.

It is twilight now. A great robin sits on the top of an evergreen in Professor Chapin's yard and sings solemnly. As the day closes and I listen to him, I am a little serious and sad. If I worry, it is not due to distrust of you. When I think of you, there comes to my mind the truest friendship I have ever known and a thousand delicate sweet kindnesses. My wish is that I could trust myself as confidently as I trust you.

DORA

XXI THE RIDE TO THE HOUSE PARTY 

With the first crow of the earliest and most optimistic cock, Joe arose in the darkness of a mid-July morning. 

He gathered a few articles that he wished to take to the house party, dressed in his corduroy bicycle suit, and stole downstairs with lighted lamp. He was about to use the oilstove to make his coffee, when Amanda called to him from her bedroom and assured him that she would soon prepare his breakfast. "Don't get up, Amanda. Just say good-bye to the others for me, and let me get off without disturbing anybody." "I might as well get up and get the washing started. 

I think it's going to be a hot day.” "Gosh, Joe, you're goin' to have an early start," exclaimed his father who was standing in his night clothes at the kitchen door. "Yes, I want to ride as far as I can while it's cool," he answered and busied himself with fastening his baggage to the handle bars of his bicycle. 

A few minutes later on the lawn before the house he said good-bye affectionately to his father and Amanda; and, mounting his bicycle, started on his long journey. 

Up the little slope to town he passed with strong strokes as silent as a shadow. It was still dark when he dismounted and climbed the hill near Jones's; the earliest 156

THE RIDE TO THE HOUSE PARTY 

157 birds were singing when he reached the rim of the valley; they were singing their loudest when he rode swiftly around the curves in and out among the little hills that cluster near the summit of the road to Clifton. 

Now and then a hare leaped leisurely from the beaten track to the roadside, and crows aroused from their night perch suddenly winged away with vehement and startling calls. The east was the color of rubies and at the zenith were streaks of pale pink clouds. In the hollows blotches and bars of silver mist were lying or water gleamed gray. An unsubstantial fairy country was fashioned of earth and sky.

As Joe coasted down to the richer soil near Clifton, the odor of ground-nut and raspberries filled the air. 

The village of old houses with porches rose-laden, with lawns crowded with fragrant shrubbery, flitted by as silent and unreal as the glowing heavens. He turned right and followed the valley. In the growing light the great clumps of elderbery blossom loomed up in snowy whiteness. Masses of daisies stood at the roadside and chestnut trees were in bloom.

He turned into a level stretch of highway almost shut in by the mountains and dashed onward like a centaur rejoicing in its strength. No weariness delayed him. He was exhilarated by his speed and the fresh morning air. The miles flew by and the sun rose and lighted the summits of the surrounding peaks but did not yet shine in the deep valley. At length he came to a fork in the road where the route indicated by the map he had drawn required a turn to the left from the smooth highway into a rough mountain trail difficult even for a foot passenger.

He dismounted and pushed his bicycle steadily up the rough trail but grew warm and languid before he

reached the top. The sun beat unmercifully upon his shoulders. At last, however, he rounded the curve at the summit. There he saw the valley spread before him. Loon Lake gleamed in the foreground and Marcy and Whiteface and numerous other peaks thrust themselves up from among the mass of distant hills. A few minutes he lingered and then began the rocky descent. 

Coming soon to a better road he sped on until in the lowlands he found a gang of men constructing a telephone line. There he forgot his bicycle and studied with eager interest every detail of the undertaking, asked innumerable questions, and even assisted in the work. About noon he came to broad fields of black muck in which a group of boys and women were weeding onions. He searched out the owner and talked for an hour or more on the best method of cultivation and finally remained at the farmer's house for dinner. 

Then he resumed his journey but discovered that his legs were sore and weary and, unaccustomed as he was to long trips, his progress became comparatively slow. 

It was dark when he reached the Ausable River and turned into the Lake Placid road. Near John Brown's grave he found the trail that was to take him to the Van Alstyne lodge. Two or three miles beyond he came to a cottage, but all was dark. His knocking brought no response. The place evidently was unoccupied. He mounted and rode on. In the gloom, although his acetylene light shone brightly, he hit obstructions in the rough trail. Finally he decided to walk. Around a bend in the road drifted the sound of music, and a cottage brilliantly illuminated came into view. A number of people were playing the piano and singing in a room that opened on the porch. For a

THE RIDE TO THE HOUSE PARTY 

159 moment he hesitated, and then knocked at a rear 

The door was opened by a buxom red-cheeked Irish girl who looked curiously at the tired and muddy rider. "Does Mrs. Van Alstyne live here?" he inquired. "Yes; this is Mrs. Van Alstyne's. What do you want?" "I am Joseph Clay. Isn't Dora Reed staying here?" he asked. "You are not the young gentleman expected today for dinner, are you?" inquired the maid in surprise, and she looked critically at Joe from head to foot. "I came to this door because I wanted to wash up before I saw anybody," explained Joe in loud and embarrassed tones. "What is this?" inquired an energetic gray-haired woman who overhearing part of the conversation entered the kitchen from an adjoining room. "Is this 

Joseph Clay? I am Mrs. Van Alstyne," she announced with a cordial smile. "We were afraid we were not to see you until tomorrow. I think I would know you, now the light shines on your face, even if you had not told your name. You resemble your father." 

"They say I look like him, ma'am." "You came all the way from Hemlock on your bicycle?" she asked, glancing involuntarily at his mudbespattered trousers. "I don't see how you ever rode up from the corner after dark." "I don't know how I did either," said Joe. "I want to look at it in the daylight tomorrow to see what 

I came over." "You must be very tired. William will show you your room, and when you are ready we shall have something to eat."

"Oh, I'm not particular. I'll wash up right here and not take all the dirt upstairs." "Just as you wish, but I think you would find everything convenient in your room," said Mrs. Van Alstyne smiling kindly.

After she left the kitchen Joe engaged in some parley with the servants but soon all seemed to be adjusted to their wishes for with coat removed he was permitted to scrub his hands, face, and hair to his complete satisfaction. After William had been twice assured that Joe possessed no baggage except the bag tied to the handle bars of his bicycle, the tired guest was shown to his room.

There Joe continued his toilet for some time, and when he reappeared was neatly dressed in a fresh bicycle suit. At the bottom of the stairs he found Dora and Mrs. Van Alstyne. Dora had never looked more beautiful. Her hair was arranged in a new and becoming manner, and she wore a dainty dress quite different from any Joe had seen her wear before. A warm light shone in her eyes as she greeted him and led him to the adjoining room to meet her friends.

The first was Elizabeth Fairchild, a petite girl with light brown hair, blue eyes, and dainty regular features. 

She told Joe he must waste no time in becoming acquainted with her, for she knew him already. Dora had told her all about him. The next was Henrietta 

Smith, who was slight and apparently not active or strong. She had light curly hair, and wore eyeglasses.

She said it was too bad that he had not come a few minutes earlier, as Mr. Vogler had been playing some delightful mazurkas. Otto Vogler stood beside her.

He was of medium height, and fair, and had a face of German cast, and spoke with a German accent. Next

THE RIDE TO THE HOUSE PARTY 

161 came Arthur Pendleton. who was tall, bright, and vivacious. He had an agreeable easy manner, and asked Joe how far he had ridden, and inquired concerning the roads and the make and model of his bicycle. 

Then he met Walter Rexford, who yawned as if he had been roused from a nap, and like the other boys was dressed in white flannel trousers and a blue serge coat. 

The last was Fanny Wheeler. Fanny was tall and redcheeked, and evidently was as happy and healthy as a flower. She said she had been waiting for him two or three days. The double-bladed paddle was broken and she wanted somebody to help her mend it. The boys in the party hadn't been able to repair it, and Dora had said that when Mr. Clay came he would know how.

Mrs. Van Alstyne broke off the conversation by inviting Joe to walk into the dining-room. She, Elizabeth, Dora, and Arthur went with Joe; the others went to the porch. "Did you say we are only eighty-one miles from 

Hemlock?" inquired Dora, after Joe had been seated at the table and was supplied with food. "Yes; it is eighty-one miles the way I came, and in a direct line it must be fifteen or twenty miles shorter." "I don't believe Mr. Clay would say 'only eighty-one miles,'" laughed Mrs. Van Alstyne. "He looks very tired after his journey." "It's a rough ride. I crossed three notches and there wasn't ten miles of smooth straight road all the way." "How long were you coming?" inquired Arthur. 'I started before light, but I stopped several times to examine things that interested me." "There must have been a great deal of interest in such a trip," suggested Elizabeth.

"Yes, just out of Clifton there's the finest silo in the world, but I didn't stop there because I've seen it before." "Do you mean a mile southeast of Clifton where the road branches?" asked Arthur. "Yes," replied Joe with surprise, and he returned to the table untasted the dish of preserves from which he was about to eat. "Have you been there?" "That's on a farm my uncle used to own. I didn't know it was the best silo in the world." "That's what they call it. It cost two thousand dollars. Your uncle must have been Williams who owned the place before Watkins bought it." "Yes, my uncle is Josiah Williams. I visited him once when he lived on that farm." "What else did you see besides the silo?" inquired Elizabeth.

Joe reached for a plate of cold meat and, pushing off the slices that remained replied, "Something that interested me a good deal and that I think will be of interest to Dora is a farm where they raise onions and celery. 

The owner told me he is making a hundred dollars an acre on his best land, and the soil didn't look as good as that they will be turning up next spring on Dora's farm. You see," he said to Mrs. Van Alstyne, "they've been draining the big swamp in Hemlock and it makes a lot of Dora's land fit for the plow." "I am surprised to hear that," exclaimed Mrs. Van

Alstyne, turning toward Joe with interest. "How was that enterprise started?" "Oh a company was formed last fall to blast through the limestone and straighten the channel of the creek." "Then it will come out just as I predicted," said Mrs.

Van Alstyne in tones that evinced much satisfaction.

THE RIDE TO THE HOUSE Party 

163 "I always told my brother that some day the swamp would prove to be the best part of his farm. I urged him to drain it, but somehow he never seemed to be able to carry the plan through." "It's certainly going to be carried through this time," asserted Joe, taking a bite from a huge sandwich he had made, "and next summer the whole swamp will be dry land." "You have followed your father's example and are a farmer, I suppose?" asked Mrs. Van Alstyne, seizing the opportunity while Joe was busy with his food to regard him curiously. "Yes, I have always lived on a farm, but lately I have been interested in the telephone business. I didn't notice any telephone line here when I came in." "No, William and the horses is our only means of communication with the outside world," she replied. "When Mr. Van Alstyne built this lodge he was looking for a place where he wouldn't be troubled with unpleasant neighbors or other curious people. He said he wanted the road to be only good enough to get in the building materials and furniture." "Yes," replied Joe, "when I have a vacation I always like to be where everything's natural and wild.” "Joe is a fine botanist," volunteered Dora a little proudly, "and besides he knows almost every bird and animal." "I suppose it was Miss Stevens who interested you in such things," inquired Mrs. Van Alstyne with a smile. "Yes, did you know her?" cried Joe in surprise. "Oh, no, I lived in Hemlock before the Renaissance," laughed Mrs. Van Alstyne, "but Dora has told me all

about it. Won't you have something else, Mr. Clay? 

Let me ask William to bring some meat." "No, thanks. Don't make any more trouble. I shall get along very nicely by eating some more bread and cake. Is there any telephone line," Joe continued after taking a swallow of tea, "nearer than the Lake Placid road?" "Yes," answered Arthur, "the other day when I was climbing the mountain I noticed a gang of workmen putting a line right through the woods over the south slope." "Can you sketch me a map so I can find it?" inquired 

Joe eagerly. "I'll lead you to it some day when we climb the mountain." replied Arthur. "I'd like to have you tell me all about line construction. Dora said you're interested in telephones. I had a short course at Tech this year in telephone engineering, but I don't know much about the practical side." "Don't make plans in a hurry," advised Elizabeth. "I want to take you down to the end of the lake to name those birds I see every day running along the shore." "You can't do that until you have helped me repair the paddle," shouted Fanny Wheeler who was just entering the room. "Your principal business, remember," said Dora archly, "is to tell me everything that has happened in Hemlock." "Yes, Mr. Clay, you must beware," said Mrs. Van

Alstyne laughing, "or these boys and girls will take all your time and leave you no opportunity to visit with 

Dora and me."

XXII 

DORA'S FRIENDS 

The next morning when Joe looked from his window he saw a clear sky and a grove of large hardwood trees among which was scattered here and there a pine. 

Before the cottage, distant only one or two hundred feet, was a sparkling lake. Along the rocky shore grew innumerable white birches. Before a boathouse a rowboat and a canoe lay on a float.

When breakfast was over Joe and Dora walked to the shore where Otto Vogler and Henrietta were standing beside the boats. "Were you going out on the lake?" asked Joe. "I never row," answered Vogler moodily. "Such rough work injures my hands.” "Otto has spent a great deal of time in developing and training the muscles of his hands for piano playing," explained Dora, as Joe stared a moment in wonder at Vogler's white hands and long delicate fingers. "Hard and rough work could destroy all he has gained." "Yes, I have arrived late in a city, and unable to find any one to carry my bag, I have in a few minutes disorganized the practice of weeks," remarked Vogler mournfully. "In a place like this you must miss a lot of fun,” observed Joe. 165

"Otto has to give up everything that can injure his technique," remarked Dora. "I never hear Otto play," sighed Henrietta, "but I think of the sacrifices he has made to perfect his fingering."

The four entered a large rowboat and went out on the lake. At the left of the float a slender point jutted far. Broken rocks marked the water's edge, and white birches with delicate pendulous branches leaned from one another at varying angles. Underneath grew witch-hazel bushes and a mat of golden-green grass. 

Across the lake wooded hillsides rose from the shore, and beyond in several directions mountains were visible. "Let's go up the inlet to the pond," exclaimed Dora, after they had rowed about more or less aimlessly for a few minutes. "There's a bog along the edge, and we can see ever so many marsh flowers without leaving the boat."

Joe leaned forward about to send the craft in the direction indicated with vigorous strokes, but Otto begged to be taken back to the lodge. He said he did not wish to exhaust himself before the morning practice. 

On their return Mrs. Van Alstyne announced that she had planned a noonday picnic on the mountainside.

A mile up the trail was a shaded level spot from which they could get a remarkable view of the valley. Accordingly William loaded the station wagon with baskets and rugs, and an hour or two later with Mrs. Van 

Alstyne, Walter Rexford, Elizabeth, Henrietta, and 

Otto Vogler as passengers, headed up the rough trail. 

Arthur, Dora, Fanny, and Joe followed on foot. They walked leisurely, Dora using the opportunity to display Joe's knowledge of birds and plants. Flowers were

DORA'S FRIENDS 

167 numerous along the way, and Joe seemed to know them all. Fanny, with Arthur at her heels, went to damper ground below the road, seeking rarities. Her call soon brought Joe and Dora to see a Philadelphia lily, standing tall and stately in the grass, with orange bloom and formal whorls of fresh green leaves. A little later as they were climbing the bank below the place selected for the picnic, they found another orange lily growing in matted colonies. "Why, this is the orange day lily growing away up here," exclaimed Dora. "Then there must have been a house somewhere near," shouted Joe. "I never heard of any house on this trail," said Mrs.

Van Alstyne, who was standing at the top of the bank. "It might have been destroyed years ago," answered 

Joe, as he came over the crest. "Yes, you can see the lilac bushes over there, and apple trees are all around." "And here are the household weeds, burdocks, motherwort, and catnip," added Dora.

Beyond a screen of second growth and bushes, where the picnickers found a level space carpeted with periwinkle, there was a remarkable view of the valley. In a little hollow in the foreground lay the Van Alstyne lake and lodge, and through a notch in the hills could be seen a section of Lake Placid, so distant it was almost merged in the sky. A little to the rear of the level space where they were seated, they saw piles of stone indicating the location of a house, and beyond were more extensive remains of the foundation of a barn. "Well, there certainly must have been a house here," exclaimed Mrs. Van Alstyne, "and our trail must have

been cut by the people who lived in it. We have always wondered who first used the road." "It might have been made by somebody who took out logs," suggested Arthur. "It doesn't look as if there'd ever been timber on this slope," observed Joe. "What made the farmers choose this spot for a house?" asked Henrietta. "They couldn't have been influenced by the view. Probably they didn't notice it at all." "Probably they thought the land was good up here, and left when they found they were mistaken," suggested Walter. "More likely they kidded themselves into thinking the land was good because they liked the view," said Joe. "They must have been exceptional farmers then," remarked Henrietta. "Most farmers seem to take pleasure in shutting out the best views with their barns." "I guess the people who lived up here were exceptional," answered Joe. "If they hadn't liked this view, they wouldn't have lived so long on this poor ground. 

They must have been here a good while before they could build that large barn, and the trail shows years of travel. It took years to spread these weeds around and to grow the orchard." "Let's make a story about it," exclaimed Dora brightly. "Perhaps a bride and groom first lived on this spot.' 99 "The man drove his ox team," added Arthur obediently, "and cleared and plowed the land, and gathered stones for the foundations of the barn.”

DORA'S FRIENDS 

169 "And here the first winter the little bride and her groom quarreled, but as they lived alone they had to make up again," laughed Walter cynically. 

Dora glanced at Walter with reproving eyes. "In the spring they set the lilacs and planted the apple trees." "Summer after summer they worked and improved their place," added Joe. "Children were born," suggested Mrs. Van Alstyne; "where we now sit, they dragged their carts and played with their dolls." "There came hours of anticipation and joy, of illness and anxiety, of frightening isolation in blizzards and storms," volunteered Elizabeth. “And the children grew up, and one by one disap-. peared down the valley," continued Dora. "But they never forgot the view of the valley and mountains," added Fanny. "You are ascribing to these pioneers your own emotions," objected Henrietta. "While you have been working with music, you have been trained to respond to a view like this, but those who farmed this stony ground probably lost the little feeling they had when they came. Feelings are like skin. Mother Nature hardens both where people live rough lives." "They had some aesthetic feeling," protested Dora, "or they wouldn't have cultivated the lilies, lilacs, and periwinkle." "On the whole," said Mrs. Van Alstyne, "I am inclined to agree with Henrietta. If these people really had cared for finer things, they wouldn't have turned their backs on every sort of social opportunity, and if in their isolation they found they missed such things, they wouldn't have stayed here so long."

"No musician would come to such a place," said Otto. "His art would be useless with no one to listen." "That matter of audience," said Arthur, "is exactly where the people who lived in this house were different from musicians. They didn't care for an audience. 

They lived their lives and said nothing. They never tried to tell how they felt." "Probably they couldn't have told if they had tried," said Walter. "I agree with that," said Fanny, "feeling and its expression seem to be two different matters.' "On our farm," commented Joe, "we have a man called Theodore. When the wind blows over the timothy, and there are white clouds in the sky, and birds are singing, he enjoys it just as you do, but he couldn't describe what he sees or tell how he feels." "If these farmers, like Theodore, did appreciate nature, and were attracted in the first place by the view," remarked Henrietta, "why, after years of toil to make a home, did they leave? The view hadn't changed." "Some disaster," answered Arthur, "ill health, death, fire, financial ruin-some human disaster drove them away." "The wonder," commented Mrs. Van Alstyne, "is not why they left, but why they stayed as long as they did, toiling hard and suffering severely." "Even disaster can't drive some farmers away," Joe remarked. "Everybody knows of places where some old widower or bachelor lives alone because he won't give up the fields he has cultivated so long." "Lack of initiative and old habit make them hang on," said Walter. "No doubt about that," said Fanny, "but among their old habits there may be a habit of feeling."

DORA'S FRIENDS 

171 "And to conclude our story," remarked Dora as she caught Mrs. Van Alstyne's monitory glance, "all over these mountains men and women have lived their lives and died, and their only memorials are lilacs and piles of stone.' 

After dinner that evening Mrs. Van Alstyne had provided in Joe's honor an elaborate program of music, although she must have known that he lacked the training that would fit him to enjoy many of the difficult classical selections. Otto had not been inclined to take part in the program, as he said that the exertions of the day would keep him from doing his best. His obligations to Mrs. Van Alstyne were such, however, that he could not well refuse. Walter, also, with his customary desire for ease, would have preferred to remain outstretched on the studio couch that he had occupied most of the time since the return from the picnic. Mrs. Van 

Alstyne swept aside all objections and insisted that each of her proteges and guests must be put through his paces.

When Joe entered the drawing-room with Dora he found the men of the party dressed, as on the previous evening, in white flannel trousers and blue serge coats.

He was slightly disturbed because of his costume, especially as Walter Rexford gazed searchingly at his bicycle breeches. Mrs. Van Alstyne, however, did not seem to see anything unusual, and was the personification of courtesy. She selected an easy chair, and placed it in the best location, and insisted that Joe should occupy it, as the concert had been arranged for his pleasure.

Dora and Henrietta, who were devoting themselves to musical composition and were skillful with more than one instrument, entertained with violin solos. Walter

Rexford sang exquisitely. He had a wonderful tenor voice, for which (by those unacquainted with his indolence), a great future was predicted. Arthur, also, had a remarkable voice, and had studied with good teachers. 

As leader of the Tech glee club, he had attracted favorable attention, and might, perhaps, have looked forward to success equal to Walter's, but he alone among the company was not inclined to take a musical career seriously. Elizabeth had been instructed in music from earliest childhood, and was a soprano of unusual range. 

Fanny Wheeler distinguished herself at the piano, at which Otto was almost a master. Joe, in an attempt to express his appreciation of the honor being shown him, commented favorably as Otto played several selections, but in so doing revealed his utter lack of musical training.

Dora had been slightly troubled as to whether Mrs. 

Van Alstyne would appreciate Joe on brief acquaintance. She never doubted that her aunt would value him highly if given opportunity to determine for herself his many interests and virtues; but appreciation of music, which was foremost in her aunt's life, was exactly the domain where Joe appeared to poorest advantage. Dora thought it wise, therefore, to take him from the drawing-room.

Acordingly when Otto with scarcely any movement of body or arms, but with perfection of finger play, concluded with brilliant phrasing one of Chopin's sonatas, she approached Joe's chair, and notwithstanding Mrs. Van Alstyne's desire to have the program proceed undisturbed, she excused herself and Joe, on the plea that Joe had been at the lake an entire day and she had had no opportunity to learn what had been happening in Hemlock.

DORA'S FRIENDS 

173 The moon had risen and had dappled and flecked the ground with the shadows of leaves. A tiny breeze, not strong enough to bring noise from the foliage, moved the shadows at intervals. Away from the porch and the music they walked to a seat underneath a large maple not far from the water. Across the lake was flung a path of gold. "Now tell me," Dora said, "all about Hemlock."

Joe told her all that came to his mind, and answered all her questions concerning the people, the church, the school, and the country round about. "But all that," he said as he finished, "must seem to you like a story, for you are now living a new life with new friends." "There's no such thing as a new life," answered 

Dora. "When we make new friends, we carry the old life into the new scenes." 99 "Then you haven't forgotten how we drove along the swamp road, and how we met John Murphy, and how happy we were?" "I haven't forgotten, and long before that I remember the first day you came to see me. Dandelions were blossoming everywhere." "I never see dandelions without thinking of that day."

A little convulsive movement of Dora's was revealed as a leaf moved in the moonlight. Joe drew her closer, and she whispered, "No one else knows how I grew up and understands." "That's why I love you." "But if I should change?" "You won't change. You said it's the old life that goes into the new places.' 99 "Sometimes I'm sure; and again, I don't know. 

When I have my best days, I resolve never to let any-

thing interfere with my plans, but before long I feel as if I were struggling to make myself into something I never can be, and I long to go back and be my own self again." "You are now. This very minute you are the old 

Dora I have always loved." "Then hold me; comfort me," she murmured. But added, "Think for me." "It's moonlight," said Joe, "we are together. Let's forget everything else."

The moon had set, the lodge was dark, when they returned to the veranda. At the foot of the stairs tender sentiments were again spoken, and a good-night kiss ameliorated their parting.

XXIII 

MRS. VAN ALSTYNE'S MOTHERLY ADVICE 

After breakfast the next morning, Arthur and Joe discussed telephone line construction. Arthur, who had taken a course in telephone engineering at Tech, was surprised to find how far Joe's study of textbooks had acquainted him with the basic principles. "When a prof figured all that for us on the blackboard, we thought it tough sledding," commented Arthur. "I was helped by an engineer in Clifton when I got stuck," admitted Joe. "And I've had plenty of opportunity to become acquainted with ordinary construction, but I have never seen one of the latest metallic circuits. Before I leave, I want to go over the mountain to examine that new Bell line you told me of." "Why don't you boys look over that telephone line this morning?" suggested Mrs. Van Alstyne, who had entered and overheard Joe's remark. "There's nothing especial planned for today, and I have matters that can occupy the girls." 

An hour later Mrs. Van Alstyne handed Arthur and 

Joe their luncheon, and saw them start on the trail that leads to the ruined farmhouse; she had induced the other members of the party, excepting Dora, to busy themselves with their music; Dora, she led, carrying 175

a basket of sewing, to the little seat where Joe had talked to Dora the night before.

It was not long before their conversation turned to Joe. Mrs. Van Alstyne regretted that he had been brought up in Hemlock where opportunities were so limited. Dora said that Joe was not the kind that could be known well on short acquaintance and that his country manners obscured his worth. "I understand people of his type very well," replied 

Mrs. Van Alstyne. "I knew his father and mother before him. I lived in Hemlock when I was more mature than you and I came to understand the country people perfectly." "But Joseph Clay is different from the rest; he conceals his best qualities." "You grew up with him, Dora, and no doubt know his good qualities better than the rest of us. But are you sure," asked Mrs. Van Alstyne hesitating a little, "that you do not know him too well?" "I have known him since I was six years old, and have never seen anything in him that was dishonorable." "If you have known him so long and your acquaintance has developed gradually, are you sure that your relations with him have not become more intimate than you realize? Isn't it possible that you have failed to assume the responsibility that ought to be yours, especially if he is a person of sincere feeling as you say he is?" 

Dora did not answer. "Dora," said Mrs. Van

Alstyne tenderly, and she put her arm around the girl, "Dora, you surely do not intend to marry this farmer boy, do you? If you do not, think what you are doing. 

If he is your childhood friend, you cannot trifle with

MRS. VAN ALSTYNE'S MOTHERLY ADVICE 177 

him. Are you not leading him toward a catastrophe? 

Unless you intend to marry Joseph Clay, it is a cruel and heartless flirtation to speak and act as you did last night." As Dora was unable to reply, Mrs. Van

Alstyne continued, "Last night I worried about you, and I came down stairs as you entered. Think of your responsibility, Dora, and your future. I am your aunt, but I speak for your mother." "I expect to marry him some day," replied Dora sobbing. "You are deceiving yourself. The courses you are taking at Houghton and the plans you and I have made show your real aim. You are not seeking the training that would fit you to be his wife. You said you intend to marry him, but your thought and work for a year show that your real choice is something else." Mrs.

Van Alstyne paused for a moment and then continued with the utmost tenderness of voice and manner, "Let me help you, Dora, if I can. Tell me, are you not at times in doubt whether you love this man?"

Dora who in her indecision had felt desperately the need of sympathy and the advice of some one older and wiser than herself, yielded readily to her aunt's motherly advances and little by little told the story of her early friendship with Joe, of her ambitious plans for an education, of Joe's declaration of love, of her own uncertainty, of his generous offer to postpone a decision indefinitely, of her varying moods, both of the times like last evening when she was ready to sacrifice everything for love, and also of the hours of despondency that always followed. Dora said that she had felt continually that she had been unjust to Joe, but she had been unable to make a decision. Although at times she had believed that the noblest thing she could do was to

devote herself to study and try to develop her talents to the highest degree, at other times it seemed that by planning a long course of study and training she was sacrificing to selfish ambition the most sacred sentiments. If her aunt would only advise her to do so, if her aunt would encourage her by a word, she would end the struggle and prove the sincerity of her love for 

Joseph Clay by marrying him at once.

If "We can't win victories," said Mrs. Van Alstyne as she gazed at Dora with a sad look in her eyes, "by ceasing to struggle. Marriage is too sacred to be used as a means of escape. It ought to crown success. now you give up the struggle because the right is not all on one side, and the course of action difficult to choose, you will be like ten thousand others who were content with an easy way. If your love for Joseph Clay is such that you dare not trust it until the day of your graduation, how dare you base a marriage on it?"

No thought of resentment on account of her aunt's words was aroused in Dora's mind. On the other hand she was overwhelmed with a sense of guilt for having unduly encouraged Joe's love while she was not sure of her own feeling. "Do you advise me to tell him I think we ought not to be married until I am through college?" she asked. "To say even that would still be unjust and unkind. 

If you are not certain of your love now, you cannot tell whether it will increase or become less. The possibility that you may not care for him when you are through college, should induce you to free him from all bonds, as he offers to free you. This freedom you cannot grant him if you tie him to you by any thread of hope. It is your duty to terminate absolutely your present relations with Joseph Clay.”

MRS. VAN ALSTYNE'S MOTHERLY ADVICE 179 

"How can I ever tell him of my decision," moaned 

Dora. "How can I ever hurt him so!" "Joseph Clay, I fancy, is like his father. He may be untutored, but he is not the kind that would discharge the surgeon for fear of his knife," said Mrs. Van 

Alstyne. "You should speak definitely, so that he may feel free to form other attachments; but you need not speak harshly. If you neither see him nor correspond with him until you are graduated, and you find that neither of you has cared to make any alliance elsewhere, it will not be difficult then to determine a course satisfactory both to yourself and to him." "I shall attempt to do as you say," said Dora, putting her arms around her aunt. "I have been miserable all this year although I tried to think I was happy." 

For a few minutes Dora and Mrs. Van Alstyne. worked at their sewing, but as Dora was unable to apply herself she rose, "I believe I'll get Fanny to climb 

West Hill with me." "Better rest before you attempt anything so hard," advised Mrs. Van Alstyne solicitously. "Your nerves and feelings have been put to a severe strain. Better rest a while in your room. I'll send Elizabeth to read to you."

In the middle of the afternoon Joe and Arthur returned from their expedition to the telephone line. "Where is Dora? Where is Dora?" shouted Joe. "I want to ask her a question." 

Joe went in search of Dora, and Arthur talked with 

Fanny. "I don't see how that fellow can yell like that after what we've been through. He's strong as an ox." "He looks as if he didn't know what it is to get tired," said Fanny.

THE COAT of Many Colors 

'He led me an awful chase, but I'd go out with him again tomorrow. He's almost made me decide to make telephone engineering my profession. He sees a great future for telephony, and I think he's right. I get so confounded tired of this eternal twaddle about music and art. It's all 'my opinion' and 'your opinion' and the only standard is the opinion of the latest 'authority.' 

I like to get away from their emotions and jealousies and talk with Clay about something that can be measured with a yardstick and caliper." "Somebody else ought to have your gift for music. 

You are no better than a Philistine," said Fanny laughing. "I won't dispute it," said Arthur. "All I know about music I'd trade this minute for what Clay knows about telephones."

Joe returned from his search for Dora. Mrs. Van 

Alstyne had said that Dora was not feeling well and would not be down to dinner. In the evening, nevertheless, Dora accompanied by Elizabeth, appeared on the veranda where the others were visiting. Joe looked for a seat near Dora, but Elizabeth clung closely to her. 

He began a conversation by telling of the changes that would be made by draining the swamp. He described the probable appearance of the broad stretches of farm land covered with luxuriant crops. "I can hardly imagine how everything will look with the woods cut off," said Dora. "And all those lovely swamp flowers that Dora has told us about, the pink eupatorium, the boneset, and the balm, they all must go," sighed Elizabeth. "It seems as if some one always had a good reason for spoiling the loveliest places," remarked Henrietta.

MRS. VAN ALSTYNE'S MOTHERLY ADVICE 181 

"By making all that land productive," answered Joe courteously, "many folks will be able to make their homes more attractive. They will be relieved from hardship, and they will be able to secure more luxury, and will find time to put their minds on something else than just struggling to make both ends meet." "People who have had to struggle to make both ends meet have produced some of the finest things in the world," argued Henrietta. "That's true," admitted Joe, "but the most unfortunate and poorest people have lived in lands where art and music were most advanced. I think the finest sight in the world is a farming community that raises good enough crops so that the fields are clean, the fences in repair, the buildings kept up and painted, and none of the people poor, but all well-fed and happy. In such circumstances there isn't any temptation to steal or cheat, but everybody lives good lives, and has leisure to walk out and enjoy the flowers and birds, or if you please to listen to music or to look at pictures." "They couldn't tell good pictures from bad if they weren't educated," observed Elizabeth. "Why couldn't they be just as happy with those they thought were good?" asked Fanny. "If it's necessary to have an education in art," said Joe, looking surprised because of the feeling he seemed to be stirring, "they can get it if they have satisfactory incomes." 99 "They shouldn't destroy the beauties of nature in increasing their incomes, said Henrietta. "People ought to be taught to get their living in some way that will make the world more attractive."

"That's a good idea, Henrietta," rejoined Arthur "I have to study economics with a touch of sarcasm. next fall and I'd like to hear about your plan.” "That couldn't always be done in a small town, Henrietta," said Mrs. Van Alstyne admonishingly. "People in remote communities have to eke out a living as best they can. We are all proud of Dora's success with the organ, but what would it amount to in a small town? Not only would she fail in most places to find a suitable instrument, but there would be few if any people who would know how to value her ability. 

There's a good organ in Hemlock, but I don't suppose that you would ever care to return to your old home, would you, Dora?" "There have been so many changes I don't believe I shall ever care to go back," replied Dora. 

Walter Rexford broke the silence. "Tomorrow's the last day I can go to Upper Jay to call on Mrs. Cheney," he remarked to Dora. "You can have the buckboard tomorrow just as well as not," volunteered Mrs. Van Alstyne with sprightly manner. "Mrs. Cheney," Dora explained to Joe, in an attempt to be nonchalant, "is the mother of Elsie Cheney, one of the Mu Phi girls who is studying art abroad. 

Walter Rexford used to live next door to the Cheneys in Hartford. Before Elsie went away I told her I would try to call on her mother with Walter." "How many are going?" asked Arthur in tantalizing tones. "Three rode to Cascade Lakes. There's plenty of room for Clay or me.' 

Joe protested that he would remain at the lodge very contentedly.

MRS. VAN ALSTYNE'S MOTHERLY ADVICE 183 

"Clay came on his bicycle," growled Walter, staring at Joe's knickerbockers. "I don't believe he wants to go calling."

Mrs. Van Alstyne turned with her most severe air. "If Mr. Clay for any reason does not care to go to

Upper Jay, we shall enjoy visiting with him here, but 

Mr. Clay will make his own choice, Walter." "Walter is so particular that I don't believe I could please him if I should go," rejoined Dora spitefully. "At any rate, I am not going."

Thereupon Dora excused herself and went to her room. Several of the others walked to the beach, leaving only Walter, Henrietta, and Elizabeth on the veranda. “I think it was very, very rude of you, Walter, to call attention to Mr. Clay's clothing as you did a few minutes ago," remarked Elizabeth. 

Walter laughed. "If I appeared here in a costume like that I'd think I got off easy to escape with anything less than sudden death." "Well, it ought to require no more than good breeding to keep you from calling attention to a guest's deficiencies," said Elizabeth. Mrs. Van Alstyne's pet abomination is country manners, but you should have observed that she invariably shows Mr. Clay the greatest courtesy." "I've noticed that," answered Walter eagerly. "I could scarcely believe my ears when I heard her speaking so sweetly to him as he entered her dining-room the other night in those trousers. I have known her to grow fidgety when a fellow has merely worn socks a shade too strong for his tie." "I don't want Walter to think I am defending him for making Mr. Clay appear ridiculous, but," said 

Henrietta, "I can't understand what Dora has found in

Mr. Clay to admire or why Mrs. Van Alstyne should wish us to meet him here." "Perhaps Mrs. Van Alstyne is wiser than Walter is," suggested Elizabeth. "She probably has concluded that direct opposition of Dora's interest in Mr. Clay would only serve to increase it, and that the surest way to rid her of any undue affection is to set her rustic hero among a group of well-bred people where she will be forced to make her own comparisons." "Clay's all right in his place," nodded Walter generously. "I've no doubt he will occupy a very respectable position in his community." "Living where he does, I guess it is just as well that he doesn't appreciate music," observed Henrietta with a sigh. "Probably he has his own sort of enjoyment, and people in his circumstances get along better without too much feeling."

XXIV 

LOVE IS DIFFERENT FROM ALL OTHER THINGS 

When plans for the day were being discussed Arthur demanded that Dora reserve time for reading a novel that they had begun together at the beginning of the house party. "I am sure she will be glad to do that," remarked

Mrs. Van Alstyne, turning toward Dora with an affectionate smile. "Indeed I shall," answered Dora cordially. "We left it last time in the most interesting place.' 99 Fanny, Arthur, Dora, and Joe walked to a seat in the grove. Fanny began her lace-making and Joe lay on the grass near by. Arthur and Dora remained standing, and for a time joined in the conversation, but finally disappeared in the direction of the bridge that crosses the brook, taking their novel with them.

Fanny laughed. "It looks as if you and I would have to entertain each other."

That did not prove difficult, for Fanny was famous for her versatility. She was not only a sufficiently accomplished musician to be welcomed in Mrs. Van

Alstyne's circle, but she also managed efficiently the finances of the Mu Phi house, and she could hold her own in all day rambles with nature enthusiasts. Without apologies or condescension, she was able to discuss 185

Joe's favorite topics from novel points of view, and Joe and she each found the discussion entertaining. 

Joe went for the broken paddle, and Fanny put down her lacework to help him. She proved an efficient assistant. She told of a trip in Maine that she had taken with her father and an Indian guide. They had shot swift rapids, and slept one night in the open, and the next day had caught many fish from a little lake hidden among the hills. When the paddle was mended, Fanny resumed her lace-making and Joe lay on the ground near by. Their talk flowed easily, although one or the other glanced now and then across the brook where 

Dora and Arthur had disappeared. "They say you came here to see Dora Reed," remarked Fanny, "but you don't seem to pay much attention to her."

Fanny smiled. "I suppose the right answer isanything but the truth." "The truth is I don't want to take all her time when she has so many friends to entertain. Something takes her attention every minute." "That's the way it seems to me. "That's not your fault. The things you say don't rub me the wrong way." "In that case I'll talk more to you."

Meanwhile Dora and Arthur had crossed the bridge over the little brook that enters the lake at the left of the lodge, and near the shore under some large trees had found a comfortable spot in which to continue reading their book. Dora read, and Arthur in truly romantic fashion reclined at the foot of a gray beech a few feet away. The tale was drawing near its climax.

The hero had declared his love, and the heroine was about to renounce her opportunity to secure the rich

uncle's wealth, and was about to decide to marry her poor lover.

love." He took from his pocket and handed to Dora a paper that had been worn and crumpled, but was neatly folded now.

Sir Kenneth's Watch on St. George's Mount 

Dora Reed 

I think Sir Kenneth made a mistake when he left his post of duty to go to Edith. He did not believe that the life of the princess was in danger; he seemed to regard the summons as a test of his obedience. The words, "She who dropped roses, can bestow laurels," rather promised a reward than revealed the need of his aid. His leaving the post of duty proved nothing except that his love was servile and that he had formed a low estimate of Edith's ideals. Had his conception of 

Edith's virtues been nobler, he could not have been trapped.

He should never have doubted that the woman he loved, far from asking him to do an ignoble act, would have preferred to suffer rather than have him prove unfaithful. He should have believed that the only affection that Edith could prize must be founded, not on blind obedience, but on the best motives and the most lofty principles of honor. If Sir Kenneth had acted in accordance with such an ideal, he would have shown the nobility of his own love and he would have ennobled 

Edith's character.

After the story came Miss Stevens's note: "I have read with interest what you have written, and shall look forward to your comments on other situations further on in the book. I approve of the view you have taken, but wonder if you are not a little harsh with Sir Kenneth. Was he certain that Edith was not in distress? Be alert, as we read on, to discover whether his conception of Edith's character was either too high or too low. "Ask me for a book that contains a poem written by a knight of later days, who states your view in these words: 'I could not love thee, dear so much, Loved I not honor more.' "Observe the changes I have made, especially your errors in spelling. You need not copy."

XXV 

One afternoon in the September following the house party the repair wagon of the Hemlock Telephone Company stood at the door of the storage shed at the rear of the exchange building and Joe and Henry Brush were busily engaged in loading the wire, cable, and poles required for the afternoon's work. The task was nearly completed and they were about to start for a point several miles southeast when Dr. Dawson emerged from the telephone building and with very evident excitement addressed Joe. "Don't go out on the line this afternoon. Send Brush to get Horton to help him and come right into the office. There's a 

W. and E. man upstairs who has been talking with me for half an hour. I got Richards to send him here. 

He wants to buy our company. I've telephoned your father and he's already on his way."

Joe looked in the direction of the supplies in the wagon and responded somewhat petulantly. "For a month I've been wanting to do that job up at 82.

There are several places near Flanagan's where the line needs attention." "You'd save the company money if you weren't so particular. Flanagan's line is working perfectly, and probably will for months to come. It's time enough to make repairs when you know it's necessary." 195

"That's not my doctrine," answered Joe. "When we take a telephone franchise we agree to furnish our subscribers continuous service day and night. That's our obligation. A telephone is more than just poles and wire." "Yes, it is more than poles and wire, and engineering, and switchboard operation; it's investment, and it's diplomacy, and even politics. Today there isn't one of us who would know what to ask if we wanted to sell, and it's only fair that the stockholders should learn what their investment's worth. I'm going to draw these W. and E. fellows out, and make them set a value on our stock. That man in the office has already told me things that it's dollars in our pocket to know." "Well, replied Joe, reluctantly, "I think you're following the wrong lead. I don't want to sell, but I'll tell 

Henry to work in the shed, and I'll go to Flanagan's tomorrow."

A few minutes later Joe and his father entered the inner office of the telephone company and were presented to a heavily-built, red-faced man whom the doctor called Mr. J. Henry Rogers. Mr. Rogers announced that he was District Manager of the Western and Eastern Company for Northern New York. He said he had been called to Clifton on business and had taken occasion to inquire into the telephone situation in Adams County. He had become much interested in the progressive maner in which some of the local companies, particularly those at Clifton and Hemlock, were conducting their affairs, and as a consequence of the favorable opinion he had formed, he had a proposition to lay before them.

He would proceed to the point. Since it was never the policy of the Western and Eastern to duplicate local

service where it was up to date and satisfactory, he thought that it would be for the mutual benefit of his company and the country people if some alliance could be arranged between the Western and Eastern and the companies operating in Adams County. It was evident that such an alliance when effected ought to make use of the best and most liberal charter possessed by any of the coalescing organizations. The Clifton and the 

Hemlock companies, because they were small and inconspicuous, had been able to obtain, he said, charters conferring almost unprecedented powers of operation throughout Van Buren and Adams Counties.

He had communicated with the president of his company, he said, and was happy to announce that the Western and Eastern was ready to assume the financial responsibility for the enterprise. He had already approached Mr. Wade, the largest stockholder in the 

Clifton Company, and had found that the Clifton charter could be obtained on satisfactory terms, but he preferred to secure the charter of the Hemlock company, for their Clifton-Boughton wire would make a link in a new line that the W. and E. were planning to build toward Utica. "Well," said the doctor, as Mr. J. Henry Rogers paused a moment to light a fresh cigar, "we'd like to hear your offer. If you can make it worth while, we can call a directors meeting now and take action at once.

We three men and the two girls at the switchboard,” he added with a wise smile, "constitute the board of directors and hold over sixty per cent of the stock." "Fine!" exclaimed Rogers, 'it's a pleasure to do business with men who come at once to the point. The 

Western and Eastern will issue to all stockholders of the Hemlock company one share of W. and E. stock

for every share they own in the Hemlock company; and if any of your gentlemen," said he, dropping his voice to a confidential tone and looking at the doctor, "feel you ought to have special consideration because of your help in carrying through the transaction or because of your strategic position as officers, we might add a small additional payment which we could charge up to agents' or attorneys' fees."

In the silence that ensued, George Clay followed the gaze of Rogers and looked keenly at Doctor Dawson; but as the latter seemed busy with some mental calculation, Clay spoke for himself. "We aren't interested in personal bonuses and secret understandin's"; he retorted energetically. "It's our business to look out for our subscribers and stockholders. What we would want first, before we would consider lettin' our lines go out of our control, is a guarantee of good service." "We'll guarantee you good service; give you a bonus of five thousand dollars that you can pass on to your stockholders or divide among the directors just as you wish; and exchange stock with you share for share," said Rogers. "An alliance that would put more territory at the call of our subscribers," remarked Joe, "would undoubtedly be beneficial, if it didn't cause the Clifton and Hemlock companies to lose control of the local lines."

Mr. Rogers turned with a conciliating smile toward 

Joe, "Have no fear of that, my young friend. We wish to obtain the charter of the Hemlock company for the express purpose of permitting that corporation with its splendid powers to control the affairs of all the local companies, including even the W. and E. lines in Van 

Buren and Adams counties."

199 "I don't doubt that you'd let the Hemlock corporation control the lines," replied Joe, "but as soon as you got our stock you'd appoint as directors men who would look out for W. and E. interests instead of ours." "Remember that it was my plan," answered Mr.

Rogers with a patient smile, "that for every share of your stock you were to receive one of W. and E., and you would have the same right as other stockholders to vote for men who would carry out whatever policies you favor." 

George Clay laughed, "I guess our hundred shares of stock wouldn't stand much chance of dictatin' W. and E. policy." "You could scarcely expect," rejoined Mr. Rogers with paternal condescension, "that a minority should shape the affairs of a larger number. They can't do that, no matter whether they affiliate or remain sepa- That is the very reason why a small company needs to ally itself with an organization large enough to protect it." rate. "Whom do we need to be protected from? Where are you expecting trouble?" inquired Dr. Dawson, hoping to elicit information concerning the policies of rivals. "Who? Why the Bell of course. You country people don't appreciate," said Mr. Rogers with an injured look, "that the W. and E. was organized as an independent company to fight the Bell telephone trust and has been fighting it for years. If it hadn't been for the W. and E., all small companies would long ago have been forced to discontinue business." "I had no idea the Bell was so bad," said George Clay with an air of innocent surprise. "What's it been doin'?"

"Doing? Why, it's choking the whole industry to death. It won't tolerate competition. It takes fifty-one per cent of the stock of every company that operates under its patents. It has bought up the courts and it controls legislation. It owns the engineering magazines; and it stifles all progressive inventions. It won't let the trade use any telephone material that its own companies do not produce—' 99 "That's because they own the basic patents," interrupted Dr. Dawson in authoritative tones. "Bell invented the telephone and nobody else has been able to make one that will work. I don't know as I blame them for protecting their rights." "Nonsense," ejaculated Mr. Rogers. "The W. and

E. have a transmitter far superior to the Bell's and it conflicts in no way with the Bell's patents. The reason it is not in use now is that it is cheaper for us to wait a year or so until the Bell patents expire than to fight the matter in the courts. The money it would take to win a legal battle we are using to extend our lines. 

Every concern in the country will ask for our transmitter when the Bell patents expire. Stock won't be worth ten cents then in any company not affiliated with us. In the West where the news about our transmitter has leaked out and they understand the situation, all the local companies are begging for our stock in exchange for their lines. 

Dr. Dawson was visibly impressed by Mr. Rogers's remarks; but as he had no data for testing the truth of the statements, he decided to drive the discussion into a different quarter until he had had an opportunity to question Richards. "What decision the courts will give about the validity of Bell patents," Dawson responded gruffly, "neither

you nor I know; but a matter that ought to be of interest to you this morning, is where the W. and E. is going to get a wire through the mountains, and a charter liberal enough to let it buy and sell telephone lines. 

If the W. and E. ever intends to fight the Bell it had better seize the opportunity today to make Hemlock a pretty offer. We directors understand what

Hemlock's charter is worth, and we know that we don't have to sell to the first fellow who comes along." "You might be very fortunate to sell to the first fellow who comes along," retorted Mr. Rogers making a pretense of trembling with anger, but hopeful because of Dawson's manner that he had already won his point. "Your whole outfit may not be worth a cent after the 

Bell patents expire. It's going to be a free for all fight in which no small company can survive. The question to be decided by you and me today is whether the Hemlock Telephone Company is going to fall into the clutches of the Bell, or will unite with an independent concern to fight the trust."

Although Joe felt that Rogers was a liar and a humbug, he tried to answer deliberately and calmly. “I have always regarded competition," he began, “as of doubtful value in the telephone business. Any town that has two telephone systems will have half service or double cost. You can't get around it. Sooner or later all lines ought to be merged into one system. Any man who has a telephone ought to be able to call any other man who has a telephone. The more systems, the worse it is.

The nature of the business makes it a natural monopoly-" "Those arguments," retorted Mr. Rogers, waving his hand scornfully, “are manufactured by the Bell Pub-

licity Bureau. No monopoly is natural. Nature gives us two ears. Why not two telephones?"

Joe became angry, "All right then; if we have to choose between you and the Bell we'll consider the interests of our patrons; we'll base our choice first of all on the character of the service rendered. In this section the W. and E. gives the worst, and the Bell gives the best. You can figure it for yourself what my choice will be." "You see, Mr. Rogers," said Dr. Dawson gesticulating dramatically in the direction of Joe, "it is useless to speak with the directors about buying our property unless you are prepared to make a very attractive offer."

Rogers gave no heed to Dawson but glared like a wild beast at Joe, "Young man," he said, "your lines connect with the business world at Clifton; and the Bell is still eighteen miles distant at Hawley, and will never reach Clifton. You have undertaken a very big proposition when you choose to fight the whole Western and Eastern system. I bid you good day."

Rogers walked heavily across the outer office, passed down the stairs, stepped into a carriage which a driver was holding for him at the door, and drove rapidly toward the west.

He had scarcely left when Dr. Dawson began to laugh. "He came to protect us against the Bell, ha, ha!

Wasn't it great the way Joe rubbed it into him about W. and E. service? He took us for a bunch of suckers, ha, ha! But I guess he's got a different opinion now. 

He seized Mr. Clay by the shoulder. "George," he said, "don't you think it mighty probable he wants to protect us against the Bell?"

When the quarrel was over, Mildred took the switchboard; and Joe sat near Sophrona at the office desk. If I wait a minute too long they scold me because they want to call another number. Listening-in slows up everything, and they accuse me of eavesdropping. And now you come and scold me because I ask them if they're through" There Sophrona paused; she looked toward Joe and her anger slowly faded. "I am sorry," at last she said apologetically. "If you'll tell me how to find out when they are done talking, I'll try to do just as you say." "I've found," suggested Joe, "that if we ask 'Did you get them?' they think we are trying to help them and they answer, 'Yes, we're talking'; but if we say, 'Are you through?' they take it that we want to give somebody else the line, and they get mad at once.' 

"I'll try to remember that,” replied Sophrona meekly. "I used to think little things like that didn't make any

JOE FEELS Like Work 

205 difference and that people got cranky as soon as they began talking through a telephone; but-I know now 

I was wrong."

Joe was pleased with her reply. Sophrona had spirit enough to get angry now and then, he thought, but underneath her outbursts and occasional frivolity was a warm heart and a desire to do the best she could for the subscribers and the company. "I remember," continued Sophrona, 'I used to think you were fussy when you told us to use short rings because long rings irritate; and now I wonder why I was so stupid as not to discover that myself; and I told 

Mildred you wanted me to be a mother to all of Adams county because you asked me to ring up any number that hadn't called up for some time, for fear a house might be burned or somebody dead. Now people are awful sweet to me when I inquire if everything's all right; and I get the credit—but it really belongs to you. 

Joe was embarrassed. Conversation of this character with Sophrona was unusual. "Since you talk with the subscribers more than any one else," he replied, "I guess you're entitled to all the credit for good-will.

This morning I noticed that as soon as you came into the office you called the end numbers to see that each wire was operating well." "I know how hard you work to keep the line in order," responded Sophrona, as she laid the ledger on the desk, "and I want to please you."

Two pens scratched busily for a few minutes, and then Joe laid his aside. His mind went back two years to the masked ball when he had failed to recognize Sophrona in her disguise and had thought her something new, something strange, something from far

away. She had then appealed to his sense of romance. 

Why had he been so influenced by the far away and strange? That wasn't in accordance with his creed.

He professed to love Hemlock, and to be content to spend his life in trying to make it as good or better than places that were far away. Sophrona was the personification of Hemlock, of the best that was in Hemlock. 

She cherished no illusions. She believed in winning by work in a world circumscribed by her personal contacts. Her ideal was a well-kept, cheerful house, warmed with cordiality and comfort. George Clay had been her best teacher. On the village street she greeted young and old with equal favor. Although a girl, long-bearded farmers joked or talked seriously with her about the time to sow buckwheat; and children everywhere ran to her. Over the wires for miles around, her voice was welcomed. If sometimes she flared into momentary anger, it was only Sophrona's way, and was soon forgotten. Her cheerful sympathetic voice in many a lonely farmhouse had transformed isolation into snug security That voice he had been taking as a matter of course, and also that face with rounded features, eyes never furtive, always warm with a glow, golden green like a brown thrasher's as he had said to tease her, that face that in his own home he had seen deepen and ripen through the years. 

The sun was low when Sophrona roused him from his reverie. "It's time to go home," she announced with a sigh. "I'll get the horse." "Let me get it!" responded Joe jumping up gallantly. "Let's get it together," replied Sophrona, attempting to be playful.

Together they walked to the shed at the rear of the office. Sophrona led out the horse, and Joe threw the

harness over its back and held up the thills. He had the bridle adjusted and the trace on his side fastened while Sophrona fumbled awkwardly, buckling the bellyband to the thill-strap. "Anybody'd think you'd never hitched up a horse," laughed Joe as he walked to Sophrona's side and buckled the straps correctly. "What under the sun are you thinking about?" "I was thinking," began Sophrona as she gathered up the reins and drove to the street, "I was thinking how glad father and mother and I ought to be that we've lived in your home so many years.' "No gladder than father and I have been to have you," answered Joe warmly. "Don't call it my home.

Better say it's your home that father and I have lived in. There's scarcely a good fence on the place or a well-trimmed tree in the orchard that's not the result of your father's care. Besides if we gave your mother the whole farm it wouldn't pay her for what she did long ago for father and me. Father knows that as well as I do; and he cares more for you, I guess, than he does for me. You never talked like this before. 

What's come up to make you think such thoughts?" "I am eighteen years old,” replied Sophrona, inclined to weep, "but because you've grown up with me and know all my faults, you think I'm rattle-brained." "Because I've grown up with you I know dozens of your good traits. Your faults we won't count. Perhaps they are what makes you so human." "You always think of me as if I were a little girl and don't talk to me. I know I'd be a great deal happier if we talked more together," sobbed Sophrona. "It's not too late to begin now," responded Joe tenderly, and he put his arm about her and tried to stop

the flow of her tears. "All my life I've relied on your support and loyalty; but only lately have I appreciated how much I owe to you and how much I care for you."

Sophrona responded warmly to his words. As they drove home their reserve vanished. They promised, as they put the horse into the stable, that hereafter their mutual affection would not be taken as a matter of course. 

With solemn faces that startled Amanda as she turned to greet them, they entered the kitchen door.

XXVI 

With increasing zeal Sophrona worked throughout the fall of 1892 to promote the interests of the Hemlock 

Telephone ompany. The patrons of the line continually commended her courtesy, and Dr. Dawson even hinted at an increase in salary. Joe was so appreciative of 

Sophrona's growing interest in business that he ventured to ask her advice concerning some of the larger matters of telephone administration. Sophrona no longer had reason to complain that Joe treated her as if she were a little girl, or failed to take her into his confidence.

During the early part of the winter Joe continued to urge on Dr. Dawson the desirability of raising the standard of the company's service. Like insurance, Joe urged, the value of a telephone is in proportion to its dependability. The patrons of the line should be confident that at any emergency, day or night, they would be able to communicate with friends or summon aid.

Dr. Dawson admitted it was a good theory to follow as far as practicable. He was expecting to invite 

Richards to spend a week end; he didn't want to wait any longer to find out about that new transmitter. When 

Richards came he would also inquire as to how much per mile the W. and E. was giving to maintenance. 

When he got those figures, he would consider repairs. 209

An unfortunate incident, however, occurred before 

Richards came.

Belle Jones, it seems, had been at a party in Clifton and accompanied home by a young man had returned late at night. On the floor of the living room she found her father unconscious with the telephone receiver in his hand, and her mother unconscious in bed in an adjoining room. The coal stove had filled the house with gas; and her father had attempted to call for help; but had found the telephone out of order. Within a few days Belle's mother died from pneumonia.

The doctor said that Mrs. Jones's life might have been saved had her husband been able to summon aid. 

Although it was true that the line had failed because of the improper installation of poles that Mr. Jones himself had set across his own property, Dr. Dawson was ready at last to admit the justice of Joe's contention that those who own telephone lines must consider their investment more than a financial undertaking and must accept on entering the business a moral obligation to maintain efficient and reliable service.

The doctor had dreamed of the time when the Hemlock Telephone Company would grow into a great organization with lines radiating throughout the northern part of the state. He saw his own name enrolled among the captains of industry, for he credited himself with unusual shrewdness and insight; and he had unbounded confidence in his ability as administrator. As a first step toward such a financial career, he had insisted on thrift and economy; but he had never deliberately sought to sacrifice the welfare of the community to a desire for profit. The altruistic traits of character that had made him a successful and conscientious country physician had kept him from that. Accordingly

after Mrs. Jones's death it required comparatively little persuasion from Joe to induce him to consent to a general overhauling of the system as soon as the weather would permit. 

On the Saturday afternoon that Tyler Richards was invited to Dr. Dawson's, the doctor brought him to the exchange and left him with Mildred and Sophrona while he went to make a professional call. Richards was very agreeable; but Joe kept a sour face from the time that Tyler entered. Tyler suggested that he and 

Joe take the girls that evening to a dance at the hotel in Clifton; but Joe did not seem to respond to Tyler's friendly advances. In spite of the fact that Sophrona seized his arm and pranced playfully about him and teased him to go, he turned the proposition down; and the trip was abandoned.

Whether or not the result of his interview with 

Richards, Dr. Dawson soon agreed that the South

Line, which connected with a number of local companies in the mountains and was known to be especially, weak, should receive first attention when spring opened. Joe said he feared there would be trouble on this route even before the winter was over. Such proved to be the case. 

One morning late in February Sophrona obtained no reply from Ed Flanagan who lived three miles above the Clifton line crossing. Thompson, his nearest neighbor, did not know where the break was, and he had not seen the Flanagans for several days. It was six miles from Hemlock to the point of difficulty, and the county was so rough and sparsely settled that it was not an attractive field of labor in winter. Although only a temporary repair could be made while the ground was frozen, Joe thought it wise to secure enough help

to meet any emergency and make sure that the line could be put into working order in a single day. While 

Brush went to the Van Alstyne cottage for Strong, he induced Silas Horton to consent somewhat reluctantly to assist in the task, although Silas complained that his rheumatism predicted a change in the weather.
