The long low repair sleigh with four roughly dressed men and a curious assemblage of reels of wire and cable, tools, cross-arms, brackets, and insulators, made its way with difficulty over the uneven road. The snow of the previous months had drifted badly. A thaw had come and had broken the surface into a succession of hillocks and holes. This indifferent road-bed, moreover, had been covered by an inch or two of light snow that obscured the icy places and rendered travelling difficult and treacherous. Once or twice one of the horses fell, and often the sleigh slid alarmingly toward the brink of the little hills or the roadside bank. 

The road was so bad and they had stopped so often to strengthen poles that seemed in danger of falling that it was one o'clock before they approached the region where they expected to find the broken wire. "I don't see how we're going to do any work this afternoon and get back over this road. We can't drive it after dark," grumbled Strong. "We'll be lucky to get home at all," said Silas who had been anxiously scanning the sky. "See that snowflake! There'll be plenty more to follow; and the wind's coming up." "I should think we ought to be able to splice the wire and get back by evening," said Joe, regarding the sky critically, "but if worst comes to worst we can stay all night at Flanagan's.' 

---

"None of Flanagan's for mine!" exclaimed Silas with alarm. "I didn't want to come anyway. I'm not going to leave Livy all alone tonight.' 99 "What do you suggest doing?" asked Joe. "You wouldn't come as far as this to repair a break in the line and then go back without trying to remedy the trouble, would you?" "I wouldn't be such a fool as to drive into the mountains in a blizzard just because a telephone's busted. I guess if folks have done without a telephone for the most of their lives they can get along without it for a few days," said Silas, nodding his head toward Strong in hope of support. "It's one of Joe's notions that the world can't turn round if his telephone's busted," responded Strong glumly. "Just look at this snow. It's coming faster every minute; and I never saw such fine flakes. When we come to a good place let's turn around," suggested Silas.

Strong looked for further support to Henry who was driving the horses, but Brush remained silent. 

"You can go back to town as soon as you're ready,' replied Joe. "Probably everything's all right at Flanagan's, but we don't know; and as long as there's a break in the line, he couldn't get service no matter what emergency. And on south, there are several villages that have poor connections and will depend on us if their other lines fail. You fellows can go back to town as soon as you like, but I'm going to finish this job." 

Silas and Strong angrily shook the snow from their shoulders and partly threw back the robes preparatory to turning.

"Better keep on a little further before we turn," urged Henry. "We are less than two miles from Flanagan's and most likely will soon come to the break in the line where we can throw off some stuff too heavy for Joe to carry." 

Silas and Strong gazed sullenly at the blinding gale which was now shutting out all view of the landscape; and mumbling something to each other, rode a few rods further to the crest of a little hill where they came upon a dead pine which had fallen across the road and had broken the wire.

Joe secured an axe and a bag of tools into which he put two or three brackets and some insulators. Brush threw out a coil of wire and some cable; and then handed Strong the reins. "Aren't you going with us, Henry?" Strong inquired nervously. "No, I'll stay with Joe." "Better go, Henry," urged Joe, "I shall make only temporary splices and won't need you." 

Henry indicated rather positively that he intended to stay. "He's a young fool," Silas muttered to Strong; "he wouldn't stay out in this storm if he knew what chances he's taking."

Strong quickly turned the team and was about to start. "Wait a minute," called Henry. "Don't go until

Joe has had a chance to call up both ways to be sure there're no more wires down.”

But the men did not hear, or pretended that they did not hear, and the sleigh disappeared in the blinding storm.

Joe found an end of the broken wire and attached his telephone and attempted to call Flanagan's; but there

was no response. He signalled for central on the South 

Line and there was no reply. "There must be another break between here and Flanagan's," he said, “I didn't get any answer."

While Henry joined a wire to one end of the broken line, Joe used his axe to remove enough of the fallen tree to prevent its blocking the road. Then the new wire was drawn taut, and Joe removed his overcoat preparatory to climbing the pole to make the splice. It was desperate work for the wind was blowing like a hurricane and drove the snow up his sleeves and under his clothing until he was chilled through. At last he slipped down the pole and announced that the wires. were joined. Then they discovered that an adjacent pole had been made insecure by the shock of the falling pine and needed to be guyed with cable to a neighboring tree. When that was done they called Hemlock central and Sophrona replied. 

There was a terrible storm at Hemlock. Strong and

Horton had just driven in and had said that he and Henry had to repair another break and would stay all night at Flanagan's. She didn't see how Strong and

Horton could drive away and leave them in the mountains. She had a mind to take the cutter and come for them. "Don't worry," responded Joe cheerfully. "We'll soon be at Flanagan's." "Then call me there," continued Sophrona in determined tones, "for I'm coming if I don't hear from you."

Joe and Brush cached most of their material at the foot of a pole and trudged forward with difficulty along the narrow road. The new snow was nearly to their knees and in the driving storm it was almost impossible

to obtain a sufficiently clear view of the line to make sure it was intact. After proceeding half a mile two fallen poles were discovered on a hill-top curve, and the line seemed to be out of repair for several rods. It was clearly impossible under the present conditions to put the line into working order except by means of a slack wire that might be attached to poles, or trees, or any object that chance offered for its support. Even to do that they must work speedily for it would soon be dark, and no human being then could see to join broken wires in the night that was coming on, even if he was able to endure the exposure. "I'll try to call Flanagan's and order our supper," said Joe jocularly; "for we are near his house and there will be no use in doing this job if there are other breaks beyond."

To their great joy when they had attached their telephone beyond the break Flanagan replied. He promised them food and beds on their arrival and said that the South Line was working perfectly. Cheered by the news Joe and Henry quickly placed a temporary wire in operation. When Joe called Sophrona he had the satisfaction of hearing the South central demanding right of way for a long distance message. For a wonder the W. and E. connection with the Bell was working properly. Late in the evening, when Joe after eating a good supper was on his way to bed, he found the wire still busy with messages from the south. 

One morning when warmer weather came and the snow from the great blizzard had melted, the ground was unusually soft. Sophrona failed to get a reply when she called Boughton. She could not even get a response from Ned White's. She notified Joe accord-

ingly, and he, Horton, and Henry Brush were soon driving east in the company's repair wagon. The horses plodded along slowly over the soft muddy road. 

An hour or more had passed and no sign of the expected trouble appeared. "I guess Fox's deadman must have pulled out," suggested Henry. "Yes, that's what I'm thinking," replied Joe.

They soon found that their supposition was correct. 

When the ground softened, the log that Fox had buried had not been able to hold the strain of the line. Two poles had fallen and the wire lay across the road. "This is a pretty big job for three of us," said Joe, taking in the situation at glance. "I ought to go for

Fox and make him dig, he was so obstinate about having his way and insisting that his deadman would hold."

In the course of time, however, without the help of Fox they installed the new pole that they carried on the wagon, and reset the better of the two old poles that had fallen. From the worse pole they made a longer deadman than that used by Fox and buried it more deeply. The wire was then quickly strung, and before the afternoon was half over Joe was ready to use his field telephone to test the line. "Hello, Sophrona. Is the line now all right to 

Boughton ?" "Yes, they just called. I've been waiting to get you. I'm home. Your father is not feeling well. The doctor is here and says he's getting along all right, but you'd better come home."

Further inquiry informed Joe that there was nothing at present alarming in his father's condition although at first it was considered serious. Joe was, neverthe-

less, very impatient to reach home. It required all his strength of will to keep from beating the horses and causing them to plunge forward for a few exhausting leaps in the heavy mud. A mile from the house he left the vehicle and attempted to cut through the fields. At every step he sank in the sodden earth, but was able to reach home a considerable distance ahead of the weary team. 

Outside the house Charlie Haskins was seated in the doctor's carriage. Sophrona met him at the door. "Don't worry, Joe," she said, and notwithstanding his mud-bespattered garments put her arm about him. "He's feeling better." "Aren't you a pretty sight, Joe!" exclaimed Mr.

Clay, with an attempt at a hearty laugh, as Joe entered the bedroom. "Where have you been wallerin' in the mud? Keep your eyes peeled or Amanda will chase you out of here." 

Joe greeted his father solicitously, scarcely knowing how to interpret his bantering tones and cheerful demeanor. "There's nothing serious the matter now," Dr. Dawson remarked, "but he had a close call. If Sophrona hadn't got me down here in fifteen minutes I shouldn't want to say what might have happened. Sophrona's full of spunk, but she's hard on a horse." "Sophrona's managed everything," said Mr. Clay as the doctor left the room. "There's nothing she hasn't done. She kept her head through it all." He had begun in matter of fact tones but soon the sick man's voice weakened and his eyes grew soft. "She's helped me as 

I couldn't ask my daughter to do." He extended his hand to Sophrona who was standing with Joe close beside his bed. "Joe, we mustn't ever forget what

Sophrona has done for me. There ain't many girls who would have the grit to take care of a sick person, and who could do housework as she does, and look after an office too." "Sophrona has the good-will of everybody along the line," said Joe. "The subscribers all speak well of her.” "It's Sophrona's way," Mr. Clay continued. "As a child she was always warmhearted. Like her father and mother she don't think of herself, but looks out for us. She has made herself one of us, Joe." Mr. Clay extended toward Joe the hand which still held Sophrona's shapely palm within it. For a moment he held both their hands together in his grasp. 

Amanda came to the door, "The doctor wants to speak with you about the medicine, Sophrona.' 99 

Sophrona withdrew her hand and left the room. "Lately Sophrona seems to have made a great change," remarked Joe. "Her old-time carelessness and flippancy are gone; and she has grown more thoughtful and sincere." "I don't think she's changed. You've both reached the age where you rely on each other more'n you did.” "Perhaps the reason she seems different to me is because we have recently had an understanding." "Is that so?" exclaimed Mr. Clay, rising energetically on his elbow and smiling benignly. "I hadn't suspected it. You don't mean you are goin' to be married?" "No, father; we are trying to be more like brother and sister in our sympathy and confidence. I fear," he began, and looked straight into his father's eyes, "that I can never care for Sophrona in any other way. 

I don't know that she would want to marry me, but

lately I have tried to love her. I have thought it all over. I know she is as much to you as if she were your own daughter. All that makes this place home for me makes it home for her. It would seem to be better for us all round if Sophrona and I loved each other, but it is useless to think of it." "Once I thought you were goin' to marry Dora Reed, but I concluded lately that you had given her up.' "I think as much of her as ever." "Then we can't think no more of Sophrona." He tried to speak the words cheerfully, but his tones were husky and harsh like the falling of earth on a coffin. He continued, "It is best in such matters not to meddle." "Then you wouldn't have me try any longer to love Sophrona?" "No, no, these serious matters come out best when we stan' off and let them take their own way. I found it so." Slowly a far away look came over the old man's face, as if his memory were travelling back down the years. Finally his eyes rested on his son and he gazed long and tenderly on the frank young countenance and the clustering brown hair. At last he sighed and said, "Better wash up, Joe; it's most time for supper."

Joe went to the kitchen sink; and through the window saw Horton impatiently edging away with the muddy team and wagon, and on the porch Henry 

Brush was standing before Sophrona and was looking into her face with earnest wistful eyes.

XXVII 

So impressed were several of the local companies to the south by the efficiency of the Hemlock system during the great blizzard of February, 1893, that they offered Joe and his associates the privilege of taking over their rights of way, equipment, and over one hundred and seventy miles of wire, at a nominal price, on the condition that the lines be rebuilt and made the equal or superior of those operated by the Hemlock company.

Dr. Dawson maintained that the acquisition of these lines would at least treble the value of Hemlock stock as soon as Wade constructed the connection with the Bell at Hawley. All of the long distance business of the South Lines would naturally then be routed over Hemlock wires. He was so greatly pleased with the prospect that he offered no opposition when Joe proposed that the South Line's one wire galvanized iron line with ground return should be discarded, and that the rebuilding should be done with hard drawn copper wire; and that all lines under Hemlock control should be made into a two wire metallic circuit system. 

Joe accordingly made the most of the opportunity for improvement and buried himself in books and magazines that discussed the latest methods of line construction. He corresponded with telephone supply 221

houses in both eastern and western cities, and in a few weeks was ready to let the contract for four hundred ninety-one miles of copper wire and a large number of telephone instruments and other equipment. 

Dr. Dawson, meanwhile, was doing his best to get 

Richards to supply information concerning the new transmitter that was destined to supplant the widely used Bell instrument. Whenever Richards came to town, the doctor called him into the exchange and by offering expensive cigars tried to lead him into conversation. It turned out frequently, however, that the doctor did most of the talking. Joe brought this observation to Dawson's attention and was rebuffed for his pains. "Don't think me so simple," the doctor shouted. "I have my friendly chats, and gather my information in a way Ty never suspects.' 99 Richards was so agreeable and generous that the doctor was not inclined to complain because sometimes he did fail to supply the information that was sought. 

Often on the office desk was an open box of candy that 

Richards had brought for Mildred and Sophrona; and he had on more than one occasion taken them both to Clifton to attend one of the dances. The doctor apparently did not at all resent the young man's interest in Mildred and Sophrona, for frequently at noon when they were engaged in conversation at the exchange he had asked Richards to go home with him and Mildred for dinner.

At first Richards accepted these invitations rather freely but later had seemed to prefer, when the doctor and Mildred left at noon, to remain with Sophrona in the office. Once when Sophrona left the exchange at six o'clock he had ridden with her to the Clay farm and then had walked back alone to town.

When Joe found Richards in the office, Joe nodded coldly. In fact they seldom met, for Richards seemed to have an uncanny way of knowing just when Joe was out on the line, and he timed his visits accordingly. 

Joe, for more than one reason, had not been at all pleased with Tyler's frequent visits to the office and increasing interest in Sophrona, and had spoken frankly to Sophrona about it. Sophrona had laughed and had assured Joe that he had no reason to be alarmed because she visited with Belle Jones's big sailor, referring to Richard's first appearance in Hemlock at the masked ball. Just because Ty was good-natured and jolly she had used him to while away the time. Dr. Dawson liked him; but if Joe didn't like him, she would tell him to stay at home, for Richards was nothing to her.

For a few weeks after this conversation Sophrona spent less time in Tyler's company. Later there came a change in her demeanor. Whether it was because of her old perverseness returning, or because of a growing regard for Richards, or for some other reason, she apparently received Tyler's attention with redoubled satisfaction. Again Joe brought up the subject in conversation as they sat alone in the sitting-room, "Don't you think, Sophrona, that Ty Richards is not a fit person to choose for a friend?" "Oh, I think he's awfully nice. People ought not to say he's not nice because he's not handsome. Most people don't know him.” "Everybody around here and Clifton knows him," answered Joe bitterly, "and they're beginning to wonder why he comes so often to Hemlock." "He's been in the telephone business, the same as you have," retorted Sophrona sharply. "He's put his money into the W. and E. and has done well. He's a director

or something. Every little while they send him up to the main office at Plattsburg. You'd say he was a big man if you weren't holding a grudge against him." "I don't see how he can be a very big man or hold a responsible position if he has time to loaf around Hemlock for part of every week." "He has to decide ever so many important matters. 

If you talked with him you'd learn all about it, and you'd think he was awfully nice." "People tell me he hasn't a good reputation. I don't see how you can take any pleasure in his company." 

Sophrona's lips quivered and the tears came into her eyes. "I've got to do something besides work for the telephone company. You wouldn't take time to talk to anybody. I've got to have some friends." "You'll never make friends through associating with 

Ty Richards" began Joe; but Sophrona ran sobbing to her room.

Then Wade came over from Clifton for a talk with 

George Clay. He said he feared he never would be able to build the Hawley connection with the Bell, as had been agreed with the Hemlock company when the charters were obtained. He had been blocked every time he made a move to construct it. "That's bad," said Clay. "Hemlock's got to have that connection with the Bell. Is it capital you lack?" "Not exactly," replied Wade. "The trouble comes from the W. and E. If we try to extend our line, they threaten to cut us off their wires, and so compel everybody in Clifton territory to take a W. and E. phone." "Let them take a W. and E. phone. Over at Boughton Norris has three phones in his office; and I have heard of places where there are more."

"But we have so few subscribers nobody would take our phone if we hadn't connections." "The best move for your directors, then,," said Clay, "instead of giving up that line to Hawley, would be to build it as quick as you can, to bring the Bell to Clifton to help you fight the W. and E." "There isn't much fight left in our directors," rejoined Wade. "The other day when all seemed favorable for construction, Rogers told some of them the W. and E. was too busy with contract matter to be cluttered up with Clifton traffic; and it was only as a personal favor that he continued our connections." "Did that scare them?" "They immediately gave up all idea of constructing the line. The most I could get out of them was an offer to transfer the Hawley franchise to you, thinking you might care to build it." "Hemlock won't take a back seat at the beck of J. 

Henry Rogers," asserted Clay, as a determined look came into his face. "I don't know's we've anything to fear. We had one interview with him, and that made him as mad as he can get." 

Clay called Dr. Dawson and Joe on the telephone and soon announced that the Hemlock directors were favorable to Wade's proposition. "Then I'll have the necessary papers made out at once,” replied Wade enthusiastically. "We'll build it," said Clay, "like all our lines, as a metallic circuit, and that will help one or the other of us to make a deal with the Bell. If you get the opportunity first, you can have the line back on a cost basis. 

All Hemlock wants is the Bell connection."

So it happened that the Clifton-Hawley franchise passed to Hemlock. As the term of the franchise, as

given by the legislature, required the completion of the line by June 1, 1894, it was necessary to work speedily. 

In that mountain climate the frost seldom left the ground more than six weeks before June first. Joe, however, had no doubt of his ability to complete the line on time, and Dr. Dawson never thought of worrying over such matters. In every physical emergency he had learned to rely on Joe's wise planning and hard work.

The doctor, accordingly, was not the least surprised when early in May everything proceeded according to schedule or even surpassed expectation. Never had line building gone forward more smoothly. Joe, himself, was so well-pleased with the progress of the undertaking that on a certain Saturday afternoon he had telephoned his father that he had given the men a halfholiday and would come home for over Sunday. As he drove along the road from Hawley to Clifton he looked with admiration at the line of poles already set-sturdy chestnut with just enough taper, peeled as clean as a whistle. Every one stood up as straight as a soldier parading in the commander's eye. At the curves he saw anchor cables disappear under ground; and he saw more than that. There was the wood and mortar buried beneath the soil; he saw each hidden twist of cable, each knotty log and massive stone, for he knew them all. With a builder's joy, he reviewed his work in the air and his work in the earth; and all was good. 

And he thought of what was yet to come, the tough and neatly painted cross-arms, staunch braces, brackets, and insulators, and then the daintily-drooping, glistening wires. And soon the messages would fly, sharp and clear as thrushes' songs; and distant cities and great men would talk over his beloved wires, his golden wires;

and neither distance would obscure nor the ear weary the brain, but thought unimpeded would speak with thought. Urgent summons and calls for aid, warnings of peril, tales of new inventions and brave exploits, orders to set the wheels awhir in idle factories, words of comfort, and tender thought-through the starlight and the silent night, under the glaring sun, when blizzards heaped the fields and hills with snow-the words would fly over his beautiful shining wires, his golden glistening wires. And never again could fear arise that days would come when because of enmity or greed the line would grow silent and the current cease to flow. 

From the north and the west and the distant towns on the South Line the stream of thought would flow out to the great world over these wires. "Hemlock," he said audibly, "has passed forever from the power of Rogers and his crew." 

As Joe approached his home he saw Dr. Dawson's gig at the side door and the doctor and his father were engaged in conversation on the lawn. "Is anybody sick, doctor?" Joe asked. "No," replied the doctor somewhat impatiently, "your father has not yet seen fit to tell why he called me here."

As soon, however, as the three had eaten a hearty supper and were seated in the parlor, Mr. Clay displayed a formidable document. "Here," he said, “is an injunction signed by Judge Maxford commandin' us to cease further erection of the Hawley line until we have convinced the court that our method of construction will not endanger the lines of the Western and Eastern 

Telephone Company by increasin' its hazard from lightnin'."

"Our method of construction increase the W. and 

E.'s hazard from lightning!" exclaimed the doctor while convulsed with laughter. "If their lawyers haven't anything better to do, why don't they get an injunction against the aurora borealis? That often does cause trouble. Our construction make their lines liable to be hit by lightning! You got us together just to spring that joke, did you, George?" "It's no joke," replied Mr. Clay gravely. "It's a law-suit." "What if it is?" roared Dawson. "You needn't even speak to Jackson about it at all. I could win that suit myself. There's no judge nor jury on earth that could compare their methods of construction with ours and not decide that theirs are inferior in every detail." "Precisely so," Joe responded, "but while we delay to get that decision, our franchise will run out!" 

The doctor's countenance suddenly changed. "I see," he said, closing his jaws like a trap. "That damned whelp, Rogers, is getting in his work."

The doctor never seemed to think it possible that Richards also might have had a part in the plot. Although Joe made no charges, every one including even 

Sophrona knew what he thought. Joe scornfully ignored all her attempts to find an occasion to defend 

Tyler. Finally she became exasperated and exclaimed, "Because you've a grudge against Ty it isn't fair in you to blame him for things he hasn't done." "I haven't said anything about Richards," replied 

Joe sternly. "Do you wish to blame me for having an opinion?" "You've no right to an opinion, if you don't know the facts."

"Richards can look after his own reputation,' torted Joe somewhat warmly. "It isn't your duty to defend him." "It's my business to defend my friends." "Your father and mother wouldn't like to hear you call Ty Richards one of your friends." "Why?" "For the same reasons that your other friends wouldn't want to see you in his company." "What friend besides my father and mother?" she demanded belligerently. "I, for one." "You?" she repeated and curled her lips scornfully, "what right have you to ask me to give up Ty?" "The right that comes from my caring more for you than for any other woman in the world but one." 

The last two words forced their way brutally into the most secret chambers of Sophrona's being; and she lost control of herself, "You know how you could end my interest in Ty if you thought it worth while; but you wouldn't trade a page of one of Dora Reed's letters for all the love I could ever give you. You pretend that you care for me as a sister; but all the regard you have for me you would throw in a ditch for one glance from 

Dora Reed, no matter whether the next moment she'd look at you or not." Her angry tones gave way to shrieks. "That kind of love is all right for you. But 

I'll marry somebody who'll make a return when I give up everything for him. If I go to the devil, I'll marry somebody who makes a return.”

The injunction was returnable May 23 and the franchise expired June 1. It seemed almost impossible to complete the work in the week following the termination of the injunction. Nevertheless it was done. As

soon as the wires flashed the news on the afternoon of May 23 that the court has set the injunction aside, the struggle began. Joe and his men had prepared the material to the last hair. Four or five gangs of laborers were set at work, and for a day or two all went well. 

Then one night a W. and E. foreman bribed the local men to leave the job. Only Joe, Brush, and Fox remained. George Clay then rose to the emergency. He said he "would shinny up the poles himself before he would let Rogers spoil that franchise." He found 

Wade, and was able to secure the services of one or two trusted men. And the work went on. As the end of the month drew near, the news of the race against time was noised throughout the adjoining towns and people came for miles to see Joe and his men work. They worked early in the morning, and late in the evening, and sometimes even by moonlight. And the line was completed on the last day of the month. 

One day in midsummer when the Hawley line was working finely, Joe and Dr. Dawson were sitting at leisure in the exchange. "I'm beginning to think," remarked Joe, "that there's a leak somewheres around this office."

The doctor had a very much satisfied and benign expression on his countenance. The success of the Hawley line had set him day-dreaming over possible profits this year, and next year, and the year after that—"A leak?" he repeated, looking about rather vaguely, but disliking very much to relinquish the million that was almost within his grasp. "What kind of leak?" "In some way or other I think the W. and E. is learning what we are doing, and what our plans are."

"Guessing, probably, instead of learning. Any capable business executive would plan about as we do." "It's more definite than that," answered Joe. "Fox said that when we were building the Hawley line and 

Lee Austin, the W. and E. foreman, came to the boarding house to bribe our men to quit, he told them all about our plans and policy, including even Wade's first conference with the Bell." "Fox told you? I wouldn't condemn a fly on Fox's evidence. The other day I caught him turning over the papers on your desk. I fired him out as fast as he could go, and told the girls never to let him come into this office." "Didn't Fox tell you why he was here?" "I didn't listen to what he said. He always wants to talk-especially if you tell him to do something." "I gave Fox permission to go to my desk for a copy of The Telephone Magazine." 

"When I saw him he was turning over the letters, and you don't have to turn over letters to see a magazine of that size." "Fox is always playing the fool, but he looks out for our interests. There is just one person who hangs round this office and has no business here." "Richards? Nonsense. Leave Richards to me. I'll take care of him. We'd have had a break with the 

W. and E. before now, if it hadn't been for my influence with Richards." 

The Hawley line proved to be exceptionally reliable.

Not once during the winter of 1894-95 did it fail to operate perfectly. The South Lines used it exclusively for their long-distance connections; and the W. and E.

used it whenever their poorly constructed lines were out of order.

One day a Bell inspector drove through the town and visited the Hemlock exchange. Dr. Dawson came up from his office. The inspector complimented the company on its methods of line construction and asked a great many questions about Hemlock territory and the number of subscribers. Dr. Dawson was convinced that the visit presaged an early offer from the Bell for the Hawley line, if not for the entire Hemlock system. 

As it turned out, however, the Bell began its negotiations with the Clifton company. The Clays and Dawson were very optimistic when Wade came one day and said the Bell had offered to take over the Clifton company on condition it repurchased the Hawley line. The 

Bell offered current prices, or a little better, for Clifton stock, and cost-plus-ten per cent for the Hawley line. 

Accordingly Hemlock prepared the contract transferring the Hawley line to Clifton. This arrangement would bring the Bell eighteen miles nearer Hemlock, and would remove all fear of the W. and E.'s getting control of the Clifton company.

Great secrecy was to be maintained until Wade had secured a controlling interest in the Clifton company; but no trouble was anticipated as Wade had recently been offered sufficient stock at lower prices than those he was now prepared to pay. Joe thought there might be trouble enough if Richards was able to learn why 

Wade was investing so heavily in Clifton stock. He called Sophrona into the inner office and explained the circumstances to her, for he had noticed recently that Ty had been spending more time in her company and had been addressing her with increasing familiarity.

He begged her to avoid all conversation with Richards until Wade had gained control of the Clifton company. "I'll see that Ty doesn't hear a word about it,' answered Sophrona glibly. 99 "You don't seem to appreciate the seriousness of the situation," insisted Joe. "This is something you owe to all of us." "I told you I wouldn't speak of it," said Sophrona; but there was a baffling far-away look in her face, "Why don't you speak frankly—the way you used to talk?" 

For a moment Sophrona hesitated. Then dramatically she stepped backward a foot or two, dropped her eyes, and stood with her hands behind her back, her face dimpled with smiles. "I think it's time you congratulated me." 

Joe understood dimly. "Why?" he snarled. "Why don't you congratulate me?" repeated Sophrona, more or less timidly raising her eyes and bringing forward her left hand on the third finger of which was displayed a cheap ring. "Are you engaged to Ty Richards?" asked Joe severely. "I've been engaged for four days!" replied Sophrona triumphantly. "And you wish me to congratulate you because of that?" 

Sophrona tossed her nose into the air, "You may do as you please." "When do you intend to tell your father and mother?" "They already know it." "And you are willing to leave your home and friends and place your destiny in the hands of Ty Richards?"

"Yes," replied Sophrona, and her face became as serious as if she were responding in a marriage ceremony. "Ty says he will do anything for me." "And you believe him?" "I do," answered Sophrona gravely and fervently. "Suppose I were to tell you of some of the stories that are going around town?" "I presume they're lies; but if they're not, I don't want to hear them." 

Joe gazed at Sophrona silently. Then he rose and paced the floor. Drops of sweat came on his brow.

Finally he paused before Sophrona and looked down into her eyes. "Is there something I could do that would end this trouble?" "No," replied Sophrona. "It's too late." 

Before the end of the week, Wade, George Clay, Dr. Dawson, and Joe met at the Hemlock exchange. 

Wade was very unhappy. He said he had been unable to get a controlling interest in Clifton stock. Every one seemed to know of his proposed sale to the Bell, and prices had advanced accordingly. He had continued the canvas until word had come from the president's office in Boston that the Bell had given up the idea of taking over the Clifton lines. It was said that President 

Hudson had become piqued because news of the Bell's proposed purchased of the Clifton Company had leaked out. 

Wade threw the Hawley contract on the table, remarking bitterly, "After all our work, this is a worthless sheet of paper. I have played my last card.”

This news caused as great disappointment to Dr. 

Dawson and the Clays as to Wade himself. So much depended on bringing the Bell into Hemlock territory

to aid in resisting the W. and E., that the Hemlock directors were unwilling to give up so long as there was the slightest hope of success. Not having encountered the difficulties that Wade had been meeting for some weeks, they were not so easily discouraged by the news from Boston. With both argument and financial support, they tried to make Wade take a brighter view of the situation; and, before the conference ended, were successful in reaching an agreement not to destroy the contract, but to deposit it in the Hemlock safe until further efforts had been made to purchase a controlling interest in Clifton stock at a higher price, and until Wade himself had gone to Boston, if necessary, to secure a personal conference with President Hudson of the Bell.

XXVIII 

TY AND OTHERS START ON A JOURNEY 

Mr. and Mrs. Hamblin had driven to the Clay homestead, and Mr. Hamblin was slowly tieing the horse to the hitching-post. With deliberation he walked to the side of the vehicle and helped Mrs. Hamblin out. 

They were dressed in their best clothes and had come to call. Amanda met them at the door. She was alone.

When greetings suited to the occasion had been given and a few preliminary remarks made, Amanda began to tell the story that she had already recounted so many times. "It was a week ago Thursday about ten o'clock in the morning that they started. Theodore brought round the gray team hitched to the democrat wagon, and Mr. Clay came out of his room looking so cheerful and well you wouldn't have thought there was anything the matter with him. He was joking and warned me not to go near that tomato plant there in the corner while he was gone. In the morning we'd been looking at a tomato that was beginning to turn and I had screamed when I put my hand on a worm. The doctor sat with Theodore, and Joe got up behind with his father. Theodore drove out of the yard, and then down there by that mountain-ash tree, just this side of the barn, Mr. Clay looked back and waved his hand; and that's the last time I ever saw him alive. 236

TY AND OTHERS START ON A JOURNEY 237 

Theodore said that all the way to Boughton he was just as cheerful, and just before he got on the train he went to the store and bought two boxes of candy and sent them home for Sophrona and Mildred.

Friday night Joe called up on the long-distance telephone and we talked several minutes with him and 

Mr. Clay. You could hear every word, and hear Mr.

Clay laugh just as if he was up here in the office at 

Hemlock. He cautioned me again not to go near that worm till he could go with me. Joe said his father was feeling fine; and the operation was to take place the next morning. 

We heard nothing more until Saturday afternoon when Dr. Dawson called up and said that all was over. 

It seems he had never really rallied from the operation, but the ether left him dazed like; and he grew weaker and weaker until he passed away. "I can't think he's dead," said Amanda, bursting into tears. "He was the kindest man I ever knew. He was happiest when helping the rest of us out of our troubles, but he never had any of his own. I remember when 

Theodore and I were first married we lived over there across the road. Mr. Clay and Melissa lived here, and 

Joey was only a baby then. There never was a happier family or more content. When Mr. Clay was home you could hear him laugh all day long. Soon after our

Sophrona was born, Melissa took a cold and died of pneumonia. I remember I said, 'Theodore, George 

Clay will never laugh again.' But I didn't know him then. It wasn't his way to brood over his troubles. He wanted to be near anybody who was happy, and he was always looking for some cause to laugh." "I suppose Joe takes his father's death pretty hard?" inquired Mrs. Hamblin.

"Joe doesn't say what he thinks; but when he was a boy, if anything was wrong, we could tell by the way he did his work. He put two days into one." 

"I suppose Joe will stick to the telephone and Theodore will run the farm just as he has?" inquired Mr. Hamblin. "Mr. Clay's death was so sudden that I don't believe 

Joe himself knows what changes it will make.” 

Mr. and Mrs. Hamblin rose to go and Amanda followed them down the walk. "Have you heard from 

Dora lately?" "She was expecting to go to St. Paul when she finished college, but changed her plans when her uncle Gregory died. She's in New York at her aunt's," replied Mrs. Hamblin as she took her seat in the carriage. "Ain't she ever coming back to Hemlock again?” 

Amanda inquired? "I am sure I don't know. Her aunt wants her to go to Europe to study more music." "Too bad Joe and Sophrona weren't home," Amanda called, as the horses slowly moved away.

A few days later when Joe entered the exchange, he found Dr. Dawson scolding Sophrona and Mildred because of some trivial matter. Joe at first was inclined to be amused, but grew serious when he observed that both girls were unhappy, and the doctor decidedly in earnest. For several minutes the physician continued to bang away at the subject as if he were killing a reptile that could stand an unlimited number of blows and wouldn't die until sundown. At last Sophrona burst into tears.

TY AND OTHERS START ON A JOURNEY 239 

Such manifestation of feeling was unusual on the part of Sophrona. Joe had observed that for some time she had been more silent and preoccupied than usual, and he had very naturally supposed that her depressed spirits were evidence of grief occasioned by his father's death. With quickened sympathy he walked to the high stool where Sophrona was weeping and, putting his arm around her, spoke tenderly. “There, there," he said, "don't cry; don't mind the doctor." 

Then he turned toward Dawson and reproved him, "You ought not to be so rough. Just now, I should think you could afford to be a little more considerate." 

Sophrona's sobbing became louder, "I want to talk -with you," she said.

Joe glanced toward the door of the inner office, but 

Sophrona did not move. "I might as well tell all of you," she continued, raising her tearful eyes and gazing at the little group about her, "for everybody will know. Ty has left me and gone to Texas." "Ty gone to Texas!" echoed the doctor. "You've broken your engagement?" gasped Mildred. "Ty broke it yesterday. He went to Boughton in the stage and came back about noon with a platform wagon he got at the livery. He was here in the office in the afternoon, after Mildred went away, and wrote letters. 

When he first came in he had talked with me so nicely that when he was done writing I said something about our getting married. He said he was going to Dallas

Texas, to work for a new telephone company and we had better call the engagement off. I said I would go to Texas with him, but he tried to pick a quarrel with me. When I agreed to do all he said, he got angry and told me to keep the ring, but the engagement was

off. Just then I had to answer some calls and he went out." "Let's hope that's the last of him," Joe exclaimed breathlessly. 

Sophrona continued, "I knew he wouldn't keep the livery rig long, so I went home and waited out beyond the barn. He soon came along with his trunk in the wagon. I asked him not to break the engagement, and said I'd wait till he came back. When he said I didn't know how long that would be, I offered to get some things and go with him. He laughed and started the horses. I said I'd go as I was, and tried to climb in the wagon; but he swore at me and pushed me off, and that's how I hurt my arm." "The skunk! When I get hold of him, I'll teach him!" shouted Joe clenching his hands and jumping up and down with excitement. 

"Damn him. I wasted the time I spent on him,' said Dr. Dawson. "If he's gone to Dallas, Texas, he won't have anything more to do with the telephone business around here.” "That's the only consolation," exclaimed Joe. “If he ever comes back I'll make him settle; but I guess he'll stay in Dallas, and keep out of my sight, and

Sophrona's, and not try again to snoop around here." 

Conversation was interrupted by Mildred's calling

Dr. Dawson to the booth. He talked for several minutes and then rushed from the booth in a greatly excited condition. Omitting ejaculations and digressions, he said that Wade had been on the wire; and he had sold his stock when he heard of Clay's death; and the W. and E. now owned the Clifton company body and soul. "Wade," concluded Dawson, "advised us to go ahead ourselves and try to sell our Hawley line

TY AND OTHERS START ON A JOURNEY 241 

to the Bell. As there was no further use for the 

Hemlock-Clifton contract, it ought to be destroyed that there might be no chance of its coming into improper hands." "Sophrona hasn't opened the mail yet," said Mildred, "and there's a letter from Clifton that might have something more to say about what's happened there." "Read it," directed the doctor. "Increasing traffic demands that the entire equipment of the Clifton Telephone Company be devoted exclusively to its own business. Consequently due and formal notice is hereby given the Hemlock Telephone Company that the wires of the Clifton company cannot carry its traffic after August 1, 1895." 

The doctor drew in his breath and snapped his fingers, "Now I guess we're glad we built the Hawley line.

It's all that keeps us from being shut from outside connections." "It was for such an emergency we built it," observed 

Joe with satisfaction. "And now we want to be darned sure,” said the doctor, "that the W. and E. doesn't get its hands on that 

Hawley contract. If they could get possession of itnow that they have bought the Clifton stock-there's nothing that would prevent their accepting it as delivered to Wade, and so force us to turn over to them the Hawley line on a cost-plus-ten per cent basis. I guess we can't be too quick about tearing up that paper."

Dr. Lawson walked across the office to the safe and unlocked a steel drawer, "My God," he exclaimed, "the contract is gone."

Joe jumped from his chair. Mildred and Sophrona left connections half made. Hurried questions showed that no one of the office force had removed or even handled the contract. There was but one conclusion. 

The contract returned by Wade had been stolen. "If the W. and E. can make us carry out that contract, we shall lose all connections with the rest of the world and shall be shut in like a squirrel in a cage," said Joe. "Unless we can get back that paper," said the doctor, "the W. and E. can force us to go out of business and accept for our stock whatever they are willing to give." "How could anybody get in here to steal those papers!" exclaimed Mildred. "It was Fox," shouted her father. "Yesterday when 

I came back for my bag I found him here.”

Joe turned toward Sophrona and inquired rather gently, "Did you ever leave Richards alone in the inner office where he might have got at the safe?" "He often wrote letters there when I was busy," replied Sophrona meekly. "I don't believe Richards had anything to do with it," gruffly retorted the doctor who had been pacing back and forth seeking some clue. "It was Fox; and he will take the contract to some of those W. and E. men on the Clifton board of directors. He worked on the line this forenoon, and he won't reach Clifton till late this afternoon, and-" the doctor looked at his watch-"I'll reach Clifton first." He snapped the case shut, and turned to Joe, "Can you take me at once to Clifton?" "I am sorry," Joe replied, taking from his pockets and laying on his desk several papers relating to tele-

TY AND OTHERS START ON A JOURNEY 243 

phone business, "but I'm going toward Boughton after Richards." "You're not going to Dallas, Texas, are you?" inquired Dr. Dawson with some alarm. "I don't know where I'm going, except that I'm going to find Richards."

XXIX 

JOE WRITES HIMSELF A LETTER 

Within half an hour from the time that Joe had left the office he was riding his bicycle full speed toward

Boughton. At his right, where a few years before the great swamp had extended for miles, there was now a broad and level expanse of farm land. Long rows of green and yellow celery converged and met in the distance, for it was late in July. In other fields onions half-grown spread a faint green haze over the black soil. Elsewhere he passed meadows from which enormous loads of hay had already been cut and which were growing pink for a second time with blossoming clover; and here and there dark leaved corn in hills waist high grew luxuriantly.

The swamp road which formerly had been scarcely more than a sandy trail was now worn with travel; and a half completed house near the rock cut, where a little rushing stream dashed under the highway, furnished evidence of increasing population and prosperity. Joe was not the only one who had obtained profit from draining the swamp; neighboring farmers who formerly had secured a precarious living were now finding their incomes doubled and their labor lightened. Comforts and luxuries were coming to many a home in which they had been unknown. 244

JOE WRITES Himself a Letter 

245 From the perils that were threatening the Hemlock Telephone Company Joe was able to divert his attention for only a moment. He hastened to Norris's produce office. Norris was busy, but left his work at once. He looked worried and evidently was expecting a rebuke for a recent sale of Clifton telephone stock to the W. and E. "I didn't like to sell, Joe," he began, “but I knew you could fall back on your Hawley line-" "Never mind that," interrupted Joe peremptorily. "But I want your help. Ty Richards was here yesterday, and I want to find where he went." "Is Ty mixed up in this W. and E. business?" asked

Norris with eager curiosity. "Or is this merely another of his girl affairs?” "It may be both," Joe responded in a tone that induced Norris to forego further discussion. 

Norris unbolted the door that led to the freight office and called to the station agent. Tiffany responded at once, for Norris was the source of nearly all the freight that left the Boughton station. "Clay and I wish to communicate with Ty Richards. Can you tell us where he went yesterday?" 

Tiffany responded with a smile, "I guess you'll need to write to him, for he's gone to Dallas, Texas. He went out on the 9:03 last night." "You don't think there's any doubt about his going to Dallas, do you?" inquired Joe. "You don't think it possible that he went to some other place-first?” "Oh, no, he was bound for Dallas all right. He's been talking to us all summer about going there, and he knew the price of a ticket to a penny. He wouldn't buy his ticket here. He pretended he had better offers from Albany and Utica."

"Had you just as soon call up the Junction," Norris inquired, "and make sure which way he went ?”

Tiffany went to the telegraph key but seemed to have difficulty in obtainng the information he sought. He called several stations and from time to time gave vent to exclamations of surprise. When at last he rose, he said he couldn't understand what Richards had been doing. He hadn't gone to the Junction, but had got off at Coss's and had taken the northern train back. "I guess he must have forgotten something, and had to come back," Tiffany ventured. "Probably he got a livery rig and drove back again to Hemlock." "No," responded Joe looking at his watch. "I think he went on north; and I have just time enough to get something to eat and take the afternoon train for Plattsburg.' 

A few minutes later Joe had boarded the train and was on his way to Plattsburg. As it was nearly five o'clock when he reached the town, he made haste to inquire the way to the offices of the W. and E. He had not fully decided on his course of action, but as there were several matters concerning the Clifton lines and toll exchanges that needed to be adjusted by the two companies, he thought that he could transact this business and meanwhile keep himself alert to learn whatever he could concerning the whereabouts of Richards. Accordingly he applied at the office for Mr. Rogers.

The girl at the outer desk informed him that Mr. 

Rogers was out of town and would not return for several days. Joe asked her where he could be reached if one wished to write to him. She said that Mr. Rogers was in Boston, but as he had planned to visit several points in New England probably could not be reached

JOE WRITES HIMSELF A LETTER 

247 until he went to New York to attend a directors' meeting on the 2nd. In reply to further inquiry from Joe she said he customarily stayed at the Hoffman House in the city. "Can you tell me where I can find Mr. Richards?" 

Joe asked. "He was here this morning," the girl responded. “I think he went to New York. I will look up the address." "What is that?" savagely inquired a rough but energetic looking man who attracted by the conversation had come from behind a partition and had approached the desk. "Have you been asked to report all the company's affairs to strangers?" he demanded of the girl in brutal tones. The girl with frightened demeanor thereupon left the desk and mingled with the other employees who were preparing to leave the office, for the whistles had blown for six o'clock.

The man approached Joe and studied his muscular form from head to toe and gazed searchingly into his face. "What can I do for you?" he asked in tones decidedly more gentle than those used in addressing the girl.

Joe became suspicious, because of the anger shown at the attempted giving of Richards's address, that the man was more or less familiar with some of Ty's doubtful enterprises. It therefore seemed best not to mention the Hemlock company. "I came to see Mr. Richards, but if he has gone to New York I should like to get his address." "I believe you first asked for Mr. Rogers. If your business is with the company, you may speak to me, for in his absence I am the manager of this office."

THE COAT OF MANY COLOR'S 

"My business with Mr. Rogers I can postpone; but my business with Mr. Richards is personal," said Joe, "and urgent. 99 "If you will give me your name and address I will send Richards at once any message you care to dictate," replied the manager curtly. "I prefer to see him myself," answered Joe. 1 "And Mr. Richards may prefer not to see you," replied the manager putting on his hat. "He has not authorized us to give out his address and consequently you will not obtain it here." "This is a matter of great importance-" began Joe, but he was interrupted by the manager who said spitefully, "Well, then, I shall immediately write Richards that a person of your description, name unknown, is following him to discuss a matter of great importance. 

Then if you will call in a few days, I will inform you whether he wishes to see you."

The manager bowed Joe to the door, and called sharply to the last of the departing employees, “Norton, come back and take a letter to Richards."

In chagrin Joe left the office and walked a short distance down the street. His attempt to get Richards's address had resulted in ignominious failure. Instead of securing the information he sought, he had occasioned the sending of a warning that in all probability would enable the scoundrel to elude pursuit and deliver the stolen contract to Rogers. If Rogers ever got the

Hawley line, Hemlock's only connection with the outside world, he could control the Hemlock Telephone

Company absolutely and without the necessity of purchasing a single share of stock.

Joe had thought that without Richards's address he might go to New York and haunt the entrance to the

JOE WRITES HIMSELF A LETTER 

249 

Hoffman House, where the girl said Rogers was accustomed to stay; and when Richards came on the 2nd to bargain with Rogers, he might attempt, although in a public place, to secure the document by force. But now since the W. and E. manager was putting Richards on his guard, probably even that slim chance was gone. 

Evidently the only hope for success depended upon finding Richards and lighting upon some means of obtaining the contract before he had had opportunity to deliver the paper to Rogers or receive the warning letter.

Joe walked back up the street to a stationery store opposite the W. and E. entrance. Standing near a front window he turned the pages of a magazine. Soon the manager came from the W. and E. office and quickly passed from sight. A few minutes later the employee who had been called Norton, came to the door, sealing an envelope, which he placed in his coat pocket. Joe left the store and followed Norton. The latter walked but a short distance; and then, to Joe's great joy, entered a restaurant. Joe also entered, and seated himself at Norton's table, for he felt sure that the stenographer had not observed him while he was talking to the manager.

Joe's sense of satisfaction over his success in getting into the company of the bearer of the letter soon gave way to discouragement, for he was at a loss how to secure the friendly consideration of the stranger. “Can you tell me," he finally asked, "whether I can get a train from Plattsburg going south late this evening?" "There is none late this evening," the man replied courteously. "There is one going south in about an hour; and then there's no other until 8:15 tomorrow morning."

Joe thought it best under these circumstances to leave the restaurant with Norton, and walk along the street to a post-box, and then attempt to get one swift glance of the address as the stenographer mailed the letter. If 

Norton should hold the envelope so that the address could not be seen, he could use force at least to the extent of a little twist of the hand. But the thought came, that if he thus obtained the address and rushed for the evening train, the stenographer might become angry, and telegraph to Richards a warning that would induce him to mail the contract to Rogers or to disappear. On the whole the greatest hope for success seemed to be in delaying the mailing of the letter until the evening train had gone, and in attempting later to obtain Norton's good-will and cooperation.

Joe, accordingly inquired concerning Plattsburg hotels, and asked Norton's preference; and then spoke of

Hotel Champlain and summer visitors, and finally succeeded in inducing Norton to talk freely of Ausable Chasm, of the trip across the lake to Burlington, and of other local attractions. Joe entered in a memorandum book directions for reaching one or two places that seemed to be of especial interest, and finally was able to ask the stenographer to ride with him on the street car out to Hotel Champlain, and later go to see a show which his new acquaintance had said was to be at the opera house that evening. Norton accepted gladly and never seemed to think of his letter.

On the return from Hotel Champlain they passed directly from the car to the theatre. The play had already begun; but in an interval between acts Joe led the conversation to telephone affairs and asked rather adroitly, as he believed, whether Norton was ac-

JOE WRITES Himself a Letter 

251 quainted with the employees of the W. and E. telephone company. "Why, I thought you saw me there this afternoon when you asked for Richards's address," Norton observed with a look of surprise. 

Joe saw in an instant that only frankness could serve his purpose, "Then you understand how much I need that information?" "I heard what you said to the manager." 99 "If I can get that address this evening, I may be able to block an outrageous fraud," said Joe, "and I am asking your aid because I believe you are the kind of man who would be willing to stand up for what is right.” "Of course I couldn't give you Richards's address, even if I thought your side was right," replied Norton sharply. "My relations with the manager are confidential." "Certainly as long as your employers are honorable," insinuated Joe with a smile, "but you can't be expected to assist in crooked schemes. You wouldn't keep your manager's confidence if he plotted against the country?" "I shall keep his confidence as long as I am willing to retain my position," replied Norton sullenly.

The play was resumed and Joe found no other opportunity to continue the conversation until he and Norton were again in the street. Then as they walked along the dark deserted way, Joe in the guise of a tempter, bent over the little man and offered gifts for a glance at the address, or a position in Hemlock, if Norton wished to leave the employment of a company that used dishonest methods. Norton made no reply but laughed quietly. Joe changed his key. "Then I must use force. 

I can't let disaster come to Hemlock. You are not to blame."

Norton suddenly plunged forward, but Joe was quicker than he and passed him, and took his stand before a box against a telephone pole. Norton stopped short at a lamppost, clanged an iron lid; and, turning, darted rapidly down the side street. 

Joe groaned when he discovered that he had placed his broad back against a fire-box, and that Norton had indeed posted the letter, and had fled. Joe returned to the lamppost and examined the box minutely. He discovered that the night collection was at a quarter of one. 

It was then nearly twelve. For a minute he deliberated and then hastened to a drug store not far away. He bought envelopes, paper, and stamps. He quickly wrote two letters. One he addressed to Norris at Boughton. 

He thanked the produce dealer for his recent aid and promised to call soon for his bicycle. The other he addressed to Joseph Clay. He enclosed a five dollar bill in payment of all accounts to date. He dropped both letters into the post-box without sealing or affixing stamps. Then he waited. 

On two occasions during the next hour someone dropped a letter into the box. Then the postman came.

As he unlocked the door, Joe addressed him with rapidly throbbing heart, "I dropped two letters in the box a little while ago without putting stamps on them or sealing them. In one I put a five dollar bill, and it may have dropped out. The one that ought to have the five dollar bill was addressed to Joseph Clay, Hemlock, New York; and the other, to John Norris, Boughton, New York. I have stamps here which I wish you would put on yourself, or let me put them on; and the letters ought to be sealed."

The postman grumbled something about careless people but, turning over the handful of letters he had re-

JOE WRITES Himself a Letter 

253 moved from the box, he brought into full view in the lamplight a W. and E. envelope addressed to Tyler

Richards. With wildly beating heart Joe pushed the five dollar bill into the letter addressed to himself, affixed stamps, sealed the envelopes, and hastened away in the direction of his hotel. Under the next lamppost he halted; and marking out from his memorandum book directions for reaching Ausable Chasm, he entered, "Tyler Richards, Smith and McNeill's Hotel, 197 

Washington Street, New York."

XXX 

HEMLOCK METHODS 

The next morning when Joe took the train he had formed a plan as to how he would operate after he arrived in New York. He had reached no definite conclusion as to the smaller details, for he knew too little of the great city and the arrangement and customs of hotels to be able to determine his procedure step by step. 

Nevertheless he had a tentative plan which all day long he turned over in his mind in the hope that he might prepare himself for every alternative that could arise. 

At seven o'clock when he reached New York, he hastened from the station and seeing the Grand Union Hotel across the street engaged a room and left his bag. 

He learned from a policeman the way to Washington Street, and took an elevated train to the Bridge, and crossed City Hall Park in the direction of his destination. He walked rapidly to the corner of Fulton Street and there found the old gray building known as Smith and McNeill's Hotel. Before the entrance he paused to get his breath, for the thought of the strenuous events in which he expected soon to take a part, had somewhat disturbed his customary poise. It was not fear but uncertainty that unnerved him. It was the consciousness that he might need to use his utmost ability in surroundings as strange and unfamiliar to 254

HEMLOCK METHODS 

255 him as those he would have met in exploring an unknown land.

He hastened up the steps and rapidly swept his eyes over the group of roughly clad drovers and cattle men that occupied the lobby. Richards was not among them. 

At the desk after a somewhat prolonged interview with the clerk, who was accustomed to deal with country men, he learned that Tyler Richards had that day registered at the hotel and was at the present moment probably in Room 38. Joe turned at once from the clerk and went to the street. Of a passerby he inquired the way to a first-class hotel and was directed to the New 

Ontario. There he registered as J. Henry Rogers and sent the following telegram: "Tyler Richards, Smith and McNeill's Hotel, 197 

Washington Street. Change of plans takes me to the New Ontario, 260 Broadway. Call Wednesday evening as early as possible. 

J. Henry Rogers."

After waiting in his room less than an hour Joe heard footsteps approaching and somebody knocking on the door. He stepped out of sight into the alcove and shouted in deep tones, “Come in."

Tyler Richards walked into the center of the room; and, to Joe's great surprise, was followed by another heavily-built man. That was an alternative that he had not prepared for; he had not supposed that Rogers and Richards cared to have a witness when they discussed their shady transactions. He had expected in dealing with Richards to use Hemlock methods that experience had proved effective. But two powerful men

THE COAT of Many COLORS 

were now matched against him, and it was too late to change his plans. Like a flash he dashed to the door, turned the key, and withdrew it from the lock. Richards shrunk back with a look of surprise and alarm as he recognized Joe; but quickly recovered himself and shouted a word of warning to his companion. "Look out, Lee!" 

Joe recognized Lee Austin, the W. and E. foreman who had attempted to induce the Hemlock employees to desert the company while it was struggling to complete the Hawley line. Although Joe had never spoken with Austin, he was well acquainted with the man's reputation as a rough and desperate character. For a moment Austin regarded Joe with a sullen composure, and then snarled, "Is this a trap?" "As you please. I will unlock the door if you care to go. Richards can't leave until he has returned one of my papers." "Richards may go now; unlock the door," said

Austin with a tone of finality as he produced a shining nickel revolver.

Without an instant's hesitation, Joe seized the only object within his reach, an iron candlestick that stood on the mantlepiece, and struck the man's hand a heavy blow, knocking the revolver to the floor. Austin groaned and held his injured hand to his chest. Joe threw aside the candlestick and picked the revolver from the floor. "Go into the bathroom; and make no disturbance," 

Joe commanded. 

Austin who was groaning with pain obeyed speedily, and Joe locked the door and placed the key in his pocket.

HEMLOCK METHODS 

257 Richards meanwhile stood at the farther side of the room, his face flushed with the ugly and desperate expression that was characteristic of him. "I don't need this for you," remarked Joe contemptuously, as he placed the revolver on a dresser that stood at the opposite side of the room from Richards. "Now hand me that paper." "I haven't any paper. I don't know what you mean," replied Richards sullenly. "Bring me that envelope," said Joe, pointing to a large blue envelope that Richards's unbuttoned coat rendered partly visible. 

Richards hesitated; and then glancing in the direction of the revolver, drew the envelope from his pocket and began to fumble with the papers it contained, and with the pretense of seeking a better light walked in the direction of the revolver. "Hand me that envelope," ordered Joe peremptorily.

Richards suddenly ceased to look in the direction of the dresser; something else had attracted his attention. 

He took a paper from the envelope and advanced toward Joe and held out the missing Hawley contract, but seemed to drop it awkwardly.

As Joe eagerly bent to pick it up, Richards snatched the iron candlestick from the floor and struck with all his might at the back of Joe's head. The sudden movement, however, caused Joe to draw to the side, but not sufficiently to enable him wholly to escape the blow. 

The heavy iron fell on his shoulders and left arm with awful force. Joe, nevertheless, secured the paper and as he put it in his pocket Richards struck him again on his side, and attempted to pass by him and seize the revolver. With his right hand Joe hit Richards full

in the mouth and knocked him to the floor. But 

Richards was up in an instant, striking viciously with the heavy iron and not infrequently sending a blow home, for the injury to Joe's left arm and side handicapped his superior strength. Joe could have seized the revolver and have brought the fight to a sudden close, but his scorn and contempt for Richards was such that despite the pain he suffered he disdained to use any weapon but his fists. At last he secured Richards's arm and wrenched away the candlestick, and the coward at once gave up. "You have your contract," gasped Richards, who was desperately out of breath. "Let me go." "I ought to lick you within an inch of your life because of what you said to Sophrona; and I will—” said 

Joe as he slapped Richards's face, "-if you ever speak to her again." "I don't intend to have nothing more to do with her," said Richards, wiping the blood from the end of his nose. "I know you don't, you cowardly vermin. I know you dassn't have nothing more to do with her. None of your promises! If you ever do come near her, I'll— 

I'll-" said Joe gasping for words and staring into the cowardly eyes of the trembling and bloody face before him, "I'll nail your dirty hide to our barn door." He turned to the mirror and arranged as best he could his hair and clothes; he seized his hat, slapped it against his elbow to shake off the dust, and unlocked the door. "Keep quiet! Or I'll be back!" He locked the door on the outside, laid the bathroom key on the threshold, put the revolver into his pocket, left the room key in the hotel office, and passed to the street.

HEMLOCK METHODS 

259 At a telephone booth in the Grand Union Hotel he called Dr. Dawson and reported his success. Before he went to bed he wrote a letter to Sophrona in which he enclosed a piece of paper torn from the center of the Hawley contract. He tried to console her, and promised to hasten his return.

XXXI 

ELIZABETH 

Joe slept soundly all night and until late the next morning. He awoke refreshed in body and mind. The events of the last two days troubled him no longer. 

Evil men had plotted against him but they had been overthrown. The stolen contract had been destroyed, and the telephone company was freed from peril, and could now make new plans for usefulness. If he himself was in danger of personal injury from his enemies, or if there was the possibility of trouble with the law because of the means he had taken to circumvent them, the thought had not occurred to him. His chief source of worry concerned Sophrona. He must hurry back to console her. She had suffered bitterly the last few days; and his departure had come so suddenly that he had not even said good-bye.

A cool breeze tossed the shade at the windows and revealed glimpses of clear blue sky. From the street came the rumble of trolleys and the clatter of traffic. 

Somewhere in this great city, he thought, with all its business, all its interests, among its men and women, good and bad, was Dora. She was here, perhaps with 

Arthur Pendleton, or with some other, from her circle of friends, whom she loved still better. Why think of that? The end must be right. 260

ELIZABETH 

261 Did he not know Dora's mind and nature as no other knew it? The people and mountains that had moulded his mind and personality, had shaped hers. Her world had been his. He had walked at her side as she passed from infancy to womanhood. Not even her father, nor Dr. Gregory, nor Miss Stevens, had known as he knew, the beginnings and growth of each timid hope and youthful ideal.

And his own personality had had its effect on her life and had altered it, whether for good or bad, and had left traces that time and eternity could not wipe out. Nor could time and eternity steal from him the changes that her life had made in his. Some obstacle had temporarily risen between them. Perhaps Arthur

Pendleton had for the time being obscured his image in Dora's eyes. 

Whatever the obstacle it could be discovered. His few hours must be used to best advantage. He would hasten through his business and then try to find Dora.

The thought impelled him to leave his bed at once. 

He rose with considerable pain, for his shoulder and arm were bruised and sore. After breakfast at the hotel, he went into the streets teeming with interest for one who had lived all his life in a small town. 

At a tailor shop he had his suit repaired and pressed. The tailor suggested the purchase of a new suit of up-to-date materials and style; but Joe had no time to wait for a new suit. He had promised Sophrona he would hasten his return. Only a few weeks before he had had the tailor at Clifton bind the slightly worn edges with the best mohair braid. Now that it was freshly pressed, it looked spic and span. 

At Siegel-Cooper's he purchased a cravat to replace that injured in the tussle with Richards.

While he was adjusting the new scarf at a mirror he noticed that a young lady was looking with curious interest in his direction. Something about her face or figure, as he glanced at the reflection for an instant, was familiar; and he turned and walked down the aisle to obtain full sight of her face. As their eyes met, she exclaimed with apparent surprise, "Why, isn't this 

Mr. Clay? How glad I am to see you."

The speaker was Elizabeth Fairchild. She stood before him, extending her hand and smiling cordially, and looking brighter and more attractive than ever before. "Are you living in the city?" she inquired; "or is this merely a visit?" "I'm down on business," said Joe, "but I want to see you and Dora before I go back." "Then you haven't seen Dora yet? How surprised she'll be when she finds I've seen you first. Mother is out of town or she would wish you to come to dinner. 

But you must call. Tell me when you'll come. There is so much to talk over I don't want to miss you." "I "I could come early in the afternoon," said Joe. “I intend to leave tomorrow night." "Then come this afternoon; I shall be home until evening," replied Elizabeth as she handed him her address. "Then I'll call about three o'clock." "I am so glad! I want to talk with you about Dora and everything that happened while we were in the mountains." 

On leaving the store Joe went to Greenwich Street to a supply house from which he had often purchased telephone material. After ordering a bill of goods, he had a long talk with the manager and was taken to

ELIZABETH 

263 luncheon in the vicinity. With curiosity he visited the shops along the river thoroughfares that deal in machinery and novelty goods; but exactly at three o'clock he rang the bell at the Fairchild mansion. 

He was admitted by Elizabeth herself who took him to a luxuriously furnished parlor and seated him in a large easy chair. "How quickly three years have gone by. I remember all that happened during the week we were together as if it were yesterday," she exclaimed, and touched lightly on one occurrence after another. 

Joe wished to ask her about her cousin, Arthur Pendleton; but she scarcely gave him time to ask questions or even to reply. He sat comfortably in the easy chair, and gazed at the slender graceful figure, clad in a soft blue dress. Her skin was clear and white, her cheeks slightly pink, and her features small, daintily rounded, almost doll-like. Her eyes were blue, and bright with smiles.

The picture was pleasing; and he was content to listen while she gracefully laughed her way along like a brook. What a girl Dora was! All who knew her, loved her. It was rare fortune to be her friend. For nothing would she exchange the four years she had lived with Dora. How she envied Joe because he had known her longer. He must not keep that knowledge to himself, but must paint the picture; she knew he could, of Dora's childhood. Were not those large gray eyes even then sweet and shy? How was she dressed? 

Did she wear little checkered frocks, with a yellow braid at back?

She paused; and under the urge of gentle questioning Joe told when he first saw Dora, of how she played with dolls between the great rocks beneath the cherry

tree, and of how she first appeared at school. Elizabeth listened eagerly. He spoke of skating parties, of flower-gathering over hills and dales. When a little flicker of sunlight through the window dazzled his eyes, Elizabeth rose and gently screened the light from his face. He went on, and under the spell of ease and sympathy, spoke of walks to the umbrella tree-but there quickly checked himself without revealing, as he thought, the golden secrets of his heart. He rose. Elizabeth urged him to stay. But he was obdurate. 

Elizabeth walked beside him to the door, and warmly pressed his hand as he turned to go.

He had been gone but a few minutes when she called 

Mrs. Van Alstyne on the telephone. "Mr. Clay, of

Hemlock, is in town and has been here. He says he came on business. Both because of my own love for 

Dora and because I know how you feel about him, I tried to draw him out, and was not entirely successful; but it is evident that he is still infatuated with Dora." "It's strange he came as we are about to sail. You don't suppose Dora has written him that we are to go abroad?" asked Mrs. Van Alstyne earnestly. "I don't know; but from questions he asked, I think he has not communicated with her since they parted at your lodge." "Is he as rough and untutored as when we saw him in the Adirondacks?" "His dress and manners are crude, but I thought his language was good." "Some of those farmers up there read the North American Review through the long winters, but are unacquainted with the commonest conventionalities. I must decide quickly what to do."

265 "You managed affairs rather successfully at the house party," laughed Elizabeth. "Perhaps it might be well to let them meet again and hope-" "No, that impulse soon wore out.
