XXXII 

A few hours after parting from Elizabeth, Joe, with his heart beating tumultously, rang the bell at Mrs. Van 

Alstyne's home, and was admitted by William in butler's dress. Mrs. Van Alstyne soon entered, and greeted Joe with friendly manner. She appeared older and grayer than when Joe last saw her in the Adirondacks. "William says Dora is out?" remarked Joe inquiringly. "Yes, she is spending the evening with a friend." "If she will be back tomorrow, I'd like to take her to the Eden Musee," said Joe, attempting to recall metropolitan attractions recommended by his friends in 

Hemlock-"or to the opera in the evening." "There is no opera in summer," replied Mrs. Van 

Alstyne haughtily; "and I am sure she wouldn't care to go to the Musee. I think she will be glad to see you, if you will call; but she will be very busy, as we sail for Europe next Saturday." "Then I will come back for a few minutes tomorrow evening." "I wish you would remain for a few minutes now. 

Since I am Dora's nearest relative, and her guardian, and have made her my heir, I should like to speak to you in her behalf. I have often thought that my atti- 266

tude when you were my guest in the mountains may have given you a wrong impression. You won't deny my right, I suppose, to discuss Dora's affairs with you, or to ask you some questions-since you have come to my house seeking her company?" "No," said Joe, with a look of surprise. "Dora has told me that when she was a young girl she thought of marrying you. Perhaps the idea of making her your wife has not entirely passed from your mind?" asked Mrs. Van Alstyne, while she regarded Joe critically through small steel-gray eyes. 

Joe was somewhat startled by the directness of the question but he replied with dignity, “Any hope that I have is of little consequence unless it is shared by Dora.” "I beg your pardon," insisted Mrs. Van Alstyne firmly. "What I was seeking was to ascertain your personal aspirations and sentiments-to aid me in determining whether it is fitting for you to cultivate Dora's esteem." "From childhood, as you know, she has been my best friend," answered Joe impulsively, "and my regard for her has increased during recent years rather than grown less.' 

Mrs. Van Alstyne looked straight into Joe's eyes, "I suppose, then, if Dora consented you would marry her." "I would." "May I, then, speak plainly, Mr. Clay, perhaps bluntly? Will you for the sake of your regard for Dora, sacrifice your pride for a few minutes and listen as impersonally as you can, even though at first I seem to be in error, or am unjust?" "I will try to do that," answered Joe.

"Thank you. I would not enter upon what I am attempting had you not shown by success in business that you are possessed of a mature mind and judgment. 

I cannot believe that you would plan to marry my niece because of a mere romantic impulse. No doubt you have considered the matter carefully and have determined exactly in what respects you are fitted to be her husband?

Joe did not at once reply, and Mrs. Van Alstyne waited patiently. At last he spoke. "I admit that I have left it to Dora to estimate my fitness. When she told me she cared for me, I presume I took it for granted that she considered me in certain ways fitted to be her husband." "I am surprised, Mr. Clay, that a person of your ability and character would permit so vital a matter to be determined solely by the impulse of an inexperienced girl. What about your own responsibility? Just because you happened to know my niece at a time when she was in need of friends and sympathy, would you take advantage of her, if you could, and ask her to sacrifice all her ambition, and years of labor and devotion in music to marry a person untrained in her art? What would you do with Dora if you married her-take her to Hemlock?" Mrs. Van Alstyne concluded with almost a sneer. 

Because of the permission he had given Mrs. Van

Alstyne to speak plainly, Joe had determined to listen in patience, however insulting were her insinuations; but the strain was too great. Seeking emotional relief, he avoided the issue, and burst forth in a defense of 

Hemlock. "Why not live in Hemlock? I wouldn't ask for a better place. It has the finest location, with the best farms around it. It has the best views in the

world, and the finest water; and the folks are honest and open-hearted. Perhaps they don't do things like they're done outside, but the Hemlock way may be just as good." "Pardon me-" began Mrs. Van Alstyne, but Joe interrupted her. "Never mind the 'pardon me'; go on and say what you like." "Very well then. I once lived in Hemlock." "Yes, I know all about it."

Mrs. Van Alstyne drew her lips more tightly; but she continued. "I found the people there satisfied with 

Hemlock because they had left their own county but once or twice in their lives. They were content to grow and die like the mulleins in their rocky pastures. Not to mention the physical discomforts of Hemlock homes, what could Dora do among such people? Her social position in New York is secure, and she has intimate friends in the most distinguished families. Socially she so far exceeds the standards of Hemlock that no one in Hemlock even comprehends what her success means. 

The same is true of her proficiency in music. The best performers on the organ call her their equal; and her musical compositions have been played in the churches. 

The source of her highest pleasure is the masters of music who live in the city, or come from abroad. Has 

Hemlock anything to take their place? What about her mind and intellect, adjusted with exquisite care to a thousand interests? What would she do with it, if she went to Hemlock? Would she discard it and lay it aside with her evening dresses and musical scores?" "Mrs. Van Alstyne," said Joe in a voice indicating that he was deeply moved, "I wish to remind you that I came for a brief visit with Dora, not to ask her to

marry me, nor to attempt to take her from you. It may please you to know that, despite your inconsiderate language, I have not missed the point of your argument. 

As Dora is not at home, I will go." "Please be seated, Mr. Clay," said Mrs. Van Alstyne calmly. "Let us think this thing through. I asked you what you would do with Dora if you married her, because I wished you to think out fully just what marrying Dora would mean. And so I should like to repeat my question. Would you take Dora to Hemlock to live?" 99 "I would be just as considerate of Dora's happiness as you or anyone else. And, I admit, you have made me see that I could not ask her to live in Hemlock,' said Joe falteringly, "and would have to take her somewheres else."

A new light filled Mrs. Van Alstyne's eyes. She had obtained the answer she desired; but she hesitated. As she gazed searchingly into Joe's face, the look of triumph left her countenance; and a wistful far-away sympathy took its place. "I must go on," she added, "but I wish I knew a better way. For the sake of

Dora's welfare, I must be brutally, cruelly frank." 

Joe never moved nor spoke; and she continued. "Where else, Mr. Clay, could you live? For what other place are your experience and training suited? 

If I dare utter the words-where else could you live acceptably but in another Hemlock?" 

Joe's face grew livid with anger, but he answered quietly. "Not only are your remarks insulting, Mrs. 

Van Alstyne, but they are illogical. You have been making a pretense of estimating my character; but you are interested only in things that are external, as clothing and city manners. Even in Hemlock they don't

rate people that way. They consider dress and manners as something outside, a sort of garment put over character." "In a sense that is true, Mr. Clay, but anyone who ventures far from home without that garment will look woefully bare. You speak of clothing, for instance, as if it were something purely superficial. If the dress of refined people is so easily slipped on, why not make your own attire correct? There are plenty of men's shops and I presume you do not lack money. 

If you have failed to show good taste in dress, the failure is not due to anything that is external. That garment of conventionality that you speak of, is part of you. If you have missed the opportunity for proper breeding and training, all the effort in the world cannot bring your native courtesy outside of you and turn it into good manners. You may say that refining education and good manners are only a garment; but without this garment, this coat of many colors, you cannot pass acceptably from Hemlock into the outer world-least of all into the world in which Dora Reed is living."

With sympathetic manner and voice Mrs. Van Alstyne continued. "Once, perhaps, when Dora lived in

Hemlock, you were fitted to be her friend and companion; but while you were occupied with your business interests she left you; and passed on, a long way, into a land of music and art and convention that you have never seen. Your interests and Dora's are no longer the same. You cannot now share her thoughts and hopes. She could not be happy with you in Hemlock, nor in New York, nor anywhere else; because the things she values most you do not understand."

Mrs. Van Alstyne ceased speaking. The merciless punishment had come to its end. It had left her victim with brain and will benumbed. Joe felt that somewhere there was a flaw in the argument. Somehow there must be a way out. He longed to retreat until he could weigh each element in the situation; but Mrs. Van 

Alstyne's unwavering eyes restrained him. At last he broke the silence with, "You are right. 

Let me go."

Mrs. Van Alstyne's features relaxed; for a moment her determination almost failed her. "Perhaps I shouldn't have told you this, but-" Her attention was diverted through the open windows to the stopping of a carriage before the door. The bell quickly rang, and through the hall into the parlor walked 

Dora.

There she stood with all her old-time beauty amplified, clad in a white dress, and wearing a broad straw hat adorned with a single flower, looking keen and smiling cordially. "Professor Ziehmlich trains only three or four pupils he chooses from his most talented candidates. Dora's teacher formerly studied with Ziehmlich and has prevailed on the professor to accept Dora."

During the previous ordeal Mrs. Van Alstyne had convinced Joe that Dora was living a life he was not fitted to share. He did not blame Mrs. Van Alstyne for stating that fact; but he did resent the needlessly cruel means she had used to enforce it, and he despised her for the insincerity she showed in suddenly assuming a gracious manner when Dora entered. He was inclined to expose her duplicity. But there sat Dorano longer a slight adolescent girl. She had grown taller and more womanly. Never before had she seemed so beautiful, but in her eyes was a questioning look. 

There was no need to involve Dora in any argument with Mrs. Van Alstyne. He would ignore Mrs. Van 

Alstyne's presence.

He began an account of the house painting, lawn making, and flower gardening undertaken by the young people who were growing up in Hemlock. He had not gone far when William entered, bringing cards. With a smile of satisfaction Mrs. Van Alstyne directed that

THE GREEN-EYED MONSTER KNOWS NO LOGIC 275 

the visitors be shown into the room where she, Dora, and Joe were seated. Dora turned to remonstrate; but thinking better of it, sprang to Joe, seized his hand, and darted into the adjoining library, closing the door after them.

There she drew two chairs under a soft light and turned to Joe with a cordial smile. While he answered questions about Hemlock, he studied her face. 

He had not realized how beautiful she was. Beyond attractiveness of features and complexion, he saw poise and competence-understanding, sympathy. Was it truly sympathy-or was it pity? She had talked to him about Hemlock. Mrs. Van Alstyne had left no doubt about it. Dora had spoken about Hemlock because that was something he could understand. He had missed the advantages that had come to her other friends. 

When she talked with Arthur Pendleton (there on her desk was his portrait) how varied would be her conversation? With other friends she would speak of music and art. Dora knew his limitations, but she was kind to him. He resented her cordiality. It was forced loyalty to an old friend.

She had ceased to speak of Hemlock and was saying, "You must stay and see us sail. I may be gone a long time." "You spoke of returning in the fall?" "Something made me contrary. Aunt provokes me so, I have to rebel. I can't go on always using my time just as she plans it." "I thought she was doing everything she could to help you." 

Her early "I never understood her until lately. marriage interfered with her music. Mr. Van Alstyne kept a large establishment and she had no time for

"Did she talk with you?" inquired Dora eagerly, hoping to find an explanation for the tense looks she had seen as she entered. "Did she say anything about me? What did she say?" "Oh, nothing—nothing of consequence. She showed how eager she is to have you succeed, and what fine progress you're making.' "Always telling people what a wonderful creature 

I am! She embarrasses me dreadfully. It makes me feel sometimes as if I never wanted to study a day more." "I guess you have gone too far to give up your career now." "That's what everybody tells me," she replied. "My teacher says I've reached a stage where I must have instruction I can't get in this country. He has arranged everything. I used to hope I might sometime go abroad and hear concerts in Vienna; but I never dreamed I could study there-and with Ziehmlich, the greatest teacher of them all." 

What would you do with Dora if she would marry you? 

He was roused when Dora spoke again. "Did you come to New York just to see me, Joe?" "I had business for the telephone company. Besides there was something that concerned Sophrona. I hardly know whether I can tell you. It's a hard story to tell." "You needn't tell me, I think I understand."

THE GREEN-EYED MONSTER KNOWS NO LOGIC 277 

"You know how much father and I always thought of Sophrona-and how she grew up with me." "Yes, Joe, I think I understand.' 

"I promised Sophrona I'd hurry back, but I want to look over the Bell exchange before I go."

Dora walked slowly to her desk. She returned with the photograph of Arthur Pentleton. 

"Perhaps this man could help you when you visit the exchange." "Is Pendleton working for the Bell?" "Oh, yes, don't you know?" she answered with animation. "Arthur is one of their most trusted engineers. He says you started him in the profession by speaking so persuasively of telephones at the house party." "I'll try to see him if I can find time." "You must find time." "Is Pendleton still interested in music?" "Arthur's not singing now, but he's a wonderful critic. At a concert the other evening I couldn't help thinking that if his comments were printed in the papers in place of the usual criticism, people would soon learn what good music really is. I could call him now, but it's rather late. I'll telephone the first thing in the morning to let him know the time you plan to call." "It may be as late as four o'clock. I don't think you'd better ask him to make an appointment.' "It won't be the least trouble.” 99 "You know him well and-see him often?" "Arthur will tell you all about that. If he is in town, he will be in his office at 4 P.M."

As they walked through the hall Dora gave up her forced sprightliness. "I won't see you again, Joe.” "I might write," he suggested doubtfully.

"I've missed your letters. I'll send my address as soon as I reach Vienna."

He went up the Avenue to Seventy-second street and turned into the Park. On the farm he had done his best thinking while he plowed. Later it became more convenient to think as he walked. First to his mind came an image of Pendleton, indolently lying under a tree in the adirondacks while Dora read to him; and then Pendleton again, posed smiling and corpulent for the photograph that Dora kept on her desk. Provoked with himself, he tried to banish these thoughts as of no consequence.

What was of consequence, was what could be learned from his interview with Mrs. Van Alstyne. The time had come to weigh her argument. She had hurt his pride. No matter. The question was, did she speak truth? Was she attempting to raise a barrier between him and Dora, or was she merely pointing to a barrier that had long existed-the barrier that had kept them apart when they drove along the swamp road, that had separated them at the house party, that this very evening had prevented a natural flow of affection. 

Was he fitted by training and discipline for Dora's companionship? Pendleton, with perhaps fewer natural gifts, was fitted to be her companion. He understood her art and was capable of sharing the greatest of her pleasures. But why revert to Pendleton? It was illogical. Mrs. Van Alstyne's elaborate efforts to induce him to forego his suit of Dora should be sufficient, he now thought, to convince him that Dora had not pledged her heart to Pendleton-nor to anyone else. He could see that clearly now.

If it were possible that Dora Reed still loved him; and endowed with a thousand attractions had held her

THE GREEN-EYED MONSTER KNOWS NO LOGIC 279 

heart in reserve for him all these years, and yet hesitated to acknowledge her love, it was because she knew that although she might join him in a physical union, they would in the finer aspects of life be separated by the width of a world. While he had been occupied with business, she had learned, as Mrs. Van Alstyne had said, to express her life in symbols he did not understand. 

Mrs. Van Alstyne's hard words, as he reviewed them now, were as kind as caresses. They raised the hope that Dora still loved him; and they not only showed the barrier that lay between him and Dora, butlittle as Mrs. Van Alstyne had intended it—they pointed a way of escape.

A vast new idea, a far-reaching plan, flooded his mind, submerging thoughts of business problems and even of rivals and jealousy in love. His unfitness to be Dora Reed's husband was to Mrs. Van Alstyne a reason for terminating all hope of associating with Dora; to him it was a compelling summons to arise, to leave his old plane of life, and begin a long journey into the enchanted land where Dora Reed dwelt; not necessarily with the expectation of making her his bride, but with the hope of becoming at least a citizen of the world in which she lived. This was the farreaching plan that had come to him so suddenly.

XXXIV 

AN INTERVIEW WITH PRESIDENT HUDSON 

Late in the afternoon, after attending to a variety of matters, among which was a visit to the public library to consult telephone books, Joe entered the Bell Telephone building and asked for Arthur Pendleton. The latter soon appeared looking, Joe thought, disagreeably complaisant and well-fed. He received Joe cordially and led him to his office. He had heard of the excellent service given by the Hemlock Telephone Company and of its expansion; and he had guessed that 

Joe had had a prominent part in carrying out the up-to-date ideas that were known to be characteristic of the system. He induced Joe to show on a map the location of the lines; and then to give an account of his contest with the W. and E. including even his recent struggle with Richards. Pendleton became so enthusiastic over the outcome of the story that he insisted on taking Joe to the president of the Bell company, who at that time was maintaining an office in 

New York City. 

While they awaited their interview Arthur gave Joe an account of the president's personality. John Elbridge Hudson was a very unusual sort of person to be placed at the head of one of the largest business corporations in America. He was a man of gracious 280

AN INTERVIEW WITH PRESIDENT HUDSON 281 

presence and profound learning. His tastes inclined him to the collection of rare books and engravings and to explorations into various domains of scholarship. 

He had been a professor of Greek at Harvard, but subsequently had turned to law. In the latter profession he had won a wide reputation as a corporation lawyer. One organization after another had come under his direction, and finally he had been called to the presidency of the great Bell system. His administration had been most successful. Not only had there been a remarkable increase in the territory covered and the miles of wire in use, but the service had been immeasureably improved.

When they were ushered into the great man's presence, Arthur introduced Joe as the person who had turned his attention to telephones. He outlined Joe's contest with the W. and E. and spoke of the Hemlock company's long-continued cooperation with the Bell. 

President Hudson said that the affairs of the Hemlock 

Telephone Company had some six months previously been brought to his attention in Boston when Mr. 

Wade's proposition for the sale of the Clifton lines had been discussed. He was glad to meet the man who had been instrumental in establishing so high a standard of service as had been maintained over the rural lines that centered in Hemlock. After he had asked 

Joe several questions concerning both his struggle with the W. and E. and the present policy of the Hemlock company, he offered to extend to the latter organization the terms formerly given Mr. Wade, including the taking over of the Hawley route. He paused and reached for his pad, and made some notes in curious characters; and then inquired concerning Joe's plans for the future, and remarked that the Bell Telephone Company

had plenty of work that could be done by men of Joe's ability. 

While Arthur smiled with satisfaction because of the president's words, Joe answered that he could not at the present time accept a position for he was about to go to college. "You will never regret that decision," said President Hudson. “And I hope furthermore that you plan to attend a first-class institution." 

Arthur had gazed in astonishment when Joe announced his intention of going to college; but he quickly recovered himself and partly because of his enthusiasm for his alma mater and partly in the hope of suggesting to Joe an acceptable institution, he exclaimed, "We mustn't let Mr. Clay go anywhere except to Tech, President Hudson; we must induce Mr. Clay to go to Tech." "I haven't decided on the college," John answered with some hesitation, "but I am planning to take a classical course." "An excellent idea, an excellent idea," remarked 

Hudson with enthusiasm. "At the present time the

Bell has less need for employees who can deal with materials than for those who can deal with men. When you complete your course," added rising, "come to us, either here or in Boston, and we will find you something to do." 

They had scarcely left the president's office when Arthur questioned Joe about his decision to go to college. "Yes," said Joe, "I came to that conclusion last night and I shall enter next fall." "How under the sun did you happen to tell President Hudson that you are going to take a classical course? You couldn't have thought of anything that would please him better. When you said that, it al-

AN INTERVIEW WITH PRESIDENT HUDSON 283 

most took my breath away, for I had been watching him make his notes on that pad in Greek." "I don't know anything about Mr. Hudson's ideas regarding colleges," said Joe, "but I have come to the conclusion that what I need is a classical education." "Nonsense," replied Arthur, "don't you let Hudson nor anybody else talk you into studying the dead languages. All the other big men in the company say 

Hudson's a crank when he talks about Greek. What a telephone man needs is a scientific education. The reason Hudson's brought so many companies into the Bell is because he insists on a square deal and good service. His classical training hasn't had anything to do with it." "No," said Joe, "I don't think I shall go to Tech." "Of course you'll go to Tech," said Arthur, smiling and looking at his watch, "but now you're going to dinner with me, and there we'll have time to talk it over. Just a minute while I sign these letters."

While Joe waited, his mind turned gloomily to Arthur's relations to Dora, and he pondered as to what question he might ask Arthur to induce him to tell, as 

Dora said, "all about that." Arthur soon rose from his desk and they went to the street and took a car. 

All the way uptown, as his car was too noisy for conversation, Joe's thoughts were occupied with the foregoing theme. From the car they walked to a house in a street not far from the Park. 

Arthur put his key into the lock and opened the door. As Joe entered, Fanny Wheeler stepped from the parlor. She extended her hand cordially, but before she could speak, Arthur kissed her and said, "My wife-Mr. Clay."

Joe took Fanny's hand, and seemed to be overcome with surprise, for he exclaimed mechanically, "Fanny 

Wheeler, Fanny Wheeler." "Arthur and I have often wished we might welcome you here," said Fanny, her face aglow with smiles. "I never even dreamed of seeing you here," replied

Joe with glistening eyes, "but that makes me all the happier to be here now." "You never dreamed, I suppose, that Fanny Wheeler would ever be subdued by a fellow of my calibre," laughed Arthur, as he gazed with the pride of a young husband at Fanny, dear Fanny Wheeler, who stood before them comely and tall, and just as strong and red-cheeked as when Joe last saw her in the mountains.

In a few minutes the three sat down to dinner; and 

Joe found that Fanny Wheeler managed dinners as she drove barges-gracefully, easily, without hairbreadth escapes or hint of catastrophes. Joe thought he had never tasted better food, or had been with more cheerful hosts. He felt that after long absence he was with old friends. 

And Fanny said that if she had known that Joe was to be in town she would have asked Dora to come to the dinner, too. Then Fanny told Joe how Dora had been honored at her graduation, of the praise she had received after a concert in the city, and how lovers of music sought her company. All the praise and honors had not turned Dora's head; she was still the sweet and simple girl that Fanny Wheeler, then a senior, had found one autumn day on the rocky hill near Houghton. 

It was too bad that Dora thought it necessary to go abroad to study. How her friends would miss her.

When the dinner was over and Joe was visiting with Arthur in his den, Fanny came with her sewing and

AN INTERVIEW WITH PRESIDENT HUDSON 285 

sat near. "Fanny," said Arthur, "you are just in time, for I want you to help me convince Clay that he ought to go to Tech. I think he is biased in his judgment because he was lucky enough to tell President Hudson that he expects to attend a classical school. Of course the president, who is famous for making his memoranda in Greek, couldn't resist that idea; and he offered him a job at once." "I think he ought to remember that the Bell's most promising young engineer came from Tech," laughed

Fanny. 

"I'd go to Tech in a minute if I wanted telephone engineering," replied Joe, "but I don't believe Tech gives the kind of training I need." "You astonish me," exclaimed Arthur. "Are you going to devote yourself to the dead languages and give up everything you've learned from a successful business career? In the Adirondacks you inspired me with your ideals of telephone service. Are you going to forget all that and bury yourself in Latin and Greek?" "No," said Joe, "I don't intend to forget any ideals; but for the present I've decided to give up business and try to get the kind of education that fits anybody to get along with educated people.” "I think it's an antiquated idea," said Arthur, "that a person has to study the classics to get a liberal education. I haven't a doubt that the study of the sciences will give just as satisfactory training as can be got from Latin, and besides will furnish knowledge valuable to possess. You don't have to give up the finer things when you study science. A person can't think deeply even about the nature of a telephone transmitter without considering some of the greatest truths in the universe."

"I feel as you do about the value of science, but I shouldn't like to be an expert on the telephone transmitter and fail to understand the folks around me. I don't want to have to avoid talking with educated people because I don't know about painting, and can't appreciate music, and haven't read the great authors.

I haven't the courage to go through life handicapped that way."

As Joe's voice grew earnest, Fanny dropped her sewing, and read from his face the part of the story that he had not put into words. "Oh, Mr. Clay," she exclaimed, "I think those forms of art you speak of are merely the means that some people use to express their thoughts. They could be expressed in other ways.

In heroism there is something superior to any conception of a painter. Friendship and understanding, I think, can be based on things deeper than either education or employment." "Perhaps you are right, but many people hold the contrary opinion," said Joe gloomily, "and I don't dare experiment. I shall go to an academic college, for I do not wish to take a chance."

Joe's feeling was so evident and his tones so final that Arthur thought it best to leave the subject. "Well," he said, "then I'll try to be thankful because you are not planning to leave the telephone business altogether." "Of course a person can't tell so long ahead, but 

I shouldn't wonder if after I get through college I might do as President Hudson suggested and come back here and ask Bell for a job." "Arthur and I shall look forward to that," replied

Fanny, "for we would enjoy having you near us; and

AN INTERVIEW WITH PResident HudSON 

287 perhaps Dora by that time will be back from abroad, and then we can all be together." "That's a fine prospect," said Joe, with a faint smile, "and I've had a fine visit here this evening."

After they had exchanged other pleasant hopes and good wishes, Joe said good-by to Fanny and Arthur, and went to the Grand Union Hotel. Before he took his train, he walked around the corner to a book shop, and purchased a beginner's book in Greek and an old copy of The Iliad.

XXXV 

On the eleventh of September, 1895, Joseph Clay stepped from a railway train in the village of Franklin. 

He was surprised to find a pretty little station set in a green lawn adorned with flowers. From the baggagemaster he obtained directions for finding the home of the president of the college and was soon on his way uptown. He passed boarding-houses and fraternity houses now almost deserted but soon to be the scene of great acivity. In the distance he saw at the top of the hill the college chapel surmounted with a square tower. North College stood at the left, and on the right was South College, the first structures in the row of college buildings that extended in each direction along the crest of the hill.

From College Street Joe followed the broad central path that, accommodating itself to the terraces with landings and flights of steps, leads up the steep slope of the campus to the chapel. He paused to admire the large elms and oaks that grew on the narrow strip of level ground before the college buildings. He had a glimpse of a great green lawn behind the chapel. But he turned square about and, facing down the hill, beheld again the steep village street up which he had climbed; and through the branches of the trees and over their tops he caught here and there a vision of 288

blue hills that led the eye on into the misty distance. 

For a moment he longed to climb to the top of the college tower to obtain an unimpeded view over the campus trees; but resisting the impulse, he followed the concrete walk along the brow of the hill before the college buildings south to the street that borders the side of the campus. There, he had been told, in the second house the president dwelt.

Both house and grounds were ample and attractive. 

On a bench not far from the entrance sat two tall, fairhaired girls reading books. On one of the side porches another young lady was busily writing at a small table. 

Joe approached the door and through the open window at his left saw two other maidens. One, a mere girl, was leaning with mischievous manner over the back of a chair in which the other was seated. The two were clearly sisters for their features were similar. 

Beyond all question their noses, straight and regular as saw teeth, were cast in the same mould. In general appearance, nevertheless, the two girls were strongly contrasted, for while the younger was fair-haired, blueeyed, plump, pink, and fresh, the other was thin, sallow, sombre, and mature. When Joe touched the bell, the elder sister laid aside her sewing and with a pleasant smile came to the door. After offering him a seat, she crossed the hall to an open study, "Father, a young man wishes to see you."

Soon at the door appeared a pompous-looking person of heavy build whose massive head was covered with clustering dark hair somewhat inclined toward gray. In his straight thin nose Joe saw the prototype that the features of his daughters had copied so faithfully.

President Dorchester greeted Joe cordially and invited him into his study. "And what is your name?" he inquired. "Clay. Joseph Clay of Hemlock, sir." "Oh, I am glad to see you. I was in doubt whether you were coming." "I began Greek only this summer and I do not know whether I can pass the examination." "At least you can take the Latin-Scientific course.

Mr. Andrews's letter and certificate permits us to enter you without conditions for the Latin-Scientific course." "I want to take the classical course.

Isn't it possible for me to enter for the classical course by doing more work out of school hours?" inquired Joe rather timorously.

President Dorchester had had much experience in answering the eloquent appeals of young men who wished to substitute science for the study of one of the ancient languages. It was seldom, however, that anyone qualified for college entrance had appeared before him to plead for permission to undertake the study of Greek. The president was delighted. His face grew warm with smiles. "We will try to take care of you, young man," he said, laying his hand on Joe's shoulder. "We will give you credit for whatever Greek you can pass at the examinations next Tuesday and Wednesday; and we will provide a way by which somehow you can obtain what you lack and you will be admitted as a candidate for the degree of bachelor of arts at least temporarily."

Joe arose and President Dorchester followed him into the hall and then led him to the parlor, "I want you to meet my daughter. Margaret," he said, turn-

ing toward the girl who had opened the door for Joe, "this is Mr. Clay."

For a few minutes Margaret's light and easy talk held Joe's and her father's attention. Meanwhile from the other side of the room the younger sister, a girl of about thirteen, came and stole behind her father, and took his hand, and insistently made known her presence. President Dorchester smiled, "Mr. Clay," he said, "this is Lucy, my youngest daughter."

Then requesting Joe to call for further advice as soon as he had learned the results of his examinations, the president retired to his study. For a few minutes

Joe conversed with the two sisters; and when he rose to leave, he received from Margaret an invitation to call. The girl who had been writing on the porch entered, as he was about to leave the parlor, and he learned that her name was Laura. She was vivacious and playful, and Joe thought he would like to see more of her. She, too, had a Dorchester nose. This mark of family relationship he also noticed, as he walked back to the street, in the faces of the two girls who were reading on the lawn. As he left the president's house, he concluded that Dr. Dorchester was the father of at least five attractive daughters.

Joe went down the other side of the hill and found the business center of Franklin. For some time he studied the windows of the clothing stores and tailor shops, and finally entered the door of the establishment that seemed to him to have the most tasteful appearance. He was met by a person of dignified bearing, who in reply to his inquiry announced that he was the proprietor. Joe said that he expected to live in Franklin for four years, that he wanted an outfit of clothing at once, and should probably want clothes in the future.

He preferred attire that would make him inconspicu- He said he had lived in a distant region and was not acquainted with the sort of clothing that was suited to Franklin. He needed advice as well as clothes, and was willing to pay for both.

The tailor received Joe's proposal sympathetically. 

He described in detail the kind of clothing Joe would need and even offered to assume responsibility for the purchase of haberdashery. Before Joe left the shop he had been measured for two or three suits, one of which was to be ready within three days.

Somewhat removed from the business center Joe found a little hotel where he obtained a comfortable room in which to sequester himself while preparing for the examinations. From time to time during the next few days, when weary with study, he issued from his retreat seeking to gather such information as he could concerning the desirability of dormitories, fraternity houses, and private homes as places of residence. From the college library he obtained books on college life and social etiquette and determined, when he could find leisure, to study them industriously. In the late afternoons he explored the campus, examined each building, and read every inscription, including that on the large elm near the chapel, which recounts how Isaiah 

Pritchford, missionary to the Indians, planted the tree in 1747 beside the log church in which he carried on his pastoral labors for more than twenty years. 

On Saturday afternoon Joe rode his bicycle to the city of Pomeroy, and continued on into the country for five or six miles to Houghton. There he strolled about the campus and the quiet streets of the elm-shaded old town where Dora had lived for four years. He climbed the rocky hill where she met Fanny Wheeler and there

found Cornus canadensis and Spiranthes gracilis growing on the slope just as Dora had described them. 

In the evening on his return to Franklin he took his bath with more than usual aggressiveness. He then dressed throughout in the new clothing which the tailor had provided; and placing his old apparel, to the last stitch, including even his hat and shoes, in his old Hemlock bag, he stole down a side street-feeling, he thought, very like Miss Stevens' milk-snake dressed in a new skin-to the Salvation Army barracks, where he left the entire outfit of clothing that he had worn when he came to Franklin.

On Sunday he attended church in the village and saw 

President Dorchester seated with his tall and slender wife and his five demure daughters. Tuesday he tried his examinations in Greek; and Wednesday he took a room in the dormitory known as South College, and in the evening went for dinner to a students' boardinghouse which had begun to serve meals, for college was about to open.

At the boarding house old students entered from time to time and were greeted noisily by their friends. 

Strange college nicknames were shouted back and forth.

Vacation experiences were told in single sentences; and the college news, concerning students who were not to return or changes in the faculty, was recounted hastily to each newcomer. Good natured jibes flew about and continually brought forth peals of laughter. Except for temporary outbursts the sophomores were more reserved than the juniors, for their attention was centered on the freshman tables, and they seemed to be discussing the appearance of each of their traditional victims. 

Among the sophomores the most active was a stocky, smug-looking fellow who answered to the name of

"Riley" Waters. He kept his eyes continually on the freshmen and from time to time made notes in a small memorandum book that he carried.

Two tables at this boarding-house had been allotted to new students but thus far only one had been filled. 

Across from Joe sat a fat moon-faced freshman who loquaciously introduced himself to all at the table as 

George Montgomery. He urged all freshmen to make use of every opportunity to become acquainted. He took it upon himself to ask each newcomer his name which he ostentatiously announced to the table. At 

Joe's right sat a tall genteel-looking boy named Walter 

Gould, who though utterly different from Montgomery seemed as eager as he to make acquaintances. He had an exuberant handshake. He was delighted to meet every freshman and he hoped that very soon he would come to know each one well.

The last to enter was placed by the proprietor at 

Joe's left. The boy was a dark-haired little fellow named Dickley. He had a pug nose and very bright eyes. He evidently had lived in the country and had seldom left home, for when asked by the waiter whether his dinner order had been taken, he said he did not know as he had just come that day. Instantly comprehending his error, the boy showed so much embarrassment that Joe leaned over and sought to relieve his discomfiture by questioning him concernng the electives he expected to choose.

The waiter soon brought Dickley's order, and finding a moment of leisure began to converse with Montgomery, the fat freshman who sat opposite Joe. The waiter said he was a junior and he hated to have the freshmen run over too easily by the sophomores. He said so large an entering class was expected that they

could have everything their way, if only they were properly organized. If they were not properly organized, every one of them would be painted by the sophomores, or would be ducked at the college well, or might even be kidnapped and made to give Jim shows. 

Joe was about to inquire into the nature of Jim shows when the waiter's duties took him again to the kitchen. "Riley" Waters and a sophomore known as Wes Martin passed by on their way out and commented audibly on the unpromising appearance of the freshmen. As Joe was about to leave the dining-room he was met at the door by a well-built, keen-looking fellow who had strolled over from one of the upper classmen tables. He introduced himself courteously as Burke, a junior, and asked Joe whether he was entering as a freshman or was to come in with advanced standing. He chatted pleasantly about improvements at the college and said that if '99 had two or three big fellows like Joe that they would be able to make it hot for the sophomores. He asked Joe whether he played football, and whether he was pledged to a fraternity; and he remarked that Joe would be at a great disadvantage during protection week if he had no upper class fraternity brothers to escort him across the campus. After taking Joe's name and address he gave Joe his card, and said that if he could serve him in any way he could be found at the Alpha Beta Gamma house.

Burke had scarcely departed when the proprietor of the boarding-house rushed excitedly to Joe and inquired whether he knew to whom he had been speaking. He had been honored by a conversation with Chuck Burke, the captain of the football team, the greatest halfback in any eastern college, and the most popular man in

Franklin. The boarding-house keeper said encouragingly that if Joe could get the protection of Chuck Burke he could probably get through the dangerous first week of the term without any bad results.

The next day the freshman class assembled at the call of the dean. Montgomery, Walter Gould, and one or two others who had attended large preparatory schools, came early and passed to the front of the room and talked noisily; but most of the new students went quietly to their seats and twisted their necks in an attempt to satisfy their curiosity concerning the physical appearance of their class which then for the first time. was taking form and being. Joe was surprised at the boyish appearance of many of his classmates, and they in turn were temporarily awed by his maturity and size.

The dean began his address by making announcements concerning the payment of college bills and changes in office hours and the program of recitations. 

He then spoke somewhat eloquently concerning the object for which young men are supposed to come to college. He said that students at Franklin would learn how to secure information of all sorts easily and quickly. They would have the opportunity to enrich their emotional and intellectual life by gaining a knowledge of books and the power to read them appreciatively and with imagination. Through a better understanding of the great personalities of history and literature they would gain higher ideals of life, conduct, and civic duty; and thus through a knowledge of human nature, widened in many ways and by many means, they would attain to a more dynamic and fruitful understanding of their neighbors and themselves.

Possibly Joe was the only one in the room who listened thoughtfully to the dean's statement; on Joe's mind it made a deep impression. He took out his notebook and recorded the dean's words as well as he could. 

If he had been uncertain hitherto concerning the wisdom of his choice of Franklin instead of Tech, the dean's remarks swept away every vestige of doubt.

The hope of gaining the power to see emotional and intellectual objects with a more penetrating insight, such as would fit him for the companionship of Dora and her friends, had caused him to turn his back on business interests and come to Franklin. He was now overjoyed. The dean's statement of the aims of the course of study showed conclusively that he had come to the right place.

The Y.M.C.A. secretary folowed the dean, and after inviting the freshmen to make use of the privileges afforded by his organization gave his hearers some kindly advice about college customs. He suggested that it would be well for all freshmen unaccompanied by upper classmen to remain at home the coming evening, or if they ventured out at least to wear old clothes. 

The sophomores would be running around with cans of green paint and would probably daub such freshmen as they could capture. He also said that the first six days following the coming Sunday was known as protection week. During that time freshmen unaccompanied by upper classmen ought to move around only in considerable groups, for if the sophomores could capture any of the freshmen, they might sequester them and compel them on Saturday night to take part in a foolish entertainment called Jim shows.

As soon as the Y.M.C.A. secretary left the room, Walter Gould, who had been shaking hands even more

industriously than he had at the boarding-house, was nominated for president of the class and was elected almost unanimously. George Montgomery then arose and was vociferously applauded. Something about 

Montgomery's manner or appearance stirred the freshman sense of humor and he was greeted with a great variety of cat-calls and epithets. The constraint produced during the last few hours by new surroundings and unfamiliar customs had been so ill-endured that Montgomery's attempt to speak was made the occasion for a sudden return to prep school noisiness and horseplay. Montgomery, although young in years, was evidently old and experienced in schoolboy chaffing in all its aspects. He replied to his classmates' gibes with a readiness and vigor that for the time being served well in place of wit. After a few minutes the racket began to die down, and Montgomery, having by common consent been christened "Mickey," was permitted to continue. He said he had been informed it was a college custom that the freshmen each fall should rake the campus. He would like to know what the class was going to do about it. If the work was to be undertaken, arrangements ought to be made as far as possible in advance.

There was no return to nonsense at the conclusion of Montgomery's remarks for it seemed to be the common opinion that the matter of which he spoke deserved serious attention. As the president waited in vain for some one to volunteer information, he called on several freshmen to give advice or express their opinions.

When Joe was asked to speak he said that the campus was so large that the class probably would not attempt to rake it more than once and therefore it ought to be raked after practically all the leaves had fallen, That

implied a date so far ahead that there was no need for haste. There was ample time for a committee to look into the matter and learn whether the college provided the tools, what was to be done with the debris, and so forth. All freshmen undoubtedly were agreed that if '99 was to rake the campus, the job must be done in such a way as to bring honor to the class.

Although the boys listened attentively while Joe was speaking they burst once more into riotous applause as soon as he had concluded. Their expressions of approval were so varied in character and great in volume that Joe was a little fearful that the demonstration was not intended to be entirely complimentary. 

"Make Daddy Clay the chairman! Make Pop Clay the chairman!" came from all quarters of the room. 

Joe was not surprised that his classmates should address him with epithets that reflected his superior maturity and age, but he could not understand why mingled with these terms was an occasional reference to "Farmer Clay." Surely the disagreeable implication clinging to the latter name was not the result of any failure to conform to the prevailing styles in dress; for when the tailor had finally turned over to him his somewhat elaborate outfit of clothing, Joe had been assured that during the coming term no man in Franklin would be better dressed. What was it then that marked him?

Was it peculiarity of manner, gait, or tone of voice? 

Or was it an indefinable intangible something of which Mrs. Van Alstyne had had cognizance and from which it was not possible through any effort on his part to escape? While these thoughts were passing through his mind, the president had appointed Clay, Dickley, and Montgomery a committee on raking the campus. 

The meeting was then adjourned.

Joe was late at dinner that evening. During the. afternoon he had attended his first recitations and had returned to his room and had tarried so long at the lessons that had been assigned that he was the last freshman to come to dinner. The sophomore table was absolutely vacant. As Burke was going out he stopped to speak to Joe. He said there was considerable risk in crossing the campus after seven o'clock on paint night. A freshman ought not to do it wearing such clothes as Joe had on. 

He offered to accompany Joe to his room in the dormitory, but Joe declined to receive his aid. The distance was short Joe said, and there probably would not be a crowd at so early an hour, and he felt quite competent to defend himself against stragglers.

When Joe stepped from the porch, the night seemed as impenetrable as the traditional darkness of Egypt.

He walked rapidly along the street until he approached the lower edge of the campus. There he thought he saw at the landing on the first terrace, dimly revealed by a neighboring electric light, two or three figures that skulked in and out among the shadows. He turned and darted into the narrow footpath that winds among the evergreens diagonally up the hill. Over the soft pine needles he hastened without a sound. In a little glade, however, where barely a beam of light penetrated the evergreens, his sprinting came to a sudden end. He fell headlong, and a dozen or more men piled on top of him. 

When he rose, out of breath, three or four husky fellows held each of his arms, and others lighted matches in an effort to determine whom they had captured. Among those who peered into his face Joe recognized the countenance of Riley Waters. The latter gave one look and

exclaimed, "The gods are kind to us. freshman called Clay."

Joe recognized Burke stood near awaiting the fun.

Disparaging criticism of Farmer Clay was shouted; and

"Bring on the paint!" they shouted to Riley Waters. "Paint on his skin. Don't spoil that good suit," a senior suggested.

Joe was permitted to remove his coat and hand it to Burke. "He can have his choice," announced Riley graciously. "We will paint pretty green stripes around his trousers, or he can take them off, and we'll paint his legs, just as he prefers."

One of the sophomores who had been holding Joe's right arm had been amusing himself by twisting Joe's wrist a little now and then, in the hope of making him docile. "Come on," he called, "which will you choose?" and to hurry the decision gave Joe's arm a vigorous twist.

Instantly Joe wrenched himself loose from all who were holding him, and seized the sophomore who had twisted his arm, and threw him to the ground, and was bending over him about to speak advice, when Wes.

Martin dashed around with his nail protruding slat and gave to Joe's rear a resounding thwack. Joe left the

prostrate sophomore and pursuing Wes Martin knocked him down with a single blow. Then he caught up with another sophomore and knocked him down. 

Shades of Fegley's blacksmith shop! In a moment Joe stood alone under the light with the sophomores in a circle at a respectful distance shouting, "Slugger! Rough-neck!"

What might have been the outcome, had there not been an interruption, is uncertain. It is a matter that in the light of Joe's subsequent football record has often been discussed by Franklin undergraduates and alumni. What did happen, is that a short, energetic old man stepped from the shadows into the light and shouted, "Disperse! Go to your rooms! Get you hence, you miserable puppies; get you hence!"

The effect was magical. "It's Swampy; it's 

Swampy," was whispered on every side, and the sophomores scattered like sheep. Burke, who was carrying 

Joe's coat, was bold enough to approach the little old man, whom he addressed as Professor Marsh. The professor immediately rated Burke for appearing in bad company. Burke, however, displayed Joe's coat which he had guarded, and explained that while he did not think it was his duty to interfere with the observance of long-established college customs, he had used his influence to make the ceremonies as little objectionable as possible. The professor seemed to give slight heed to Burke's remarks, and began to read him a lecture, in which he unfavorably compared Burke's conduct with that of his father and uncle who had attended Franklin twenty-five years before.

As soon as Professor Marsh had departed, Burke insisted on accompanying Joe to his room.

"I don't need your protection," responded Joe. "Swampy has made that unnecessary," observed 

Burke, "but I have something to say to you."

When their conversation was resumed in the dormitory, Burke commented on the ease with which Joe had disposed of his sophomore adversaries.

Joe replied, "I want to have as little as possible to do with class fights and rowing. I think such nonsense is better suited to boys in a prep school." "There isn't much hazing after protection week.

After that everybody is interested in athletics. You certainly could be a champion in boxing." 

Joe was provoked. "I didn't come to Franklin to learn to box." "In a college atmosphere there's a good deal that can be learned from boxing," commented Burke sug gestively. 

"Yes, I heard them call me rough-neck and slugger," retorted Joe, "but I didn't hear them say anything about the fellow who twisted my arm, or Wes Martin who slapped me with a board with a nail in it.” "Wes Martin has a cowardly disposition, and is not a typical Franklin man," explained Burke. "The others I guess didn't hurt you much. You were able to use your arm to pretty good advantage a few seconds after it was twisted." "I lost my temper," admitted Joe. "I had to leave a good business to come to college, and I suppose I hate to waste my time in horseplay." "Would you mind telling your purpose in coming to 

Franklin?" inquired Burke courteously. 

Joe started to express his convictions concerning the purposes of education, but finding his words less imagi-

native than those used by the dean, he produced his notebook and quoted from the dean's statement. "Most of that could be included under scholarship," suggested Burke. "Yes," Joe admitted somewhat hastily. "I came to Franklin for fours years of college life,' said Burke, "not for study, or society, or athletics such as I could find in Worcester where I lived, but for the sort that exists here." "Our purposes are very similar," exclaimed Joe with a smile of satisfaction. "I came to find life different from that in Hemlock." "No," said Burke, “I think our purposes are decidedly unlike. You came to fatten yourself on the course of study. I came to win popularity-which I consider better evidence of success in college than grades." "I consider popularity a low ideal,” said Joe. "A person who takes college life as he finds it, and conforms to its customs, is bound to be popular," responded Burke, looking at his watch. "But we'll talk more about that another time. The follows at the 

Alpha Beta Gamma house have a little social gathering this evening and they asked me to bring you over."

At the chapter house Joe found an interesting group of students and spent the evening pleasantly. While he was not actually asked to join the fraternity, it seemed to be understood that an invitation would be forthcoming. He talked with two or three members of the football squad, and he promised, when Burke left him at South College, to come out for football practice the next afternoon.

When alone in his room Joe's mind reverted to the paradox that had been uttered by his new friend, “popularity is a better test than grades of success in a college

career." Was it possible to reconcile that statement with his reasons for coming to college, or with the aims presented by the dean? He had come to Franklin in the hope of fitting himself for acceptable association with refined people. "Acceptable association." Did not that imply a certain sort of popularity? After all perhaps the two aims were not entirely different, but his was further reaching than Burke's. He was looking forward to life after college; and college, Joe thought, is only preparation for life.

The next day while crossing the campus Dickley asked Joe whether he had made any arrangement for purchasing rakes, and suggested that they consult a group of seniors who were then approaching, for he thought the matter ought not to be neglected too long. 

The seniors said that rakes could not be secured too promptly. They pointed to the leaves that were already changing color. If the freshmen did not look out some would be caught. There never were rakes enough to go around. If the sophomores captured any freshman during protection week who had not provided himself with a rake, it certainly would go hard with the poor fellow. 

Joe said they had given the information to the right people for he and Dickley were members of the committee that had been appointed to superintend the raking of the campus. Later in the afternoon he decided that it might be just as well to buy himself a rake at once, although the actual raking could best be done much later. Accordingly, when he went to town in the evening to get his mail, he purchased a large and strong rake at the hardware store next to the post office.

The only letter he received was from the registrar. 

It informed him that he had passed an examination in Xenophon and had failed in Greek prose composition 306

and in The Iliad. In accordance with the directions he had previously received, he went the next morning to report the results to the president.

The seniors were right, he thought, as he walked across the campus; the season was advancing very rapidly. The lower leaves, he observed, had fallen from the ivy on the chapel and the foliage on the upper branches was becoming yellow and crimson. The dogwood no longer green was thickly studded with ripe berries. Chickadees along the southern slope explored the bark of scraggly limbs and whistled sweetly from tree to tree. Near the south campus wall sumacs were scarlet and squirrels raced through their fallen leaves. 

As Joe turned down Faculty Street to make his report he caught a vision of a boudoir cap at one of President Dorchester's upper windows; and busily gardening at the border before the house were the tallest fair-haired daughter and the little sister, Lucy. Lucy rose with a pleased smile of recognition and talked with Joe a few moments wth dignified mien before she vouchsafed to introduce him to her sister whom she called Winifred. With Winifred, a sophomore at 

Houghton, Joe discussed gardening, and spoke of the fall that was coming on, and said that as chairman of the committee to rake the campus, he would soon be busy. Winifred's eyes twinkled as she asked about the plans of his committee and sympathetically inquired concerning his experiences on paint night. Joe thought she seemed to be much interested in college affairs and student life.

With some regret Joe left her and went on to transact his business with the president. He was met at the door by Margaret who said that her father was engaged with one of the professors, but that she would

find out whether the delay would be too long to make it worth while to wait. President Dorchester returned for a moment with his daughter, and upon learning the result of Joe's examinations, said that it would be necesary for Joe to take an approved tutor in Greek composition, but that the examination in The Iliad could be taken without additional instruction the following spring at the conclusion of the freshman course in The Odessey.

make fun of you. Freshmen never rake the campus. 

Selling rakes is only a sophomore trick." 

Joe's face turned scarlet. He bowed his thanks and went at once to Gould's room, and it was there arranged to call a class meeting at the conclusion of the five o'clock recitations. When the class had assembled 

Gould, now nicknamed Percival because of his elegant ways, announced that the chairman of the committee on raking the campus was ready to make a report.

When Joe rose he was again greeted vociferously; but on this occasion there were fewer cat-calls, for his classmates were jubilant over the outcome of his paint night struggle with the sophomores.

He said that the committee had found a way by which the freshmen could play a joke on their enemies. 

Selling rakes was devised by the sophomores to trick freshmen, but the trick could be turned against its makers. The sophomores themselves could be induced to buy rakes in the hope of selling them to the freshmen. Rakes already purchased by the freshmen were, therefore, to be put on sale at low prices by juniors. 

Freshmen were to advertise for rakes, and talk continualy about getting rakes, but for one reason or another were to postpone purchase. If every freshman would do as directed and would keep the secret, the committee thought that almost every sophomore could be induced to buy a rake for the second time in his college career. 

The next day the committee announced on various bulletin boards that as there seemed to be a scarcity of rakes they would be glad to communicate with any persons who had such tools for sale. A large number of smiling sophomores responded promptly to the advertisement and offered to sell the freshmen rakes. On

Monday of protection week the climax came. The town was deluged with posters printed in red ink which announced in large letters that the following was a list of names of trusty sophomores who had purchased rakes and had written the committee that they had them for sale-undoubtedly at reduced prices. After the list of names the notice was signed by Joseph Clay, Harry 

Dickley, and George Montgomery, committee.

The sophomores were so chagrined that they did not even attempt to conceal their anger. They swore that they would not let protection week come to an end without capturing and exhibiting at a Jim show the three members of the committee that had tricked them.

As Joe had accepted an invitation to become a member of Alpha Beta Gamma, Chuck Burke made arrangements to have Joe accompanied by a fraternity upper classman on each occasion that he went from his room during the week. Mickey Montgomery was guarded by the Chi Psi Omegas. Little Dickley was not pledged to any fraternity, and while Joe and some of his classmates debated what was best to be done, he mysteriously disappeared. As he was returning to the dormitory late one afternoon after mowing the dean's lawn, he was suddenly snatched away.

Posters printed in green ink soon appeared announcing that on Saturday evening of protection week at Tige O'Shea's barn a Jim show would be given in which Freshman Dickley would be the principal actor and “a well-known sophomore" would be choragus. 

The next day following a lecture the freshman class met secretly. Whatever plans were made were not divulged. A nonchalant attitude was asumed by all. 

On Saturday evening about nine o'clock at Tige 

O'Shea's barn Riley Waters stepped upon the platform

NEW STYLES FOR HOME-SPUN GOODS 311 

made of a barn door supported by barrels, and under the glare of temporary electric lights presented Dickley to the audience. Juniors and seniors to the number of forty or fifty and the greater part of the sophomore class were present. Riley had planned a rather long and elaborate introduction to the ceremonies, but his speech was never completed.

The large doors at opposite sides of the barn were suddenly thrown back and the freshman class came in with a rush. Joe and Mickey Montgomery seized Riley

Waters, and another group pulled the staple of the granary door and held it open while their classmates, who greatly outnumbered their opponents, hustled the most prominent of the sophomores into the gloomy jail. 

In a few minutes the tumult died down; and, while most of the freshmen were seated on prostrate sophomores, Percival Gould stood on a barrel and announced that due to unforeseen difficulties, the Riley Waters Amusement Company had been compelled to depart for a new stand. But carriages were waiting to take the freshmen and their friends among the upper classmen to the new destination; and that there, under more favorable auspices, the show would continue with a slight change in the program. 

In the street before O'Shea's house was an assemblage of barges and farm wagons. The upper classmen found seats and were quickly driven away. As the freshmen began to take their places, the sophomores rallied and were able to cut a harness, but the freshmen thus delayed were taken into other vehicles and not one member of the class was finally left behind. 

At the first corner the vehicles began to scatter. They passed through various streets of the town, but sooner or later all found their way into the Pomeroy Road. At

the second four corners they turned right and proceeding half a mile stopped before the large farmhouse owned by the father of Frank Smith, a member of the freshman class. The conditions at O'Shea's barn were nearly duplicated here, except that there was more room, and the illumination afforded by kerosene lanterns was scarcely equal to that given by electric lights. 

Two negro fiddlers who had been engaged to embellish the program were seated at the rear of the stage. 

Joe had been offered the position of choragus, but he had transferred the honor to Dickley. The latter mounted the platform and taking Riley Waters by the hand gallantly presented him to the audience. Dickley spoke of Riley Waters as one of the most public-spirited persons he had ever known. He had recently purchased at a most liberal figure rakes that certain members of the student body finding no use for had wished to dispose of; and more important, when the freshmen had contemplated furnishing a little amusement for the college, he insisted on taking the burden of the entertainment on himself. Dickley then announced that the chief performer would at once open the show by singing to the tune of John Brown an original song which a member of the freshman class had been kind enough to prepare for the occasion. 

Waters had never prided himself on his singing; in fact he considered himself unable to sing; but, with a decidedly unappreciative expression on his countenance, he did the best he could until he was hooted down by the audience. In a clog which followed, he had the benefit of training and practice and succeeded so well that he was roundly applauded. Then he was led to the front of the platform and was asked to give the freshman yell. As he seemed to lack enthusiasm, Dickley

313 applied the paddle and shouted, "Louder." Then

Mickey Montgomery applied his paddle until it was thought that the maximum degree of efficiency had been attained.

Next the unfortunate sophomore was requested to make a speech in which he was to describe his sensations and inform the audience concerning his liking for freshman Jim shows. Despite the fact that he was still smarting from blows of the paddle and was interrupted at almost every word, Waters was able to introduce into his speech numerous jibes directed at the freshman class. 

Throughout his talk he displayed so much wit and goodnature that he secured the good-will of the audience and was declared a first-class sport. In appreciation it was decreed that there should be no more paddling. At the conclusion of his speech Waters was asked to scratch for worms like a hen and cackle; and then to walk back and forth across the stage like Winifred Dorchester. 

Then the minstrels struck up a lively tune and again Waters danced. He showed so much vim and energy and the fiddlers played so well, that the spectators could not be restrained in their enthusiasm. They left their seats and danced-not only the upper classmen and the freshmen-but even Frank Smith's father and mother and his sisters and lady friends. When the music died away, Frank Smith leaped to the platform and asked the company to find seats.

Then the Smiths, large and small, passed among the students with pans of doughnuts and pitchers of new cider. Everyone was engaged in eating and drinking when suddenly the great door was rolled back, and there appeared in the lamplight the figure of a little old man. 

It was none other than Swampy. He was very much

bespattered with mud and apparently was much excited.

Chuck Burke stepped forward, "Professor Marsh, the freshmen are having a little merry-making and dance.' When I went to investigate I was told that you were already free. At your early convenience I shall expect you to provide me with a written deposition in which you give a full account of all that occurred during the period of your detention and the names of all who during that time came in contact with you." 

The smile passed from Dickley's face. "Yes, sir," he said and stepped meekly aside. "Is Mr. Waters here?" Swampy then inquired.

Waters hastily put on somebody's coat and without a collar and with much disshevelled hair issued from the crowd and stood before Swampy. "I understand, sir, you have been brought to this place by force and against your will. If that is the case, give me now a minute account of any indignities you have been subjected to."

"I have suffered no indignities, Professor Marsh," said Waters with a smile and twinkling eyes. "On the contrary I have had a most enjoyable time."

Professor Marsh seemed somewhat disconcerted by the reply, and the more because he observed the expression of pleased surprise that ran over the faces of the onlookers. The fact that one of the professors was willing to be present gave her great delight. She wished Professor Marsh to meet her daughters and some of their friends from the neighborhood.

While Professor Marsh was occupied with the proper conventionalities, a formal dance was begun. Mr.

Swampy took a little sip and then a generous one. He cleared his throat, "No, no," he said, "this cider is right." 

Swampy and Frank Smith's father sipped cider and ate doughnuts for a long while. The dances seemed to grow merrier and merrier, and Swampy's eyes sparkled brighter and brighter, "These social occasions," he said, "do much to enliven the tedium of college life. It would be better if we had more of them. Perhaps my barn,' he observed rather doubtfully, "might be large enough to accommodate such an affair."

However good were Swampy's intentions when he uttered these words, there has never been a Jim show held in his barn. While riding home he spoke again. somewhat enthusiastically of the entertainment, but he has never been known to refer to the matter since that evening.
