Frank Smith's Jim show.

As soon as protection week was over the college settled down to routine work. Joe devoted himself heart and soul to study. In addition to preparing his lessons faultlessly, he drew from the library books that he hoped would discuss the assigned matter from different points of view; and he attempted also to read critical works dealing with music and art. When late afternoon came, he was quite content to seek relaxation in football. He soon became interested in the game. His powerful

NEW STYLES FOR HOME-SPUN GOODS 

317 physique and mature judgment stood him in good stead, and within the course of a few weeks he was able to give satisfactory account of himself in more than one position. If there had been a feeling of resentment, due to his unconventional and over-zealous repulse of sophomore opponents on paint-night, it gradually wore away. His success in football and his association with 

Chuck Burke and the members of the Alpha Beta 

Gamma fraternity, helped him to make friends among the students of all classes. 

His best friend among the freshmen was Harry Dickley. Although known to everybody after Frank Smith's 

Jim show, only Joe had gained his confidence. Dickley worked several hours each day at odd jobs about town; and it was said was dependent on his own efforts for his living. Joe roomed near him in the dormitory and was summoned one evening in February to appear at Dickley's room. He responded without delay and found that Dickley had received a box from home; and some one, according to college custom, had stolen it and had sent out invitations for an opening. The box had been returned and was in the middle of the room. Dickley had been provided with a hatchet; and a circle of students stood about. Dickley seemed undisturbed by his visitors and was in his usual good humor. He warned the boys that they might be disappointed, that the box might contain nothing good to eat. It might contain only his laundry which he was accustomed to have done at home. "Too late to save your cake by that bluff, Dickley," exclaimed Wes. Martin. "I carried the box, and I know it's got something in it that's heavier than washing."

When the cover was removed, out dropped a letter. 

Then a box of cookies was discovered, and was quickly disposed of. Underneath a newspaper was a quantity of apples packed about two tin butterpails. "There's the cake! There's the cake!" they shouted. "Open the pails!"

When the covers were lifted no cake appeared but each was full to the brim with rich golden butter. "By gad, Dick," said Mickey Montgomery, as he filled his pockets with apples, "I guess your folks think you like butter." 

After the others had gone, while Dickley was reading his letter, Joe sought to find a way by which he might win his classmate's confidence, and perhaps gain the opportunity of offering aid. He hesitated how to proceed. He was no adept, as Elizabeth Fairchild and 

Mrs. Van Alstyne were, in cozening people of their feelings and making no return. He had been accustomed to give value received, and this he now attempted to do in purchasing Dickley's confidence. 

Accordingly he spoke of school days at Hemlock, his life on the farm, his work for the telephone company, and finally of how without regret he left the old home and had come to spend four years at Franklin. He told it all except he did not speak of Dora, or of the estrangement that followed when Dora's widening interests thrust an obstacle between them; nor of his sudden. abandonment of his old ambitions and his recent resolve to make himself worthy again to be her companion and a companion to her friends. Of these thoughts, so over-mastering at times that they threatened to distort his judgment concerning what was of real worth in college life, he did not speak; but he told Dickley all the rest.

NEW STYLES FOR HOME-SPUN GOODS 319 

Dickley said his father was a man of somewhat advanced years who worked hard on a New England farm in a region where many farms had been abandoned. 

Dickley was the only living son, and when he had left home for college, his trunk placed on a load of grain a neighbor was taking to town, his father had given him five dollars and had regretted that it was not more. 

The letter said his mother had sent two pails of butter in lieu of money, hoping that they could be bartered at a store for some article of clothing he needed. "I wish they hadn't sent it," Dickley continued. “I'm getting along all right. My scholarship pays my tuition, and I wait on table for my board, and the dean gives me all the work I want to do." "I should think it would pay you to borrow money while you are in college," remarked Joe, “and use more time for study. Anybody would be glad to lend it to you, and after you are graduated you will be able to earn money more easily." "No, not while I am getting along as well as I am now," Dickley responded confidently. “At any rate,” said Joe, "why don't you do something easier than hustling ashes and mowing lawns, especially since you could do a dozen things you'd like better?” "Do what?" asked Dickley. "Well, for instance," replied Joe, "you say that butter here is worth ten cents a pound more than at your home. 

What's to hinder your father's buying you a few hundred pounds from his neighbors every week? You could sell it down here at the boarding-houses." "I believe I could do that," exclaimed Dickley eagerly.

"Of course you could do that. If your father couldn't get the butter, I could get it for you at a little higher price from a creamery near my home. You ought to sell both butter and eggs, and perhaps cheese. Put your eggs in pasteboard boxes. You could take orders at the boarding-houses and fraternity houses, and all over town. There isn't anybody in Franklin who makes a specialty of such trade. With your push you could soon build up a big business." "May be I could, if I had had your experience," responded Dickley. "No experience needed. It's easy. At first it might not be much pleasanter than your old jobs, but next fall you ought to be able to hire a room downtown and get a boy to make all your deliveries. I'll lend you the money and you needn't pay me until after you are graduated." "I don't know why you should do that for me.” "Never mind why.' "I'll think it over." 99 "Think it over later. Send your father a check tonight, and have him buy butter and eggs; you can start tomorrow to take orders." 

Another friend of Joe's was the elegant "Percival"

Gould. He took no part in athletics, but had literary tastes, for he had published a poem in the October number of the Franklin Monthly. He took Joe to call on 

Matilda Burton of Pomeroy, an old friend of the family, who later invited them to a party where they met several people. Gould made a point of attending every faculty reception and often persuaded Joe to go with him. On such an occasion Gould's delight was to talk with à group of the faculty wives, although most of the stud-

ents sought younger guests. At these receptions Joe too seemed to prefer the society of his elders, for he usually visited with the professors, and was just as likely to be with the President or Mrs. Dorchester as with their daughters. The president seemed to enjoy conversing with the serious-minded freshman who had insisted on studying Greek. 

Burke, Joe's fraternity mentor, came frequently to Joe's room. Joe always welcomed him, although he often called at the most inopportune times, as on the evening preceding an important examination, when Joe had laid out several hours hard work. During one of their conferences Burke took occasion to warn Joe against Percival Gould. "He is a sissy, and a super who leg-pulls the faculty. He is no more a typical 

Franklin man than is Wes. Martin," concluded Burke. "They are diametrically different," cried Joe, offended because of the criticism of his friend. "Gould was brought up with several sisters, in a refinded home, under the best conditions." "If he had a good bringing up," answered Burke, “he is the more to blame for not following Franklin customs.❞ Col Joe set little store on this criticism. Although Burke was looked up to as a leader by almost every student in college, he seemed always to be afraid of violating college customs or infringing on conventionality. Joe determined that he would not be so subservient. lege for him was only preparation for living after college days were over. From that point of view, he would choose his friends as he pleased among faculty or students. A little later when Gould suggested that they go to see a play in Pomeroy, Joe quickly agreed to invite

THE COAT of Many COLORS 

garet. To further show his independence, when the 

Alpha Beta Gamma party came round in March, instead of importing a girl from Houghton or Pomeroy, as was the usual custom of the students, he again invited Laura Dorchester; and he was glad he did, for in his opinion no girl at the party appeared to better advantage or was more popular. 

Finally the snow left the campus and spring came on. 

As Joe walked up the hill one morning he observed that more than one lawn was growing green. Forsythias were blooming in many of the dooryards. Over the campus a western breeze was blowing as fresh and sweet as if it had swept over a mountain of hepaticas. 

The bushy slopes leading down to Faculty Street were ringing with bird song, juncos, robins, song-sparrows.

Near the college church Joe thought that once he heard a distant bluebird call-but so faintly that he scarcely knew whether the breeze had brought him the memory or the sound.

Everywhere dandelions were blossoming. It brought to his mind Dora's early college letter in which she had written, "The dandelions blossom through all my dreams; and through all, you gather them with me.'

But that sentiment was the flower of a spring long gone. 

From her last letter the old spontaneity was missing. 

Enthusiasm flared only in plans for continued study. 

The first year of his college course was drawing to its close. Had it in any respect brought him nearer his goal? A dozen little foolish things it had taught him.

It had taught him something about the dress of people who did not live in Hemlock. He had learned how to get in and out of Dr. Dorchester's drawing-room—in retrospect a matter not so difficult as getting the large hay wagon up the slope into the barn.

NEW STYLES FOR HOME-SPUN GOODS 323 

When first he marched preliminary parasangs with Xenophan he had been confident that he had at last found the route to the land of Dora's dreams; and he thought he had scented its very air when he sailed with 

Odysseus over the violet sea. There he felt the spell of Circe as in the joy of her eternal youth she fared to and fro before her mighty web singing a sweet song; and on the campus many an evening in his awakened fancy, the golden cloud that drifted in the western sky had become the home of Aeolus, god of winds, who dwelt with his six sons and six daughters in a floating island walled about with brass. College studies had brought him new and pleasant thoughts; and possibly had furnished him with a glimpse, misty and distant, of the yet unvisited country whither he was bound. 

Perhaps he ought not to expect more than this in freshman year.

XXXVII 

ACADEMIC DISCIPLINE 

The next autumn, the autumn of Joe's sophomore year, Dickley was seated in his room figuring on accounts when the door opened and Joe entered. "Yes, I was away only a few days," replied Dickley after greetings had been exchanged. "I found all going well at home. Dad's let most of the farm and is tickled to death with the butter and egg business; so I didn't have to stay.' "And I spent only a few days in Hemlock. All the rest of the summer I was with the Bell in Boston." "You went back at the time of the wedding, I suppose?" "Yes, sir. Didn't Sophrona look beautiful! Brush will make her a good husband too. He's going to live on the old place across from Sophrona's father and mother. He's helping Dr. Dawson run the telephone business and seems to have stepped right into my shoes." "Then everything's right at Hemlock?'' "Yes, indeed. Theodore is keeping the farm up fine; and before I went back to Boston I got rid of most of my swamp land, and put more money into Bell telephone stock."

The next day football practice began, and again Joe temporarily left Dickley and his old boarding-house and 324

ACADEMIC DISCIPLINE 

325 took his meals at the football training table. The regimen of trainnig excluded him from most of the nocturnal activity of the rowing season; but, excepting one memorable occasion, his classmates did very well without him.

That critical occasion came one morning about two weeks after college opened when the freshmen succeeded, during the early sophomore recitation, in hanging a flag to a rope stretched from Pritchford's tree to a neighboring elm. When the news spread, the sophomores came with a rush, determined to sweep the freshmen before them and if necessary carry the fight monkey-wise into the branches of the two great trees; but 

Swampy arrived ahead of them, and commanded both classes to disperse. His orders were obeyed with the greatest reluctance but without argument; and the college janitor removed the flag.

The dissatisfaction of the sophomores became almost riotous when the upper classmen, mainly as it happened, juniors with keen recollections of their own discomfiture the previous year, decided without any regard for the traditional desire to preserve the modesty of the freshmen, that the second-year class had lost the rush. 

Only Swampy's presence and authority restrained the sophomores from joining the contest with redoubled ferocity. 

To Joe it seemed almost miraculous that so desperate a struggle could be terminated speedily by the command of one short deaf old man who wore a wig. College tradition, stronger than law or reason at Franklin, was the chief source of his power. Professor Humphrey

Marsh was a man of importance in the town of Franklin and by some means or other had influenced the election of the college trustees one by one until the board

was composed of men who valued his advice and desired to execute his plans. Dr. Dorchester, as was well known, had been Swampy's choice for president. Because of a variety of reasons Swampy was consulted in every town and college emergency. If the college needed temporary funds, he supplied them if necessary from his own ample means. Members of the faculty seldom opposed him although he was tolerant of criticism; and students knew that his word was sufficient to expel any offender from college. Yet even more than his authority, students feared his sharp tongue. It cut like a whip; and its stroke was made more poignant through the working of a memory that if need be ransacked two generations. His influence with the students, however, was not due so much to the active exercise of these powers as to the traditions inspired by their possession through thirty years of college history. 

In the class in calculus Joe discovered that Professor Marsh's conduct of recitations was quite as peculiar as his method of maintaining college discipline. The professor came to the recitation hall early and visited with the students or gave them advice concerning the solving of problems. When the strokes of the Chapel bell began to grow rapid signifying the approaching end of the interval allowed for the changing of classes, he grew restless and stood before the class with his right hand thrust under his coat at the second button. At the last clang of the bell out came the hand with an upward flourish and fell pointing at the student who was first to recite. Any person who came in haste, and dropped out of breath at the last instant into his seat, was as a matter of course predestined to be the victim. All who were actually late were refused admission to the classroom.

ACADEMIC DISCIPLINE 

327 Communication between students during a recitation in mathematics, Professor Marsh especially abominated. 

The professor's eyes were very bright and active, but his ears were dull. Students at blackboard or at their seats had been known to take advantage of this fact by assuming the aspect of studious application while exchanging bits of information with their neighbors. 

Swampy was altogether too alert and far too well versed in the ways of generations of college students to allow any such irregularity to escape his notice. Because he found it difficult to detect the culprits through the use of his ears, he was doubly severe with those who gave visible evidence of communicating. 

Early in the term Dickley had asked Joe to repeat the number of the page to which Professor Marsh had referred and was detected in the act of communication. 

Swampy at once leaped from his desk, and pointing to the door shouted with an appalling expression of anger, "Get out of here! Get out of here!" As soon as Dickley started, Swampy accelerated his exit very materially by making a feint of pursuing him to the door. 

No student, however large, powerful, and dignified, ever wasted any time in leaving the room after Swampy once pointed at the door. This unhesitating compliance was due of course to respect for his traditional authority rather than to any fear of unpleasant consequences of a physical nature. Although Swampy's face assumed at such times an expression of almost insane ferocity, it was perfectly well understood that the professor in his pursuit of the fleeing student would take pains never to catch him. Indeed there was in circulation in the college an old silly story that a sophomore once in rushing for the door had stumbled and fallen, and Swampy in

desperate straits to prevent overtaking his victim had cast himself to the floor and had lost his wig.

Dickley smarted considerably when the boys taunted him because of his ignominious exit, and in his ignorance of Swampy's ways determined if possible to turn the laugh on Professor Marsh. In this resolve he was encouraged by his classmates who had not forgotten the blot that had come on their record through Swampy's interference in the fall rush. Each was willing to urge the rest to take some means for securing revenge, but hitherto no one had been found who was rash enough to enter on a contest with so dangerous an antagonist. 

Dickley, however, did not appear to be at all deterred by the desperate character of the undertaking. 

To the detriment of his butter and egg business he devoted himself for a week most assiduously to mathematics; and as he had ability for the subject he made excellent progress. The lesson assigned for a certain 

Monday he learned to perfection. All possible related problems and corrollaries, both fore and aft, he had placed at his tongue's end. He arrived early at 

Swampy's class room but did not enter. He tarried until the next to the last stroke of the Chapel bell, and then rushed and fell precipitously into his seat at the instant of the last clang. 

As he had anticipated, the descending finger with the certainty of fate pointed at him. Apparently out of breath he arose and stammered out words that answered perfectly the assigned question. Swampy hesitated but a moment; and then asked Dickley to solve the most important problem assigned for the lesson. Dickley stepped to the board and speedily worked out a correct solution. He did not, however, resume his seat at

ACADEMIC DISCIPLINE 

329 once but began to number his equations and write words of explanation in the margins. "What are you doing there?" Shouted Swampy indignantly. "You finished your proof some time ago.

It is just as bad to slop over as to leak, Mr. Dickley." "I thought a word of explanation might be useful to some of the students," responded Dickley, who now considered it proper to relinquish his assumed embarrassment, "since some of the processes I used are rather obscure." "Where did you obtain that method?" demanded 

Swampy sharply. "Jones's Calculus, page 71," replied Dickley with assurance. "That method's all right for Horatio P. Jones of 

Rutgers," responded Swampy energetically tossing his head from side to side with the rhythm of his words; "but I don't care a bawbie for what Horatio P. Jones thinks. This is the way to solve that problem." 

Swampy then went to the board and entered on an explanation which, to Joe's secret satisfaction, occupied the attention of the class for five or ten minutes. Joe felt sure that the professor on concluding his extended explanation would forget to resume his inquisition of Dickley. 

Joe, however, was wrong. Swampy had no sooner completed his explanation than he interrogated Dickley concerning a still more difficult matter. This also Dickley disposed of correctly and promptly. An expression of good-natured sagacity followed the look of surprise that had hitherto been evident in Swampy's face; and indicated that at last the old rogue was fully cognizant of the situation. He asked more searching questions, and still Dickley acquitted himself well. But, finally,

when the professor was dangerously near violating the rules of the game by asking for information not strictly connected with the subject of the lesson, Dickley made a slip. Swampy heartlessly followed up his advantage until he had clearly demonstrated that he had found a matter that Dickley did not understand. Then, like a savage about to scalp his victim, he sang his customary song of triumph, "I can't pour mathematics into a pail without a bail, bottom, or sides." That completed, he proceeded in routine manner to denounce the unlucky sophomore for his ignorance.

The measure of success that had attended Dickley in his tilt with Swampy brought him praise that more than compensated for the harsh words that Professor Marsh had spoken. Dickley's success at the Jim show the previous fall had brought him friends and admirers. 

This new evidence of his wit and ability increased his popularity. The widespread approval that was accorded him together with his new prosperity made it easy for Joe to carry out a plan that he long had cherished. He proposed Dickley for membership in the Alpha Beta 

Gamma fraternity and his name went through not only without opposition but with enthusiasm.

Joe's own relations with Swampy were uniformly pleasant. His ability in mathematics was no greater than Dickley's but he pursued his work more seriously. 

As he was accustomed to arrive at the class room early, he frequently engaged Swampy in conversation connected with the day's asignment. Sometimes he talked to the old professor of practical matters that had no interest for the rest of the class, such as problems suggested by his study of telephone theory. All who sought knowledge were welcomed by Professor Marsh; but it was possible that Joe was received with especial

Academic Discipline 

331 cordiality both because of his maturity and because of a growing acquaintance.

Not only with Swampy, but throughout the faculty circle Joe's acquaintance was widening. In social adventures with Gould he was meeting professors that he never met in recitation. His friendship with Laura and 

Margaret Dorchester brought him into contact with the families of the faculty in both colleges. Even on evenings when there were attractive student gatherings he sometimes preferred to go to a faculty affair, for he felt that he needed the social training that he could find in the more or less formal society of the professors and their families. 

Late in the fall came the traditional football game with Tech. Old grads were back and the town was in gala dress. Franklin reversed the football scores of the previous two years and won a great victory. Burke and Joe again became popular heroes. On the second evening after the game Burke had walked with Joe to his room. They talked over the details of the contest and the manner in which each member of the Franklin team had acquitted himself. Then they discussed the value of football. "Some lazy fellows," said Burke, “find for the first time that they don't have to stop when they are tired and ache, nor even when they can't see that their work is doing any good. They learn to keep at it for the sake of the team." "Yes, football helps to develop college spirit," said Joe. "And college life and spirit is what makes college worth while," added Burke. "Yes, that and the faculty," assented Joe.

"Why drag in the faculty?" demanded Burke, as if he had a chip on his shoulder. "Franklin never had a great professor; and see what she's done! And the men she's turned out! That comes of the life we live here, with its old traditions and customs." "I think it's mainly due to her faculty," insisted Joe. "What you mean when you say Franklin never had a great professor, is evidently a great scholar or scientist such as writes books at a university. I think a great professor is a man like Swampy who can't write books because he gives all his time to the college and his pupils." "If you keep on dogging Swampy or any other professor, you'll surely be called a super," snapped Burke. "I'd rather be called a super than give up the pur- I had in coming to college," retorted Joe. pose "It's the idea that you're leg-pulling for favors," explained Burke. "You stay after class to talk to the professors; and several times a week you go with Gould to call on the families of the faculty, or you run around with Laura Dorchester. And worst of all is that every day you are seen parading the campus with some professor. For instance, what were you doing so long yesterday back of Agassiz Museum with Prexie?" "Huh, I found a new aster; and while I was examining it, Dr. Dorchester came along. He said they were planning an extension, and he asked me whether I thought the slope at that end was too abrupt." "I avoid the faculty as I would a pestilence." "Avoiding the faculty is a most senseless custom,” retorted Joe energetically. "It may be senseless, but it's the custom here. You have to do as others do wherever you live. If you didn't make a mistake in coming to Franklin, you'd better take

ACADEMIC DISCIPLINE 

333 the college as you find it, and not try to make it over to suit yourself."

After Burke had returned to his room, Joe considered anew his purpose in coming to college. Burke maintained that college custom forbade students to avail themselves of training obtained through companionship with the faculty. If he were to conform to that view he would be deprived of one of the most direct means of attaining his ends. Burke seemed to regard college training not as something that could be learned from teachers or books, but as a series of ceremonies prescribed for the initiation of youth. The college traditions in some way were supposed to transform freshmen into alumni who would be easily distinguishable from the hoi polloi and from all who had not been made wise concerning the various conventions of college living. 

The more he considered Burke's view of college training the more primitive and shallow it seemed. He determined to argue it out with Burke; and he prepared for the encounter by formulating his arguments. He also prepared a list of some of the inconsistencies inherent in Burke's view, hoping thereby to spur Burke to a vigorous defense.

A day or two later he found Burke in the lounge of the Alpha Beta Gamma house. Two or three of the boys were visiting in the corner, but Burke sat by himself. It seemed just the time to start the discussion. 

Joe said, "According to your views a fellow in college should cultivate a careless manner, but be scrupulous about social forms; be negligent in attire, but wear becoming clothes of current mode; avoid the faculty, but be a good mixer; seldom study, but be a satisfactory student."

To Joe's surprise Burke showed no inclination to argue. "Thank God, you're learning," he said, and joined the group in the corner.

Joe might have been piqued because of Burke's abrupt departure. Certainly he was in no mood to agree with Burke. He decided that as soon as a suitable opportunity presented itself he would ask someone wiser than Burke-either the dean or Swampy-what was to be done when college traditions and customs interfered with the social and scholastic standards of the college.

XXXVIII 

When Franklin and Pomeroy were shut in by winter weather, Joe was often seen at social affairs in the company of Laura Dorchester. Early in his career at 

Franklin he had distributed his attention among various young ladies of the college circle; but finding himself better entertained by Laura, and well received by her family, he had gradually spent a larger proportion of his time with her.

Joe's preference for Laura had not escaped the notice of his companions. When Dr. Dorchester singled him out from a group of students for a greeting, the boys laughingly attributed the presidential favor to Joe's interest in Laura. It is possible that they erred in this conclusion, for from Joe's first appearance at Franklin Dr. Dorchester had been impressed by his maturity and scholarship. When he was at the Dorchester home the doctor often called him into his study and inquired about his grades, and which studies he preferred, and the reasons for this preference. The doctor no doubt thought his questioning astute, but Joe soon discovered that what the president really sought was trustworthy information concerning the teaching methods of the professors. There was, however, no familiarity in these interviews. All conversation between the president and 

Joe was carried on with formal decorum. 335

With the other members of the family there was no such formality. Mrs. Dorchester used her social graces to place student callers quickly at ease. Agreeable entertainment was a matter of course at the Dorchester home. Each of the girls by inheritance or training was possessed of their mother's social talent. Laura, although younger than Margaret and Charlotte, was most sought after. The advice was often given in the faculty circle when it was feared that a company would be slow or not mix well, "Get Laura Dorchester; she will make it go." She talked easily with everyone. She had the gift of ascertaining speedily the tastes and disposition of the person with whom she conversed, and into the setting thus obtained, she entered with all her talents as if in drama. Some people said she posed. However that might be, she was able to induce guests to converse about matters in which they were interested, and everyone found her entertaining.

Margaret was more practical and mature. She held responsible positions in most of the clubs and charitable organizations; and as her father's secretary knew all who were influential in the administration of the college and town. Charlotte's talents were not inferior to those of her sisters, but as she seemed to be occupied with her duties as teacher in the Franklin Grammar School she was less frequently seen in the Dorchester parlor or at social affairs. Winifred's interests centered in Houghton and Lucy was still in her first year at high school. All of these sisters, except Laura, in complexion and features resembled their father. Laura, like her mother, was tall, slender, and dark. She followed college athletics keenly, and was always ready to go to a game, or to the theatre at Pomeroy. Such excursions were readily undertaken, as sister Margaret

proved to be an agreeable and convenient chaperon, no small factor in the success of a social foray in Franklin. 

Near the middle of sophomore year there came a sudden change in Joe's relations with Laura. He discovered that although Laura knew almost everyone in the college circle and chatted easily with all, she used her vivacity as a screen. In reality she was shy. Rarely did she permit glimpses of her inner self. She consumed volumes of poetry and fiction and was romantic to an extreme degree. When Joe became aware of this aspect of Laura's character he seized the opportunity to read with her. Together they discussed characters from classic tales and were surprised to find that they shared like opinions. When a line of poetry stirred each of them deeply, they should have concluded, perhaps, that the poet had demonstrated that within each dwelt age-old human nature. No such commonplace conclusion was theirs. It was a discovery of momentous import that out of the innermost chambers of two hearts could come like aspirations and sentiments. 

Often when they encountered picturesque situations in a novel, Laura, urged by her love of drama and romance, assumed a pose, somewhat as she had been accustomed to do in social conversations, and spoke in character; and Joe accepted the challenge, and so in a sort of drama they bandied back and forth the matter of the novel; and sometimes, pretending they expressed the feeilngs of fictitious characters, they played with fire, using thinly veiled their own sentiments, and going far beyond what they would have dared say in plain talk; and sometimes later when they met, even as they passed in the street, Laura would toss to Joe-loaded with bewitching hidden meaning-a fragment from these dramas.

One evening by appointment Joe came to help Laura in the interpretation of a difficult poem. Mrs. Dorchester and the girls were entertaining company in the large parlor; and he and Laura withdrew to the adjoining room where a fire burned on the grate. From a copy of Shakespeare they read, "Let me not to the marriage of true minds 

Admit impediments. Love is not love

Which alters when it alteration finds, Or bends with the remover to remove: 

O, no! it is an ever-fixed mark 

That looks on tempests and is never shaken;

It is the star to every wandering bark, Whose worth's unknown although his height be taken. 

Love's not Time's fool, though rosy lips and cheeks 

Within his bending sickle's compass come;

Love alters not with his brief hours and weeks, But bears it out even to the edge of doom. 

If this be error and upon me proved, I never writ, nor no man ever loved." 

-William Shakespeare 

"One thing I've determined," Laura announced. "Shakespeare is thinking of the marriage service. Don't you remember how dramatically in Jane Eyre someone at the back of the church objected to the marriage of 

Rochester and Jane?" "Then he believes that any man might marry any woman?" "Oh, no. In this sonnet he's not thinking especially of the love of a man for a woman. The love he speaks of here goes back of that. It could be called friendship.

It's more like charity in the New Testament. He means that the kind of love he speaks of is not impossible between people of different stations, races, creeds, if only it is the outgrowth of true minds. Nothing in the heavens above or the earth beneath can stop such love." "If that is his thesis," observed Joe, “in the rest of the sonnet he will, no doubt, attempt to prove it, or at least explain it." "In the next line," continued Laura, "if you make which refer to the first love it is clearer; but it means the same either way. People who say, 'I'll love you as long as you will love me,' don't love at all. They are

The kind of love Shakespeare speaks of here, is a free gift-so free it doesn't even demand a return." merely bargaining. "Yes, and he states the same thought in the next line more picturesquely," added Joe. "Bends, stoops, does the ignoble thing to withdraw his love because the other has withdrawn his." "And now the poetry really begins," exclaimed 

Laura. “This kind of love is a lighthouse for such as must go through tempests." "And again," said Joe, "Shakespeare restates his thought in a further-reaching metaphor. The lighthouse seemed to him too much shaken of storm and earthquake, and so he goes out of the reach of mundane catastrope and uses a star to guide his wandering ship. 

The love that Shakespeare had in mind is such a mariner-guiding star." "That's right," said Laura, "but the next line I have been utterly unable to solve. I am not even sure of the antecedent of whose." "I can help. Whose refers to star. The mariner uses his sextant to determine his latitude. To him the

star is only a point of light that aids in directing his course; but, immeasurably beyond the mariner's conception, the star may be a world greater and better than earth." "And that world, that star, that kind of love, he tells us," said Laura, "may endure when our world is swept away. He believes that true love will last forever, and is not subject to the caprices of time. Although time can take rosy lips and cheeks, he cannot control true love. Love will endure unchanged beyond the uttermost reach of time." "Do you suppose Browning got his conception of unending love from this sonnet?" asked Joe. "The poets may have come by different routes to a similar conclusion," answered Laura, "if this is the right way to look at love." "The last two lines," said Joe, "seem clear except I never writ. Of course when Shakespeare says, no man ever loved, he means that if anyone tried to love differently from this conception of unselfish, timeless love, it wouldn't be love at all, but would be only a self-seeking flow of affection, a temporary social arrangement made in consideration of a return." "I think I know what he means by I never writ," replied Laura. "Shakespeare had occasion to depict many kinds of love, but this sonnet gives his view of what true love really is. On such a conception of unselfish love is founded his philosophy of human life. If this basic idea is wrong, all he wrote is naught." 

"This is a very great poem." "The greatest of Shakespeare's sonnets," remarked 

Laura, "but not the most beautiful." "Which is the most beautiful?"

"I never can tell. His most beautiful thought's not all in one sonnet. It's a gleam here and a gleam there. 

Sometimes I think there's nothing more beautiful than the sonnet where the sun comes over the mountains lighting the sky and rivers of the English landscape. 

Surely no one who has seen the reflections of clouds and dewy foliage in quiet waters can ask for anything better than, Kissing with golden face the meadows green,

Gilding pale streams with heavenly alchemy. 

But the other night when Shakespeare came in here and sat before my fireplace and said he was going to take to court his past life and mine, I thought as I heard, When to the sessions of sweet silent thought I summon up remembrance of things past. -I thought then, I was listening to the most beautiful of all his sonnets. In that poem he expresses the view of love we've been reading about. He speaks of friends hid in death's dateless night-friends hid, not nonexistent, but become dateless; for it doesn't matter what the tombstone records, since love's not time's fool. Also when he says he will weep afresh love's long-since-cancelled woe, I find so much in cancelled. Shakespeare couldn't cure his wound, but he wouldn't ask for sympathy. For a few moments he let the memories stir again in his heart with their former poignancy. Then he drew a line through the account and laid it aside." "I could easily have passed these sonnets by, if it hadn't been for you," said Joe.

"Our reading together has been a wonderful experience," replied Laura. When I read alone, and fill myself with great thoughts, I am almost consumed with loneliness." "Don't you talk about poems with Margaret?" "If I read with her; but usually I read alone, and all the rest are doing something else, and I can see a look in their eyes that says, 'Laura is in her moods again, all worked up over something'; and so I leave them to themselves." "If your sisters hadn't been busy, I might never have known you as you really are," said Joe. "Only two or three weeks ago I never dreamed we could talk as we do." "I came to your house many times and walked round and round, but there seemed to be no door," said Joe, falling easily into their make-believe. "When I came to your house and found the door open," continued Laura, "I didn't dare enter. I guess

I must have lived all my life as a savage, for I feared to go inside." "If you are a savage, you are not the less welcome," said Joe. "Fellow savage?" “—No, a long time ago I passed through a door where all was lovely within-" "Then don't tell me," cried Laura. "If you can't enter my door as a savage, come as a stranger!" "The way is dark and stormy," responded Joe. "I have seen a light shining through a chink in your walls. I knock at your door and say, 'I am a stranger. 

May I come in and sit an hour by your fire, and—then go my way?'"

"And I answer, 'Come in, Stranger. Sit by my fire.

There's wood aplenty. For an hour we will converse. 

Then you may go your way.' 999 The flames that had lighted the room grew shorter. 

Shadows with increasing boldness raced from dark corners. The air grew chill; and they drew closer together. The heart has its own fuel.

They were roused by the sudden flare of an outlying splinter or twig, but when it died away, only coals glowed in the darkness. Said Joe, "The wood is consumed; the hour is almost passed; I must go." "Stay, Stranger, stay!" cried Laura. "I am sure we have not talked an hour." "Not the full hour-for another time we must save a part. We must not spend it all tonight."

Near the middle of the college year a group of students were discussing the approaching examinations. 

Someone offered to bet that he could be the first to leave the examination in Bib. Lit. and would still pass. 

The bet awakened no enthusiasm, as no one had ever been known to fail in Bib. Lit. Then Dickley, still elated by his successful tilt with Swampy, offered to bet that he could be the first man out in the test in mathematics and still pass. The bet was quickly taken, for Professor Marsh's examinations in calculus were notoriously difficult and every year several students were conditioned. Then Mickey Montgomery, attempting to clown it as usual, offered to bet that he could be the second man out in mathematics and would still pass. 

The suggestion was received with shouts and jeers, as it was not believed that Mickey could pass even if he did his best until the close of the examination. A near

riot ensued while it was being decided who first offered to take Mickey's bet.

Joe was not pleased with Dickley's rashness. Dickley maintained, however, that since he already had the highest mark in the class, he could prepare himself during the remainng days to write out perfectly the first six problems, and so obtain a passing grade. "You can't afford to sacrifice your record in mathematics," protested Joe. "You will lose more if you win than if you lose." 

Dickley was obstinate.

At the examination all proceeded smoothly according to Professor Marsh's long followed customs until 

Dickley completed his sixth problem. "Good-bye," he said under his breath to Joe as he gathered his papers. 

Joe roused himself from his work and turned his face toward Dickley. "Don't go," he whispered hurriedly. "Did you speak, Mr. Clay?" shouted Professor Marsh. "Yes," replied Joe. "Hand in your paper!"

Joe bit his lip, but said nothing. He handed in his paper and started slowly for the door. Dickley passed him on the way out. Mickey Montgomery started immediately after. "Mr. Montgomery," shouted Swampy in his shrill "Mr. Montgomery, where are you nervous tones. going? Are you ill?" "No, Professor, I have worked very rapidly; and 

I think I have covered the most important parts of the examination." "I hoped you were ill, Mr. Montgomery. But it seems to be another instance of your inability to direct

your acts with judgment. You have a deplorably bad judgment, Mr. Montgomery. It handicaps you. No doubt you are ambitious to become a leader, Mr. Montgomery, but you have misjudged yourself; you have overestimated yourself. You are sadly confused, Mr. 

Montgomery. As a leader you are not meeting with success. Have you forgotten that you were not fortunate as a leader when you were in preparatory school?

I remember that you accepted the advice of the faculty and left Norwich very suddenly. The school at Bronson was apparently more agreeable to you. I have come to that conclusion because you stayed there one or two terms longer than you planned. Your plans seldom succeed, Mr. Montgomery. I think you are in need of instruction. Sit down there at the side, Mr. 

Montgomery, and after the examination is concluded. 

I will give you advice.” 

While Swampy had been speaking the scratching pens stopped and the room grew deathly still. Mickey's rotund countenance gradually had taken on a sheepish look and his face and neck were fiery red when he took his seat. Once or twice Swampy paced across the room and then he addressed the class. "Inasmuch as no other examination follows this, and since I have occupied so much time in speaking, I shall extend the period of this examination one-half hour. Each student is expected to remain at work until the close." 

The evening after the examination Joe, Dickley, and others were at the chapter house talking over the events of the day. Dickley was vigorously maintaining that he would be passed in calculus. "I'll bet ten dollars more with anybody," he said, "that Swampy will pass me without a word."

"I'll take you," said Burke. "You are getting altogether too eager to bet your money, and I want to teach you a lesson. You can't make me believe that when you stayed but thirty minutes, Swampy would keep the rest of the class two hours and a half and not say anything to you." "You have lost. I know I have passed," exclaimed

Dickley, triumphantly taking a paper from his pocket. "I took a carbon copy of my work, and I have gone over it, and each of the six problems is absolutely correct." "If you did only six," said Joe, "you could get but sixty per cent as a maximum; and the smallest mistake in any of them would throw you out." "And you can trust Swampy to find that mistake somewhere," shouted Burke. "Before the second semester begins you and Pop Clay will have your names posted among the flunkers."

A day or two later Dickley was summoned by letter to appear at Swampy's office. He entered and found 

Swampy busily working. A green shaded electric light threw its rays over the desk and the professor's wrinkled visage. "Take a seat," said Swampy, courteously pointing to a chair at his right elbow. 

In a few minutes the professor laid aside his work, and from a shelf above the desk took a very neat bundle of papers tied with two cords. He carefully removed each cord and hung each on hooks that were screwed into the bottom of the shelf. He then ran down the alphabetical order and, taking care not to disturb the symmetry of the pile, withdrew Dickley's paper and placed it on the slide before Dickley. "That is a very

fine paper, Mr. Dickley-as far as it goes. Where are the other sheets?" "That is all I wrote, professor. I had an urgent matter of business to attend to, and as I do not believe in working for marks and was confident of my ability to work any of the problems, I decided to stop when I had done sufficient to pass. Since the examination, I have verified the work; and I think, professor, you will find each solution is correct." "Hem-perhaps we might go over the paper?" "Certainly," replied Dickley, in cheerful tones; and he hitched his chair a little closer to Swampy. 

Swampy read problem number one deliberately, interposing from time to time a "let's see" or a "yes." When he came to the answer he declared it to be correct. 

Problem number two he also inspected very cautiously and slowly, and at the end said it contained no flaw. 

Problems three, four, and five in turn he admitted were satisfactory in all respects. He examined problem number six with double care. His progress through the several equations was painfully slow; but at last he reached the end. "Isn't 317.54 the right answer?" asked Dickley with a bright smile. "Ye-e-s," responded Swampy, but he seemed to give his assent grudgingly. He stared steadily at the paper and even lifted it from the desk to get a better light. He turned it this way and that. At last from a drawer he drew a reading glass and scrutinized the last line with great vigilance. "What is the matter, professor? Is there anything you would like to ask me?" Dickley inquired in benevolent tones.

"Well, yes," assented Swampy slowly and with apparent diffidence. "Can you tell me why that decimal point in the answer looks so peculiar?" 

Dickley laughed. "Certainly I can," he replied loquaciously. "That peculiarity is evidence of my thrift, professor. I saw a fly-speck on the paper, and to save time, I placed the number so that I didn't have to make any point."

Swampy looked at Dickley with an expression of extreme mental confusion. "You don't mean to say," he inquired, "that you used a fly-speck and didn't make any decimal point?" "That's what I did," chuckled Dickley. "If you made no decimal point, your work's incomplete. You're conditioned," snapped Swampy; and before Dickley realized what had happened, the old professor had taken one of the strings from the hooks and was already tying up the package of papers. 

If Joe felt chagrined because of the exclusion of his paper in calculus, he kept his feelings to himself. He saw his name posted in the list of failures and made no comment. On the day of the make-up examination he and Dickley appeared at Swampy's recitation hall with the group of chronic flunkers. Wes. Martin and all the other old skates in college seemed to be there. Some had gone over the subject two or three times. Each except Dickley was given a card calling for three problems to be worked at the blackboard. Dickley was distinguished by being seated at a table where he was given the original examination and was asked to solve all the problems including the three alternatives. Joe fared better. As soon as he had taken his place at the board and had made a good start in laying off the

work, Swampy tapped him on the shoulder. "That is sufficient, Mr. Clay," he said. "You are passed.”

As Joe was about to leave the room, Swampy addressed him with some trivial remark and seemed inclined to engage him in conversation while the others were working their problems. Joe thought it a good time to ask the question that Burke had failed to answer to his satisfaction. "Professor Marsh," he said, "I came to Franklin seeking the best preparation for life, and I have carefully devoted myself to the course of study, and I have wished to conform to the college traditions which I know you value highly; but what am I to do when the college customs conflict with the scholastic standards of the college?" 

Professor Marsh stared at Joe as if offended with his boldness in asking such a question and snarled this reply, "College is more than preparation for life. It is life. It has its own way of living. If you can't succeed with your fellows in college, you can't succeed anywhere. You can never learn from books how to be successful with men."

Although Joe continued to see Laura often, his adventures with her in playland gradually became less frequent. This was not the result of an attempt to follow 

Burke's advice to curtail association with the faculty and their families. His interest in Laura had advanced so far that he spurned all such considerations. He hesitated to follow up the love-dramas because he feared he was being led in a direction in which he had not planned to go. He had begun to suspect that the makebelieve was gradually changing in his own heart to a sincere affection.

In a metaphor that Laura clearly understood he had informed her of the close attachment he once had with Dora. He was not certain whether he had used the past tense in speaking with Laura, nor was he now certain what tense should be used. He could not easily admit that his love for Dora was becoming a matter of the past. He had come to college to fit himself for her companionship and the companionship of her friends; but after he had secured some of the training he had missed, and had gained a view of the world outside Hemlock, he had sometimes glimpsed a picture of himself that was unpleasing. He did not choose to act the part of a rejected suitor looking at life with sympathyseeking eyes because a girl hadn't loved him. Obviously the right thing to do was to leave Dora to follow her ambition wherever it might lead her and to start all over again in his relations with women. 

Less definitely he had come to the same conclusion before he had entered college. He had tried to love 

Sophrona, only to discover that she was incapable of filling the place Dora had occupied. In Franklin the possibility of falling in love with any of the women he had met had seemed remote, until suddenly this new intimate acquaintance had opened with Laura. The unexpected fervor of this attachment warned him to lay his course at once. It was necessary to decide without delay whether his affection for Dora had really become a matter of the past and whether it seemed possible that his growing regard for Laura would change into love. 

It was fortunate that Laura's interest in drama and romance had permitted an experiment in love. By the figure of the "hour" he had made clear to her that their attachment was of a temporary nature and would prob-

ably soon be ended. He had been careful that there should be no mistake. Although he had held her in his arms, he had never kissed her. He had never spoken a word of love in his own person. Laura, no doubt, regarded their affair as only another romance like those she found in her books, a story in which she had discovered a part she could act well. Each of them, he believed, was holding himself in reserve, awaiting the end of the drama; and when it ended, each would choose one of two alternatives. Each would be able to resume his normal self unscathed; or if their experiment gave evidence that sincere love was possible between them, each could cast away the make-believe and thereupon speak straight from the heart. A decision,

Joe thought, should be made first by himself and by Laura later. But as the days had passed he had lacked courage to renounce his love for Dora; and he had come to no decision.

One evening in early spring, after returning from a play in Pomeroy, Margaret had left Laura and Joe to their own devices. At the close of a long intriguing talk Joe had said, "The greatest wonder of all is that nature can take a few handfuls of her cosmic dust and make it into you, so full of thought, so warm, so mysterious." But he quickly added, “I must not speak like that. The time has come when I must leave your house." "I am sure it is not an hour." "Only a few moments remain." "How can you tell, Stranger? In my house there is no clock. We measure time neither by pendulums nor minutes. Only when we have lived an hour, has an hour passed."

"Even by that reckoning I must go. I must go while a few moments are left. I would die of loneliness to know I could never enter your house again." 

Once more he left the Dorchester home having postponed his decision.

Dickley and Mickey Montgomery were worried because Riley Waters and other juniors had accused the sophomore class of lack of spirit. They said the freshmen had won the fall rush, and the sophomores never had spunk enough to start another. Franklin would surely go to the dogs if it had many classes like '99.

After enduring the taunts for several days Dickley and Montomery decided to take means to end the criticism. 

The most conspicuous point from which a sophomore flag could be flown was the pole on the top of the Chapel tower. The Chapel stood on the crest of College Hill, and its tower extended far above the treetops. When on public holidays Old Glory flew from the lofty pole it was visible for miles around. The tower was always pictured on college publications as the symbol of Franklin. No spot on the campus was more carefully guarded. Only at certain times were the students permitted to take their friends from Houghton to gaze upon the widespread view from the upper deck. Customarily the tower door remained locked. The caution shown by the college authorities was accounted for by legendary stories of bell-clapper stealings and other escapades that had occurred in old grad days. Perhaps the real reason why the faculty limited the use of the tower was the danger incurred in climbing the frail, illlighted, winding stairways.

The dangerous and forbidden character of the location made it seem to Dickley and his friends exactly

suitable for the display of their banner. If they could keep a sophomore flag flying from the pole on the Chapel until all members of the college could see it, they surely would be accorded a triumph. Joe did not like the plan to stage a rush inside the Chapel. As the building was revered by Franklin men, the faculty would bitterly resent any rowing within its precincts. Dickley did not heed this protest, but went ahead without Joe's aid.

On the evening before the morning selected for the display of the flag, Dickley allowed himself to be locked in the Chapel. When it was dark he admitted several sophomores bringing tools. A system of signals given by watchers without informed of the rounds of Old Tom, the night watchman. While Tom was at a distance, those in the Chapel forced the tower door, climbed the stairs, and fastened their flag to the pole. 

Several sophomores remained in the Chapel all night to prevent an inopportune alarm in case the attendant who opened the Chapel for morning exercises should notice the cracked and splintered tower door.

The next morning the entire sophomore class came early. No one but sophomores seemed to have noticed the flag, but the concerted movement of the class attracted attention and the flag was discovered. The freshmen rushed toward the Chapel without waiting for breakfasts. A fierce fight was in progress before the time for ringing the Chapel bell; and that morning it was not rung. At the hour that chapel services were customarily held, seniors, juniors, townfolk, and some of the faculty were scattered about the hill viewing the fierce contest that was then in progress. The freshmen, balked at the entrance of the tower, had brought an extension ladder, and on the precipitous roof were

battling for entrance through the tower windows.

There was the sound of splintering wood and breaking glass. When finally Swampy arrived both tower windows had lost their sash, the tower door in the Chapel at the right of the speaker's platform was torn from its hinges, and the bannisters of the tower stairs were in ruins; but the sophomore flag still floated from its lofty pole.

There were no chapel exercises that morning; and the early recitations might as well had been abandoned, for the most inveterate pluggers were unable to give their attention to studies. In the early afternoon 

Dickley, Mickey, and one or two others, were summoned to Swampy's office. Later in the afternoon notices announcing that five o'clock recitations would be given up on account of a faculty meeting were posted on the bulletin boards of recitation halls. In the evening it was reported that Dickley and Montgomery were suspended from college.

Late in the evening a number of students met at Chuck Burke's room to make plans for an attempt to have Dickley and Montgomery restored to their classes. 

It was the prevailing opinion that Dickley at least must not be allowed to leave Franklin. His cheerfulness and spirit of fun together with his respectful observance of college traditions, made him too valuable a man to lose. 

The courage he had shown on previous occasions, as well as in this rush, deserved the recognition of the student body; and it was decided that no effort should be spared to secure his reinstatement. Several members of the college glee club and college choir were present and it was suggested that, until more effective means could be taken, the first move should be a strike the next morning by the college choir.

When the Chapel bell rang the next morning nearly every student came out. With the last clang of the bell the president arose as usual and announced that the choir would sing four stanzas of No. 45. He then noticed that the choir was not in its place, and he turned and looked nervously at the door. No one entered. The prelude was rapidly covered by the organist and the first note of the hymn was only five seconds distant and still no member of the choir had come. 

Then the president rose to the occasion. He stepped a little aside from his desk and with the first note began to roar out the hymn with his really fine baritone voice. 

No student sang and the few members of the faculty who were present either lacked ability to sing, or were so filled with laughter that they could not sing; at any rate they gave the president very little aid. During the introduction to the second stanza Dr. Dorchester mopped his face and neck which had become very red.

It had become evident that he wished that he had called for fewer stanzas, but he stuck to his task. Although at first the audience seemed to enjoy the president's predicament, his pluck and persistency were such that in the end he gained their sympathy. The instant he completed the hymn, the entire audience applauded. 

Dickley appreciated the sympathy that was shown him on every side and apparently did not take his suspension very much to heart. He planned, if he was not reinstated in college, to take a room in town and devote himself at least temporarily to his dairy business, for it had become too profitable to be cast aside. Joe was more despondent. He determined to do all he could to obtain a remission of Dickley's punishment, but he was not hopeful of success.

In the evening he called at Professor Marsh's home. 

Swampy received him with tenseness almost equivalent to embarrassment. Yet something in the professor's manner made it evident that he was anxious to see Joe. 

The old fox, however, didn't intend to give the impression that he was the least interested in Dickley's reinstatement. "What do you know about that debating society that's just been started?" he began. "Not much. They asked me to join, but as it's being pushed by that radical group I didn't think I'd care for it." "Oh, they wouldn't do you any harm," said Swampy. "When boys first untie themselves from their mother's apron strings they like to astonish their elders by picking up snakes; and after they come to college they show the same spirit by taking up snaky ideas. It's a harmless peculiarity of the tadpole stage." "Yes," laughed Joe, "harmless, unless they keep on flirting with snakes after they become frogs. I came to ask you what I can do to get Dickley back in college." "You think his offense was not sufficient to merit expulsion?" asked Swampy severely. "He helped break open the tower door. He admits he did considerable damage there."

As Joe was about to offer to make good the damage, there came to his mind something his father once said. 

Changing his plan, he asked with feeling, "Is the tower door worth more than a fellow like Dickley?" "Hem," ejaculated Swampy. "There's not a hard spot in Dickley's heart," continued Joe, trying to follow up his advantage. “What is his offense? Nothing but lack of judgment in his

fun. He had no desire to injure the college. In future years, if they tell the story of what Dickley did, will alumni and students love the college less?" "If he did not wish to injure the college, why did he not use his influence to prevent destruction when he saw what was being done?" "Because he didn't want to be called a super.' "Do you mean to say," asked Swampy, lifting his eyebrows, "that all other considerations are of less importance to Dickley than the mere approval of his classmates ?" 

Joe very diplomatically resisted an impulse to point out the inconsistence between Swampy's present position and that which the old professor had taken in his conversation at the conclusion of the make-up examination in calculus. He replied discreetly, "I shouldn't like to say so. I think his views concerning supering are like those of most Franklin students. My own views happen to be different from his; but I wouldn't think it right to blame him because he holds the customary view, nor to expel him because he didn't attempt to change the views of others." "You contend, then," said Swampy less sternly, "that he entered on this escapade with a spirit of comparatively innocent playfulness and became more deeply involved because of his regard for a college custom?" "I do." "Swampy leaned back in his chair and looked at the ceiling. "While it is not necessarily a part of the matter in hand," he asked, "would you mind informing me how your views concerning supering differ from Dickley's?"

"I think the common view is illogical. Most students come here for an education but consider it a disgrace to have a reputation for hard study. They cultivate the society of their classmates in preference to that of the learned men who compose the faculty. I know that practices based on these ideas have been handed down through generations of students, but it is my personal opinion that through all that time the college has been hindered by such customs." "You didn't mention the fact that when students at Franklin deliver declamations or speak in public it is the custom for their classmates to jeer at them and attempt to break up the speeches," observed Swampy cheerfully. "No," said Joe, "I didn't think of that." "Yet in oratory Franklin has the best reputation in the country." "That's true," admitted Joe. "And at Franklin in spite of the practices you mentioned most students get what they come for," remarked Swampy, gazing at Joe very fixedly. "Why, Professor Marsh," exclaimed Joe with alarm, "you are not defending those who discourage seriousminded attention to college duties, are you?" "No," replied Professor Marsh hastily, "but I am defending those who like the old customs." "Then-" began Joe, but he hesitated. "What?" asked Swampy encouragingly. "Then why are you so severe with student misdemeanors like this of Dickley's, and why do you break up our rushes?" "Old customs," said Swampy taking down a book from the shelf above his head as if he wished to end

the interview, "old customs embrace more than student conduct; they embrace the acts of the faculty." 

As they walked to the door Joe again referred to Dickley's reinstatement and impulsively asked Professor Marsh to do what he could in Dickley's behalf. "Oh, I suppose we'll have to take the boys back," said 

Swampy. "I'll bring up the matter with the president.”

XXXIX HIGH SOCIETY 

When at the beginning of his junior year Joe came back to Franklin after a summer spent in the Bell offices at Boston, he had to a considerable extent reconciled his purpose in coming to Franklin with Burke's paradox and also with Professor Marsh's advice. If scholarship had been all that was needed, he might as well have stayed at home, for he had been a successful student before he entered college. His essential need, as had been indicated by Burke and Swampy, and also by his own purpose in coming to Franklin, was the ability to associate acceptably with his fellows. He had become convinced that neither in Franklin nor anywhere else could that ability be gained from books or from living in college or elsewhere as he had lived in Hemlock, but only by conforming to the standards of conduct current in the community in which he dwelt. 

During junior year he resolved that he would give more attention to student activities and would avoid practices that could be regarded as supering. Both in classrooms and homes he would visit less with the faculty. Only in the case of Laura Dorchester would he make an exception. She was too valuable a friend to be sacrificed. It was only an accident anyway that she happened to be the daughter of the president. But even with her he would try to arrange that their social 360

HIGH SOCIETY 

361 activity centered in Pomeroy rather than in Franklin.

When he discussed these resolves with Dickley the latter was sympathetic. The terms of his reinstatement in Franklin made it advisable that he adhere to the junior custom to divert himself with social affairs rather than with college pranks. He suggested that he and Joe join the Pomeroy Country Club where there would be opportunity to play golf, and possibly polo, when the football season was over. To extend their acquaintance in Pomeroy they ought to become acquainted with Mrs. Deslauries, the recognized social leader of the city. "That's easier said than done. Although Mrs.

Deslauries is a special friend of the Dorchesters, Margaret has been to her house but once. She doesn't take to the younger set." "Leave that to me." "I left the Chapel rush to you and if it hadn't been for Swampy you wouldn't be here now. You'd better get my advice before you start anything. I don't want you to form any plan to drag in the Dorchesters." "If I get into trouble it will be my own affair," replied 

Dickley severely. "Neither you nor the Dorchesters will be involved." "Well," responded Joe with a sigh, "I don't see how you're going to get an invitation without using an inordinate amount of cheek and impudence." "You wait two or three weeks and I may tell you how," replied Dickley, "but for the present I'm going to keep my own counsel."

About three weeks later on a bright afternoon early in October Dickley and Joe, having at last received the coveted invitation, entered the spacious grounds that

surrounded the Deslauries residence in Pomeroy. A smooth unbroken lawn extended to the colonial dwelling where tasteful planting led the eye to a quaint entrance. 

At the right a far-reaching oak extended its arms almost to the building. Along the left boundary, salvia flamed; and to the rear were numerous beds of chrysanthemums and asters. Beyond, in an old-fashioned garden, dahlias towered to unbelievable heights. Evidently 

Mrs. Deslauries was a lover of flowers. 

As Joe and Dickley approached the place where Mrs. 

Deslauries was receiving her guests they observed that their hostess and Mrs. Dorchester were examining a vase of flowers. Mrs. Dorchester glanced up, “Oh, now we shall know," she remarked. 'Here comes Mr. Clay."

Mrs. Deslauries greeted them cordially, and to Joe's surprise said she remembered meeting him at Mrs.

Marsh's nearly a year before. "They tell me you know all the flowers," she added. "Not all," said Joe; "but I'm always interested to learn new ones." "I don't suppose this will be new to you," she remarked, as she bent over the vase and withdrew from it a large rose purple blossom. "This flower grows in my garden, but Mrs. Dorchester and I have never seen it anywhere else, and we don't know its name." "Why, it's a wild flower," exclaimed Joe. "I didn't know that it could be cultivated. They call it meadow beauty. I found it last fall in a sandy field over by the river." "Meadow beauty! I am so glad to know its name.

In my garden I have a little bed of wild flowers that were given me by a friend several years ago. This plant has never blossomed until this fall,”

HIGH SOCIETY 

363 "With your permission," said Joe, "I'm going to walk through your garden when I leave. I never before saw such wonderful dahlias and chrysanthemums as you have in your borders." "I shall not let you see them alone," replied Mrs. 

Deslauries. "No one who likes flowers ever escapes until I have shown him my garden."

On entering the adjoining room they found Gould's friend, Matilda Burton. She presented them to Mr.

Deslauries, and they met some guests with whom he was talking. They found that they knew a considerable part of the distinguished company. Some were from 

Franklin, and many of the others they had seen previously at social affairs in Houghton or Pomeroy. Joe looked in vain for Laura. Her presence seemed to be the only thing lacking in an enjoyable occasion. After an hour or so he sought out Mrs. Dorchester and took her in for refreshments; and Dickley came along with 

Miss Burton. In the room where the refreshments were served they found Wes. Martin and some companions who were standing about a pert girl and were conducting themselves so noisily that other guests were glad to leave the room as soon as they had drunk their tea.

Outside in an alcove the four were conversing when 

Mrs. Deslauries approached, "Oh, here you are," she said; "I've been looking for you. I want to slip away for a moment with Mr. Clay and ask him to name some of my other wildflowers-if Mrs. Dorchester will take my place while I am gone-and if Mr. Clay's friends can spare him?" She turned toward Miss Burton who had been conversing with Joe. "And so you and Mrs. 

Dorchester both know Mr. Clay, do you?"

"Oh, yes," replied Miss Burton smiling playfully, "everybody in both colleges knows Mr. Clay. He plays center on the Franklin team." "Why, I am learning every minute of his accomplishments," responded the hostess to Miss Burton, and then she led Joe out through a side door to the garden. 

Near the house, marigolds, zinnias, and other flowers made a riot of bloom; but beyond, the ground sloped to the boulder-strewn course of a dry brook where there were a few large trees and a dogwood thicket. "In my grandfather's day a stream of clear water ran over these rocks, but now there is barely enough moisture to make my wood plants grow." 

There in the dampest location they found the meadow beauty, and nearby in a shaded spot were an interesting group of berry-bearing woodland plants. Joe gave 

Mrs. Deslauries their names, and remarked that she must have spent much time in getting together so large a collection. "Most of them," replied Mrs. Deslauries, "were given me two years ago by a friend, a very lovely girl who knows all the plants, and is now studying music abroad." "You don't mean Miss Reed, do you?" inquired Joe. "Yes, yes, Dora Reed," exclaimed Mrs. Deslauries with enthusiasm. "Do you know her?" "We went to school together. We came from the same town." "Then you know a very charming girl. We think everything of her here in Pomeroy. And no doubt you too are interested in music," she said turning and looking eagerly into Joe's face. "I want to hear you sing.” "No," laughed Joe, "I have no skill in music." "I shall have to ask Mrs. Dorchester about that,"

HIGH SOCIETY 

365 replied Mrs. Deslauries with a skeptical smile. "I've no doubt you're a fine musician."

On the way back Mrs. Deslauries led Joe to a fenceside border where there were chrysanthemums of enormous proportions. "How are you able to make them grow so large?" asked Joe. "By making the ground rich and cultivating continually," replied Mrs. Deslauries. "Their size is a testimonial to my work; but I enjoy some of the smaller plants just as much." "Yes, small flowers are often as wonderful as large ones for instance that motherwort over the fence." "What, that weed?" "It has a blossom as beautiful as an orchid." He reached for the spindling mint-like plant, and asked Mrs. Deslauries to examine the one or two remaining blooms with his pocket lens. "Why, it is exquisite, it is wonderful," she exclaimed; and when she had completed her examination added, "That ought to take the conceit out of us humans." "Almost anything in nature ought to be able to do that," assented Joe. "Yes, but this flower is so clearly intended for creatures smaller than man-for flies and beetles." "I don't know," he replied. "With a man-made lens we saw it and admired it." "But beetles can see it without lenses." "Then perhaps they can't see chrysanthemums," answered Joe as they reentered the house.

A few minutes later Joe and Dickley took leave of their hostess. "Come again when the dahlias are in better bloom. Come and bring Mr. Dickley with you," urged Mrs. Deslauries, "I want to talk to you again about beetles and lenses."

They walked rapidly to the railway station and were just in time to catch the train for Franklin. They dropped into their seats a little out of breath, and finding that they had the car almost to themselves sprawled in sweet contentment over the cushions. "Didn't we have a dandy time!" remarked Joe. "Certainly we did. Everything went off all right,” said Dickley, his face fairly beaming with smiles. "Nobody can beat Mrs. Deslauries in pulling off an affair like that," said Joe. "That's right," assented Dickley. "All she needs is a little help in making out her list of guests." "Why, I thought they were the finest sort of people," said Joe in astonishment, "the best people in Pomeroy and the best people in Franklin-except Wes. Martin and his gang. How under the sun did they happen to be there?" "Oh, that's easy," replied Dickley with an assumed air of carelessness. "They were there because I invited them." "What do you mean?" inquired Joe opening his eyes to their very greatest capacity.

Dickley took a toothpick from his pocket and began to pick his teeth. The wood was not sharp enough to suit him and he used his pocket knife to improve the point. "Well," he remarked with judicial deliberation, "you see I took a good deal of pains in making out that list. I went through all the society items in the Pomeroy papers for two seasons, and when I found anybody who had been invited to Mrs. Deslauries's at least twice, I set down the name. Then I added a few prominent people whom she seemed to have overlooked, and some of our friends in Pomeroy and Franklin—” "What are you trying to tell me, Dick?”

HIGH SOCIETY 

367 "I didn't intend to tell you for a good while yet, but you made yourself so solid with Mrs. Deslauries today, I thought I might as well come out with it." "Did you ask Mrs. Deslauries to give this party?" demanded Joe indignantly. "No," responded Dickley in matter of fact tones, “I didn't feel that I knew her well enough for that. I just ordered the invitations engraved and sent them out myself; and, as you were just saying, everything went through beautifully." "You sent out the invitations without her knowledge or consent!" shouted Joe, leaping to his feet in excite- "I don't believe it. Then why didn't she tell the people it was all a hoax and give up the party?" ment. "Oh, I sent the invitations to the most prominent people first," said Dickley with a shrewd smile, "and when their acceptances began to come in, she didn't dare call it off." "You're a genius, Dick," said Joe who seemed to be gasping for breath, "but don't ever try anything like this again." "I won't need to do it again. By the time she spent with you, I should judge that you are already on her approved list." "Why didn't you send an invitation to Laura?" asked 

Joe regretfully. "I didn't dare ask too many of the younger set. You said Mrs. Deslauries didn't take to them.” "But that terrible Wes. Martin and his crowd, why did you invite them to her house?" "I knew they would make so much noise that she would think they pulled off the job, and so nobody would suspect us."

XL  

At the Pomeroy Country Club a young man named Waldo induced Joe to play polo. On several occasions 

Joe had taken part in matches and had enjoyed the game, for he was a good horseman. During junior year he had found it more agreeable to ride a horse over the country roads around Franklin than to pedal his bicycle. Sometimes he drove. To hold the reins taut over the back of a fast stepping horse and hear the crunch of gravel under wheels was a pleasant reminder of days on the farm. With horse and buggy, one June afternoon, he entered the Dorchester driveway.
