It had been arranged that Margaret, Laura, and he were to drive through the country, stop somewhere for dinner, and then leave Margaret in Pomeroy to spend the night with Matilda Burton.

They turned into the hills north of Franklin where the uplands were pink with blossoming laurel, and where from outlooks they could see the distant looping river and the spires of Pomeroy. On the winding trail that descends to the lowlands they stopped to admire the pasture roses and the spikes of pale blue lupine.

Below in the bottoms they passed meadows bright with clover and tall buttercups. There bobolinks sprang from the grass, soaring and pitching in acrobatic and vocal contest. Then came level freshly-ploughed fields 368

in which farmers were growing tobacco. In an old oxbow marsh-plants grew; and red-winged blackbirds, spreading their wings and calling "kongurree," clung to the tops of budding milkweed. 

Near the river where the highway was shaded by tall elms, at a large colonial house known as the Mather 

Homestead, they stopped for dinner. The place was famous for a good orchestra and good food, and was patronized by the faculty and students of both colleges. 

Among other acquaintances they found Waldo with a friend of Margaret's. They visited on the broad porches until it was time to take Margaret to Pomeroy. 

At twilight Joe and Laura returned through the shaded streets of the old New England town. They traversed a neighborhood where there were houses with well-kept spacious grounds, Mrs. Deslauries's home among others. As they came to the first farmhouse, the air was fragrant with sweet clover and the evening was so alluring that they avoided the direct much travelled highway to Franklin and turned north to the river road. On coming again to the Mather Homestead, where they had dined, they found the place brilliantly lighted and dancing in progress. Joe tethered his horse to the iron rail, and they entered for a waltz or two before going on. At one end of the large room Joe saw to his dismay that he was again afflicted with the presence of Wes. Martin. He and his companions were creating as much disturbance as they had at Mrs. Deslauries's garden party; but to the relief of all they soon left. Joe and Laura again found friends and could have spent the entire evening to their satisfaction had not Laura wished to reach her home at an early hour.

When they went for their carriage it was nowhere in sight. Inquiry brought no information except a suspi-

cion that Martin and his gang while prowling around might have had something to do with its disappearance. 

As the search was extended, the buggy was found beside the road a few hundred feet distant, but the horse was missing, and evidently had been turned loose to make its way home as best it could. Joe, accordingly, used the telephone to report his predicament to the livery stable in Franklin, and was able to make arrangements to have the driver he customarily employed come with a carriage at once.

There was some delay; but just as the dancing at the 

Mather homestead was being brought to a close, the carriage came. Laura had wished to return early, but she had not inclined to worry. Her father and mother had gone to Boston, and as usual had left the household in Margaret's charge. Margaret lately had sought to be a more rigorous chaperon, as she had been displeased with Laura's and Joe's somewhat inconsiderate use in her presence of occult conceits, as well as their increasing self-sufficiency. On this occasion, however, to suit her own convenience, she had ruled that it would be right for them to return to Franklin alone. Now by no fault of theirs they were making the journey at a later hour than was considered proper in the Dorchester family. The situation appealed to Laura's humorous sensibilities as well as to her love of adventure.

On the ride home Joe thought Laura never had been more entertaining or confiding, but he was ill at ease, for the intimate character of their talk had emphasized the necessity of its termination-unless they should decide that it could be indefinitely prolonged. To drift was dangerous. That night a decision ought to be made. They had already reached the outskirts of 

Franklin and would soon approach the Dorchester

home. The faint fragrance of her hair, the soft warm pressure of her body against his, the surge of her breath, were not incentives to clear thought. "Why don't you talk to me?" Laura suddenly exclaimed. "All the evening I have talked to you." "As plain Joe Clay you never talk to me as you do in make-believe." "Isn't that the better way?" "Do you wish to leave my house?" she asked, assuming her make-believe manner and voice. "When the hour is up the make-believe must end." “You need not hasten, Stranger, unless you wish to go. Once I told you we had no clock. Since then I have learned that if we had a clock it would have no meaning. In my house we cannot speak of hours, or days, or months, or years. I built my house in a land where there is no time."

The sound of the horses' hoofs was sharp on the pavement. The beat continued a few moments, grew slower, and the carriage stopped. The coachman drew back his window. "Dr. Dorchester's, sir." 

No light shone in the Dorchester hall. Laura was alarmed. "Father must have returned a day earlier than he expected," she exclaimed with agitation. "What of it?" demanded Joe. "I'll ring the bell and will explain to him where we have been and why we were delayed." "Don't do it. Don't waken him," insisted Laura. "I don't want to make trouble for mother and the others."

Joe did not understand the full import of Laura's words, but from her excessive anxiety, as well as from observations he had previously made, he guessed that

Dr. Dorchester's family discipline was severe. The thought of such conditions in the Dorchester home stirred his ire. His interviews with Dr. Dorchester had always been conducted with the greatest dignity and decorum. If behind this stately ceremony Dr. Dorchester at home was playing the bully to the women of his family, he would welcome an opportunity to tell 

Dorchester exactly what he thought of him.

He told Laura that they had done nothing wrong, and that he was not afraid to call her father to the door and talk to him man to man. Laura would not consider it. She called softly to Charlotte with whom she shared her room over the porch, and to Lucy who slept in the adjacent room, and who, she hoped, might steal down to open the door. She would not permit 

Joe to throw pebbles against the windowscreen for fear the noise might waken her father. Finally she thought of the ladder that she and Joe had used in a nearby cherry tree earlier in the afternoon, and she asked Joe to fetch it.

Again Joe tried to persuade her that there was no need to engage in clandestine acts as they had been guilty of no wrong; but Laura only begged him the more insistently if he had any regard for her to do as she asked. His customary dignity and self-consciousness made any porch-climbing adventure most repugnant; nevertheless he did bring the ladder, he placed it against the porch, and he and the now-awakened Charlotte helped Laura through the window. 

As he was returning to the ladder there was a rattling at the front door and the sound of rushing footsteps.

As he approached the edge of the roof he saw a whiteclad massive form at the bottom of the ladder.

"What's this? What's going on on my porch roof?" roared Dr. Dorchester in tones such as he used in athletic stadiums. "I'll come down and speak to you," replied Joe in hushed voice. "You will not," shouted the doctor, snatching the ladder from the eaves and holding it vertical. “I have you trapped. You will stay right where you are until 

I summon the police." "There's nothing wrong," Joe explained in tones barely audible. "I am Joseph Clay, sir." "Joseph Clay!" roared the doctor. "At my daughter's chamber in the middle of the night. Come down!" and he slammed the ladder against the eaves. "Dr. Dorchester," said Joe as he began to descend, "calm yourself. I have done nothing that I am ashamed of." "You brazen scalawag, come down. Let me get my hands on your throat.' 99 "Dr. Dorchester," began Joe, who thought he heard windows opening in various directions, “you are beside yourself, or you would not proclaim your suspicions to the town. You are not in the proper costume, nor is this the place or time for either of us to consider this matter rationally. It would be better to postpone discussion until tomorrow morning."

The president, for the first time conscious of the publicity he was providing with his stentorian tones, as well as of his unconventional attire, took a step or two toward the door. "On pain of severest penalties," he said more quietly, "you may be at my office tomorrow morning at ten. Explain as best you can your presence here, and make a statement of your intentions in seeking my daughter's company."

When Joe returned to the dormitory, Dickley called to him from the adjoining room. Dickley was jubilant because he had learned he was exempted in each of the final examinations, and had only two or three recitations to attend before completing the junior year. Joe tried to congratulate him, but quickly swung the conversation to his association with the Dorchester family. He was half of a mind to confide in Dickley as he was deeply in need of consolation and advice, and he felt that he must unbosom himself to somebody. "Your speaking of the Dorchesters," remarked

Dickley with a smile, "reminds me of something the boys were talking about the other evening. Did you ever hear that old story of how some fellows one night when Lucy was a baby robbed the Dorchester clothes line and hung the seven nightgowns on a rope stretched from Pritchford's tree to the Chapel tower?" "I never heard of it," replied Joe. "Then there was another story," continued Dickley, unconscious of the torture he was inflicting on his friend, “about a sneaking sort of fellow in '96 called Harris, a regular super. He elected all the snap courses and he'd sit up in front and ask questions. He took everything the college offered in Bib. Lit. and astronomy. They say he got through the history of art by pushing Professor Ducker's baby carriage. But in spite of all his supering tricks he came within an inch of losing; and he never would have got his diploma, if he hadn't kept a trump card to play at the end." "How did he get his diploma?" "After two or three professors had told him he never would be graduated, he made love to Charlotte Dorchester and got engaged just before they made out the

senior list, so Prexie handed out a degree in as fine style as anybody would wish." "I didn't know that Charlotte Dorchester had ever been engaged." “Huh, he never went near her after he got his diploma." "I'll see you in the morning," said Joe; and he went down the stairs to the street. 

Before he met Dr. Dorchester in the morning he had to choose between Dora and Laura. Only a few hours remained in which to make the decision he had feared to make. That evening as an actor in their drama

Laura had indicated that her regard had changed from friendship to love. Dora had wavered in her affection; perhaps her love had wholly died out. At best it was but a glow as compared with Laura's fervor. As he reviewed all the circumstances that involved each, it seemed better from every point of view that he give up 

Dora for Laura.

As he walked the campus paths he examined the arguments one by one. But why did he need to argue? 

Before what judge was he pleading? Was he not a free agent? A third time he wearily went over the problem, and in the end concluded that it was useless to attempt to direct his course by reason. He seemed to be under the control of a power greater than himself, and however unwise his decision, however severe the pangs that might come to Laura or to himself, he was unable to renounce his love for Dora.

At ten he entered Dr. Dorchester's office and found the president sitting quietly at his desk. The strain that had been experienced was evident in the doctor's face. Without interruption the president listened to

Joe's story as he told of his association with Laura and of the decision that the events of the previous evening had forced him to make. "From what my daughter has told me," said Dr. 

Dorchester, "as well as from what you have reported,

I believe that your part in this affair has not been dishonorable, but it has been most unfortunate. When 

I agreed to accept the presidency of Franklin College, I expected to sacrifice certain of my personal and private rights, but I did not foresee the extent to which my family would be similarly involved. It is this situation, rather than want of feeling on your part, that I hold responsible for this misfortune." He paused; but added, "Under the circumstances it is obvious that your association with my daughter should end." "Shall I speak to Laura before I go?" asked Joe huskily. "I will summon her.”

When she entered, Joe started to speak, but she cut him off. "I understand," she said. Then into her face came a faint smile, and she extended her hand, "Goodbye, Stranger."

XLI A MAY MORNING 

As Joe walked a Franklin street the day after he had said farewell to Laura, and passed some of the younger women of the faculty, he caught a gleam in their eyes that was disturbing. When he encountered dignified Mrs. Marsh, who went out of her way to speak to him, but burst suddenly into laughter on the merest pretext, he could no longer cherish illusions.

That his midnight parley with Dr. Dorchester had been overheard by gossiping neighbours, was evident. 

He hastened to the bulletin boards to get his schedule of final examinations and found he had not been so fortunate as Dickley, as his grade in history was little above passing. Without delay he interviewed the easygoing professor, and on the plea of urgent work in Boston, obtained permission to substitute an oral quiz for the formal examination. The next morning before most of the students had heard of his adventure on the ladder, he took the train for Boston.

The additional days gained by his early departure were to his advantage. President Hudson had not lost his interest in the young man who after efficiently directing the affairs of a local telephone company was now devoting himself to classic literature. He provided employment that permitted Joe to observe the sort of problems that passed through the executive offices as 377

well as occasion to meet men prominent in telephone affairs. Joe made the most of the latter opportunity, for he had taken to heart Swampy's dictum that one cannot learn from books how to deal with men. 

After a successful summer he 

September to begin his senior year. returned in mid- He 

That Laura had taken a position to teach in a girls school near Philadelphia he was glad, as he did not wish her to share in the disagreeable notoriety he expected to encounter. foresaw during the coming weeks continual humorous reference to his love affair as well as misinterpretation of his conduct. The ridicule, he did encounter just as he expected; but to his surprise both town and gown, instead of condemning him, seemed to understand the exact situation. They were unwilling, however, to let it go at that; they chose to add to his annoyance by laughing at him as a gay and bold Lothario.

Among his friends only Dickley was sufficiently sympathetic to refrain from joking. To him Joe gave a full account of his relations with Laura. He did not mince words but called himself worse names than he deserved. "Dr. Dorchester was most to blame for the way it came out," said Dickley. "I kept watch of this affair and I could see it was headed for keeps. Dorchester was a fool; if he hadn't meddled at the wrong time, it would have ended all right."

Joe devoted himself to football more earnestly than ever. Before the season closed it was the common opinion that his work at center was a principal cause of the success of the team in the fall of '98. In the game with Tech, Franklin's traditional rival, he executed some brilliant plays, and a great victory was won. At the conclusion of the game he was carried about the

A MAY MORNING 

379 field on the shoulders of his over-joyed college mates. 

In the evening the town was astir with excitement. 

Student correspondents flashed over the land the news that Franklin had won. The streets became lively with people, and everywhere college cheers and songs were audible. Caterers' wagons rushed to and fro busy with their preparations for festal suppers. Shopkeepers vended horns and other noise producing devices. Draymen brought boxes and pine stumps to the angle of the driveways before the observatory where the bonfire was to be built.

As there was no wind when the pile was lighted, a slender flame, fed by oil, shot up like an arrow from the top of college hill and became visible for miles. The entire town and college had assembled for the celebration. Howling students, accompanied by an incessant din from horns and rattles, danced like savages around the fire, and drank cider and ate crackers and cakes.

Then followed various elaborate serpentine marches and dances. Finally the boys captured Swampy and forced him to ascend a short ladder to the pedestal of 

Pritchford's statue to make a speech. Then they called for Joe. The fame and favor that resulted from his midnight interview with Dr. Dorchester, increased by his recent success in football, made him the most popular man in college. He ascended slowly to the pedestal. "No, no," they shouted, 'don't stand on the pedestal ; stand on the ladder. You speak best from a ladder!”

Inured during the past weeks to jibes, Joe goodnaturedly consented.

The second semester of the senior year those who had distinguished themselves in English were permitted to elect a seminar in English literature. Twice a week

the ten or twelve students who had been given permission to take this course were grouped about a table at the head of which sat the old white-haired professor. Except a long thesis or report concerning a special study undertaken, little was required of the members of the seminar beyond the reading of literature and the informal criticism of works which had afforded them the most pleasure. In the course of time Joe, and indeed each member of the little group, looked forward with eagerness to the semi-weekly meetings.

Not the least in enthusiasm was the instructor who apparently was less eager to express his own views than to listen to the opinions of his pupils. If the criticism was crude, by skilful inquiry and comparison he often induced the speakers to revise their hasty generalizations. When no reports were presented, he read his favorite selections with such appreciation that the class for the time forgot the authors and received the words as the expression of the professor's own thought and sentiment. 

So intimate was the exchange of opinions in these conferences that a feeling of friendship was developed among those who took part in the seminar. Joe was especially attracted to Percival Gould. As Gould and his fellows had matured and a senior spirit of tolerance had become evident in the class, Gould had been favorably re-rated by his classmates. He was recognized as the most promising student in English. Joe and he had taken as the subjects of their theses, investigations connected with the progress of English poetry.. Often in the early spring they came together to engage in reading that had a common bearing on their individual studies. They read of the shepherds "nursed upon the self-same hill," and Thyrsis who with his mates at

A MAY MORNING 

381 Oxford roamed the wooded slopes "behind whose ridge the sunset burned." And emulating such comradeship, when the days grew milder, they climbed together the rugged hills north of the village; and on the southern slopes, in hollows sheltered from the winds and warmed by the sunshine, they found the first arbutus bloom.

One afternoon early in April they descended over the broken stones of a steep hillside where there were yet but few signs of life. Only a crow called from a blackened stub. Near the highway, however, juncos fluttered before them through a little thicket. At the sides of the road the low sun lighted the brown grass with splendor and cast long shadows before them as they walked homeward. They talked of Wordsworth and his gifted sister, of Coleridge, Keats, and Shelley, as if they spoke of old friends. Anecdotes from biographies were recounted and favorite passages quoted. 

Gould said, as if no one could have said it before, that he would willingly work all his life to write five stanzas such as those in the Grecian Urn. The road seemed all too short. At the parting of their ways they lingered with regret as each was about to turn toward his lodgings. And sometimes at night they walked from the village along the valley road where heavy darkness shut out all sights, and where the only sounds that came to their ears were the noise of distant streams and the cries of hylas in far away swamps. There with the fervor of youth they discussed the world-old questions of God and human destiny. However fantastic their philosophy it offered no support for chaos or pessimism. 

The future was filled with hope, and the end was good. 

One May morning the air was so sweet and nature so lovely Joe suddenly determined to cut all classes and spend the whole day out of doors alone. Across the

campus the grass was dotted with dandelions. The fields beyond were ringing with bird song. The woods in the distance were hazy and soft, and those nearer by were a maze of lace-like branches. Tapestries of wools and silks could not rival these hangings woven in the greens, grays, and pinks of opening buds and expanding leaves. Blue skies and white clouds overhung the glades and where the trees grew thicker the sunshine filtered through like golden rain. Underneath were uncoiling ferns and spring flowers. Somewhere an invisible thrush called loudly with ringing voice. Joe with senses all alert passed onward silently. Where was the power that had pushed forth these buds, compounded these colors, and given voice to these songsters? Not in some distant star. Here, everywhere still damp with newness, was his handiwork. Had Pan just disappeared down the dingle? Had the approach of a mortal frightened away the nymphs with whom he had been disporting? Gleams of more than earthly loveliness lighted Joe's eyes. Not Pan nor Bacchus; none other than the god of light and beauty, Phoebus Apollo, could have laid these tints, have made the working plan that ten thousand servitors with the aid of branch and bud, plant and bird, cloud and sky, had made visible to human sight. 

He needed to escape from the emotion that was overpowering him. He turned from the crest of the ridge and leaving the oaks and beeches plunged under dark pines, where little birds with tiny voices flitted in the shadows. Until he became calm and chill he wandered in the gloom, and then followed a brook down into a bright warm valley where birch trees were alight with candles catkins shedding golden pollen. "Brief illumination," he thought. "Bright only one day in the

A MAY MORNING 

383 year. Underneath were violets. He would have turned from the stream to explore the bank for an unfamiliar species, but the brook wooed him with many tones. A gurgle of limpid coolness appealed to his parched throat, and he paused.

Below a little cascade, where the current glided between obstructing rocks and then spread itself over the golden and brown gravel in which the sunlight glinted and gleamed, he stooped and drank the cool water. 

Soliloquizing murmurs detained him and he stayed to eat his luncheon. 

Sweet was the voice of the stream. Long ago in the ravine that leads to the umbrella tree he had grown familiar with its limid tones. Dora, too, had listened to it there. Wordsworth had painted the picture "and she shall lean her ear

In many a secret place 

Where rivulets dance their wayward round, And beauty born of murmuring sound, Shall pass into her face." 

Yes, that was the secret of Dora's beauty, Joe thought. It was as if all the lovely aspects of nature had entered into her consciousness and had left their impress in her face. But more than that, each early hope had written a record, like the beauty of a spring day, in her countenance.

In spring no harvest maturity dulls aspiration. 

Spring is loved by poets for its view beyond the present, its vision, its revelation of spirit building according to some god's design.

He rose, and left the brook, and walked along the edge of the woods. Above him hung blue sky and

opening buds. What in creation's vast plan was the purpose of these visible tokens, these symbols? Why was it that he had been fashioned to respond to their influences? What was light, color, or bird song without the human senses and brain? Only undulations in the ether, only wire without receivers. As he had constructed telephones that intelligence might speak with intelligence, so some power had fashioned him (let the processes be what they may) to receive and interpret these spiritual essences made mortal. Receiving was as necessary as transmitting. His part was vastly greater than he had realized when he had stood in the morning among the beeches and oaks.

Apollo, but the great Jove himself had communicated these symbols.

Not Along the border of the beech and oak woods he came over the crest of the ridge and saw the sunset before him. With his whole being he felt the majesty of the power that rolls the world and paints the sky and at that moment called to him so wistfully. Called -but he could not answer. Some force seemed to implore him to reply; but he could not speak. He was fitted to receive, but not transmit. 

As he slipped across the fields toward the campus, a star came into view. He saw its beauty, but it was beyond any influence of his. Might that be true of 

Dora? If Aeneas-like he sought to clasp her in his arms, would she prove to be only a shade-lovely, but unattainable?

XLII  

One evening early in June Joe walked to the postoffice and found a note inviting him to a dinner at Mrs. Deslauries's. He had received similar invitations previously, for Mrs. Deslauries had become one of his best friends. On this occasion his hostess had written a personal note in which she had informed him she was about to give a dinner for an old friend of his who soon would be her guest for a few days. The name of the friend she wished to keep secret until they met, nor would she tell the old friend that Mr. Clay would be at the dinner.

Not a little excited by the mysterious statements in Mrs. Deslauries's note, and scarcely daring to guess who was the old friend he was about to meet, he entered Mrs. Deslauries's parlor at the appointed time. As he swept his eyes over the room he noted Mr. Deslauries and a lady conversing with Dr. Dorchester. 

Nearer were Professor Ernest Chapin, the essayist of Houghton, and Mrs. Dorchester. After talking a few moments with the latter concerning a subject suggested by Mrs. Deslauries's greeting, his hostess led him to an alcove where Charles Waldo and Miss Burton were conversing with a lady that he did not seem to recognize. As they approached, the lady slowly turned, and 

Joe was face to face with Mrs. Van Alstyne. 385

Nearly four years had passed since he had said farewell to Dora at her aunt's home in New York. The years that had slipped by had not been so kind to Mrs. Van Alstyne as they had been to him. Increasing age had left its marks. Her figure was feeble and shrunken, but her steel-gray eyes, almost as sharp as when they smiled a welcome at the house party, regarded Joe searchingly and proudly, refusing to admit that the form in which they dwelt was now bent and weak.

Mrs. Van Alstyne saw before her a young man in the prime of life and health, fresh in color, clear-eyed, above the common size, with form suggesting both activity and strength. With easy grace he stood, in attire faultless, unobtrusive. 

While Mrs. Deslauries smiled expectantly, Mrs. Van 

Alstyne extended her hand, "I am surprised, Mr. Clay, to find you in Pomeroy." “I am indebted to you for the suggestion that caused me to come," responded Joe with twinkling eyes. "You set forth the advantages of a college education so effectively that I gave up my business and entered Franklin." "Then I too am indebted," laughed Mrs. Deslauries, "for Mr. Clay has become one of my most valued friends." "I was not aware," began Mrs. Van Alstyne with some tenseness, "that I am entitled to credit, but I am pleased if any word of mine encouraged Mr. Clay to prolong his education." "I shall not be jealous of praise due you for that," observed Mrs. Deslauries gaily, "if I get credit for bringing you two together. Now you must let Mr. Clay go for a few minutes to meet Mrs. Chapin. After that

you will have ample opportunity to talk over the old times, for Mr. Clay is to take you out to dinner." 

A few minutes later the guests at dinner were discussing recent social events in Pomeroy. "You should have been at the polo game last Thursday," remarked 

Mrs. Chapin to Mrs. Van Alstyne. "You would have met all your old friends. It was a very gay affair. 

Everybody turned out." "It was a gay affair," echoed Miss Burton, "and

Mr. Clay's friends were disappointed that he didn't ride. Mr. Waldo says that if it hadn't been for Mr. 

Clay's modesty Pomeroy might have won." "Trust to Mr. Waldo to overrate my ability," responded Joe. "He taught me to play and has a teacher's prejudice." 66 'And from my prejudice, I suppose the scorekeeper became infected," laughed Waldo. "Prejudice or no prejudice, polo is an interesting game," said Mr. Deslauries to Mrs. Van Alstyne. "Do you ever go to the matches in New York?" "No, but I remember seeing one of the first games played in Pomeroy. Mrs. Fairchild and I attended a match at the Country Club." "That must have been when Dora was in college," remarked Joe. "Yes." "Tell us about Dora," exclaimed Mrs. Deslauries. "I haven't heard half enough about Dora. I guess all of us here know Dora Reed. She must have been one of your pupils, Professor Chapin." “Indeed, a very promising pupil until she gave up my courses for music.” "Is she still in Vienna?" asked Mrs. Dorchester.

"Oh, no; last April Elsie Cheney persuaded her to go to Florence." "Has she set a date for coming home?" asked Joe. "I am sure I do not know," replied Mrs. Van Alstyne. "Then I guess we'll have to send Mr. Clay as an emissary to find out," laughed Mrs. Deslauries. "He and Dora went to school together."

For a moment the company waited Mrs. Van Alstyne's comment; but as she did not speak Dr. Dorchester filled the gap by returning to a discussion in which he and Mr. Deslauries had engaged before being summoned to dinner. The lengthy discourse silenced all general talk.

After the coffee when the guests were seated in the drawing-room, someone said the Osbornes were about to leave Pomeroy to take up a residence in the country. "Why is it," asked Miss Burton, "that so many city people like the Osbornes go to the country to live, or at least plan to go sooner or later?" "Mr. Osborne is going back to the old homestead," responded Mrs. Dorchester. "I think the impulse to leave the city comes naturally to people who were born in the country. Late in life they seem to feel an instinctive desire to return to the old homes and haunts. 

Goldsmith, you remember, in The Deserted Village, expresses the idea in one of his finest passages." "Goldsmith didn't tell why they return to the country," commented Professor Chapin. "I have often thought that what such people seek is their lost youth. 

They go to the country hoping to find the familiar fields lighted as they were in childhood." "And that is the reason, I suppose," said Mrs. Deslauries, "that such families often return to the city in

disappointment. What they seek, can't be found in the country nor anywhere else." "City people will find innumerable causes for disappointment if they decide to make a permanent home in the country," asserted Mrs. Van Alstyne. “One thing they'll miss, if they live among farmers, is congenial friends." "Very, very true,” observed Mr. Waldo, nodding his head. "In some parts of the country really nice people are as rare as skyscrapers." 

Encouraged by Mr. Waldo's comment, Mrs. Van

Alstyne continued. "It has been my experience that most farmers lack both the opportunity and the desire for refined surroundings. Because they have servants as their companions, or altogether lack servants, they make servants of themselves. They are continually face to face with hard labor and the crudest facts of life. 

Chickens crowd their dooryards. They have no sanitary conveniences. They swink and they sweat. From week's end to week's end they reek with their cattle and their work. How can people have refined tastes if they cannot even be clean?" "Isn't it possible that acquaintance with what you call 'the facts of life' might atone for a little dirt?" chuckled Professor Chapin maliciously. "I was born on a farm," ejaculated Mrs. Dorchester with flashing eyes, "and I was clean. We didn't have porcelain tubs, but we did have soap and hot water; and every member of my father's family was clean." "How could anyone question that, my dear?" laughed 

Mrs. Van Alstyne. "What interests us in your case is that your family left the country and moved into town. 

Besides, your family had the breeding that most farmers lack. It takes more than one generation to

THE COAT OF MANY Colors make a good stock. Most country people lack the background for social training.”

During the previous discussion Dr. Dorchester moved uneasily in his chair. All his life he had been training country boys. In that domain he was an authority. As educator he felt he ought not to let

Mrs. Van Alstyne's prejudiced statements go uncorrected. "Your opinion concerning the training of country people," he began with oratorical mien and voice, "is exactly at variance with the facts. The city boy gets his education chiefly from print and talk. He has little occasion to think. The country boy, on the other hand, becomes familiar with what you and Professor Chapin termed "the facts of life." He solves his problems from first-hand data. Lack of companionship gives him a meditative mind. When he comes to college he is prepared to profit by instruction in the sciences. Classical literature that embodies the accumulated wisdom of urban communities is precisely suited for equipping his receptive mind with the stock of ideas and the modes of thought used by educated people. 

Through the humanities he can acquire tastes as refined as those of the best bred inhabitant of the city. On the other hand no system of training has yet been devised that can furnish the city dweller with a satisfactory substitute for a boy's life on a farm."

When Dr. Dorchester ceased speaking, the group of listeners was silent and disturbed. Mrs. Van Alstyne was visibly embarrassed. Age and illness had robbed her of the assurance with which she once had met every crisis. Joe pitied her gray hairs. "Dr. Dorchester,' he said, "at least in, one respect Mrs. Van Alstyne is right. The country boy's ignorance of convention is certainly a serious handicap. Man is a social animal. 99 2

Until the boy has learned how to dress, speak, and act, he is treated as if he were a bee from a different hive. 

He is distrusted and misunderstood." 

Mrs. Van Alstyne was conscious that Joe was trying to shield her and she scorned his aid. She hastened to speak, but what she said was beside the point, and no one seemed to notice her remark. "Country boys and city boys must all travel the same highway," replied Dr. Dorchester to Joe. "If such matters as you mentioned cause the farmer's boy to walk farther to school, he may thereby learn to travel faster when school days are over." "This is not the first time," said Joe, still wishing to divert the conversation from Mrs. Van Alstyne, "that I have heard you or Professor Chapin liken education to a highway, but I think it curious that I never heard either of you tell where the road goes. In college we are furnished with aids and tools, such as languages, that help us advance. Microscopes and telescopes extend our observation, but they do not show us the end. 

How ever far we go, our quest seems doomed to failure.

We never come, like Bunyan's Pilgrim, to a celestial city." "You call such experience failure," exclaimed Professor Chapin. "I call it an illustration of the highest success. There comes to youth a delight in nature, an impulse to plant or create, a desire to explore truth and beauty, the love of a woman—some form of aspiration sends his soul on the endless quest. It is not the end that rewards. It's the aim and the endeavor." "Even if it isn't the end that rewards," answered

Joe; "without some end there could scarcely be an aim."

THE COAT OF MANY Colors 

"You yourself pointed out the end," responded Professor Chapin. "You said that man is a social animal. 

The progress of the human race is the end. The individual is nothing. The earth is filled with the bones of countless generations of individuals, and no one remembers that they ever lived. But the human race moves on. What the individual does for the race, some people call service. That is the element that lives. The rest of the individual is buried in the earth." "Yes," assented Dr. Dorchester fervently, "that is the proper basis for morals, the relation of the individual to the race; and it is also the proper basis for education. The individual who wishes to develop his powers solely to increase his enjoyment misses his part in creation. He lacks the vital element. He is a clod even before he dies."

A guest took his leave; and the company soon broke up. Along the gravelled path Joe walked alone to the street. The night was dark. Overhead innumerable stars glittered brightly. "Men die," Joe thought, “but human endeavor goes on, and on, like light-like light that aeons ago sprang from a star, and swept to earth, and still passed on, and on, endlessly. Endlessly?—the human race? Again Professor Chapin and Dr. Dorchester, like other philosophers, had extended the course -from individual to race—but they had not found the end.

Joe wondered, "Is it human destiny, that the tide turn back on itself, to furnish more complete living for the individual? . . . Or is it something else?"

Toward the close of his course at Franklin, it seemed to Joe, that events followed one another with indecorous haste. Almost before he was aware of it came the

last recitations. Then followed the examinations and the various farewell observances one after another. Of these class-day was by far the gayest and most interesting. And then came commencement; and the class sat in alphabetical order on the platform in College Hall, almost unwilling listeners during the slow-moving program and Dr. Dorchester's learned but wearisome address. Finally, however, each received his diploma, and the graduates passed through the door at the side of the stage, and on the lawn, in military order, assembled for the last time. They were drawn up as usual for gymnasium drill in three platoons, and the customary student officers were in their places, with Captain Frank 

Smith in command. According to the college custom, the new graduates were to march around the campus, and say farewell to the college buildings.

The boys were somewhat tired, and were anxious to rejoin their friends and complete arrangements for their approaching departure from town. Yet in spite of weariness and conflicting personal interests, the solemnity of the occasion had enforced on nearly all members of the class a sort of Sabbath day restraint.

To be sure the flippant and the habitual jokers had shown some inclination to personal jibes and mild horesplay, but even these were sobered when Smith took his place before them, and exclaimed in earnest tones, "Fellows, do it well-for the last time." "Now give the college yell, and the class yell, and 'College Hall,' three times, on the end!"

When the cheers had been given as directed, the class passed in due order to each of the other college buildings on the campus, coming last of all to the Chapel. 

For some reason, perhaps because it is the oldest building of all, a Franklin man loves the Chapel.

"Here, boys, we'll change our cheer," said Smith. "We'll give the college yell, and the class yell; and then, not naming the Chapel, each fellow, without any attempt to keep with the others, will shout three times for himself, "Good-bye.'"

When the last of the clattering, ragged good-byes had ceased to echo between the dormitories and the tall tower, Smith called sharply, "Break ranks!" and Joe's course at Franklin was ended.

XLIII BIG BUSINESS 

In July, 1899, Joseph Clay returned to Boston where during the three previous summers he had been employed in the executive offices of the Bell. "Look who's coming here!" exclaimed the president, extending his hand cordially. "I thought it was about time you appeared." "I'm glad you haven't forgotten me," answered Clay. "I hope I've come to stay this time." "You can't get away from us. Your desk hasn't been disturbed. Your secretary has looked after visitors and personnel there all the while you've been gone."

Then Hudson chatted pleasantly about one matter after another until Clay felt he was taking too much of the executive's time and asked whether any instructions were to be given before he began work. "Instructions?" echoed the president pleasantly. The first time you came to my office-do you remember? 

You declined a position with the Bell, because you said you wished to take a classical course." "Yes, I recall that." "And I said you had made a wise choice, since the

Bell at that time had more need of employees who could deal with men than of those who could manage materials." 395

"I remember," replied Clay smiling reminiscently. "The situation has not changed. At your desk in the cuter office you will continue in charge of personnel and the reception of visitors. In your work there I hope you will justify the four years you devoted to classical training."

Somewhat crestfallen Clay retired to his desk. He had cherished visions of receiving an executive assignment to which he could devote unlimited work and all the ability he possessed; and now he was asked to supervise the stenographer who kept the work sheets and wrote the checks and was supposed to drive the cranks and beggars from the president's door. Besides that, he would like to know what Hudson meant by asking him to justify his classical training. The words sounded like a sneer. Wasn't it the president himself who had encouraged him to go to Franklin to study Latin and Greek?

He passed an unpleasant day in the office, but the next morning returned in better frame of mind. As the result of a night's sleep he was able to take a less emotional view of the situation. His previous work for 

Bell had not been without its responsibilities. At first he had conducted visitors through the building and arranged interviews as directed by executives; but later, as he had become more familiar with routine inquiries, he had been asked to dispose of as many matters as possible at his own desk. That was a procedure that could be expanded indefinitely. This last thought surely improved the outlook.

Two or three days later Clay went to the treasurer's office to complete a file that concerned a coming interview with the representative of a wire company. Riggs, the treasurer, supplied the desired information, and

BIG BUSINESS 

397 seemed inclined to talk. He asked Clay how everything was going. Clay made a conventional reply, but had an impulse to ask Riggs for advice. Riggs had always been friendly. He was an old man about to retire.

The treasurer laughed heartily at Clay's account of his interview with the president. "Characteristic and very flattering," he commented. "After fifteen years with the Bell, Hudson still has the habits of a Greek professor. As you know, his office looks like a college classroom, and he makes engineers draw on his blackboard. You needn't worry because he asked you to demonstrate the worth of your classical training. That is something he takes for granted. It's to the rest of us he wants you to demonstrate. He thinks you'll get along with us better because of your Greek. He wouldn't have sent you to your desk with short shrift if he hadn't thought you could solve the problems." 

Clay was not inclined to agree with Riggs that 

President Hudson's failure to give specific directions was flattering. To say the least, it was discouraging and somewhat inconsiderate to send him to his desk. without even indicating whether his duties were executive or subordinate. He saw a way by which he could very quickly make President Hudson tell what was expected of him. He didn't have to work for Bell. With a sophomoric sense of humor he was inclined to exercise all the powers of his position and so far overstep them that the president would be forced to set the limits of his authority. There was no need, however, of being radical or rash. If necessary he could gradually increase the boldness of his moves. He could begin with personnel and move slowly, giving his best ability to the job. While he continued the routine at his desk.

he could look around and study the working force as a whole and the general working conditions. 

He began by improving lighting and ventilation. 

Then he shifted equipment, and even bought new appliances. He did not know whether the president would approve of the expense, but he did not intend to worry. 

The duties of each employee were next considered and he tried to use each according to his ability. The incompatible were separated. Gossipers worked by themselves. Idlers were placed where their work was fed to them. Even the old moss-backs, who for some reason or other were considered permanent fixtures and had slept in their positions for years, were shifted to duties that required some degree of initiative and alertness. Several of these rushed in alarm to Clay's desk to complain, but nothing very serious came of it. Clay seemed to have a gift for soothing their injured feelings. 

There was no precedent for such an unpheaval at Bell headquarters. The older employees did not know what to make of the innovations. They talked furtively in small groups, but did not dispute Clay's authority. 

They were pleased with the improvements in ventilation and equipment. It was strange he signed his name without the addition of any official title. They wondered how he had risen so rapidly into executive favor. 

He had had only rural telephone experience and moderate technical training. Some attributed his success to exaggerated accounts of his investment in Bell stock, and others to nothing more than his ability to read the memoranda that Hudson wrote in Greek letters each morning on the office pad.

After a few weeks had gone by, and his work had gone smoothly, and no one had disputed his judgment or questioned his acts, he gave up his foolish idea of

BIG BUSINESS 

399 learning the limits of his authority by exceeding it. It did not seem fair to make others responsible for his deeds. In a right about face, he tried to forestall appeals to superiors who no doubt were already overburdened. Each morning President Hudson passed through the outer office and spoke pleasantly of the news and the weather, but never a word about new equipment or Clay's work with personnel. Evidence of satisfaction, however, was not lacking. On some pretext or other he usually required Clay's attendance at company conferences; and when visiting telephone men conferred in his office he was accustomed to call for Clay and often seemed to be persuaded by his opinion. 

Clay's official position, if any, had not been announced. 

The nearest was stenographers' gossip, that the president in addressing the board of directors once referred to Clay as "my assistant who is expert in the rural telephone problem."

When at last Clay's work seemed to be progressing successfully beyond the shadow of a doubt, Clay had a great desire to ask President Hudson whether he was “justifying his classical training." The president had implied that classical education gave ability to deal with It was true that during the earlier part of his career at Franklin, Burke had forced him to think seriously about his relations with his fellows, and Swampy of course had never missed an opportunity to inculcate the importance of learning how to get along with people. 

He had come into contact with Swampy and Burke while taking a classical course, but their advice had no direct connection with the study of Latin and Greek. 

As far as he could see, every real benefit he had obtained from working for an A.B. could have been got equally well from studying science. Down in New York

Arthur had expressed a similar opinion. It might be that his personal experience had been different from that of others. Possibly in his course at Franklin he had missed something that Hudson believed he had gained. 

In the late afternoons after most of the employees had gone Hudson liked to bring Clay to his office for a quiet talk. Once he drifted away from the Bell's business and spoke of his days at Harvard. Clay seized the opportunity to ask the question he so long had wished to ask. John Elbridge Hudson's face lighted with the thought-provoking smile of an old professor. He was back in his classroom providing enigmas for youth. "The study of science," he said, "without a doubt, could be as beneficial as the study of the classics, if it were possible to study science with the same teachers, in the same classes, in the same ways."

There was a large rural telephone company in northern New England that the Bell had wished to control. 

The company was in bad financial condition and had been negotiating with the Bell for some time, but on each occasion that a favorable outcome seemed imminent something had arisen to break off the deal. Thinking Clay's experience qualified him for such a mission, President Hudson sent Clay to New Hampshire to attend a stockholders meeting.

Clay spoke of the struggles of the Hemlock company under somewhat similar circumstances and was successful in getting the attention of his listeners. In a few days he was back in Boston with a new proposition. 

This proposition was approved by a committee of Bell officials and was sent to the board for ratification. 

At the meeting of the board Clay was called in and was asked to explain his method of appraisal, and was

BIG BUSINESS 

401 adversely criticized for offering too liberal terms. The 

Bell had no serious rival in that region, and as the rural company was approaching bankruptcy it would have to accept almost any offer the Bell cared to make. Clay said that in setting the price he had been influenced by a desire to get the favorable opinion of the stockholders and the community. As valuable as the wires and right of way, he thought, if the Bell intended to operate there, was the good-will that would result from a just valuation.

One or two of the members of the board supported Clay's view, but the majority objected to any mixing of propaganda and business. The measure hung in the balance until an eloquent speech from President Hudson carried it over. As a result of this discussion Clay was regarded with favorable interest by a few of the directors. Most of them marked him off as Hudson's new yes-man.

Perhaps because Clay reminded him of students he had had at Harvard, Hudson's regard for Clay increased. He formed the habit, despite his best judgment, of talking to Clay about executive problems. The young man might not be able to give useful advice, but at least he was a sympathetic listener. In time of stress there was no one the president would sooner trust.

After an exhausting day with contentious associates, he looked forward to a quiet talk with his young assistant. He had found that Clay's ethical view of telephone service and youthful outlook would bring new courage.

The greatest moment in Hudson's life had come on that morning in 1889, when as newly elected president, he entered the board room of the Bell to address the directors. During years of labor he had glimpsed the

future of telephony and the destiny of the Bell; but as a subordinate he had had no opportunity to enforce his views. At last it was his right to formulate Bell policy. 

He told them that while a young lawyer he had decided to cast his lot with the Bell because of the company's reputation for efficiency and service. Good service, he believed then and he believed now, produces good-will; and good-will, good business. Through experience he had become convinced that the best telephone service can be provided only by a single unified organization, with the largest possible number of subscribers, with the longest continuous wires having standard equipment, and the fewest possible switches and exchanges. Such a unified system could be realized only through the amalgamation of all local and independent lines into one great company. Best fitted by efficiency and size to be the central company in such a union was the Bell. It therefore was his intention, as long as he remained chief executive, to devote himself to the realization of that end. 

The response was instantaneous and enthusiastic. 

The board was prepared to back him to a man. But within a very few weeks serious disruption occurred. 

While the president planned to effect the union by drawing subscribers and lines into the Bell system through the lure of efficient service on the one hand and friendly cooperation on the other, several directors demanded speedier action. They attempted to force independents into the Bell system by means very much resembling those used by J. Henry Rogers in northern New York.

From that time on Hudson worked in an atmosphere of strife. He was usually able to gain support for structural improvements and financial undertakings, for no one could deny his ability. But any proposal

BIG BUSINESS 

403 remotely altruistic could be carried through only after exhausting struggle. A little group of directors stood staunchly with him, but the majority considered him a theorist who required constant watching. Too many of his plans, they said, were of the same cloth as his habit of writing his memoranda in Greek. The years he had spent as a college professor had put him out of touch with modern competitive conditions. His idea of cooperating with rivals was absurd, as was his statement that the welfare of the Bell was dependent on the welfare of all other telephone lines-even those across the sea.

In one of his afternoon talks with Clay he told the young man that when he had failed to inspire the board with better standards of telephone service and the desire to form a mutually beneficial union of lines, he had turned to the task of building up the Bell financially and territorially, that there might be a company fit to assume leadership when the opportunity came. He had been successful in perfecting a great organization, but he feared he had been playing into greedy hands. He would have withdrawn from the telephone business had he not believed that the right way to reform a large business organization is from the inside. 

Once in one of his happier imaginative moments he said he did not regard the telephone as a rival of the telegraph, as merely a device for sending staccato messages, but rather as a social facility of vast scope, as a means of opening out the spirit and expanding life. 

He confided to Clay his hope-his belief-that the day would come, not in his life, perhaps not in Clay's, but he knew the day would come, when any executive, whether in Europe, America, Asia, or the isles of the sea, would be able to converse instantly with any other

executive; and what such ease of communication would bring to pass, in developing a common language, in promoting trade and exchange, in banishing over-production, misunderstanding, and war, he could not venture to predict. In his work as president he had taken few into his confidence, but he had labored unceasingly for these aims.

The chief obstacle at this time to the union of lines that President Hudson had envisaged was a difference of opinion regarding the best means for supplying electric current to the wires. At an early date Bell engineers had despaired of keeping at the peak of efficiency the thousands of wet or dry cells scattered at the homes of subscribers. They favored the use of a great central battery placed at the exchange where it would be kept in perfect condition. To escape the payment of royalties on central battery apparatus, the independents tried to use almost any device that gave promise of circumventing Bell patents. This difference in equipment prevented them from connecting with the Bell system even when inclined to cooperate.

The multiplicity of devices used by the independent companies and their failure to adopt the central battery made the standardization of equipment impossible. The 

Bell's Western Electric Company had attempted to supply all lines at home and abroad with every sort of apparatus needed, but the constantly increasing diversity in design greatly complicated their business and increased manufacturing costs. President Hudson advocated free engineering advice and a reduction of royalties on Bell patents as a means of inducing independents to use standard central battery supplies, but the board would not follow his suggestion. They tried to force rivals to use Bell apparatus by making it diffi-

BIG BUSINESS 

405 cult to obtain any other kind of equipment. The results of this policy were disastrous both at home and abroad. 

Some American independents began to make their own supplies, and overseas business fell to a low ebb. The loss of foreign trade was a very serious matter, as it was the most profitable part of the Western Electric's business. In alarm the board quickly sent two of the 

Bell's best engineers to England to acquaint the British with the advantages of the central battery system. 

In the spring previous to Joseph Clay's graduation from college these representatives of the Bell met the principal engineers in England, including Gavey, engineer-in-chief of the government owned postal system, and Gill of the National, the only other telephone company in England. With the help of McElroy, the Bell's resident agent, they found no great difficulty in convincing nearly every engineer they met of the rather obvious superiority of the central battery. They were in fact so successful that they returned to America in the summer of 1899 with a contract with the National Company for the installation at Bristol of a central battery exchange.

The future of the Western Electric's foreign trade then seemed assured. All that was necessary now was to build a model exchange at Bristol. While the equipment was being manufactured, Bell engineers were able to incorporate improvements that made it better adapted to English use, especially where extension lines were contemplated. A new repeater coil was devised. 

The greatest care was taken to have each item exactly right. When in September McElroy acknowledged receipt of the equipment, he informed the board that for some reason the late enthusiasm for the central battery seemed to be dying out. In December the Bristol

exchange was completed, but McElroy cabled that the National Company was thinking of dismantling it without even giving it a trial. 

The Bell directors were furious when they received this report. They angrily attributed the National 

Company's repudiation of the central battery system to 

British stupidity and British hate for everything American. They also blamed McElroy and the two engineers they had sent abroad. In spite of their customary heckling and opposition the directors looked to Hudson to carry them through every crisis. After several conferences they decided to ask the president himself to go to England to ferret out the trouble and enlighten the British concerning the advantages of the central battery system. They said that if he did not feel that he could go, he should search the country over to find the best engineer to send.

President Hudson kept his own counsel. He did not believe that the situation called for another engineer. If the telephone authorities in England were not now familiar with the advantages of the central battery, it was because they were unwilling to listen. What was needed was not an engineer, but an envoy, a man who could gain their good-will, and get them to lay aside their prejudices, and consider telephone affairs with an open mind. As the Bell representative he sent 

Joseph Clay.

On a bright day in late March Joseph Clay sat snugly wrapped on the deck of a ship that was rapidly approaching the shores of England. The first sight of land would soon be due. Most of the passengers were in the cabins for the sea was rough and the time convenient for making preparations for going ashore. As

BIG BUSINESS 

407 Clay watched the prow of the vessel rise and fall with each on-coming wave, his thoughts turned to the difficult task before him. By what means he could win the good-will of telephone executives and engineers under a different form of government, under circumstances different socially and financially from those to which he had been accustomed, he did not know. A great conference of telephone men had been planned for May 11 at the Trocadero restaurant. At that time the central battery system would be thoroughly. discussed. It was necessary that he gain the cooperation of as many engineers as possible before that date. He tried to plan a campaign, but lacked fruitful ideas. 

The ship that every minute was taking him nearer to Europe was taking him nearer Dora Reed. It had been some months since Dora had written, but the evening before he sailed he had seen the Pendletons. Fanny had a letter from Elsie Cheney, Dora's artist friend.

She and Dora had left Florence and were staying in Milan. Mrs. Van Alstyne's death made it necessary that they return to New York early in June. If he could finish in London in time, he could go to Milan before June first and find Dora-find her perhaps in the great white cathedral-or at the resort that the letter described as hidden among the northern mountains. He checked his riotous thoughts. Had he gone abroad to find Dora Reed and sail with her to America?

A few hours later Clay stepped from the boat train and found McElroy waiting to take him to his hotel. 

At dinner McElroy recounted the familiar story of the British telephone. In 1869 the post office had taken over all telegraph lines but had operated them at a loss. 

Later it seized the telephone wires, assuming that their competition caused the loss. As deficits increased and

the price of service was excessive, the public demanded telephone competition, and a license was given to the National Company to operate its lines until 1911. The 

Postal System continued to be grievously mismanaged, and nobody knew whether the National Company would be permitted to continue after its license expired. 

The two systems failed to cooperate, and telephone business was a mess. Those in authority were vacillating in policy and always ultra-conservative. Far too often telephone affairs were used as a political pawn. 

The two Bell engineers who had been sent over the previous summer had gained engineering support for the central battery, both in the National Company and in the Postal System, but what they had accomplished had been wiped out through the influence of the chairman of the government committee on telephones, a member of Parliament called Brayton. "He scares the wits out of telephone men in every crisis," concluded 

McElroy. "He thinks he is an expert, and is so curt and peremptory he won't listen to argument." "If he won't listen to us," suggested Clay, "we ought to get him into a public meeting where he'll hear the opinions of the best telephone men in England." "You're thinking of the conference May 11 at the 

Trocadero ?" "Yes. There's time to canvass all the engineers and executives. We'll begin right away to have the newspapers give the meeting publicity. We'll see that our arguments are presented so strongly that Brayton can't deny the superiority of the central battery system." "Perhaps not, if he will attend," murmured McElroy gloomily. "Will attend? How can he afford not to attend if he considers himself an authority on telephones?"

BIG BUSINESS 

409 For the next few days Clay and McElroy were busy getting out lists of men to be interviewed and in deciding who were the best persons to approach each. 

Clay took upon himself the task of winning over the engineers, and left McElroy to pull wires with government officials and, most important of all, to find a way to interview Brayton.

About the middle of April Clay and McElroy called in their agents. The results were encouraging. It was evident that nearly every telephone man in England would attend the meeting and that the discussion would favor the central battery. Every one seemed optimistic except McElroy. He handed Clay a letter that contained Brayton's curt announcement that he would not attend the meeting on May 11 as he expected to be in the country at that time. "I couldn't get near him," wailed McElroy. "I sent one of his friends, a member of the House, but it didn't do any good. Brayton told his friend that he had already noticed that the newspapers were being stuffed with copy concerning that meeting." "Who is this man Brayton that everybody's afraid of?" demanded Clay. "What's back of it all? How does he happen to be interested in telephones?" "He's a bachelor who inherited a country estate at Bridgeton," answered McElroy. "He studied law but never practiced. He likes golf. Several years ago he formed a club in his neighborhood, and donated most of the land for the links. When the National Company surveyed for a line across land the club needed, and cut some of the largest trees, he protested. The National 

Company continued operations, and he took the matter to the court and lost. But the old bullhead wouldn't give up. He stood for Parliament in his borough and

by influence in the House of Commons was able to get rid of the National Company's wires. He immediately set himself up as a critic of telephone legislation. As a matter of principle, he opposes every move we make to improve the telephone business. As chairman of the government committee on telephones he has been so pestered by Bell agents that, they say, he even suspects his own butler of being a telephone spy." "Did you ever try to reach him through golf?" "Two years ago we found a newspaper man who played championship golf. We got him the privilege of the links, and the right introductions, and sent him to an inn kept by the Bridgeton pro, a distant relative of Brayton's. All went finely for several games, but as soon as our man mentioned telephones, Brayton shut like a clam and never played with him again.” "I believe I'll see Brayton myself," said Clay. "Are you a top player?" demanded McElroy. "I don't know much about golf, but I think Bridgeton would be a good place to learn. If you can get the proper introductions I believe I'll go to that inn and get the pro to teach me how to play." 

McElroy gazed a moment at Clay incredulously. "Very well," he said, "I'll make the arrangements. 

President Hudson directed me to do everything that could add to your success or comfort."

XLIV  

When Clay opened his eyes one morning in late April he saw the quaint furnishings of an old English inn, the sombre trim and many-paned casement windows. 

Outside birds were singing and a cart was creaking along the road. There tilted against the oaken dresser was a golf-bag full of clubs; and downstairs, he remembered, Higbee would soon be waiting to give his first lesson. 

From his open window he saw a silver stream full to the brim and spilling over into damp meadows and pastures. The grass was still so short that the country looked like a smoothly shaven lawn. At the water's edge were elms; and on higher ground scattered oaks, not yet in full leaf, spread broadly into the light and air. 

Well-kept hedgerows separated pastures from cultivated fields in which growing crops were faintly green. At the head of the valley the gray gables and roofs of the Brayton estate rose above trees. 

In the parlor Clay found Higbee, a man of thirty-five or forty years, slim and bald, and with a restless manner quite out of keeping with athletic proclivities. Perhaps the pro was disturbed with the prospect of instructing an American, for while Clay ate breakfast he sat at the hearth on one of the heavy settles and asked many questions about golf as played in the United 411

States. Were there many courses, were there any in the interior back from the seashore, and who were the people who played? All of which information Clay supplied to the best of his ability.

When breakfast was over Clay stepped into the village street, seen but dimly the previous evening. On the left was a stone church with churchyard and rectory, and on the right a group of stone cottages and a shop. 

In the distance a high-arched bridge crossed the stream. 

Shading the inn in the forks of the road were three or four old oaks. Higbee, who was waiting with the caddy, scarcely allowed Clay a glance. The pro was anxious to take on a new pupil, as the course at this time of year was almost deserted. At the hunting season there were more players, but the links were too rough and unconventional to be popular. 

They followed a path that led to the stone stables and up the bank to a level expanse where the course conveniently extended toward the inn. The links, Higbee said, using the privilege of a relative to criticize his patron, had been laid out by Brayton himself. A guest would do well to speak of the defects as hazards. 

Except at the beginning and end, the fairways ran along slopes, or zigzagged among copses and hills, and the greens were usually artificial levels. Everywhere the rough was wild, but Brayton was not inclined to make improvements.

After a shower the sun was breaking through the clouds. Strange birds were singing in the thickets on the bank. Smoke rose above the thatched cottages and from the tall chimneys of the inn. The valley stream reflected its bordering elms. Daisies fresh and crimson were underfoot. Clay found it hard to keep his mind on golf. Back at Pomeroy, just as the pro had become

interested in his game, Waldo had torn him away to play polo. With thoughts on the spring morning and birds, he teed off. Higbee did not deign to look where the ball went. He murmured something about the 

American style of playing, and dismissed the caddy for the day.

Thereupon a lecture ensued concerning the game of golf that had been played in the British Isles for hundreds of years. The form that had been developed was merely the natural way of making the necessary movements, the way the human body had found easiest. 

Fundamentals must come first; on them the science. and art of the game could be built. Accordingly he drilled Clay on stance, elbow, upswing, and downswing until Clay became weary and he irritable. At last the pro said he wished to postpone instruction until the following morning. He believed Clay would be better able to keep his mind on the lessons if he used the remainder of the day to satisfy his curiosity concerning the creatures that inhabited the course. 

At the stables Clay paused, thinking he would call for the hunting horse that McElroy had quartered there for his use, but birds were singing loud in the nearby copses, and he decided to proceed on foot. Sparrows were busy in the ivy that covered the buttressed church. 

Swifts circled overhead. At the rear of the rectory rooks cawed from tall trees. In front of the stone cottages women were working in gardens, and further down the street where the houses stood close to the rough stone walk, women were working in gardens at the rear. Open cottage doors revealed barest necessities, but floors and cumbersome tables were scrubbed with scrupulous neatness.

At the arched bridge he gazed into the slow moving water. Forget-me-nots grew at the stream's edge, and on the bank was blue speedwell-bluer than that at home, and taller, and growing in greater mass. Down stream white blossoms floated, almost as showy as if apple trees had shed their bloom on the dark surface. 

Around an abutment he followed a path and found that water crowfoot had produced the floating floral display. 

It trailed its slender waving stems in the narrowed stream while it sent white petals into the light and air. 

Where the path curved around a bend a water bird rose in hurried flight. Everywhere birds were singing. 

Beside a hedgerow that came down from the slope he paused to consult a guide he had brought from London. 

In plainest view a blackbird with glossy plumage was singing a flute-like song, and only a few feet away was a chaffinch with reddish breast and ringing voice. 

From distant trees came the soft call of the cuckoo. 

The path followed the edge of a pasture speckled with crimson-tipped daisies. In the shade of the hedge conspicuous lords-and-ladies were in bloom, stiff bold arums, like jacks-in-the-pulpit, and here and there were fritillaries with ivory cups. Where hedges crossed, he sat on a stile to listen to the songsters. One near at hand with much animation sang a variety of clear rich notes. It was shyer than the others, but when finally located showed a spotted breast, looking in every respect like a thrush. Without a doubt it was the mavis or throstle.

Over the stile and up the slope he followed the path to a park-like woodland having little undergrowth.

There thousands of bluebells filled the hollows with blue water pools, thousands of blue flowerlets that tinted the ground with the color of the sky. At home such a

floral display might be found in a meadow, but never in a forest. Once near Hemlock he had seen viper's bugloss cover acres of gravelly pasture with bright blue, a sight so startling that judgment almost questioned its reality, but a single plant growing alone was only a vile weed imported from Europe. Following a different hedge on his return he saw the white thorn in bloom and the twisted black thorn crowded with crimson buds. 

In the late afternoon wishing to explore more widely, he called for his horse and asked Higbee about roads. "Roads? That's a proper question to take to the squire," answered Higbee who evidently had been awaiting a fit occasion for presenting the newcomer to his patron.

As they rode up the valley to the manor house Higbee explained that Brayton was a collector of maps. He seldom returned from town without bringing a new sheet. It was all the same to him whether it showed 

Britain or Cathay, or whether the ink was scarcely dry or was hundreds of years old. He pored over maps of every sort with the interest that some people read fiction. Nothing could please him more than to answer questions about roads, as it would give him an opportunity to display his collection. 

At an iron gate in a low-coped wall overgrown with vines they entered a drive that climbed the hillside. 

Massive beeches covered the slope. Higbee said the squire loved trees. He had made a map that showed the location of every tree of importance on his estate. 

They soon came to terraces and stone steps that led to a Tudor manor house with mullioned windows of many panes. This entrance, however, was more ornamental than practicable, as the drive continued to the level plot of ground on the southern exposure.

They were soon shown into the library where Higbee introduced Clay as an American whom Wolverton had sent up to learn to play golf in a quiet place. Brayton's greeting was formal. He was a stocky, clean-shaven, middle-aged man, with large nose, heavy eyebrows, and stiff black hair. Like Higbee he inquired about the state of golf in America, and soon revealed an appalling ignorance of conditions in the United States. One could believe he feared that if he were to step into the rough on an American course he would expose himself to capture by the Indians. "Higbee thought perhaps you would be willing to show me a map of this region," remarked Clay, ill enduring Brayton's superior smile and inappropriate questioning. "I am never quite comfortable in new surroundings until I orient myself." "Yes, indeed; I think perhaps I might find a map that could serve your purpose," replied Brayton with the suspicion of a twinkle in his deep-set eyes. He opened the door into a small room that had recently been extended from the side of the library. There was a large globe and tables. The walls displayed a modern map of England and several framed mercator maps in ancient faded colors. Map cabinets with wide shallow drawers had been set between the windows. He seated 

Clay at a small table where a map of the surrounding region was shown under glass. "There," Brayton said, "is a sheet covering about ten miles radius from Bridgeton." He waved Higbee to a chair, and stood awaiting results, evidently having his doubts whether an American could have cartographic sense.

"Let's see," murmured Clay, shifting about in his seat, and noting the angle at which the sunlight entered the window. "The table has been set by compass." "You might change it a point possibly," answered

Brayton with suddenly aroused interest, and he continued to observe Clay intently as he studied the map. "From either this point or this," remarked Clay with pencil on the glass, "one could get the most extended view of this valley." "Oh, if it's outlook you want, let me get you a contour map," exclaimed Brayton pulling out a drawer. "No, no, thank you," answered Clay, "the drainage seems carefully done. That will serve my purpose."

Brayton was visibly pleased. He drew up a chair and joined Clay in studying the map. He soon became so impressed with the visitor's imaginative interpretation of maps that he enthusiastically pointed out several matters of local interest, among which was the site of a ruined castle. He said it was best reached by horse, and evidently was about to suggest that they ride together to the location-but he suddenly checked himself, rose from the table, and closed the half-opened cabinet drawer.

After dinner that evening, while the sun was a half hour high, Clay set out alone to find the castle. From the inn he took the left fork and rode a mile and turned into a westward running lane that Brayton had pointed out. The banks at the sides were covered with primroses, the freshest of spring flowers, yet exotic-looking to American eyes. Soon the banks became so high that, mounted as he was, he could not see into the adjacent fields. Apparently this deserted lane, before it had been eroded by the rains of unnumbered years, had been the highway that led to the castle. Now it

was little better than a watercourse. His horse picked its way with difficulty as he mounted the slope. After crossing the remains of a moat he climbed to a level area a hundred yards in diameter, in the center of which were the low ruins of a keep. The knoll was enclosed by a fence, and the level was well sodded and contained four or five large oaks.
