Higbee had said that Brayton had studied the history of the castle and was caring for it at his own expense. The ruins at some time had probably been robbed of stone, for there was no loose refuse, grass grew to the castle's edge, and the walls though solidly built were now but twenty or thirty feet high. A variety of blossoming plants and ferns grew in crevices and on the broad stones. Swallows that looked like those that used to live in the old barn in 

Hemlock circled overhead.

Clay seated himself on one of the huge blocks. The sun was sinking behind the hills at the rear. It lighted the valley with its slender river and the hedgerows that divided the farm-land into plots of various shapes and sizes. The church tower and the gables and chimneys of the inn rose from among trees. Smoke curled upward from cottages. English rain and the agriculture of generations had rounded and smoothed each wild roughness of the landscape and had given the easilymoulded country a soft and velvety appearance. Every field was cleanly cultivated, not a frambly corner anywhere. Every inch of the soil was used to best advantage. Even the thickets and woods had been husbanded to produce the best return in fuel and poles. Year after year the soil had supplied food, and its fertility had been maintained. All that the eye could see was the result of thought and labor through hundreds of

years. Nor was this peaceful scene unique. It could be duplicated all through this land. It was the slow but certain product of English love of order and stability.

But this past did not belong solely to England. Had not his forefathers lived here when these stone cottages were built? Was it not in very truth his people that had won the Magna Charta and laid the foundations of constitutional government? What was the source of the freedom he had enjoyed in America? Not unoccupied land, nor anything the pioneers found on that side of the Atlantic. It was the product of the principles his ancestors had established here in England by blood and sweat. Who could deny him a share in scenes like these? As he turned his eyes to the glimmering landscape a more devout and greater patriotism filled his mind and heart.

Perhaps at this very castle some victory for freedom had been won. In the far-off old unhappy days, swords and shields had clashed over this hilltop and blood had drenched these stones. His people had fought here long ago in a half-savage age. They had shed their blood that coming generations might practice the arts of peace. Civilization had gone far since then. Homes and farms had replaced castles. It was not probable that war would ever again disturb these peaceful fields. 

At too great a cost, step by step, had the human race gained its freedom to have it ever again contested by brutal men. 

Bats fluttered about the ruin. the east reflected the red sunset. 

One small cloud in 

The horse neighed uneasily from a sapling in the moat where it had been tied. From the gable of the inn a light shone in the

dusk. As he rode toward the village he felt that from one day in the country he had gained a better understanding of England than from five weeks in London. 

The next morning the lessons in golf were resumed. 

Higbee guaranteed rapid improvement if Clay was able to keep his mind on the game. The squire had a similar fault. Once he could round the links in 72, but he had deteriorated because of half-hearted playing. When alone he would stride over the course with his eyes on the birds and the sky, and delayed so long between strokes he forgot his score. No one could play golf who did not keep his mind on the game. 

Clay was now in a mood to follow this advice and soon regained the form he had learned at Pomeroy. 

As he had been active in a variety of athletic sports and had acquired, contrary to Higbee's belief, no bad habits in golf, he was soon able to use his muscles according to the desired pattern. The pro congratulated himself on the success of his instruction. He thought he never had had a more responsive pupil. Within a day or two he was smiling with satisfaction because of Clay's long straight shots without spin.

While they were engaged in practice at the first tee Brayton and the Bridgeton rector came up the path from the inn. After conversation concerning Clay's visit to the castle, Brayton stood back to see the newcomer drive. Clay foozled the ball. Without comment

Brayton shrugged his shoulders, and waved the rector to the tee. Higbee, who had waited expectantly, was more embarrassed than Clay. He growled about the squire's not remaining to see a second drive. As he was a relative he would speak plainly. The squire was too self-satisfied; lately he had been content to equal the

rector's 85 or 90. The best thing that could happen would be to have some one come who could trounce him roundly. He wouldn't listen to advice. For instance, the squire was accustomed at the second stroke of the sixteenth, where the ground slopes off in a deceiving way, to send the ball hard over the hill straight toward the green although it was certain to slide off to the right into the rough. He always lost a stroke there. 

He had been advised to cut it gently to the left where it would stop on the fairway. The squire was too obstinate to learn, or had been told so often he was determined to use a contrary plan. Another matter-one he hadn't yet told the squire—he would tell Clay. While the squire was in London he had found that it didn't pay to try to escape the sand-trap beyond the copse in the dog-leg of the eleventh. If one sent the ball over the copse into the sand-trap, a good niblick could drop it near the green in the hope of a birdie. If ever the squire should ask Clay to play, he must remember this advice.

Brayton knew every blade of grass on the course, but he had no more strategy than a May beetle. In playing golf, or anything else he did, the squire's greatest delight was to succeed by using the wrong way. 

During succeeding days Brayton gave no evidence of having observed Clay's rapid improvement in golf, but he had noticed the visitor's interest in the unfamiliar creatures that inhabited the course. It was so easy to satisfy the American's curiosity, and name the birds and plants that lived about the links. Once when suddenly a lark sprang singing from the rough and Clay leaped and shouted in excitement, Brayton smiled indulgently, and then to the pro's astonishment ambled up the slope humming a song.

One morning when Clay was probing the thicket near the first green for birds, Higbee rushed from the inn. "The squire's down at the stables talking with the hostler. The rector has sent word he is ill, and the squire is going to ask you to play."

Higbee was greatly excited. While he devoutly hoped that some one would be able to administer punishment to his patron, he thought that a game with the squire was an honor for any man. Clay listened attentively while Higbee rehearsed the strategy of the course. 

The pro advised that every effort should be made to gain points at the beginning as Brayton would play in his usual careless manner until he found it necessary to do better. 

When Brayton appeared, Higbee said he wished to reserve the right to coach his pupil and therefore would not take a part in the game. Brayton made no objection and signaled to Clay to tee off. Before they had passed three holes Clay's long straight shots, to Brayton's surprise, had put the visitor two strokes ahead. 

Higbee smiled confidently. Brayton also seemed pleased and, evidently wishing to encourage the beginner, continued to play in his nonchalant manner until they had sunk the tenth, Clay still two strokes up. 

At the eleventh tee Clay caught the pro's monitory glance, and inspired by hope of a birdie was successful in sending his ball with moderate force down the slope to a point in the fairway slightly above the copse that grows in the dog's-leg. Brayton evidently was not impressed by the shot, but as the game was getting well along and he had not yet overtaken his competitor he assumed a more careful poise, and stretched his muscles rhythmically, and determined his direction carefully before he drove. His ball went diagonally into

the dog's-leg and stopped on the fairway far ahead of Clay's. It was evidently exactly where he wanted it, for his deep-set eyes gleamed with satisfaction. Then 

Clay used his brassie to send the ball high over the copse, cutting out all of the dog's-leg. It fell, as they soon ascertained, smack into the trap, not sinking an inch in the stiff sand. "Sorry," exclaimed Brayton, "I've been in that trap more than once myself."

Brayton's next stroke sent him across the angle of the dog's-leg where another long straight shot would place him on the green. Ahead of him on the right was the sand-trap that held Clay's ball; on the left was the copse. As Higbee had expected, Brayton's next shot was unfortunate, for he sent the ball into the copse adjacent to the green. Then Clay with niblick-mashie lofted the ball with a strong stroke. It fell within the entrance of the green and rolled toward the cup. Brayton took three strokes to sink the hole, and Clay's birdie stood him three up. 

In playing the twelfth, thirteenth, and fourteenth,

Brayton went into his stride and was able to reduce 

Clay's lead to one. "Hold steady," whispered Higbee. "The squire's giving it all he has."

The ground approaching the fifteenth is fairly level, and gave Clay the opportunity to use one or two of his long strokes. With his drive he reached a point on the fairway that Brayton could not equal, but finally the balls lay on the green side by side, on even terms, ten or twelve feet from the cup. Clay's putt overshot its mark, and Brayton sank his. When Clay got on with his second putt he was so disturbed with his first failure that he missed again, and the score became one in Brayton's favor,

Higbee was disappointed, but the next hole was the sixteenth where the squire nearly always fumbled the second stroke. Brayton hastened to the tee. He seemed elated with his recent gains. His drive was good, and Clay was equally successful. At the next stroke Brayton took unusual care. His ball rose with speed, and seemed to be bearing right as it passed over the hill. Higbee watched its course critically but gave no judgment. Whether Brayton had been guilty of his habitual error and had overshot, Clay had no way of knowing. All he could do was to follow the routine. provided by the pro. Accordingly he struck the ball with moderate force and directed it toward the left. It passed over the hill barely clearing the grass. When the little group walked over the crest they found, sure enough, that Brayton had sent his ball to a point where it had glanced down the hill into the dense rough far to the right of the green, but Clay's lay where an easy shot would place it near the cup. Brayton glanced furtively at Higbee and evidently was disturbed. When the balls were finally holed, Clay was one point up.

Despite the best the Clay could do, Brayton evened it up on the seventeenth.

The fairway is well maintained and level as it leads to the eighteenth and par is four. As Clay prepared to tee off he caught the tense gaze with which at a little distance Brayton and the caddies were regarding him. 

Higbee on the other side was wringing one hand in the other as if he would give a year of his life to have hold of Clay's driver. Clay tried to summon up the control that had served in college in so many contests, but a different sort of skill was needed now. His ball did not seem to travel with the speed he had hoped for, but it went straight and true down the fairway nearly

half way to the green. It was not an unusual drive, but 

Brayton took a long time in preparing to follow. He knew that he lacked control if he attempted to drive too far. He decided to be content with distance equaling Clay's. His ball kept to the fairway and rolled a few feet beyond the opponent's. 

As Clay again addressed his ball he gazed down the fairway several hundred yards to the narrowed opening, only ten or twelve feet wide, where it entered the green. 

It seemed impossible to pass through so narrow a gap even if one could send the ball so far. It was the wisest plan not to try for the green but to put the ball into position before the gap, and to concede an additional stroke in approaching the cup. 

But that was exactly what Brayton would wish. He was superior in putting.

If he himself had any special gift in golf, it was distance with control. He decided to make a supreme effort. 

The ball rose a little higher than before, but not too high; and it kept on going, on and on, meeting the ground at a sharp angle between the bunkers, and disappearing on the green. Unrestrained cheers were in order, for it was a magnificent second shot. "I'd give ten guineas to see the squire beaten," Higbee whispered hoarsely to a caddy.

As Brayton seemed certain to lose unless he reached the green he tried to duplicate Clay's feat, but his ball stopped in a bunker. 

They found Clay's ball only six feet from the cup. "Well, my American friend," exclaimed Brayton cheerfully, "you will win with an easy putt. I congratulate you. You have given me the finest game I have played in months."

With great skill Brayton with niblick raised the ball from the bunker and set it down on the green about

twenty feet from the cup. Brayton then studied the situation carefully. He examined the cup and examined his putter. He took his time in sighting from ball to cup and in studying the roll of the green. Once or twice he altered his stance, but at last he hit the ball. 

It ran, Clay thought, just like a chipmunk to its holeand, just like chipmunk, it dived in. 

Every one was smiling when Clay stepped to his ball. 

He would win if he made the six foot putt. "Click."

The ball went straight to the cup, but turned on the lip -and remained poised. ball. 

Higbee and Brayton stared blankly at the cup and 

Clay was the first to laugh, and they all laughed. 

It seemed ridiculous that a blade of grass could make the contest end in a tie.

The next morning the play-off occurred, and Brayton won. He was so genial he took Clay home for luncheon. On succeeding mornings if the rector came, or the friend from Lyall, Higbee made out the foursome. If Brayton and Clay played together the game often lagged while their attention was diverted to the birds or plants. Clay found abundant evidence, as he had already suspected, that Brayton's devotion to golf was largely due to his desire to be afield. When the sun appeared after rain Brayton was eager to ride while the drops were still sparkling on twigs and leaves. Wet as it was Clay would have preferred to walk, for May was at its freshest. Even when mounted Brayton was an excellent guide. It was surprising what he could see from the back of a horse.

When the rain continued into the evening and Brayton was not occupied with his Times or communications from London, he sent his carriage to bring Clay to the Manor. He had found that his new acquaintance

was apt at turning discussion into new quarters, or at least in giving old topics a fresh connotation. After dinner one evening when Brayton had traced on a map the boundaries of an extensive gorse common, Clay inquired about the peculiarities of the plant. "Doesn't gorse grow in America?" Brayton asked in surprise. "I have never heard of its growing in the United States." "Well, you Americans can't expect to have every thing. I understand that one of your millionaires did carry away a castle and was disappointed that he couldn't make nightingales and skylarks sing around it." "Perhaps too many English sparrows lived in the neighborhood," replied Clay with a quizzical smile. "About my home your house sparrows drive off our other birds." 

They have "Our birds don't mind the sparrow. learned his ways and know how to deal with him. They have a mutual understanding that keeps him in his place. In time you and your birds will learn." "Perhaps it's not wholly a matter of time and learning. Conditions of environment and climate may be more potent. No sooner had your tall white daisy reached our shores than it spread through the fields and blossomed as it never had in England." "What, the ox-eye daisy? I don't doubt it. That glaring cosmopolitan flower? That's a typical emi- 

On the contrary, our grant. Nothing clings to it. little dooryard daisy loves England too well to leave it. It has had English ways bred into it so long that it thrives only in an English sod."

"I don't wonder you Englishman break into poetry when you speak of your crimson-tipped daisy." “Poetry? Isn't it a fact that our daisy grows in every grassy plot in these islands because it has learned to adapt itself to English agriculture?" "I think English history is too short for that." "It hasn't been too short to teach weeds how to resist a farmer's efforts to destroy them." “Are you sure of that? Perhaps weeds grew originally in the open spaces-the prairies, flood-plains, scars of fires and tornadoes. When pioneers cut away the forest, perhaps plants from the open spaces were already fitted to occupy cultivated fields." 

Then as soon as the American Indian began to cultivate maise with a crooked stick, I suppose he had to fight weeds." "I suppose he did. Almost as soon as America was settled we sent you a ragweed and black-eyed Susan as samples of our native weeds." "And, I understand we showed our appreciation by returning burdock, shepherd's-purse, plantain, and dock. That's one occasion when we over-reached you. 

Your weeds didn't prove so troublesome as ours. I don't think your theory of ready-made weeds accounts for their aggressiveness and persistence." "As agriculture moved westward, I believe, it gathered the most aggressive and persistent weeds from one country after another." "Hadn't we better say developed instead of gathered?

Perhaps the weeds of each country had learned how to circumvent the type of agriculture prevailing in that country."

"Then, perhaps, similar species of weeds occurring in different countries indicate likenesses in the culture to these countries?" "Why not? I don't doubt that you Americans have had to fight English weeds because you follow English ways of living." "True enough. But we in America are also afflicted with an army of weeds that nature fitted to survive only where large areas are slightly cultivated in the American way with minimum labor." "Perhaps such weeds would learn in time to survive when cultivation became more intense. Your theory that nature endowed certain plants with the characteristics of weeds ignores the adaptations that come from changed environment. Until some one can point out the land where the apple and other such fruits grew ready-made and wild, I shall cling to my view that weeds are plants that have gradually learned to resist man's efforts to destroy them." 

Clay rose to take his leave. "The apple? Didn't it flourish to perfection in the Garden of Eden?"

On a subsequent evening Brayton reverted to the proposed visit to the gorse common and suggested that the excursion be postponed until the gorse came into better bloom. "I have to be in London next week," said Clay. "Perhaps you plan to attend the telephone meeting on May 11?" 

Clay was startled. "Yes, I am connected with the 

Bell telephone company. I wish to see that the advantages of the central battery system are properly presented at that meeting."

"What are the advantages?" Brayton asked as easily as if he was speaking of birds or weeds.

Clay stated the advantages as explicitly as he could, and concluded from Brayton's questioning that the Bell's traditional enemy was not so ignorant of telephone theory as McElroy believed. "If one were to use the central battery system it would be necessary to buy apparatus covered by Bell patents, would it not?" "Yes," replied Clay. "That would place a public utility under the control of a private monopoly?" "A better way to think of it is that the public utility was obtaining interchange of service by cooperating with lines that used standardized equipment." 

As they discussed the advantages of a union of lines it soon became evident that Brayton's stubborn opposition to telephone advancement had arisen rather from a patriotic desire to protect the postal system from the inroads of greedy corporations, than from any selfish motive. Clay drew a parallel between Brayton's desires and those of President Hudson, and in so doing was able to present President Hudson's aims in detail.

A day or two later Clay returned to London. On 

May 11th he and McElroy attended the dinner at the 

Trocadero. Four or five hundred telephone men were present, among whom were most of those whose opinion had weight with either the Post Office or the National Company. The discussion at first concerned the unfriendly legislation with which the Government was threatening the National Company, but it soon shifted to the use of the central battery. McElroy and Clay did not speak, but they had the satisfaction of hearing engi-

neers whom they had recently interviewed present the arguments they would have used had they taken part in the discussion. Before the evening was over, it became evident that British engineers, with very few exceptions, favored the opening of the Bristol exchange, and a wider use of the central battery system. 

On Clay's return to Bridgeton one mid-May morning

Brayton received him cordially, and insisted that they start at once to visit the gorse common. Their friendship, like the gorse, seemed to have flourished in the interim. Near at hand Clay saw the gorse as only an ugly prickly shrub with a pea-like blossom, but from a slight eminence the view of the gorse common was inspiring. The rough earth was clothed in mounds and billows of flame-golden bloom. Brayton liked to call it furze-furze for fuel, fences, peated, enjoying the alliteration. He went into great detail in describing the various ways in which the plant can be used. and forage, he re- On their return they spoke of telephones. Brayton said his Times had contained reports of the meeting. 

He had concluded that his aims were not materially different from President Hudson's. It was self-evident that standardized apparatus would have to be used if the telephone were ever to have the extended social use that President Hudson had envisaged. He thought the Bristol exchange could be opened at once, and he would try to have the new exchange on Victoria Street equipped with the central battery. Very soon additional exchanges would be required at Sutherland and at Hull. These naturally would follow the central battery plan.

XLV 

As soon as it was assured that the British telephone authorities would operate the Bristol exchange and construct other exchanges on the central battery plan Clay reported his success to President Hudson by cable, and spoke of extending his journey to Italy. Hudson replied in a long message of congratulation. The reply was so devoid of telegraphic brevity that it reminded 

Clay of their afternoon talks. Hudson mentioned problems that loomed and said he lacked Clay's advice, but since Clay was abroad, it might be well to use the opportunity to inspect one of the automatic exchanges at that time being tested on the Continent. He need not hasten. What he had already accomplished in London was the equivalent of a year's work. 

Then came a letter from Dora, more than a month old. It had gone from Milan to Boston and back across the Atlantic to London. Like Elsie Cheney's letter that Fanny had read the evening before he sailed, it told of Mrs. Van Alstyne's death and the need of 

Dora's return to New York early in June. More important than those facts to Clay was a change in Dora's manner of writing. There was something different in the style, perhaps a little of the old romantic fervor. 

Dora had left Milan and had gone with Elsie Cheney to a mountain resort where Elsie wished to paint. They 432

had found a view resembling one they had seen in the background of a painting in the Brera gallery in Milan. 

Perhaps the old master, shut in from the tumultuous world by staunch stone walls, as she had been, had in that very spot painted his picture, for the place had been famous as prison or villa for hundreds of years. It was possible they might stay for some weeks. Elsie was still sketching. She had not decided what aspect of the scene she could best undertake. The light was constantly changing. One could scarcely believe what a difference that made.

In mid-morning the distances were shortened. The winding shore of Lake Maggiore was clearly visible. 

Valleys and villages were etched with needle fineness. 

Rocky buttresses, twisted and warped, showed every seam and wrinkle. Under the brilliant illumination yellow-tinted vineyards, gray olive orchards, green meadows, were moist with fresh color. If the wind blew from Monte Rosa, these same objects might be seen with every gradation of sun and shadow-often with cloud-swept loveliness. Before late afternoon details became less prominent, and were softly moulded, an effect difficult to reproduce with color. Elsie said that if she looked over the cliff to the pasture below, where the big chestnuts spread their branches, she saw only green islands in a green sea.

Dora spent much time at the outlook. The place was not suited to her work, but it gave the time, and more important the will, to consider what she had accomplished by her years of study. In her reflection she had interwoven her own experiences with the ancient legends. She had made the story parallel her own life, and had dreamed that Joe himself had rescued her from her prison.

The letter ran to a more extended description of the mountain resort but made no further mention of a prison. Joe could not guess what Dora meant by her figurative language, but the letter filled him with a great desire to go to Italy. At Milan he could inspect the automatic exchange and then try to find Dora. 

The next afternoon he crossed to Paris and boarded a train for Basel. A night and a day he ill-endured the slow progress. As he approached the Italian border in mid-afternoon he hoped to get a view of the Alps. The train was weaving this way and that as it followed up a rushing stream. In the dim light of the narrow valley it seemed to increase its speed. It crossed bridges with a roar. Little stations or tool sheds swept by. Just as it seemed that a peak was coming into view, the train turned on a curve, or entered screening foliage or a rock cut. He was baffled until suddenly a stream entered from the east, and he saw a vast peak, rising like the flash of polar light, up into the blue air, where the afternoon sun shed its splendor on ice and rock. The train rumbled through the tunnel, and at evening came to Milan. He was driven to a hotel situated in the angle of two broad streets that converged to a piazza. 

The next morning he hastened to the exchange. It used automatic devices such as the Bell had tried out in its laboratories. The apparatus was cumbersome and uncertain, and required continual attention-how different from the dream of exchanges operating without the touch of a human hand. He saw no reason to change his view, the opinion commonly held by Bell engineers, that automatic switchboards would require additional years of experiment and improvement before they could be depended on to give satisfactory service.

Having thus hastily concluded his inspection, he sought out the Milan address given in Dora's letter. 

He found a quaint pension in a crooked narrow street, and was admitted by a red-cheeked little man whose English was as slight as his Italian. After much talk and gesticulation he learned that Miss Reed and 

Miss Cheney had ten days previously gone to Borlindo.

His informant did not know the name of the inn where they intended to stay, nor whether they would return to Milan before sailing for America.

The train for the north left in the afternoon, and although he had barely an hour, Clay drove to the Piazza del Duomo and stared at the great white cathedral with its infinite pinnacles and its infinite statues. 

In the dim nave he threaded his way among the mighty columns, but soon tiptoed out. An hour or two later he reached Borlindo.

There he inquired for an inn, and was quickly surrounded by a group of beggars, guides, and cabmen, who attempted to address him in broken English and other languages that he did not understand. To escape unpleasant attention, he hastily entered a carriage, and was driven to a shabby hotel. After dinner when he attempted to ascertain the location of the mountain inn he sought, he realized that he had entered on a difficult quest. "An ancient villa conducted as an inn? There were dozens of them," so the albergatore informed him.

Then he asked for an account of the places most commonly visited by tourists, and especially of inns from which the best mountain views could be obtained. 

His informant told him of an ancient monastery that contained many relics of the local saint; of a rock, that falling from the precipitous mountainside and about to

plunge through a chapel roof and destroy the high altar, had been arrested on a tiny ledge and though overbalanced was still miraculously poised in the air; of the mediaeval stronghold where a princess was detained until her lover discovered her through the use of carrier pigeons; of a ruined castle with its subterranean dungeons where instruments of torture were still displayed and where a skeleton was chained by rusty links to the wall; of the mountain grotto long ago discovered by a dumb shepherd girl who found therein an image of the Virgin and suddenly regained her speech, a place now visited by all the dumb or stammerers and those who had difficulty in speaking any language.

These and other historical and religious marvels were described, but as none seemed to give a hint of the place he was seeking, he decided to accept the albergatore's suggestion to engage a carriage the next day and make the circuit followed by most of the travelers who visited the locality.

Driven by a cocchiere who spoke fairly intelligible English, he passed in the clear June morning by villas gay with climbing roses, and soon came to cherry orchards, and red-tiled farmhouses with projecting eaves. In a damp meadow peasant women were swinging scythes. They drove through vineyards where the vines were on trellises, or on broad-topped mulberry trees, once on blossoming almonds. White oxen were drawing a plow. Happy contadini in bright costumes were working in the fields. The cocchiere sang a little song. Clay felt assured that he would overtake Dora before evening came.

Along the way were more villas with wrought iron balconies and gardens with peonies, wistaria arbors, and always more roses. To these a sombre beauty was

often added by groups of dark cypress, planted sparingly as if it were feared that cheapness might come with abundance. In contrast were the gray olive orchards, extending sometimes as far as one could see. They passed through a village where women and children in picturesque costumes stood in the doorways. From time to time the driver stopped to point out a mediaeval building or the wonders of a wayside shrine.

As they climbed higher the country became park-like with groves of beeches and chestnuts. Villas were built on the shoulders of the hills. On the south were the plains of Lombardy, and in the distance a city that might even be far Milan. To the north, across the valley, was a little hillside town enclosed with walls. On several of the heights were ancient churches. At the head of the main valley was a mountain peak topped with snow. When they came to an ancient stronghold of the Sforzas, the cocchiere suggested they remain for luncheon, but as the hillside location of the place did not conform to the description in Dora's letter, Clay urged him on.

The road then turned into a narrower and steeper valley where white glacier water dashed over the boulders. The fresh air of spring came down from the heights, spring like that of New England that he had missed. Flowers vernally delicate grew among the fallen leaves. In a colony of violets he thought he found some of the same species that grew in woods near 

Franklin. The highway then left the stream and descended around the mountain. They passed again through chestnut groves, and came to cultivated land. 

The cocchiere said they were approaching Villa Fontana, where the view was divine, the food excellent, the wine of ancient vintage. Clay's interest rose, as

through the trees that bordered the road he had been catching glimpses of peaks and a lake in a low valley. 

At a bare plastered gateway with the sign, Villa Fontana, they turned in, and followed an avenue of trees until they came to a large villa enclosed within a high wall. Over the wall rose the tops of ancient trees, and through the entrance could be seen carefully kept gardens. Flocks of pigeons circled about the buildings. 

The albergator came to meet his guest; but as he spoke only Italian, summoned his wife, a comely woman, who formerly had been maid to an English lady, and spoke English fluently. Few travelers visited this hostelry in 

June, and the few who were sojourning there had gone that morning on excursion to the top of a neighboring mountain. While Clay ate his luncheon, the hostess recounted the inn's legend. 

In 1627 when Vincenzo II, the last male representative of the Gonzaga family that long had held the duchy of Montferrat, fell ill of an incurable disease, Ferdinand II hoping to win the duchy, kidnapped Bianca, the only daughter of the dying duke and spirited her away. Her lover, the young and brave Duke of Nevers, believing that she had been captured by brigands and was held for ransom, searched widely for her and offered great rewards, but in vain.

The lady had been taken to an ancient villa on the mountainside near Borlindo. There shut in by high walls, she had been provided by Ferdinand II with every comfort, but was kept closely guarded by soldiers, and no one was allowed to enter or leave her prison.

Meanwhile her lover having exhausted other means sent out a company of his sworn companions who took oath never to return until some one had obtained infor-

mation concerning the maiden. One of these, learning that in Piedmont there was an old villa that was closely guarded, found the place and managed to obtain entrance in the guise of a huntsman, and on the pretense of presenting the lady with game, gave her a white pigeon and a whispered word.

The guards becoming suspicious seized the huntsman on his departure and cast him over the cliff, but by means of the white carrier pigeon Bianca sent a message to her lover who came with an army and rescued her. Thus the lovers were united, and Charles, Duke of Nevers, succeeded to the duchy of Montferrat. 

When his hostess finished the story, Clay went to the garden. Roses were everywhere. Near the stable was a quaint urn into which water ran. The structure seemed to be half drinking trough for animals and half fountain. From a paved court made gay with geraniums, stairs descended to a lower level on the verge of the precipice. There in a vine-covered pergola he seated himself. To the north he could see, over wooded hills and mountains, the lofty Alps and Monte Rosa clad in snow. A little to the side where the hills opened was a glimpse of blue Lake Maggiore, and next the walk that ran along the brow of the precipice, were four ancient cypress trees-"four cypress trees," "blue lake with winding shore," "a snowy summit," he read from 

Dora's letter.

This was the very place. And she had said its legend suggested a parallel with her life. It was clear that the idea of her being held in a figurative prison had been suggested by the story, but the meaning she intended to convey, he could not guess. Bianca and her lover had been sweethearts in youth and after absence were again united. He would take that as the parallel. He would

believe, as the result of his journey into distant Italy, he had been able to peep into Dora's heart and learn that her love had not grown cold. The significance of the prison he would learn when he saw Dora. 

He hastened to his amiable hostess. "Yes," she replied eagerly. "Yes, Miss Reed and Miss Cheney have been here." "Where are they now?" "They left scarcely a week since to go to Havre to take ship for America." "Did they return first to Milan?" "No, they went directly to Havre and feared they would not be in time to catch their ship." "Then perhaps they missed it, and are waiting in 

Havre for the next boat," Clay muttered half audibly.

Clay found his driver seated at an outdoor table and asked him to bring the horses at once, as he wished to catch the first train going north from Borlindo. The cocchiere continued to sip his wine and tried blandly to explain that the return from Villa Fontana would be much shorter than the route by which they had come. 

It would require only two or three hours, and besides there was only one train north in the afternoon. It left Borlindo soon after five-too late to reach that day. "Stay in Borlindo all night!" exclaimed Clay as he looked at his watch. "It is not yet three. You said you could make it in two hours. Bring the horses at once.' 99 

The cocchiere glanced at Clay's angry face, and gulped down his wine. He soon brought the carriage; and they sped through the gateway, but at the next slope, where the weary team with low-hanging heads toiled slowly up the grade, Clay thought better of it.

"Don't hasten," he said, "I won't try to make the train this afternoon."

He reached Borlindo in the early evening and studied train connections. The next morning he telephoned

Milan to inquire about ships sailing from Havre, but could get no satisfactory information. If he had been able to take that train the previous afternoon, he thought, it would have given him the day he might lack. 

He wandered about Borlindo and explored old buildings and fortresses. Over an ancient doorway he found a 

Latin inscription, in strange lettering, that he studied with epigraphic interest. He was much elated when a cleric at the neighboring church told him that he had not come far from the proper interpretation. 

The train that he took at five in the afternoon was a local making connections between main lines and requiring a change of cars at Como. It was a short run, but Clay was glad to leave the jerking, groaning conveyance. There was time to walk to a rise of ground where he could get a glimpse of the ancient town and blue lake. The region was of great classic interest. There came to his mind wistful thoughts of 

Pliny's letters and the old recitation room in the Chapel at Franklin. He gazed across the water easterly, and tried to imagine he saw Pliny's villa. A modern palace occupied the slope with terraces, balustrades, and vinecovered pergolas. He soon turned away. The view was not so satisfying as that he had seen when seated on a hard bench in Professor Cowles's classroom.

The northern express proved to be more comfortable, but whenever the train delayed at a station, or stopped to take on water, or backed up at switches or for coupling cars, Clay was tortured. He smiled; he had rushed to Italy to find Dora before she left, and now

he was rushing back again to overtake her before she sailed. Still no speed could satisfy him.

Once he forgot his haste. His mind turned again to 

Dora's letter. Considered in the light of the legend it had seemed to indicate that Dora still loved him. 

Loved whom? Her old sweetheart, Joseph Clay of

Hemlock. He could not be mistaken in that, for they had not met since he went to college. But where was this Joseph Clay of Hemlock? He was not on this train that was rushing each minute nearer Havre. 

Joseph Clay of Hemlock had vanished never to return.

Study, rigorous training, new scenes, and new associates, had produced a new Joseph Clay. Might it not be that this new Joseph Clay lacked those very traits that Dora loved?

He tossed the idea aside. It was morbid and not convincing. The changes of the last five years were superficial. There was but one Joseph Clay. He had lived recently among new scenes and associates, and had learned new modes of expression. He had clothed himself in a conventional coat of many colors, but he had not obliterated the old personality. Birth and environment had for all time determined that. He could go further. The changes of the last five thousand years were superficial. Information had been piled up. A super-language based on this information had been developed, but within the limits of civilization only to a negligible extent had the human race increased its power to think or feel. That fact was the foundation of democracy. 

Finally reaching Havre, he found Miss Cheney's and 

Miss Reed's names among the list of passengers who had sailed the previous Thursday. He crossed the channel and took ship for Boston.

XLVI THE CEDAR CHEST 

When Clay entered the Bell office in Boston the president gave him an enthusiastic welcome and took him home to Beverly Farms. That evening Hudson exhibited his collection of engravings and showed his finest bindings. Clay hardly found opportunity to report on his inspection of the Milan exchange. The president agreed that the Bell ought not install automatic exchanges until it was certain that the step would not have to be retraced. "We must remember,' he said, "that like Gaul, the

Bell is divided into three parts. There is the financial organization, that furnishes the life blood; then there is the technical division that adjusts materials to natural law; and finally there is the social structure that is the aim and end of all. Receiving is as important as transmitting. If automatic switchboards become technically perfect, and our subscribers are not prepared to use them, their adoption should be delayed. I fear it may be a long time before the public will be able to dispense with the human element in exchanges." Again the executive advised Clay to make the most of his vacation, for when he returned to the Bell he would find that strenuous times were ahead. 

The following afternoon Clay arrived in New York.

He took a cab at the Central Station and gave Arthur 443

Pendleton's address. If Arthur were still at the office,

Fanny would probably be home. He ran up the steps and rang the bell two or three times, but there was no response. He left reluctantly and walked toward the 

Park, and then turned up the Avenue in the direction of the Van Alstyne home. It might be just as well to see Dora first.

The house had a dismal look as he approached. 

Apparently it was closed for the summer. He pushed the button, but there was no reply. Again he rang the bell, and then heard a sound above. A maid was looking from an upper window. She came to the door, and in reply to Clay's inquiries, said Miss Reed had not stayed there since she returned from abroad. She had been with the Pendletons, but now was with friends in 

Montclair, at 212 Ailanthus Street. 

At his hotel that evening he tried to get Arthur on the telephone, but there was no response. He decided to go to Hemlock to see Sophrona and find out how the Hemlock company was getting along, but the next morning while on the way to the station he suddenly thought it best to go to 212 Ailanthus Street, Montclair. If he intruded, that was all right. 

Ailanthus Street was in the suburbs, and he had some difficulty in locating it. As he approached the two hundreds the residences became rather pretty. Two hundred twelve was a white square house, with a wing and porches, neatly painted. The lawn of moderate size was well cared for, and there were abundant bright flowers. At the side was a small building that Clay guessed was a playhouse for children. As he entered the gate a little breathlessly and passed up the asphalt walk, a child on a tricycle came suddenly around the corner and bumped into his shins. The tricycle cap-

THE CEDAR CHEST 

445 sized, but Clay caught the youngster before he struck the walk. The child's frightened howl brought a small girl from the playhouse. "Mamma, mamma, John has run into a man."

While Clay was trying to soothe the frightened child, a woman, followed by the little girl and another little boy, came down from the porch. "Why, John, don't cry," she called in a pleasant voice. "You are all right.' 99 Clay turned to place the child in her arms and looked into the smiling face of Alice Stevens. "Why, Joseph Clay!" she exclaimed. "Dora left here this very morning, and doesn't know that you have returned from abroad." "It seems that I can never overtake her; I have missed her by short margins on several occasions,” he said, and looked down at the child who had slipped from his mother's arms and was clinging shyly to her dress. "What fine brown eyes he has!" "He's a perfect picture of his father." "And those are all your children-" he inquired, but paused because he did not know Miss Stevens' married name. "Mrs. Brown," she supplied with a smile. “You have seen only part of my family. Come into the house and I'll show you my other little girl and the baby.” "Well, I never would have believed it," said Clay. "To think you're married and have five children." "I don't know what future you foresaw for me," she replied laughing, "but in spite of any dismal expectations, here I am, happily married to William Brown, and I am the mother of five healthy children; and there's not a thing in the world I envy of anybody."

"I thought you had too many great plans and cared too much for nature and books to settle down to housekeeping." "I haven't lost my pleasure in nature and books," she replied, "but other interests mean more to me."

She paused, and brushed the brown hair from the shy eyes of the little boy who stood at her side. "I hope when I was a teacher in Hemlock I never gave you and Dora the idea that I thought there was anything better than plans for a home.' 

Clay was startled; he hardly knew what answer was fitting. "Oh, no; you never said anything that made us think less of our homes." "I am glad if you understood it so," replied Mrs. 

Brown, "for I had begun to fear I might have led you and Dora into seeking your happiness in some sort of mirage, since neither of you ever married." "Dora has been studying all this time," responded

Clay, "and I was graduated from college only last year.' 99 "I was married at twenty-four, and I think I waited too long. I had thought of graduate study, but I concluded it was better to use life as education than try to use education as life. Here comes my husband.” 

While they were exchanging greetings, Mrs. Brown picked a tiny shaving from Mr. Brown's shoulder. "See," she said. "Mr. Brown has just come from his spoke factory. There isn't a better hickory spoke made in America than my husband produces.' 

Then she withdrew to give her children their luncheon, and Clay and Mr. Brown sat in the well-stored library and chatted about hickory wheels, hardwood forests, and books. Mr. Brown's laughter flowed

THE CEDAR CHEST 

447 easily and reminded Clay of his father. Mr. Brown seemed to be a schooled reincarnation of George Clay. 

As Mrs. Brown passed through the room she caught the expression on Clay's face. "Joe," she said, "doesn't 

Mr. Brown remind you of your father?" And, without waiting for a reply, she added, "I noticed it the first time I saw him."

During a pleasant luncheon Clay learned that Dora had gone to her Adirondack cottage where she was to entertain the Pendletons. The Browns urged him to stay, but he left early, and took a night train for the mountains.

Before noon he arrived at Lake Placid and secured a driver with horse and buggy to take him to the Van Alstyne lodge. When they had traveled a little more than half way they came to a causeway so narrow that vehicles were unable to pass. Waiting at the further end was a station wagon drawn by sorrel horses. The driver was a woman; and, sure enough, Dora. "Why, Joe," she exclaimed, "I didn't know you were back." "Three days ago I landed in Boston," he answered, as he climbed from the buggy, "and now I'm looking for a job as driver.” 'You don't know where I'm going," she laughed, as he took the reins and waited for the other vehicle to turn and precede them. "That doesn't matter," he said, as he gazed at his fellow traveler in the airy white dress. Again beside him, as when he and Dora had driven along the swamp road, he saw the slender womanly figure, the delicately rounded features, the fair fresh skin, the sunshiny hair, and gray eyes with long brown lashes. Only fallen

petals from cardinal flowers were needed to complete the vision he had cherished through the years. 

Dora dropped her eyes, embarrassed by his gaze. "Well, you've come at exactly the right time. I'm on my way to meet Fanny and Arthur." "Then before we meet Fanny and Arthur is exactly the right time to ask you to marry me." 99 "You're trying to startle me with your abruptness,” responded Dora blushing. "Don't you know that such a question requires preliminaries?" "The preliminary was a race to Milan, from Milan to 

Borlindo, from Borlindo to Villa Fontana, from Villa 

Fontana to Havre from Havre to so many other places that I don't want to delay your answer by mentioning them." "You went to Villa Fontana!" "How could I do otherwise when I heard that you had been captured by brigands?" "I am ashamed of that letter; I didn't think you'd ever see Villa Fontana or be able to decipher my nonsense." "Even now I don't know what you meant by saying you were in prison." "It's a feeling I've had ever since I left college," answered Dora seriously. “At Villa Fontana it merely came to a climax-I wanted to sacrifice everything and everybody to my own improvement. I built walls to keep everybody out, and it was some time before I realized that I had shut myself in." "That was the prison?" "Yes, and I thought how different your life had been, and it seemed to me as if you were the only person who could rescue me.”

THE CEDAR CHEST 

449 "I didn't find that idea in the letters you wrote while 

I was in college." "I was ready to give up my career when I saw you in New York just before I went abroad, but I didn't want to throw myself at you. It seemed to me then that you were holding me off." "I was cherishing the idea that I had no right to ask you to marry me until I had gone to college to get some of the training I had missed."

Dora did not seem to be pleased with his reply. "And now," she retorted scornfully, "because you wear fine clothes, have polished manners, and can understand two or three languages, I suppose you have that right." "That's not fair. You know I have always loved you." "People change." "My love has not changed. Will you marry me, Dora ?" "You are sure-very, very sure you will always love me?" 

He took her in his arms and passionately kissed her again and again. "I guess that settles it," she said a little breathlessly, wiping a tear from her eyes, and looking in alarm at the horses that had drawn the vehicle perilously near the brink of the road. "Then after this we won't have to live on different sides of the ocean?" "We'll go to Hemlock to live!" she exclaimed, exultingly throwing her arms aloft as if glad that the preliminaries were over. "We'll repair the parsonage. We'll cut balsams that hide the view. We'll level the ground

and plant a garden with paths and flowers. We'll set hollyhocks along the wall!" 'We'll have to do something like that to keep up with our neighbors. You'll be surprised to see how everything's changed. There's nothing the same but the mountains." "I wonder if they will look the same to us." "If they don't," ventured Joe, "perhaps little ones will come to tell us what they see." 

Dora hesitated, but couldn't resist the challenge. "Perhaps they will walk with us in our garden and watch the butterflies flutter over our phlox." “And laugh at bumble bees tumbling from our tiger lilies," added Joe, but he uttered the words mechanically, as if his mind were not on what he said.

She wondered at his abstraction, and for a moment they were silent. 

Suddenly Joe exclaimed, "I can't live in Hemlock!" 

Dora turned in great surprise, "You-not care to live in Hemlock?" "I want to go to Boston to work for the Bell. It offers the best opportunity in America to have my work count. President Hudson needs all the help he can get. I want to stay with him as long as he lives."

Joe regarded Dora doubtfully, fearful of the effect of his announcement. 

Dora laughed. "Then we'll live in Boston, and go to the parsonage for our vacations."

The sorrel horses plodded through the pleasant streets of Lake Placid. At the station Joe and Dora heard the whistle of the approaching train. The long express came rolling slowly by, and from an open window they saw Fanny and Arthur waving.

THE END
